# Switchboard's guide

Every feature, key and command of Switchboard, as the `switchboard` binary has them. [README.md](../README.md) is the place to start: what Switchboard is, installing it and the first keys. `M` in a key is Alt (Option on a Mac), and a capital letter after it is with Shift: `M-N` is Alt+Shift+n.

## The workspace

`switchboard` alone starts the workspace, or attaches to it when it is already running. It needs a terminal, and it refuses to run inside one of its own panes ("already inside Switchboard"), since a workspace drawn inside itself feeds on itself.

### The server

The first `switchboard` starts a server in the background, in a session of its own, and attaches your terminal to it. The server owns every pane: each pane's program runs on a terminal the server keeps, so closing the window, or losing an SSH connection, leaves every program running, Claude included. The next `switchboard` attaches to the same server and finds everything as it was. The server listens on `~/.local/state/switchboard/sock`, which only you can reach, and writes what it has to say to `~/.local/state/switchboard/server.log`, which is where to look when it doesn't come up. Only one server runs at a time.

`M-b d` detaches, leaving everything running. `switchboard detach` does the same from a script, for every attached terminal or, with `-s <name>`, those on one session. `switchboard kill-server` ends the server and every program in its panes, after saving the layout. A server also ends by itself once its last tab closes. `switchboard upgrade` restarts the server on the build installed now without ending anything in it ([Restarting on a new build](#restarting-on-a-new-build)).

### Sessions and clients

A session is a set of tabs. `switchboard` attaches to the first session there is, or makes one named `main`; `switchboard -s <name>` attaches to the session by that name, making it, with one tab, when there is none. A new session's first tab runs your login shell in your home directory.

Several terminals can attach at once. Terminals on the same session show the same tab, and the tabs are sized to whichever of them you used last. `M-a`, the sidebar and mission control can take you to a tab in another session, and your terminal moves to that session. When a session's last tab closes, its terminals move to another session, or end when there is none.

### What a pane carries

A pane runs your login shell (`$SHELL`) unless something asked for another program. Its environment carries `SWITCHBOARD`, the server's socket, and `SWITCHBOARD_PANE`, the pane's id (`%12`), which is how `switchboard <command>` and Claude Code's hooks know which server and which pane they run in. `CLAUDE_CODE_PLUGIN_DIRS` names Switchboard's plugin (`~/.local/share/switchboard/claude/plugin`, after any directories the server was started with) while Settings' "Load Switchboard as a plugin" is on, so Claude Code run there loads its hooks, skills and instructions ([Setting Claude Code up](#setting-claude-code-up)). `TERM` is `xterm-256color` and `COLORTERM` is `truecolor`. A program that asks its window's size in pixels is told your terminal's, as its cell's size times the pane's (where your terminal says its own; 8 by 16 pixels a cell where it doesn't), so pictures in Notes and the PR as an image are drawn at their own size. A pane's id stays the same for as long as the pane exists, across restarts too, so a pane named in a note or a script is the same pane after one.

A pane closes when its program ends. The colors a program asks for by name are your terminal's own, so your terminal's theme applies inside every pane; the panes without the focus are drawn on a slightly different background, and the lines around the focused pane take its tab's color.

### Saved and restored

The server saves the layout as it changes: every session with its tabs in order, each tab's name, splits, zoom and options (a reminder, a pin, a note), and each pane's directory, the program in front of it, and the Claude session it runs. It goes to `~/.local/state/switchboard/layout.json`, which only you can read, at most every ten seconds, and again when the server ends; the save before it is kept as `layout.json.1`. A layout that has lost panes is written only once it has stayed so for a minute, or when `switchboard kill-server` ends the server, so a computer shutting down, which ends the shells one by one, never leaves a save missing some of them; a tab opened in that minute is saved with the rest once it has passed. `switchboard save` writes it now and prints where.

When the server next starts with nothing open, as after a reboot, it brings the last save back: every session, tab and pane, with the same ids. Each pane starts your shell in its saved directory, `$HOME` when that is gone. A pane that ran Claude has `claude --resume <session>` typed into that shell, so the same conversation carries on and a shell is there when it ends. A pane that ran `nvim`, `vim`, `vi`, `less`, `man`, `htop` or `top` has that program typed again; any other comes back as its shell, since running a command a second time, a deploy or a migration, can do harm. Settings' "Restore on start" (`auto_restore`) turns this off. A save that can't be read is set aside as `layout.json.bad` and the server starts fresh.

Each time the server starts with nothing open, before it writes anything, it keeps the larger of `layout.json` and `layout.json.1` as `layout.json.start`, whether it restores it or not, and leaves it there until the next such start. So when the tabs didn't come back after a restart, or came back fewer, close all but one tab and choose `M-p`'s "Restore the layout from before the last start" (`switchboard restore-layout`): the tabs kept there open beside the one you have, as a start brings them back, each Claude resumed but one already running in the workspace, whose pane comes back as its shell. It refuses while other tabs are open, so no conversation is resumed twice, and when the file has no more tabs than the workspace. `switchboard restore-layout <file>` brings back another save's tabs the same way, each pane as a fresh shell, with nothing typed into it and none of what it showed, since a file named by hand can come from anywhere.

With Settings' "Save what panes show" (`save_contents`) on, each save also keeps every pane's screen and the last 2,000 lines of its history, read again once a minute, and a restored pane shows them above its new prompt; it also keeps a closed tab's last screen for the Reopen view. What a pane showed can hold secrets: turn it off to keep only the layout, and no screen of a closed tab.

### Scrollback, the mouse and the clipboard

Each pane keeps 10,000 lines of history. The wheel scrolls back through it, and scrolling down to the bottom ends it; `M-b [` is copy mode, which moves through it and selects with the keyboard, using Vim's keys as tmux's copy mode did. A cursor of its own comes up where the program's cursor is, and the view scrolls to keep it in sight:

| Key | What it does |
|---|---|
| `h` `j` `k` `l`, the arrows | The cursor a cell or a line |
| `w` `b` `e` (`W` `B` `E`) | The next word, the word before, the end of the word (words between blanks) |
| `0` `$` `^` | The start of the line, its end, its first character |
| `g` `G` | The top of the history, the bottom |
| `H` `M` `L` | The top, middle and bottom of the screen |
| `C-u` `C-d` | Half a screen up or down, the cursor with it |
| `C-b` `C-f`, `PageUp` `PageDown` | A screen up or down |
| `C-y` `C-e` | The view a line up or down, the cursor staying |
| `v` or `Space`, `V`, `C-v` | Select from the cursor: characters, whole lines, a rectangle; the same key again takes the selection away |
| `y` or `Enter` | Copy the selection, and out |
| `/` `?` | Search down or up, for text or a regular expression typed in a box (a capital makes case count) |
| `n` `N` | The next match the same way, the other way |
| `Esc` | Take the selection away; with none, out |
| `q` | Back to the bottom, and out |

While a pane is scrolled back, its top-right corner shows how many lines back it is (`[120]`), with a scroll bar down its last column under it showing where in its history the view is, which goes again at the bottom; a click on the bar's track pages toward the click and dragging its thumb scrolls with it. Other keys do nothing. A key in the wheel's scrolling brings up copy mode's cursor, and it then stays until `q`. A paste takes it back to the bottom and goes to the program.

A click focuses a pane. Dragging selects text and copies it when you let go; a double click selects a word, a triple click a line, and Alt-drag selects a rectangle. A program that asks for the mouse, such as an editor, gets the clicks, drags and wheel instead. In a full-screen program that doesn't take the mouse, the wheel sends it arrow keys. Dragging the line between two panes moves it, resizing them. A click on a tab in the status line goes to it, and the wheel over the status line goes to the tab before or after.

What you copy goes to the computer's clipboard and to your terminal's (OSC 52), which reaches the computer you are at even over SSH, as long as your terminal lets programs set its clipboard. A program in the pane in front of you can copy the same way; one in a pane you aren't looking at can't, so a command in a background tab never changes what you paste next, and Settings' "Programs in panes copy" (`pane_clipboard`) off ignores every pane's copy. One that asks to read the clipboard gets nothing back: the clipboard is yours, not every program's.

A link a program in a pane writes as a hyperlink (OSC 8), as Claude Code, `ls --hyperlink` and the views do, reaches your terminal as one, so it opens as it would outside Switchboard: Cmd-click in most terminals on a Mac, Ctrl-click elsewhere, with Shift too where the pane's program takes the mouse. A box or mission control drawn over it isn't part of the link.

### Panes and tabs

A tab is split into panes with `M-H`, `M-J`, `M-K` and `M-L`, each opening a shell in the current pane's directory on that side. `C-M-S-h`, `C-M-S-j`, `C-M-S-k` and `C-M-S-l` (Ctrl+Alt+Shift with a Vim direction) move the current pane's divider that way, five cells at a time: its divider on that side when it has one, growing it, else the one on the other side, shrinking it, as tmux's `resize-pane` did. They need a terminal that reports Ctrl+Alt+Shift letters apart, as Ghostty and kitty do; in another, bind `resize-left-5` and the rest to keys it sends in `keys.json` (see "Changing the keys"). `M-=` zooms the pane to the whole tab and back. Resizing your terminal keeps each pane's share of the tab. `M-w` closes a pane; in the tab's Claude pane, in a tab's one pane, or on its row in the sidebar, it closes the tab instead (see "Closing a busy pane" below).

A tab with one pane, or a pane zoomed with `M-=`, fills the width. On a wide screen, Settings' "Widest a lone pane gets" (`lone_pane_width`, off unless you choose one) caps it at 120, 140, 165, 200 or 240 columns, or any number from 80 typed in its menu's "Columns…" (or `switchboard setting lone_pane_width <n>`), against the sidebar, its program given that width, the columns at its right the sidebar's darker shade with a rule down the pane's right edge, so a program that pins columns to its right edge keeps them beside what they are about (UX 2.0 board 62). Beside a tab's capped pane those columns list, centered in them under "Beside this pane", what the pane can open, each with the key bound to it now and a click that does what the key does: the tab's note (`M-e`), a split to the right (`M-L`), the PR (`M-v`) and the ticket (`M-i`), shown in Claude's place with UX 2.0 on, when the tab has one and GitHub or Linear is on, and a link read beside the pane (`M-u`), with how to widen the pane under them (board 77); where they don't fit, or with Settings' "Hints beside a narrow pane" (`spare_hints`) off, the columns stay plain. What opens in a pane's place (the diff with `M-d`, lazygit, the PR and the ticket with UX 2.0 on) opens in those columns instead when at least 80 of them are spare and nothing is beside the pane, the pane keeping its width at their left (but for the diff, which takes three quarters of the width beside the sidebar, Settings' "Diff's share beside a narrow pane", narrowing the pane while it is open); the same key, `q` or `M-w` closes it and the tab is one capped pane again, with fewer columns spare it opens in the pane's place, and Settings' "Open views beside a narrow pane" (`views_beside_narrow_pane`) off always opens it there. A box about the tab that a key opens (the links, pictures and tabs the pane mentions with `M-u` and `M-p`'s commands for them, answering the tab's Claude, which note is the tab's, naming or renaming the tab, the tab's menu and `M-T`'s full title, `M-m`'s PR readiness, sending its Claude's last reply to another tab, Notes' Work on this item) opens in those columns instead, drawn as a pane: as tall as they are, a header line naming it and the tab, the rule at the pane's edge its only border, no shadow, the hints hidden while it is open (UX 2.0 board 111); Esc, a choice or a click elsewhere closes it. Every other box (`M-p`, `M-F`, `M-j`, the new tab picker, a `[Y/n]` question, a message, a notification, a tip and mission control's boxes) goes where it would without the cap, in the middle of the screen with what is behind it dimmed (board 112), and so does a box about the tab with too few columns spare or one a click opened. Settings' "Open boxes beside a narrow pane" (`boxes_beside_narrow_pane`) off puts every box in the middle of the screen. With a cap chosen, mission control showing one view is capped the same way, its bar and footer at that width too, while several views at once (`=`) fill the width, and so does a view with a pane beside its list and an item chosen (Autopilot, PRs, Tickets, Inbox, Reopen, Slack, CI, Landing, Reminders, Routines, the Calendar's day), with UX 2.0 on, whose pane is a second one. A split shares out the whole width at once, and a divider moved past the cap stays there; closing back to one pane caps it again. A click elsewhere beside a capped pane or view does nothing, but the rule at a lone pane's right edge drags as a divider does, the pane growing or shrinking as it goes, giving that tab alone a width of its own (from 80 columns; dragged to the screen's edge, that tab's pane fills the width), kept through restarts, while `lone_pane_width` stays as it is for every other tab; `C-M-S-l` and `C-M-S-h` move it five columns the same way in a tab with one pane, and in mission control with one view and no pane beside its list give mission control a width of its own. "Use the default width", in the tab's menu (a right click on its row in the sidebar, Enter on its card in the Tabs view) while it has one, gives the tab the setting's width again, and in `M-p` gives it to the tab and to mission control. A picture Claude or a skill shows you for feedback (`switchboard look <file>...`) goes in those columns too, as a pane under a header naming the file and the tab, scaled to fit, two or more stacked top to bottom with each file's name over it (the header then "Before and after" for two), while the pane keeps its keys: `M-Esc`, the header's × or a click on them closes them, and with more than fit at a readable size the wheel over them and `M-PgDn` and `M-PgUp` page through them. With no such columns (a split, a wide pane, another tab in front), more than six pictures, a terminal that draws no images or Settings' "Show images beside a narrow pane" off, each opens in the desktop's viewer, as a video or a PDF always does; any other kind of file is refused.

Every tab has a number, which the tab bar, the sidebar, mission control, the Slack relay and `switchboard tabs` show and `M-1`…`M-9` and `M-0` go to: the smallest no other open tab has when it opens, in any session, kept while the tab is open, through a restart and `switchboard upgrade` (its `@tab_number` option, saved with the layout). Closing a tab renumbers no other, and the next tab opened takes the number it freed. The tabs keep their places in the tab bar and the sidebar, which their numbers needn't follow, so moving a tab changes its place, not its number. The Notifications window takes none.

A tab something you did opens (a key, a palette command, a menu's choice, a card's action, `switchboard new-tab` without `-d`) comes to the front, so it is what you see; when one choice opens several, the first comes to the front. A tab the workspace opens on its own stays behind the one in front: autopilot's tabs, the Calendar and Slack loop tabs a view opens when it is out of date, the Notifications window, and the Slack relay's.

### Claude in the tabs

Switchboard's hooks for Claude Code, which its plugin gives the Claude in its panes (or, with the plugin off, `switchboard setup` adds to your `~/.claude/settings.json`), run `switchboard hook` at each step of a session, and the workspace marks the pane accordingly. A pane is in one of three states, or none:

- **working**: from the moment you send a prompt, through every tool call and a compaction in the middle of a turn, and while a `/compact` you typed runs. A compaction Claude Code makes on its own between turns (while idle, before the prompt cache expires, or a summary it writes ahead of time in the background) leaves the tab as it was, since nothing says when a summary written ahead ends.
- **waiting**: on your permission for a tool, or on your answer to a question, its own or one a tool asks through it. A question is a wait from the moment it is asked. A wait belongs to whoever asked, the conversation or one of its subagents, so a background agent's tool calls while Claude waits on you leave the tab waiting, and to its call, so a background agent finishing, or a monitor firing, while a question is up leaves it waiting too, under Needs you rather than its loop's stage, until the question itself is answered; a question still unanswered in the conversation whose hook never arrived is read from the transcript and marks the pane waiting too. Denying a permission request, or pressing Esc on it, ends the turn without running any hook, so the workspace reads the interruption from the conversation and hands the pane back as idle, a Claude parked behind the diff or lazygit included (its request can be denied from the Slack relay), unless it has moved on since with a new prompt or request. Pressing Esc while Claude works, on a command auto mode let run without asking, ends the turn without a hook the same way, and the pane goes idle unless a prompt came after it. A `/compact` you typed that fails or that you cancel runs no hook either, and the pane goes idle once Claude Code says its session is idle again.
- **done**: the turn ended and you haven't looked at the tab since. A turn that ends in the tab you are looking at is never marked done.

A tab shows the most urgent of its panes' states, waiting before done before working, as a badge in the status line and in the sidebar. A new or ended session clears the state, and a pane that no longer runs Claude loses its state the next time you move between tabs, so a session that died without saying so leaves nothing behind, even when the shell back at its prompt got the dead Claude's process number. A `claude -p` that a script or a loop starts in the tab doesn't mark it, and a conversation moved into the background still shows on the tab it came from.

"done" clears once the tab has been in front for a second, so passing over a tab, as moving the sidebar's cursor does, leaves the mark. Settings' "Finished tabs clear" (`done_clears`) can keep it instead until you next prompt that session ("on prompt"; "when seen" is the default).

Whatever the workspace types into a tab's Claude (`M-m`'s `/resolve-conflicts`, a CI loop started from mission control, a message answering a tab from mission control, the sidebar or the Slack relay, a reply sent from another tab) presses `i` first when Claude Code's vim mode has left the prompt in Normal mode (its mode line reading `-- NORMAL --`), where the first letters typed would otherwise be taken as vim commands and what is typed would arrive cut off.

`M-a` goes to the next tab that needs you: pinned tabs and reminders that have come due first, then the ones waiting on you, then the finished ones, then the ones under Build failing (Settings' "M-a visits Build failing tabs", `walk_build_failing`), and last the sidebar's notifications while there are any (Settings' "M-a goes to notifications", `needs_notices`). Pressed again, it goes on to the next, wrapping round, so pressing it repeatedly visits each one. It says "Nothing needs you." when nothing does. `M-A` walks the idle tabs the same way (see "What needs you, one tab at a time"). `M-c` goes to the pane running Claude in the current tab.

`M-y` copies the latest reply of the tab's Claude to the clipboard, from any pane of the tab or its sidebar: the last message Claude wrote text in, read from its session's transcript, its parts joined as written, reaching back past a prompt not answered yet and passing over subagents, API errors and Claude Code's own stand-in messages. A Claude parked behind the diff or lazygit in its pane's place counts. A box says how many lines were copied, and that Claude is still working when it is, or that the tab has no Claude or no reply yet. Settings' "Copy Claude's reply" (`copy_reply`) off hands `M-y` to the pane.

### Keeping the computer awake

While a tab's Claude is working, the workspace keeps the computer from sleeping when it is left idle, so autopilot's tabs, a review loop or a long test run carry on while you're away. A tab counts while its Claude works in the foreground, and while background work or watches it started run (a review loop, a CI watch, a Monitor), between turns or while it waits on you; a `/loop` waiting for its next run doesn't, since it may wait for hours. While autopilot is on and not paused, each of its tabs open and not yet landed counts too, waiting on you or not. Once nothing counts, the workspace lets go two minutes later, so the moment between one tool call and the next doesn't let go and take it again.

On macOS it runs `caffeinate -is` (watching the server, so it ends with it), on Linux it asks logind for a sleep inhibitor; nothing has to be installed. The display still sleeps, and a laptop closed with no display on it sleeps anyway. While it holds, a ☕ shows after the health light on the status line, or with UX 2.0 after the tab count at the top of the sidebar, and the first time work holds it a tip says so. `switchboard list` says the same in its `awake`: whether it holds (`holding`), what holds it (`why`: `3 tabs working`, `autopilot has 2 tabs open`, `until you say`; none in the two minutes after everything went idle) and whether by hand (`by_hand`).

M-p's "Keep the computer awake until I say" holds it whatever the tabs do, through a restart on a new build too, until you choose it again, which goes back to the rule above. Settings' "Keep the computer awake while tabs work" (`keep_awake`) off leaves the computer to sleep on its own schedule unless held from M-p.

### The queue for heavy work

Only so many builds, test runs and package installs go at once across every pane, so that many tabs building together share the computer's cores instead of each build taking all of them and every one running slower. Every pane finds Switchboard's own `cargo`, `npm` and `pnpm` first on its PATH (links to the binary in `~/.local/share/switchboard/bin`, made as the server starts (but a demo's), and put first again for every command Claude runs there by its session-start hook, after your shell's startup files, which may put the real ones first). Each runs the real program further down the PATH (a `cargo` the PATH lacks in rustup's usual folders: `$CARGO_HOME/bin` or `~/.cargo/bin`, then Homebrew's `/opt/homebrew/opt/rustup/bin` and `/usr/local/opt/rustup/bin` on macOS, `/home/linuxbrew/.linuxbrew/opt/rustup/bin` on Linux, run with that folder added to the end of its PATH so it finds `rustc` beside it; with none anywhere it says "cargo isn't on your PATH or in rustup's usual folders"), at once for anything light (`cargo fmt`, `cargo metadata`, `cargo run`, `npm view`, `--version`), and the heavy work through a slot of its kind:

- **Builds** (`cargo build`, `check`, `clippy`, `doc`, `install`, `fix`, and the compile half of `cargo test`), Settings' "Builds at once" (`slots_build`), a quarter of the cores by default (`auto`), each given `CARGO_BUILD_JOBS` at the cores over the cap, so the builds share them.
- **Test runs** (the run half of `cargo test` and `cargo bench`, and `cargo nextest run` whole, whose compile can't be had apart from its run), "Test runs at once" (`slots_tests`), 2 by default, each given `RUST_TEST_THREADS` and `NEXTEST_TEST_THREADS` at the cores over the cap. `cargo test` compiles first, as `cargo test --no-run` holding a build slot, then runs the same `cargo test` holding a test run slot, so a long test run never keeps a build out.
- **Package builds** (`npm ci`, `npm install`, `npm run build`, `pnpm install`, `pnpm build`), "Package builds at once" (`slots_js`), 2 by default.

A command with no slot free waits its turn, in the order the commands came, and says so once on its stderr: "Waiting for a build slot (2nd in line)". Run in a pane, it also tells the server as it starts waiting, takes its slot and lets it go (`switchboard slot-report`, never holding the command up more than a second), so the workspace shows it: a tab whose command waits stays under Working with ◌ in place of its spinner and "waits for a build slot, 2nd" as its line, and its card's Now row has the ◌ and how long it has waited, with a chip "waits for a build slot · 2nd"; a tab holding a slot looks as any working tab, with a dim chip naming it ("test slot 1 of 2"). Mission control's Resources view lists the slots above its table, a column for each kind headed by how many of its cap are held: each holder (its tab, its command and how long it has held it), a line for each free slot, then who waits, in order, with how long each has waited, or "nothing waits"; a waiting tab's row in the table says what it waits for ("cargo build, waiting for a build slot"). Enter on a holder or waiter offers to go to its tab or to stop its command and everything it started, asked first. A cap lowered while slots above it are held counts them until their commands end. A report whose process has ended without saying so is dropped within a second. A slot is a lock file in `~/.local/state/switchboard/slots/<kind>/`, held by the command's own process for as long as it runs, so a command that ends, crashes or is killed lets its slot go at once; nothing is left to clean. A command holding a slot runs with `SWITCHBOARD_SLOT` set, and nothing it starts waits for another, so an npm script running cargo or a build script running cargo can't wait on its own parent; `CARGO_BUILD_JOBS` or the thread counts set in the command's environment are kept. `switchboard slot <build|tests|js> -- <command>` runs any command through the queue the same way. A command waiting reads the caps again as it waits, so a cap raised lets it start and one turned off lets it go on at once. A cap of 0 (off) lets that kind run at once, and Settings' "Queue heavy work" (`slot_queue`) off runs everything at once, as the real programs would. `switchboard try` keeps its slots in its own home, so a try never waits on your tabs.

### A tab's history

`M-h` (or the header's `M-h History` key with UX 2.0) shows the history of the tab's Claude session: a timeline under the tab's screen, from when the session started to now, the span filling the timeline's width, so a column is a little under a minute for an hour and a half on a wide screen. Its lanes are the session's state (working blue, waiting on you yellow, finished green, idle grey), your turns (`▸`), commits (`•` and the commit's short hash), review rounds (`◆ round 2`, `round 3 clean`) and the context it used, as a chart with each compaction marked by a dashed line and what it left (`compacted 66% → 18%`); the state lane and the context chart are images at pixel resolution where the terminal draws them (Ghostty, kitty), and blocks of text elsewhere. A line through the lanes marks the moment shown, and the screen above shows the pane as it was then, with a chip under it while it waited on you saying for how long and what you chose (`waited 6m for you · you chose 1. Fix it`); the header says which moment it is (`viewing 1:41 PM, 51 minutes ago`), as the keys row does without UX 2.0. While the diff, a PR or ticket view or lazygit stands in Claude's place, `M-h` first brings Claude back beside it, as `M-p`'s "side" does, so what you type when the history closes reaches Claude.

`h` and `l` (or Left and Right, or the wheel) move a column back and on, `[` and `]` to the turn before and after, `c` to the next commit and `w` to the next wait (left off its keys row), each from the first once past the last; a click on the lanes shows that moment (with menu_mouse on), and easy mode's × at the keys row's end, or a click on the header, closes it. Esc comes back to now, and at now closes it, as `M-h` does; any other key closes it and goes on to the tab, so typing reaches Claude as before.

The state and the screens come from what the server saw: each change of state as the hooks say it, and a snapshot of the screen at most every 15 seconds while it changes and as its state changes, kept in the server's memory alone, never written to disk, the oldest going after about six hours of change, and gone when the server ends (a moment before the first shows "No screen was kept from then"). The rest is read from the session's transcript as the history opens and on while it is open, so those lanes reach back to the session's start. A new session in the pane (started, `/clear` or resumed, not a compaction) starts its history again. A tab whose Claude has run no hook since the server started (one restarted on a new build) opens from the session Switchboard reads for its pane from Claude Code's session files, its lanes read from the transcript and its screen kept from then on; a tab with no Claude session says it has no history yet. Settings' "Tab history" (`tab_history`) off keeps nothing, and `M-h` says it is off.

`M-f` pins the tab: it goes to the top of Needs you, ahead of everything else there, whatever Claude is doing in it, and stays there until you press `M-f` in it again, since visiting it doesn't clear it.

A tab holds one Claude. A session started in a tab that already runs one is moved to a tab of its own as it starts, and a box says so, since the sidebar, mission control, the Slack relay, `M-c` and `M-m` each take a tab's Claude to be one pane (Settings' "One Claude to a tab", `one_claude_per_tab`).

Each notification Claude Code sends (it waits on your permission or an answer, or has sat idle) also shows as a desktop notification, titled with the tab's number and name, `Claude Code: 3:app:fix-login`, so you know where to go: a banner on macOS, and the desktop's notifications on Linux. A reminder that comes due shows one too. Settings' "Push notifications" (`notifications`) turns them off.

## Keys

`M-` is Alt: Option on a Mac. A capital letter after `M-` is Alt with Shift (`M-H` is Alt+Shift+h). These keys work from any pane; a key not bound here goes to the program in the pane.

| Key | What it does |
|---|---|
| `M-Left` `M-Down` `M-Up` `M-Right` | Move to the pane on that side, coming round to the far side past an edge, as tmux did; `M-Left` from the leftmost pane, and `M-Right` from the rightmost, go into the sidebar, the leftmost of all. In the diff and Notes they move between the app's own windows first |
| `M-H` `M-J` `M-K` `M-L` | Split the pane to the left, below, above or to the right |
| `M-w` | Close the pane, asking first when a program runs in it; in the tab's Claude pane, in a tab's one pane, or on its sidebar row, close the tab after asking |
| `M-=` | Zoom the pane in or out |
| `C-M-S-h` `C-M-S-j` `C-M-S-k` `C-M-S-l` | Move the pane's divider left, down, up or right, growing or shrinking it (a terminal that reports Ctrl+Alt+Shift letters, such as Ghostty or kitty); with one pane, `C-M-S-h` and `C-M-S-l` move a lone pane's right edge, giving that tab a width of its own, as they give mission control one with one view there; in mission control, `C-M-S-h` and `C-M-S-l` widen and narrow the pane beside a board |
| `M-{` `M-}` | Back and forward through the tabs and mission control views you visited, as a browser's buttons, doing nothing with nowhere further to go: onto a view, mission control opens on it over the tab it was open over (the tab in front when that one has closed); onto a tab, mission control closes; a view turned off in Settings since is passed over. Opening mission control on a view, moving to another view and going to a tab each add a step; M-j's and M-o's pickers don't. With Settings' `tab_back_forward` off, the tab on the left and right, which `M-b p` and `M-b n` and the palette's Previous tab and Next tab always are, closing mission control first |
| `M-Tab` | Go back to the tab or mission control view you were on before, and pressed again, back to where you were; with Settings' `tab_back_forward` off, the tab you were on before |
| `M-1` … `M-9` | Go to the tab with that number, in whichever session it is |
| `M-0` | Go to a tab by its number, typed in a box, for the tabs past 9 |
| `C-w` then `h` `j` `k` `l` | Move to the pane on that side, as in Vim; in Vim, the diff and Notes `C-w` is theirs, and `C-w C-w` sends one `C-w` to the pane |
| `M-t`, `M-n` | New tab running Claude, in a directory you pick |
| `M-j` | Find a tab by typing its name or what its panes show, and go to it |
| `M-o` | Reopen a closed tab or resume a past Claude session |
| `M-W` | Start work on Linear tickets, each in a new tab (`wt`) |
| `M-B` | Start work on a branch, new or existing, in a new tab (`wtb`) |
| `M-I` | Start work on a note's checklist items, each in a new tab (`wtn`) |
| `M-a` | Go to the next tab that needs you, then the Build failing ones |
| `M-A` | Go to the next idle tab, in the sidebar's Idle order |
| `M-c` | Go to the pane running Claude in this tab |
| `M-y` | Copy the latest reply of this tab's Claude |
| `M-h` | This tab's history: its Claude session on a timeline under its screen as it was then (see "A tab's history") |
| `M-f` | Pin or unpin the tab, keeping it at the top of Needs you |
| `M-T` | Reopen the tab closed most recently, as a browser's Cmd-Shift-T does: in its directory, with its name and its Claude resumed, in front, as the Reopen view reopens one, and taken out of that list, so pressed again it reopens the one closed before; with nothing closed it says "No closed tab to reopen." (Settings' "M-T reopens the last closed tab", `reopen_tab_key`; off, `M-T` shows the tab's full title, as below) |
| `M-p` "Show this tab's full title" | This tab's whole title in a box, which the sidebar cuts at three lines and the status line at its width: the title as the tab bar has it, its PR number in front, wrapped, then its branch, directory, PR and Linear ticket as far as they are known, read from what the workspace holds alone, never the network; Copy the title (or `y`) copies it, and Close, Esc or a click off the options closes it (Settings' "Show a tab's full title", `tab_title`; off, `M-T` showing it reaches the pane) |
| `M-p` | The palette: find any command by what it does, and run it |
| `M-/` | Every key, in mission control's Keys view (see "The Keys view") |
| `S-F10` | The menu a right click would open, for what is chosen: the sidebar's row, as `m` there, or mission control's item, as `Enter` (the view's, as `m`, with none chosen); in a pane, the key goes on to the pane's program |
| `M-Space` | Open or close mission control |
| `M-s` | Into the sidebar, or back out of it |
| `M-R` | Filter the sidebar to a repository, or back to every repository |
| `M-O` | Go to the notifications |
| `M-v` | The branch's PR description in a pane to the right, or close it |
| `M-i` | The branch's Linear ticket, with its comments, in a pane to the right, or close it |
| `M-d` | The branch's diff against its PR's base in place of the pane, or close it |
| `M-r` | Open the branch's PR in the browser |
| `M-l` | Open the branch's Linear ticket in the browser |
| `M-m` | Check whether the branch's PR is ready to merge, or with no PR, how its CI went on GitHub |
| `M-F` | Search the open tabs, your notes, Linear, GitHub and Slack at once |
| `M-u` | Read a link in a pane to the right, typed or one this pane shows |
| `M-N` | Go to Notes, wherever it is open, or open it in a tab of its own |
| `M-e` | The tab's own Notes in a pane to the right, or, pressed again, closed (with "A Notes app in each tab" off: the one Notes moved there, or back in its own tab) |
| `M-S` | The Slack relay in a tab of its own |

The rest follow the prefix, `M-b`. While it waits for the next key, a `^` shows at the right end of the status line.

| Key | What it does |
|---|---|
| `M-b d` | Detach, leaving everything running |
| `M-b [` | Copy mode: scroll back through the pane's history, select and copy with the keyboard |
| `M-b c` | New tab, a shell in the current pane's directory (from a pane at home, Settings' `new_tab_home` folder when one is set) |
| `M-b x` | Close the pane |
| `M-b z` | Zoom the pane in or out |
| `M-b %` | Split the pane to the right |
| `M-b "` | Split the pane below |
| `M-b ?` | The list of keys |
| `M-b M-b` | Send `M-b` to the program in the pane |
| `M-b 1` … `M-b 9`, `M-b 0` | Go to that tab (`0` the tenth) |
| `M-b n`, `M-b p`, `M-b l` | The next tab, the previous one, the one you were on before |
| `M-b w` | Pick a tab from a list (as `M-j`) |
| `M-b ,` | Rename the tab |
| `M-b &` | Close the tab, after asking |
| `M-b o`, `M-b ;` | The next pane of the tab, going round; the pane you were in before |
| `M-b` and an arrow | The pane on that side |
| `M-b` then `C-M-S-h` `C-M-S-j` `C-M-S-k` `C-M-S-l` | Move the pane's divider a cell that way |

`M-b ?` shows every key bound now, yours included, in a box that `j` and `k` scroll and any other key closes. `switchboard list-keys` prints the same list, a key and what it does on each line. `M-/` shows them all with the keys of the sidebar, mission control, the diff and Notes, in mission control's Keys view.

`C-w` waits for the next key: `h`, `j`, `k` or `l` moves to the pane on that side (from the sidebar too), `C-w` again sends `C-w` itself to the pane (deleting a word in a shell or in Claude's prompt), and any other key does what it would have done. Settings' "C-w then h, j, k or l moves between panes" (`ctrl_w_panes`) off leaves `C-w` to the pane, and Settings' "Alt-arrows move between panes" (`pane_arrows`) off hands Alt-arrows to the pane.

Vim takes `C-w` for its own windows. The diff (`M-d`) and Notes take both `C-w` and the Alt-arrows: they move between their own windows (the file list and the diff's two sides; the notes sidebar and the note) and, at their edge, on to the pane on that side, as Neovim with vim-tmux-navigator did, and each greys what doesn't have the keys (the diff its file list while the file has them, and nothing while the list does, since the file beside it is what the list is showing; Notes its sidebar, the note while the plan has them, and the plan while the note has them unless the cursor is in the item whose plan it is, which it then previews); in Notes, `C-w` while typing deletes a word, as in Vim.

### Option as Alt on a Mac

On a Mac, every `M-` key needs the terminal to send Option as Alt; otherwise Option types a character (`˙`, `∆`, `∫`) into the pane and nothing happens. `switchboard setup` sets it in Ghostty, Terminal and iTerm2 after asking (its `terminal` part, [below](#option-as-alt-and-the-tmux-versions-leftovers)); by hand, set it in the terminal:

- Ghostty: `macos-option-as-alt = true` in its config (`left` keeps the right Option for typing accented characters).
- iTerm2: Settings, Profiles, Keys, then "Left Option key" (and the right one) set to "Esc+".
- Terminal: Settings, Profiles, Keyboard, then "Use Option as Meta key".
- kitty: `macos_option_as_alt yes`. WezTerm: `send_composed_key_when_left_alt_is_pressed = false`. Alacritty: `option_as_alt = "Both"` under `[window]`.

On Linux, terminals send Alt this way already. Where Option doesn't act as Alt, the first of `˙` `∆` `˚` `¬` (what Option+h, j, k and l type) brings a tip saying so, once; each still reaches the pane.

### Keys told apart

An old terminal sends some keys as the same bytes: Ctrl-Shift-P as Ctrl-P, Shift-Enter as Enter, Ctrl-I as Tab. So, as tmux's `extended-keys on` did, Switchboard asks your terminal to send such keys as keys of their own, through the kitty keyboard protocol or xterm's modifyOtherKeys, whichever it knows (Ghostty, kitty, WezTerm, Alacritty, foot, iTerm2 and xterm each know one), and gives back what it asked for when it lets go of the terminal. A terminal that knows neither sends the keys as before.

A program in a pane gets a key so only when it asked for keys told apart the same way, as Notes, Neovim, Helix and fish do: Ctrl-Shift-P reaches Notes as Ctrl-Shift-P, which opens the palette (`M-p`), while a shell, which asked for nothing, still gets Ctrl-P's byte. Settings' "Extended keys" (`extended_keys`, Help) turned off asks the terminal for nothing, and every program gets the keys a plain terminal sends; it takes effect when a terminal next attaches, and in panes opened after.

### Changing the keys

`~/.config/switchboard/keys.json` adds keys, changes them or takes them away, over the defaults above. It has up to three parts: `prefix-key`, the key that starts the prefix; `root`, keys pressed alone; and `prefix`, keys pressed after the prefix. Each key names an action, or `null` to unbind it:

```json
{
  "prefix-key": "C-a",
  "root": { "M-g": "new-tab", "M-Left": null, "M-Right": null },
  "prefix": { "y": "copy-mode" }
}
```

Keys are written as tmux wrote them: `M-` for Alt, `C-` for Ctrl, `S-` for Shift, then a character or one of `Enter`, `Escape`, `Tab`, `BTab` (Shift-Tab), `BSpace`, `Space`, `Up`, `Down`, `Left`, `Right`, `Home`, `End`, `PageUp`, `PageDown`, `Insert`, `Delete` and `F1` to `F12`. `M-F` and `M-S-f` are the same key. Unbinding `M-Left`, `M-Right`, `M-Up` and `M-Down`, as above, hands Alt-arrows to the programs in your panes. A command whose default key you unbind or bind to something else stays in `M-p`, listed with no key.

A key the file binds, or binds to something else, carries a "yours" chip in mission control's Keys view and in "Find a key"; the rest say "default".

The server reads the file as it starts and again within two seconds of a change, so a change takes effect without a restart; anything in it that can't be used is said in a box (to the first client to attach, at the start) and written to `server.log`. Settings' "Key bindings" row, under "Your files", opens it in an editor. A `~/.config/switchboard/tmux.conf` from the tmux version isn't read: the first client to attach is told so, and where its keys and options go now.

The actions, by the names the file uses:

- Panes: `select-pane-left`, `select-pane-right`, `select-pane-up`, `select-pane-down`, `next-pane`, `last-pane`, `pane-nav` (`C-w`, waiting for `h` `j` `k` `l`), `split-left`, `split-right`, `split-up`, `split-down`, `close-pane` (the pane alone, the prefix's `x`), `close-here` (`M-w`: the pane, or the tab from its Claude pane or its one pane), `zoom`, `resize-left`, `resize-right`, `resize-up`, `resize-down` (five cells; `resize-left-2` for two), `copy-mode`.
- Tabs: `prev-tab`, `next-tab`, `last-tab`, `tab-back`, `tab-forward`, `select-tab-1` (any number from 1), `go-to-tab` (`M-0`), `new-tab`, `close-tab`, `new-claude-tab`, `find-tab`, `revive-worktree`, `toggle-pin`, `remind-tab`, `reopen-last-tab` (`M-T`), `tab-title`, `rename-tab`.
- Claude and work: `next-needs-you`, `next-idle`, `focus-claude`, `copy-reply`, `work-on-tickets`, `work-on-branch`, `work-on-pr`, `work-on-note`, `work-note-continuously`, `continue-shaping`, `shape-note`, `shape-new-ticket`, `send-reply`, `update-main`.
- The workspace: `palette`, `mission-control`, `mission-<view>` (the view's name in lower case with dashes for spaces: `mission-notifications`, `mission-work-map`, `mission-calendar`, `mission-reminders`, `mission-slack`, `mission-reopen` (`mission-closed`, its name before, still works), `mission-prs`, `mission-ci`, `mission-landing` (`mission-merge-queues`, its name before, still works), `mission-autopilot`, `mission-tickets`, `mission-inbox`, `mission-resources`, `mission-usage` (`mission-sessions`, before the Sessions view was a part of Usage, opens it), `mission-keys`, `mission-settings`; a sidebar plugin's view is `mission-plugin:<name>`, its name in sidebar.json), `keys-view` (`M-/`), `notifications`, `settings`, `habits-report`, `focus-sidebar`, `toggle-sidebar`, `toggle-theme`, `reset-tips`, `list-keys`, `detach`, `send-prefix`, `slack-relay`, `stop-slack-relay`, `update-switchboard`, `run-setup`, `sync-your-files`, `upgrade`, `keep-awake`, `check-setup`, `setup-part`, `aws-login`.
- Reading: `pr-view`, `ticket-view`, `pr-image`, `ticket-image`, `clear-image-cache`, `open-pr`, `open-repo`, `open-ticket`, `open-pr-number`, `open-ticket-id`, `open-merge-queue`, `open-linear-project`, `copy-ticket-id`, `copy-ticket-url`, `copy-pr-number`, `copy-pr-url`, `pr-ready`, `search-everything`, `search-tabs`, `search-notes`, `search-linear`, `search-github`, `search-slack`, `read-link`, `open-link`, `copy-link`, `go-to-mentioned-tab`, `copy-mentioned-tab`, `open-media`, `open-link-being-read`.
- The diff: `diff`, `diff-working-tree`, `lazygit`, `tool-beside`, `tool-alone`, `copy-diff-story`, `ask-about-diff`, `clear-diff-cache`.
- Notes: `notes`, `notes-beside`, `note-tab`, `choose-tab-note`, `attach-note`.

These have no key by default and are in `M-p` alone: `work-on-pr`, `work-on-note`, `work-note-continuously`, `continue-shaping`, `shape-note`, `shape-new-ticket`, `pr-image`, `ticket-image`, `clear-image-cache`, `lazygit`, `diff-working-tree`, `settings`, `remind-tab`, `toggle-sidebar`, `toggle-theme`, `reset-tips`, `stop-slack-relay`, `open-repo`, `read-link`, `open-link`, `copy-link`, `go-to-mentioned-tab`, `copy-mentioned-tab`, `open-media`, `open-link-being-read`, `tool-beside`, `tool-alone`, `copy-diff-story`, `ask-about-diff`, `clear-diff-cache`, `note-tab`, `choose-tab-note`, `attach-note`, `update-switchboard`, `run-setup`, `sync-your-files`, `upgrade`, `keep-awake`, and those of [More in the palette](#more-in-the-palette): `rename-tab`, the `mission-<view>` commands, `habits-report`, `send-reply`, `update-main`, `update-target`, `check-setup`, `setup-part`, `open-pr-number`, `open-ticket-id` and `aws-login`. Give any of them a key in `keys.json` to have it on one, `open-merge-queue`, `open-linear-project`, `copy-ticket-id`, `copy-ticket-url`, `copy-pr-number`, `copy-pr-url`.

## Tabs and the status line

The status line along the bottom starts with the session's name on a badge in the current tab's color, then how many tabs wait on you (`!`, yellow), have finished unseen (`✓`, green) and are working (`⋯`, blue), each count shown only while it isn't zero. Then come the ⌘ Commands button, the health light when something is wrong, and the tabs, each with its number, its state's badge and its name, in its project's color, the current one bold. When the tabs don't all fit, the list scrolls to keep the current one in view, as near the middle as the ends allow, with a `<` or `>` at the end where more are hidden.

### What a tab is called

A tab is named after the directory of the pane you are in (the sidebar and a Notes pane beside your work don't count), read again every two seconds, so a `cd` renames it. In a pane running Claude, that is the directory Claude works in now, as its hooks report it (its Bash tool's `cd` moves it while Claude's own process stays where it started), so a tab whose worktree `/queue-and-clean` removed follows Claude back to the main clone. A directory removed under a pane is never shown: its nearest parent still there takes its place in `switchboard tabs` and the directory a new split or tab opens in, while the tab keeps the name, color and PR the removed directory gave it until its pane is in a directory that exists again. When that directory is the main clone of a worktree removed under the pane, as after a landing's cleanup, a tab with no name of its own keeps the worktree's name as its own (`@tab_name`), so it still says what it did; renaming the tab, or `/wt` and `/wtn` handing it new work, replaces it, and a pane moved to the main clone while its worktree is still there is named after the main clone as before. `/queue-and-clean` pins the tab's name the same way as it starts, when it has none of its own. A worktree gives its own name; any other directory gives its name with the branch after a colon (`app:fix-login`), unless the branch is `main` or `master`. When the branch names a Linear ticket that Linear confirms, the tab takes the ticket and its title instead (`ABC-123: Fix the login`). When the branch has a pull request, its number goes in front (`#123 ABC-123: Fix the login`), first because it is short and doesn't change, so the tabs can be scanned by number. In a fork's clone, where `origin` is your fork and an `upstream` remote names the project, the branch's PR is looked for in both, so one you opened from your fork against the project counts; one in your fork itself wins when both have one, and another fork's branch of the same name never counts.

A name given to the tab wins over all of that, the PR number still in front: the `@tab_name` that "Rename the tab" (and "Rename it" in mission control's menu for the tab) sets, as a skill such as `/shape` does, so `/wt` clearing it hands the tab its ticket's name; else one set by `switchboard rename-tab <name>`. An empty name hands the tab back to its directory.

The PR number and the ticket's title are asked of GitHub and Linear in the background, so a tab never waits on the network: it shows what is known and takes the rest a moment later. A PR found, or a ticket's title, is kept a day, across a restart too (in `~/.cache/switchboard/tab-names.json`); a branch with no PR is asked about again a minute later, and a lookup that fails keeps what was shown and tries again in a minute. Settings' "PRs and tickets in tab names" (`tab_lookups`) turns both lookups off, and turning GitHub or Linear off under Integrations turns off its own.

Each tab wears a color of its own, chosen as it opens: the hue farthest from those of the tabs already open, in every session, the tie going to the one nearest its directory's (worked out from the worktree, or the directory and its branch), so a lone tab wears its project's color. It is kept while the tab is open, through restarts and upgrades, and never changes as others open or close. A hue is drawn in one of two lightness steps: a tab whose hue falls within 20° of an open tab's takes the other step from that tab's, light or deep, so even close hues tell apart. It is never a red, which means an error here (a failing build, a failed stage). Claude Code's statusline, Switchboard's in its panes ([Claude Code's settings](#claude-codes-settings)), draws the same color, asking the workspace for its tab's, so a session and its tab match; outside the workspace it draws the directory's. Settings' "A color a tab" (`tab_colors_apart`) off gives every tab its directory's color, the same for every tab of one checkout, its step from the directory too. The session's badge and the lines around the focused pane take the current tab's color. Settings' "Tab colors" (`tab_colors`) picks how the color is drawn: `soft`, the default, a pastel as the UX 2.0 canvas draws its tabs, or `vivid`, the stronger colors from before; each tab keeps its own hue either way, and the statusline follows the same setting. Words on a tab's color (the header's first line, the session's badge, the sidebar's pill) are dark on a light color and light on a dark one, in either theme; with the light theme, a tab's color drawn as words (the tab bar, the second status line, the sidebar's numbers, and Claude's statusline when the theme is `light`) is a darker shade of the same hue, so it shows on the pale background. Settings' "Project colors" (`accent_colors`) turns them off, and every tab is gray.

### The ⌘ Commands button and the health light

The ⌘ Commands button opens the palette when clicked, as `M-p` does (Settings' "Commands button", `palette_button`). With UX 2.0 it is at the right end of the tab header's keys instead, lit while the palette is open, and a click on it then closes the palette; a header too narrow for the keys and the button (two tabs side by side) leaves the button out.

The health light stays off while all is well, and shows a red `●` naming what has quietly stopped, so you see it before you miss what it stopped:

- `saves 7m old`: the layout changed and the save hasn't landed for over five minutes.
- `relay down`: the Slack relay crashed, rather than being stopped.
- `Slack not connected`, or another service: a Claude connector a feature needed wasn't connected when it last tried, in the last two hours.
- `GitHub: 12% of the hour left`: under 20% of GitHub's hour's limit is left, so its lookups are slowed down until it resets.

Settings' "Health light" (`health_light`) turns it off.

After it, a ☕ shows while the workspace keeps the computer awake ([Keeping the computer awake](#keeping-the-computer-awake)).

### The second status line

Settings' "Second status line" (`second_status`, off by default) adds a line under the tabs about the current tab: its name in full, where the tab may cut it short, its PR and whether it has every commit made here ("↑2 to push" in yellow, the commits on the branch that its upstream, or origin's copy of it, hasn't got as last fetched or pushed; "pushed" when it has them all), the program in front of its pane, and in a Claude pane, what the session is doing ("waiting on you for 4m", "finished 2m ago", "working"), its name and your last prompt to it.

### UX 2.0

Settings' "UX 2.0" (`ux2`, off by default) is a new look being built a part at a time. Today it:

- takes the status line away;
- fills a screen of any width, as the board of how a view fills one says: a row's parts at their natural widths up to a cap, its charts, meters, sparklines and stage cells stretching into the rest, what belongs at the right edge there, and a pane beside a board (a PR's or a ticket's description, the Autopilot plan, a PR's or ticket's panel) keeping its share of the screen, never under 34 columns, until it is resized; on a narrow screen a view drops its parts in an order it names, the Usage view's protocols first and then its share meters;
- draws the sidebar, the tab header and mission control with one kit of pieces, so they look alike: one mark and one color for each state wherever it shows (● needs you, ⠹ working, ▲ landing or queued, ✓ done, ✕ failed, ◌ pending, ◷ a reminder, ⚑ pinned, ○ idle), section labels in capitals in their state's color with the count at the right, chips and stage cells on a tint of their color, cards and boxes with rounded corners, and the keys on every footer and bar in the accent color with what they do dim; a chip, a stage cell and a key end in half circles, as pictures of them on a terminal that gets sixel (Windows Terminal, [Pictures in Windows Terminal](#pictures-in-windows-terminal)), as the glyphs on one that draws them itself otherwise (Ghostty), and in spaces elsewhere, the same width either way;
- gives each tab a header over its panes, its first line filled with the tab's own color (the one its number has in the sidebar), and on it, dark (light on a dark vivid color): its state, its number (the one the sidebar, `M-1` to `M-9` and `switchboard tabs` know it by, kept however narrow the header is) and its name in bold, and the repository it works in (its main clone, so a worktree's long path never crowds the line) and what Claude is doing, with how long (`waiting 4m`), so after `M-a` you see how long the tab has waited on you, then how long the tab has been open (`opened 1h12m`, from when it opened, kept through a restart and an upgrade; a tab opened by a build before it has none, and `switchboard show @tab_age` prints the same), then how full its Claude's context is (`ctx 62%`), which its statusline tells the server as it draws and a new session clears; under it, the keys of what the tab can show in Claude's place (`M-c` Claude, `M-d` Diff, `M-v` PR, `M-i` Ticket, `M-e` Note), the one it shows lit as one pill, then `M-h` History, lit while the tab's history is up, and at the row's right end the ⌘ Commands button; Notes' own tab has no header while the sidebar shows, its pane taking those rows, and Notes draws its own top line instead (board 7): `Notes` over its list, and at the list's right `/ search`, which a click starts as `/` does, with `⌘ Commands` before it where there is room (with `palette_button` on), which a click opens the palette with as `M-p` does, at the right end of the note's toolbar row instead while the list is put away or a plan shows beside the note, each lit under the mouse, the list under it after a blank row;
- says in the header the branch after where the tab works (`~/code/app · main`), cut first where the line is short, so the state and where `M-a` goes next always fit, and at the right of its first line the tab `M-a` goes to next (`M-a next: 3 billing`, a long name cut) while another needs you; a click on a key in the header does what the key does, and with hover_highlight on the key under the mouse is lit;
- shows the PR (`M-v`) and the ticket (`M-i`) in Claude's place, as `M-d` shows the diff, rather than beside it, laid out as their pages are with a panel beside the description: the PR's checks, reviewers, open review threads and files, the ticket's facts, pull requests and related tickets, and people's photos by what they wrote; the same key, or `M-c`, putting Claude back;
- starts the sidebar's top line with the workspace's own buttons (boards 57 to 60): `←` and `→`, which step back and forward through the tabs and views visited as `M-{` and `M-}` do, faint with nowhere that way, and `⊞`, which opens mission control as `M-Space` does, with the count of tabs that need you beside it in yellow while there are any, lit on the accent while mission control is open, when a click on it closes it. Each lights under the mouse, and while the mouse is over `←` or `→` the rest of the line names the tab it goes to (its mark, number and name), whose row in the list lights with the arrow at its right edge, or `⊞` and the view's name; over `⊞`, "Mission control". A right click on `←` or `→` opens the whole trail in a menu by the click: the tabs and views forward, furthest first, where you are ("you are here"), then the ones back, nearest first, a view as `⊞` and its name, each with its steps away (`→ 2`, `← 1`), a choice going straight there as that many steps would. A right click on `⊞` opens mission control's views by group in a menu, Tabs with how many need you and the views of Code and Day with how many rows they hold, a choice opening mission control on that view. `M-p`'s "Back and forward: every tab and view you visited, to go straight to one" and "Mission control's views by group, to open one" open the same two menus from the keyboard. With the sidebar hidden the buttons start the tab header's first line, and with UX 2.0 off the status bar; with Settings' `tab_back_forward` off, `←` and `→` are left out, as the keys then go to the tab on either side. After them, how many tabs there are, a ☕ after the count while the computer is kept awake, and `/ filter`, in place of the session's name and the counts, and its notifications under that, a line each, gone when there are none, in place of their section at the bottom, so `k` or Up from the first tab moves up onto them and `j` or Down from the last of them back onto the first tab, while `j` from the last tab goes round to the first of them (to the first tab with none, and with Settings' `sidebar_wrap` off it stays there);
- starts each notification line with its kind's mark (board 1): ◷ the meeting about to start and a calendar change, @ Slack and the Linear inbox, ✎ a note written for you, ✓ a landing and a PR merged or approved, ✕ a PR's checks failing, a failing default branch and a routine's run that failed or was skipped, ◇ what autopilot says and a suggestion; and ends it with how long ago it came, dim at the right edge (`1m`, `12m`, `1h`), every title cut a column short of that time column, so a title never runs into it, whether or not its line keeps a time: the newest of its kind for Slack, the Linear inbox, notes written for you and landed tabs; a calendar change, the next meeting, the mode's word and a suggestion keep no time and show none;
- draws each group's heading in its color, bold, its count at the right edge, and each tab in two lines: its state's mark with no pill, its number in its project's color and its name on one line, then what it is doing, a failing count with the kit's ✕ (`✕ 1 failing`), and on the Notes tab the note Notes shows (`✎ Release plan`); the cursor's tab shaded with a bar at its left, no rule between tabs, and the keys that start, find and walk tabs (`M-t`, `M-j`, `M-a`) on the sidebar's last row;
- draws the pinned, reminder and pending-checks marks as symbols of your terminal's font (⚑ ◷ ◌) in place of emoji (📌 ⏰ ⏳), one column each;
- lays mission control's PRs and Tickets views out with the kit's board, as the Autopilot view is: the columns sharing the width a column apart, never under 16 each, each column's name in capitals in its state's color with its count right after it, an empty column a dashed slot reading "nothing here", each PR or ticket a rounded card, a row apart and as tall as what it holds: its number or ID in the accent color on its top edge and at the edge's right end the PR's checks' mark (✓ ✕ ◌) or the ticket's priority (`!!!` urgent, `!!` high, `!` medium), its title on up to two lines, then a dim line about it, the chosen card's edge in the accent color and its title bold, the one under the mouse with its edge dim; on a screen too narrow for every column at 16, the chosen card's column and its neighbors, with "‹ 1 more" and "2 more ›" over them, each a click and a Tab stop while a card is that way, `h` and `l` sliding the board as they move a column; a done ticket's card faint, its title struck through, saying how long ago it was done;
- puts a PR with changes asked for in a Changes asked column of its own between In review and Approved, and ends each card with the tab working on it in its PR's repository (`tab 3` and its state's mark), or else a PR's reviewers, requested and latest, or a ticket's project and estimate; under the columns, a faint row of the same cards for the PRs merged and the tickets canceled in the last 7 days ("Merged this week", "Closed this week"), each saying when, and who merged the PR or how the ticket was closed, which `j` and `l` reach and `h` and `l` move along; and the chosen PR or ticket beside the board on a screen 140 columns wide or more, and under it after a rule on a narrower one with room for it: its number or ID and title bold, a dim line of a PR's author, commits, lines added and removed and checks, or a ticket's state, priority, project and estimate, then its description through the PR and ticket views' Markdown, all from each board's one query, a Mermaid diagram in it drawn as a picture as those views draw it, as wide as the pane at most and cut at the pane's edges as the text is, `J` and `K` (or the wheel over the pane) scrolling what is below its bottom into sight, the pane resize keys (`C-M-S-h` wider, `C-M-S-l` narrower) or a drag of its left edge resizing it, each board keeping its own width, where the terminal draws images (Settings' `view_images` and `server_images`, both on), and as text boxes until it is drawn and anywhere else;
- in Notes, draws chips for an unticked checklist item, as board 7 draws them: `plan` when an indented `Plan: [[...]]` line is under it, which a click opens as Enter does, `needs a choice` in yellow when the item or that line says so, with what it is between when the line says (`(needs a choice: A, B)` gives `needs a choice: A, B`), and `chose: A` dim when the line says `(chose A)`. An item with a plan line has its chips in that line's place, under the item at the line's indent, the file's text unchanged; the cursor on the line shows its text to edit, the chips meanwhile at the item's right. An item with none has them at the right edge of its line, its text cut with … before them, except on the cursor's line, which shows it whole, wrapped, its chips after it (on a row of their own when they don't fit beside it); each chip a click takes is lit under the mouse; a picture an indented line under the item links is drawn under that line, as any of a note's pictures is; Settings' "Chips on checklist items" (`notes_item_chips`) off leaves items their text alone;
- draws each worktree in mission control's Landing view as a row of stage cells under the stages' names (tests, review, leak check, lock, push, applied), its name bold with where it is dim under it: done green with ✓, the step its phase names blue, its place in the landing queue amber, a failure red with ✕ (the phase `tests failed` or `review blocked` queue-and-clean sets, with the words after it), the rest dim and waiting. The cells carry the numbers the landing recorded as it went (`land-lock record`, below): the tests passed ("✓ 412", or "✕ 2 of 401" with the failing file under the cell), the review's clean rounds ("✓ 2 clean", or "round 1" with its reviewer and how long ago under it while it goes on), and the leak check ("✓ none", "✕ found"); a landing with no record says "passed", "clean" and "none". Each PR in GitHub's merge queue is the same row under its own stages (CI, approved, merge queue, merge, deploy test): CI and approval done, since GitHub queues a PR only once they are, its place in the queue, its checks running, ✕ when it can't merge, and merging. A section's heading says how its repository lands, "straight on main" (with "lands without asking" when the tab holding the lock has autopilot's "Land without asking" on) or, for a repository that lands through a PR, "through a PR · GitHub merge queue", with its counts at the right edge ("4 landing · 1 holds the lock", "1 in the merge queue"). The cells sit at the right edge, 13 columns of words each from 160 columns wide, the worktree column taking the rest; narrower, each column is as wide as its widest words, then a waiting cell shows ◌ alone, then the worktree column narrows to 20, and narrower still the view is a list. Under the rows, the chosen worktree's detail: what it waits on (the worktree holding the lock, its step and for how long), what happens then, its directory, a keycap for Enter (and on a narrow screen `i`), then its last review round (round, reviewer, how long ago, codex-review's summary) or its last tests, what each earlier round found and the commit that fixed it, and the last lines of that output, faint, as many as the room under the rows takes; under 140 columns that output is behind `i`, which shows it over the list until `i` or Esc. Enter opens the row's menu: going to its tab, landing it next, moving a waiting worktree up or down the landing queue, taking it out after asking, paging all of its last output in a pane beside, copying its directory; the detail's Enter keycap takes a click too;
- in Notes, gives unticked checklist items keys of their own, each Vim's where it has nothing to do: Enter on an item's `Plan: [[…]]` line shows the plan in a panel beside the note (see [An item's plan beside the note](#an-items-plan-beside-the-note)), while Enter elsewhere on the item ticks it, `o` on an item a tab works on or waits in goes to that tab, `ga` (or a click) types into the "Add an item" input at the bottom, Enter adding the item after the last unticked one of the note's top list and M-Enter opening a tab in the note's repository, linked to the note, whose Claude runs `/plan-item` with it (which puts the item in the note and writes its plan; the repository is asked as `gw` asks it), while `M-p`, the palette, lists autopilot working the open note first while Notes is in front (see [The palette](#the-palette)); a section for parked work (`## Defered`, `## Later`, the ones wtn and autopilot leave out) opens folded to one line with its count of items (`▸ Defered   7 items, parked`), and the footer shows Enter and `M-p`, leaving the letter keys off it, as every footer does (the palette lists each with its key), and the input its Enter and M-Enter;
- in Notes, puts the tab on an unticked item first among its chips, from a look at the workspace's tabs every few seconds off the drawing path: `⠹ tab 3` in blue with its loop's phase while it works, `● tab 2 waits on you` in yellow with what it waits on, the tab's number in the tab's color, a click on it going to the tab as `o` does, the item a tab is for read from its full title, autopilot's item or its branch's `wtnItem`, and shown only on the note that item is in, known from the note's ID in its branch's `wtnItem`, else the note the tab is linked to (a tab whose note is neither shows on any note's item of its words); and puts a green `landed <hash>` at the right of a ticked item whose indented lines name a commit;
- gives autopilot a mission control view of its own, Autopilot, after Landing, reached with `[ ]` while autopilot works a note or while it is in front (Settings' "Autopilot view", `view_autopilot`): its note bold with its tabs working and open against its slots and cap (`1 of 2 working · 3 of 5 open`), when the note's ready items should all be done and how many are left, with how many wait on you (`done by about 3:40 PM · 6 left · 2 wait on you`, the day named when it isn't today; Settings' "Estimated finish", `autopilot_eta`), whether its tabs land without asking, only items with a settled plan, today's Claude spend against its spend limit (`$5.00 of $20 today`, with a meter at the right of the heading, amber from four fifths and red once passed), the week's use against its weekly limit (`62% of 85% this week`), and why it is paused (by you, while you're away with "Keep opening tabs while I'm away" off, or what it last said, such as the spend limit passed or the weekly limit reached); then the kit's board, as the PRs and Tickets views have it, of every item it works, each a rounded card in the column where it stands: Next up (the items it will open next, in that order, each saying its place in line), Blocked (the items held by an `After:` line on an item still open, each card named `blocked` and, under a dim `after`, a line for each item it waits on, three at most and then how many more: the mark of where that one stands, its tab's number when one of autopilot's tabs works it, and its first line; Enter's menu leads with a choice for each, "Go to tab 3: …" while a tab works it, else "Open the plan of: …", then "Open its plan in Notes", and a click on one of those lines goes there at once; the pane beside the board lists every one whole under After, where each stands on the line under it; a column drawn only while an item is blocked, and an item moves to Next up once every item it waits on is ticked; one held by a design board's review stays in Next up, dim), Working, Waiting on you, Landing (in the landing queue or holding the lock), Landed (every item of its note landed today, its tab open or closed, a closed one's card saying `closed` and the commit it landed, with no tab to go to) and Skipped (the items you skipped, each card its item dim and `skipped by you`, with no meter, a column drawn only while an item is skipped, so with none the others share the width), decided from the tab's marks, its Claude's state, loop and phase, and its worktree's place in the landing queue. The estimate is arithmetic on what autopilot already keeps, with no model asked: an item is taken to need the median time, from its tab's opening to its landing, of today's last 20 landed items (of any note it worked, their tabs open or closed), leaving out any over three times the median of them all (one that waited hours on you), and half an hour until three have landed; each tab still at work ends at its opening plus that (at least a minute from now), and the items it would take, every one of the note's queue and those after an item it will take or one at work, each take the first of its slots to come free, as many as the most open leaves room for beside the tabs waiting on you or landed and not yet closed (none, and it gives no time). It leaves out what waits on you (a tab under Waiting on you, which frees its slot, an open choice it won't take, unless hands-free, a design board to review, and the items held after any of them), which it counts apart, and while it is paused, or holds off while you are away, past its spend limit or at its weekly limit, it gives no time, and once it is stopped no estimate at all. It moves as the note changes, as an item lands or a tab opens, and with the clock. Within a column the cards go from the least far along to the furthest, so landed ones come last. A card shows its state's mark and its tab's number on its top edge (an item waiting its turn its place in line, `1st`), a red ✕ at the edge's right once a stage failed, its item on up to two lines, where it is dim, how long its item has worked since its tab opened, dim (`worked 42m`), then how long it took once it landed, from when its landing pushed (`took 1h12m`), and a tab's card as its last line a meter of its eight stages done (opened, working, tests, review, leak check, lock, landed, closed) in its column's color, red once a stage failed; the meter is an image where the terminal draws them (kitty's or sixel, by Settings' "Image protocol", with `server_images` on), a line of ━ elsewhere. `h` and `l` move between columns, `j` and `k` along the cards, and the chosen card's edge is in the accent color. On a screen 200 columns wide or more the chosen item sits beside the board in full, and under it after a rule on a narrower one with room for it, the columns taking the width, with its stages one a line in Landing's colors, each done or running one with how long it took or has taken so far from the steps its landing named (`✓ passed 3m`, `testing… 4m`, `3 in line 12m`: the work from the tab's opening to its landing's first step, the tests, the review, the wait for the landing lock and the push) and its plan under them (the plan note its `Plan: [[…]]` line links, a Mermaid diagram in it as a picture), as a ticket's description sits beside the Tickets board, `J` and `K` scrolling it and the pane resize keys or a drag of its edge resizing it; an item that links no plan says "No plan" there. Before a tab joins the landing queue, its tests and review show only what its loop or phase says and its leak check waits, and closing waits while the tab is open. On a screen too narrow for the board the view is a plain list in the same order. Enter on a card or row offers only what acts on it: going to its tab, skipping its item or taking a skipped one back, and handing it off. The view's own menu (`m`, or a right click on its background) holds what changes autopilot as a whole: its settings, pausing or resuming it, having it land without asking or ask again, going hands-free or asking again (with Settings' `autopilot_handsfree` on), taking all of the note's skipped items back (while any is skipped) and stopping it; Enter on its heading or a click on the heading's `m ☰ Menu` button (lit under the mouse, and while Tab's focus is on the heading) opens the menu the Tabs view's line opened, every choice of it. With the view, the Tabs view has no autopilot line and its cursor starts on the first card, and "Autopilot: work a note", autopilot's notifications and their "Open mission control's Autopilot" open mission control on this view instead of the Tabs view; with the view off, the line above the Tabs view stays as it is;
- groups mission control's views by feature, listed down the side (boards 64 and 65): Work (Tabs, Landing, Autopilot, Routines, Reopen), Code (PRs, Tickets, CI), Day (Calendar, Slack, Inbox, Notifications) and System (Usage, Resources, Settings, Keys, then each plugin's view), each group a section's label and each view a row with what it holds at its right (how many tabs need you, how many PRs, tickets, reminders or notifications, how many CI runs are failing), the view in front lit as a list's chosen row. While mission control is open the sidebar is that list, its top line `‹ Tabs` and how many tabs need you meanwhile, a click on it going back to the tabs (as Esc and `M-Space` do), which come back as mission control closes; with Settings' "Keep the tabs sidebar in mission control" (`mission_keep_tabs_sidebar`, off by default) on, or with the sidebar hidden, mission control draws the list as a column of its own at its left, the tabs sidebar staying beside it. Over the view a one-line header names it and its group, with the tabs' counts on Tabs, the Reminders' or the Routines' words on theirs, and the filter. A click on a view's row opens it and a right click opens its `m` menu; `j` `k` and the arrows move along the rows while the list has the focus, each view coming to the front as the cursor lands on it, the list keeping the keys, Enter or Space then moving into the view, and `/` there types a filter that narrows them by name. A view walked past isn't a step for `M-{` and `M-}`: the one the cursor rests on joins them after a second, or as Enter moves into it. With Settings' "Tab moves focus" on, the list is one Tab stop: Tab from it goes into the view in front and Shift-Tab from the view's first stop comes back to it. Settings' "Show views as the list moves" (`mission_nav_follow`) off, `j` and `k` only move the cursor, Enter or Space opens the view, and Tab stops on each view's row. `M-]` and `M-[` go to the next or previous group's first view (`M-{` and `M-}` go back and forward through the tabs and views visited), and `]` and `[` round the views of the group in front; a group with no view turned on is left out;
- opens mission control (`M-Space`) right of the sidebar, from the top row down, so the sidebar stays in sight beside it, and draws its Tabs view as board 3 does ([The cards](#the-cards)): the tabs grouped as the sidebar groups them, the tabs that need you and those working each a label with its count over its cards, every other group a shelf of one line a tab under them;
- draws mission control's Calendar view as a timeline: the hours down the left from 8:00 (or the first event, or now) to 7:00 (or the last), three rows an hour, each labelled dim and right-aligned with a dashed rule across; each event a block the height of its length with a bar in its color, its title bold and its time and where to join dim, one already over dimmed and struck through, one moved since it was first seen today with a "moved" chip after its title; now as a red line with its time; and, on a screen wide enough for both (76 columns or more), the event under the cursor beside the day, the two sharing the width as board 45 lays a view out, the pane never under 34 columns;
