- gives the Calendar timeline's chosen event the board's pane: its title, then its time (with "moved from 2:00 by Ana" once it has moved, naming who organized it unless it was you), where to join and your reply, under them a rule; with no keycaps, "You", a chip of your reply ("not replied" until you answer) and "Enter: join or reply"; who is going, four by first name and "+N" for the rest, read from the feed's ATTENDEE lines, with their photos looked up by each address when Settings' "Attendees' photos in the Calendar" (`calendar_photos`) is on; the first lines of its description as its agenda; and "From your work": each ticket and PR it names with its title and state from the Tickets and PRs boards (`● AB-142 Fix the RSVP menu  In progress`, `✓` once done or merged), and your notes' unticked items that name one of them or the event's title, with the plan their `Plan: [[…]]` line links and a "needs a choice" chip when it says so, found on this computer and never sent anywhere; then, under a dashed rule, the next working day ("Next: Monday" and its date, "nothing this weekend" when an empty weekend was passed over) and its first three events dim, with how long, where to join and your reply when you aren't going; and says how soon the next event starts ("in 15m") on its second line;
- draws `M-d`'s diff as its board does: the list on a panel, headed STORY with the number of pages Haiku told, then the whole change's totals as GitHub's PR header has them (board 105: `10 files  +184 −36`, the files bold, the added lines green and the deleted red, and five cells shared out by them, green, then red, faint when there are no lines; a binary file counts as a file with no lines; with the path filter set, board 106: `5 of 10 files` and the lines of the files left, with a faint line under the list saying how many files the filter hides and how to clear it), each page its number in the accent color and its title, bold for the shown file's, the files under it with a yellow ● on those not committed yet and their counts at the right, the cursor's row shaded with a bar at its left; the files changed only since the last commit under a NOT COMMITTED YET label in yellow with their count, still the last stop for `]p` and `]c`, and there too, after the commits' files, while no story is told, and generated files last under GENERATED, LAST; over the diff, the shown file's page (`Page 1 of 3`, or `Chapter 1 · page 4 of 5` with several chapters, in the accent color and its title, or Not committed yet in yellow) with what it compares with at the right (`against origin/main (no PR)`, see "What it compares"), its why under it in at most three lines and a rule, the why no longer in the list; one bar over both sides, the path with its folders dim and its name bold, its counts and at the right the two sides compared (`origin/main  ⟷  working tree`); each hunk's note in a rounded card under the hunk, across both sides, titled ✎ Note, or with the page's number and title for a hunk of another page; and the files as one scroll, each following the last under a rule and its bar, so the wheel scrolls on from one file into the next and back, and `]c` `[c` in the diff go on to the next file's changes, the list following, while the cursor stays in its file: `j`, `k`, `Ctrl-d`, `Ctrl-u`, `Ctrl-f`, `Ctrl-b`, `G` and `gg` stop at its first and last lines (`j` at the last saying once that `]f` goes on), the wheel leaves the cursor on its file's last line in sight, or where it is once the file has scrolled out, until a key moves it and brings the view back, and `]f` `[f` go to the next file and the one before, as the list does; the bar of the file at the top stays over the diff; and a footer on the bottom row naming the keys with no letter among them, the list's (`↑↓ move  ↵ read  esc close`) or a side's (`↑↓ move  ←→ sides  esc list`), then `M-c Claude` in the workspace, left out in easy mode, whose buttons name them;
- with Settings' "Context in Claude's status line" (`statusline_context`) off, has Claude's statusline under its prompt show the model alone inside the workspace, how full Claude's context is being in mission control's Usage view under Sessions and the tab's header; with the setting on, as it is by default, with that view off (`usage`), and outside the workspace, it shows the context as before.

Images: where your terminal speaks the kitty graphics protocol (Ghostty, kitty), or gets sixel by Settings' "Image protocol" (Windows Terminal, by default, [Pictures in Windows Terminal](#pictures-in-windows-terminal)), the workspace draws its own images: a person's photo as a round avatar, a picture inside a rounded border, a meter with its tick, a series of values as a chart or, one row tall, a sparkline, a setting's on/off switch, the keyboard as a heatmap of the keys you pressed, and a soft shadow under a box (with sixel, half blocks, since a sixel can't sit under text). Each is placed in the screen's cells, so a box over it covers it, and replaced in place when it changes; in another terminal, or with Settings' "Images in the workspace" (`server_images`) off, each is drawn with characters: the initial on the person's color, the picture's name, `━` for a meter, `▁`…`█` for a series, ` ━●` or `○━ ` for a switch, each key's letter in the accent at three shades for the keyboard, half blocks for the shadow. An avatar with no photo is the initial in every terminal. An avatar beside what someone wrote or did (a Slack message, an Inbox notification or comment, a comment or review thread in the PR and ticket views, who opened a PR or ticket) is large, four columns by two rows, their name beside its first row and their words five columns in from its second; in a run of names (the Calendar's attendees, a PR's reviewers and its "wants to merge" line) it is small, two columns by one. Without the photo it is the initial on the person's color, one color per name whether they come from Slack, Linear or GitHub, the text keeping its place when the photo arrives. The parts that show them come as they are built.

Scroll bars: whatever runs past its edge has a scroll bar at its right showing where you are, a faint `│` track and the thumb a heavier `┃` as long as the share that shows (Settings' "Scroll bars", `scroll_bars`). A pane scrolled back into its history has one down its last column; the PR, ticket, link and file views at their right edge, the text laid out a column narrower for it, or in the rule beside a board's panel; the diff's file list and its lines, a column kept for it at the diff's right; Notes' sidebar and the note, in the column right of the text; the sidebar's tab list in the line between the sidebar and the panes; mission control's Tabs cards, each column of a board whose cards run past the screen, and the pane beside or under a board; a picker's, the Keys list's or a text box's rows in the box's right edge, and a menu taller than the screen on its edge; the Notifications window at its right; Notes' finder in the line between its list and the preview, and its Format menu on its edge; and the diff's answer to `ga`. A click on the track pages toward the click and dragging the thumb scrolls with it, where the surface keeps a place of its own (a pane, the views, the diff, Notes, the pane beside a board, a picker, the Keys list); the sidebar's pages half a screen as `C-d` and `C-u` do, and mission control's cards, lists and board columns, the Notifications window and the menus follow the chosen item, so their bars only show where it is. Mission control's boards with layouts of their own (the Inbox, Slack, Calendar and Usage boards among them) have none yet. The wheel works as before. Off, nothing is drawn there and the content takes the column back.

Photos: an avatar shows the person's own photo from Slack, Linear or GitHub, fetched in the background the first time something draws them, kept in `~/.cache/switchboard/photos/` and fetched again only after 30 days, each request counted in the Usage view's request audit. Slack's comes through the Slack relay's app, so it needs the relay set up and the `users:read` scope in its manifest (below); someone who kept Slack's own picture shows their initial. Linear's and GitHub's follow the `linear` and `github` settings. Someone a calendar invitation names by their address alone (the Calendar view's "Going") is looked up by it, only with Settings' "Attendees' photos in the Calendar" (`calendar_photos`) on, off by default since it sends the address to those services: as the Linear user with that email while `linear` is on, else (or when Linear knows no one by it) through the Slack relay's app with `users.lookupByEmail`, which needs the `users:read.email` scope. With Settings' "People's photos" (`photos`) off, nothing is fetched and every avatar is the initial.

A pane zoomed with `M-=` covers the header, as it covers the sidebar. With `ux2` off, the status line, the sidebar and those keys are as before.

### Theme

Settings' "Theme" (`theme`) draws the status line, the lines between panes, the panes without the focus and every box dark or light; `system` follows your terminal's background, which the workspace asks each terminal for as it attaches, and stays dark when the terminal doesn't answer. `M-p`'s "Toggle light and dark theme" switches between the two. Dark is drawn in the UX 2.0 canvas's own colors on a terminal that shows truecolor (one whose `COLORTERM` says `truecolor` or `24bit`, and Ghostty, kitty and Windows Terminal always, the last known by its `WT_SESSION`), each attached terminal by what it said as it attached, and in the 256-color palette elsewhere. "Set the terminal's colors" (`terminal_colors`, off by default) sets each attached terminal's own background and text to the dark theme's (`#15161e` and `#c9cdd9`) while the workspace is dark, so the padding a terminal leaves around its grid matches, in place of setting them in the terminal's config, and puts the terminal's own back when you detach; with it on, the programs in your panes take that background and text as their default colors too. The programs in your panes otherwise keep your terminal's own colors either way. The diff and Notes follow the setting too, `system` by the background your terminal said.

The workspace also asks each terminal as it attaches for its text and background colors and the sixteen colors of its palette, and tells a program in a pane that asks for them your terminal's own, as tmux did: the diff mixes git's green and red over your real background, draws its code in your terminal's palette, and gives its buttons and faint text shades between your background and text.

### Pictures in Windows Terminal

A note's pictures, the PR and ticket image views, the pictures and diagrams in the PR, ticket, link and file views (`M-v`, `M-i`) and the workspace's own images (avatars, meters, charts and sparklines, Settings' switches, the keyboard heatmap, a tab's history lane and chart, the diagrams beside a board, a picture shown full size) are drawn with the kitty graphics protocol, which Ghostty and kitty speak. Windows Terminal (1.22 and later, WSL included) doesn't, but draws sixel, and Settings' "Image protocol" (`image_protocol`), by the terminal (`auto`) as it is by default, has the workspace draw each picture itself as sixel for it: the workspace asks each terminal what it is as it attaches (`CSI c`), and one whose answer says it draws sixel gets it, Windows Terminal from 1.22 among them, while an older one keeps the text forms; `sixel` gives it to every terminal without asking, Ghostty and kitty included, and `kitty` gives every terminal kitty's protocol: a protocol you choose is never second-guessed, so a terminal that can't draw it shows blanks (sixel) or boxes (kitty's) in the pictures' place. After a change, open Notes, the image view or the PR or ticket view again to have it draw by it. The rounded ends of its pills (a chip, a key, a stage cell, an input), half circles Ghostty draws itself, are drawn as pictures of them the same way, so they are round there too. The apps still give it their pictures as kitty's, Notes and the image views by the PNG's path and the PR, ticket, link and file views with its data inline, and write the cells the picture covers; the server keeps the commands rather than passing them on, and draws each picture over its cells in the frame it sends, so a picture scrolls with its note, is cut off where its cells are, and is covered by a box over it, as it would be in Ghostty. The workspace's own images are drawn the same way wherever their cells show, at once as they change for a small drawing (a switch, a meter, a chart), a moment after for a photo or a large picture (an avatar, a Slack picture, a picture shown full size). Until a picture is read and drawn, a moment after it first shows, its cells are blank. A sixel is drawn over its cells, never under text, so the soft shadow under a box is half blocks along its right and bottom edges, as in a terminal without images. By the terminal, a Ghostty and a Windows Terminal attached to the same workspace each get the pictures their own way, and so does `switchboard headless`, which reads kitty's; with `sixel` chosen, every one of them gets sixel.

Where pictures reach a terminal as sixel the workspace asks it as it attaches for its cell's size in pixels (`CSI 16 t`) and draws pictures to fill their cells at that size. Windows Terminal answers with the 10 by 20 pixels a cell its sixel works in, whatever its font, and scales each cell's share of a picture to the font; where a terminal doesn't answer, the cell its window's size in pixels makes is used, else the same 10 by 20. Other terminals that draw sixel and not kitty's pictures (foot, WezTerm, xterm with sixel on, mlterm) say so when asked, and get the workspace's own pictures and pill ends as sixel the same way; Notes and the views ask the workspace what the terminals showing their tab get, so they draw theirs the same way. A half circle a program of yours prints (a powerline prompt's) is left to the terminal, which draws it from its font. A picture takes up to 256 colors, its own when it has that few, as a screenshot of text often does. A change to the setting reaches the terminals attached at once, each asked for its cell then; a workspace whose server started from a build without the setting takes it after `switchboard upgrade` (or `M-p`'s "Restart Switchboard on the latest main").

With UX 2.0 (`ux2`), a picture is drawn under the tab's header, never over it: one scrolled up past the top of its pane is cut there, its rows still in the pane drawn from the pane's first. With the status line gone, a pane reaches the screen's last row, and a sixel there would scroll the whole screen, so the part of a picture on that row is left blank; Notes and the image views keep their own last row for their messages and footer, so their pictures never reach it. Mission control's cards, which show each tab's last lines, and a peek at a tab's screen copy its text, not its pictures: the cells a picture covers show blank there, and a picture is drawn only where its own pane shows it.

## The palette, messages, tips and menus

### The palette

`M-p`, or a click on ⌘ Commands, opens the palette: every command the workspace has, by what it does, with its tags dimmed beside it and its key in a column of its own, Shift spelled out (`M-S-F`). It lists every action a key is bound to, yours included, and the ones that have no key. `M-1` to `M-9` are one row. Type to narrow the list, each word matched as you write it, and Enter runs the command as its key would, in the pane you were in. Esc closes it. With UX 2.0 (`ux2`), what you type is in a rounded field with the count matched at its right, and under the list the chosen command is previewed: what it does, a sentence or two more on how it works, and its key, or that it has none and can be given one in `keys.json`.

The commands you picked last come first, the newest at the top, then the rest by description. As you type, a command you picked before after typing the same words comes first, then one picked after longer words that start with what you typed, so `sha` finds what you picked after typing `shape`. What you typed before each pick is kept, the last 300, in `~/.local/state/switchboard/palette/`, readable only by you; Settings' "Learn from what you pick" (`palette_learn`) turns that off. After you pick a command that has a key of its own, a tip names the key, once for each key.

While GitHub or Linear is off in Settings, the commands tagged `#github` or `#linear` leave the palette, and every key bound to one (`M-v`, `M-r` and `M-m` for GitHub, `M-i`, `M-l` and `M-W` for Linear, and any you give one in `keys.json`) does nothing but show a tip saying the service is off, the first time, and nothing after. With Notes in front, the palette asks the app first and lists, ahead of the rest, "Autopilot: work “<note>”" for the note it shows (with Settings' "Autopilot" on) and Notes' own commands, each "Notes: <what it does>" with its key in Notes, the checklist item's first (with Settings' "Notes command palette" on); one picked runs in Notes as its key would. "Autopilot: work “<note>”" opens the mode's menu in mission control when it already works that note, asks "Autopilot is working “<other>”. Switch it to “<note>”? [Y/n]" when it works another, switching it with its counts, spend limit and landing kept, and, with it off, asks for the repository and sets it up as "Autopilot: work a note" does after its note. The diff's own commands, copying its story and asking Claude about it, are listed only while the pane has the diff open (asking only with Settings' "Ask Claude about the diff" on), and the two that show the pane a tool stands in for beside it, or the tool alone again, only while a tool stands in the pane's place.

`M-p`'s "Shape a note's checklist items as Linear tickets" picks a note (the one open in Notes first, then the newest), then its items (every one chosen to begin with; Enter on a row leaves it out or puts it back, and the first row shapes what is chosen), then the repository, and opens a tab running `/shape` for each item at the end of the tab bar, the first in front, its details and numbered sub-items with it. "Shape a new Linear ticket from what you type, in a new tab" does the same for one idea typed in. Both need Linear (the `linear` setting). `M-p`'s "View the branch's PR description as an image" draws the description as GitHub does, in place of the pane.

### More in the palette

These are in `M-p`, with no key but the one named, unless you give them one in `keys.json`:

- **Rename the tab**: a name typed in a box, starting from the one it has; empty gives the tab its own name back, as "Rename it" in mission control's menu does.
- **Mission control: \<view\>**, one for every view mission control shows with your settings as they are (Notifications, Calendar, Reminders, Slack, Reopen, PRs, CI, Landing, Autopilot, Routines, Tickets, Inbox, Resources, Usage, Keys, Settings, and each sidebar plugin's view by its title): mission control opened on that view, or switched to it when it is open. A view turned off in Settings isn't listed, and one needing GitHub or Linear goes while that service is off. Keys opens as `M-/` does, Settings as M-p's "Settings" does.
- **New routine**: the box a [routine](#routines) is made in, in the directory of the pane you are in.
- **Go to the notifications** (`M-O`): the Notifications window, every notification with its whole message and Enter opening its actions; with Settings' "Notifications window" (`notice_list`) off, the sidebar's cursor on the first notification instead, as `M-a` reaches them, or, in a window with no sidebar, mission control on the first one's view (its Slack, Inbox or Calendar view, Settings for a suggestion), else a box saying why there is nowhere to show them.
- **How you use Switchboard**: what [Suggestions from how you work](#suggestions-from-how-you-work) has counted, in a box: the keys you press most this week and month, the commands you pick in `M-p`, the views you open, and the suggestions now. `j` and `k` scroll it, and any other key closes it.
- **Send Claude's last reply to another tab**: the last thing this tab's Claude said in its last turn, from its transcript, pasted into the Claude of a tab you pick (its active pane when it runs none) without Enter, and that tab brought to the front, so you can add to it before sending. A Claude behind the diff, lazygit or a view in its pane's place comes back beside it first. Its line breaks stay in the text.
- **Update local main to origin's, without switching branches**: `switchboard update-main` (below) for the pane's repository, what it did in a box.
- **Bring a target branch up to date with the default branch, under its landing lock**: picks one of the pane's repository's local branches other than its default one and opens a tab in its main clone running `switchboard update-target` for it ([Landing](#landing-queue-and-clean)), or brings the tab already running it to the front.
- **Check Switchboard's setup**: `switchboard setup --check`, what it would change, in a box, changing nothing. **Run a part of Switchboard's setup, or only check it** lists the parts (`switchboard setup --parts`) and every part at once, Tab marking several, then runs the ones picked or only checks them.
- **Open a PR in the browser by number or URL**: a PR's number (a PR of the pane's repository as `gh` and `wtpr` read a bare number: the one `gh repo set-default` names, else in a fork's clone the project its `upstream` remote names, else origin's) or its link, typed in a box that starts with the clipboard's when it holds one; empty is the tab's own PR, as `M-r` opens it. Then a menu: open it in the browser, or work on it in a new tab (`wtpr`). Needs GitHub.
- **Open a Linear ticket in the browser by ID, number or URL**: the same for a ticket, a bare number taking the team key of the ticket the branch names, and working on it a new tab running `wt` in the pane's repository. Needs Linear.
- **Open the repository's merge queue in the browser**: GitHub's merge queue for the branch's PR's base branch, or for the repository's default branch when the branch has no PR. Needs GitHub.
- **Open the branch's Linear project in the browser**: the project the ticket in the branch (or the tab's name) is in, as Linear says, or a box saying why there is none. Needs Linear.
- **Copy the branch's ticket ID**, **ticket URL**, **PR number** and **PR URL**: copied, and a box saying what was. The ticket's link is the workspace's in `linear.json` when it names one, else the one Linear gives. Each needs its service.
- **Keep the computer awake until I say, or back to only while tabs work**: the computer held awake whatever the tabs do, a restart on a new build included, until you choose it again, which goes back to Settings' "Keep the computer awake while tabs work" ([Keeping the computer awake](#keeping-the-computer-awake)). A box says which it is now.
- **Filter the sidebar to a repository** and **Show every repository's tabs in the sidebar**: the sidebar's tabs of one repository in their groups, every other one's under Other repos, and the filter cleared ([Filtered to a repository](#filtered-to-a-repository)).
- **Log in to AWS (aws sso login) in your Chrome profile**: `switchboard aws-login` in a pane beside yours, which logs in to the one `[sso-session]` in `~/.aws/config` and opens AWS's login page in the Chrome profile `browser.json` gives `awsapps.com` (else its default profile, else the one Chrome picks itself, while Settings' `chrome_profiles` is on), whatever host the page is on. The pane stays until the login is done and a key is pressed, so its code and any error can be read. It needs the AWS CLI (`aws`), which Switchboard doesn't install.

### Messages

What a key has to tell you (nothing needs you, a link copied, no ticket in this branch) shows in a small box in the middle of the screen, as wide as its text, which any key or a click closes and which goes by itself after five seconds, rather than on the status line for a moment (Settings' "Messages in a box", `message_boxes`). The key that closes it goes no further. A new message takes the place of one still up. `switchboard say` shows one from a script.

### Tips

A tip is a small box in the top-right corner that teaches a key at the moment it would help, once each:

- A tab first waits on you, or first finishes, while you look elsewhere: `M-a`.
- You switch tabs with five or more open: `M-j` and `M-a`.
- You pin a tab for the first time: how it stays pinned.
- You first move into the sidebar: its keys.
- A tab's PR first merges: `/queue-and-clean`.
- A tab first loops in the background: where mission control shows it.
- A tool first takes a pane's place, as `M-d` does: how to get the pane back.
- You answer the first update offered: where to look for updates.
- You press a key of GitHub or Linear while that service is off: how to turn it back on.
- You pick a command from `M-p` that has a key of its own: the key.
- Claude first starts in a worktree (`.claude/worktrees/`), with GitHub on: `M-v`, `M-d` and `M-m` for its PR.
- Option types `˙` `∆` `˚` or `¬` where it doesn't act as Alt: how to set it (see [Option as Alt on a Mac](#option-as-alt-on-a-mac)).

Mission control's first opening says how to get around it in its own footer instead.

Any key closes a tip and then does what it would have done, so nothing you type is lost; a click or the wheel closes it and goes no further. Left alone, it goes after eight seconds. A tip that comes while another box is up (another tip, a message, a menu, mission control) waits for its next chance. Doing what a tip teaches (pressing `M-a`) counts as having seen it. The tips shown are listed in `~/.local/state/switchboard/tips`. `M-p`'s "Show the tips again" shows each once more, and Settings' "Tips" (`tips`) turns them off.

### Menus and questions

Every menu, question and list looks the same: a rounded blue edge with the title in its top, the chosen option highlighted with a yellow `›`, and the keys along the bottom, each in the accent and what it does dim. A menu's read `↑↓ move  ↵ choose  esc close` (board 0), and a long one's `/ search` before `esc close`, though the letter keys below still work. A box that waits on you (a menu, a question, a list, the palette, a message) stands out the same way too: everything behind it dims, the sidebar and status line included, the box sits one shade lighter than the background, and a soft shadow falls under it, drawn as an image where the terminal can show one and as half blocks (`▌` `▀`, meeting in a `▘` at the corner) along its right and bottom where it can't. A tip in the corner and the sidebar's peek leave the screen as it is. In a menu, `j` and `k` (the arrows, `C-n` and `C-p`, and the wheel with Settings' "Click in menus" on) move, `g` and `G` go to the first and last, Tab and Shift-Tab go round, Enter or Space takes the chosen option, a number takes that one, as does an option's own key where one has it (never one the menu moves or closes with), and Esc or `q` goes back. `/` searches it: what you type shows on a line under its text, and only the options whose label holds every word typed stay, best first, the cursor on the first; while it is open every key types (a number or an option's key too), the arrows (`C-n`, `C-p`) move, Enter takes the chosen match, and Backspace past the last character or Esc shows every option again, a second Esc going back. A menu of more than five options says `/ search` among its keys; M-m's box, the answer box and Notes' Format menu search the same way. In a list to type in, such as the palette, the arrows (`C-p` and `C-n`, Tab and Shift-Tab) move, Enter picks, and Esc closes. In every menu, list and box with a list (M-F's results, M-m's, the answer box, Notes' Format menu and its Find a note and Recent notes, the palette), the arrows, `C-n` and `C-p`, and `j` and `k` where neither is an option's own key (Format's `k` is Link…), wrap round, Down on the last option going to the first and Up on the first to the last (but M-F's Up on its first result, which goes to its chips), over the matches alone while a search is open, while PageUp, PageDown, `g`, `G`, Home, End and the wheel stop at the ends.

Every box that takes typing (a question to type an answer in such as a tab's name or a setting, the answer box, the reminder box, "Shape a new Linear ticket", a menu's or a list's search, M-F's query) types as a text box does. `←` and `→` move a character, `M-←` and `M-→` (`M-b`, `M-f`) a word, Home and End (`C-a`, `C-e`) to the line's start and end, and a click in the text puts the cursor there; Backspace and Delete take the character before or after it, `M-Backspace` and `C-w` the word before it, `C-u` everything back to the line's start and `C-k` to its end, `C-z` undoes a word at a time and `C-S-z` redoes, and a paste goes in at the cursor. A long line wraps at the box's width, at a space where there is one, the box growing a row at a time up to half the screen and then scrolling to keep the cursor in sight, where `↑` and `↓` move a row (on the first or last row, they move the box's list as before). A search is one row, which scrolls sideways instead. Where what you type can take more than a line (the answer box and "Shape a new Linear ticket"), `S-Enter` and `M-Enter` start a new line and a paste keeps its line breaks; elsewhere `S-Enter` does nothing and a paste's lines join with spaces. Enter takes what is typed. The Keys view lists them under "Boxes".

A question shows `[Y/n]` and takes Enter as yes; `y` says yes too, and `n`, Esc or any other key says no. Space answers only once Tab has moved between Yes and No, so a stray Space is still no.

Menus and questions take a click too: one click takes an option or answers Yes or No, a click on a list's row twice picks it, and a click outside the box closes it, as a right click outside a box over the workspace does, while a right click inside one does nothing (Settings' "Click in menus", `menu_mouse`). With it on, Settings' "Highlight on hover" (`hover_highlight`) lights the option under the mouse, and in Ghostty and kitty turns the mouse pointer into a hand over anything a click presses in the workspace (a box's options, the sidebar's rows, mission control's cards and rows, the header's keys, the status line's tabs) and into a resizing arrow over a line between panes, which a drag moves, keeping the text beam over a pane; and Settings' "Easy mode" (`easy_keys`, off by default) puts a `×` in each box's top-right corner, which a click, or Tab to it and Enter, closes. A box's options always take Tab and Shift-Tab; Settings' "Tab moves focus" (`tab_focus`) does the same for mission control and the sidebar. A menu or question a click opens (a right click's menu too) appears beside the place clicked, one cell right of and below it, or to its left or above it near the screen's edge, while one opened by a key opens in the middle (Settings' "Open boxes by the click", `boxes_near_click`). A box follows these settings as they are when it opens.

### Closing a busy pane

`M-w` closes a pane at once when it is a shell at its prompt. When a program runs in it, it asks first, "Close pane?", with what runs under the question, so a running agent or build isn't lost to a stray key. What runs is the program by its name with its arguments after it, paths under your home written from `~` and an `http` or `https` link shown only up to its host, or only its scheme when it carries a user (`npm run dev`, `vim ~/code/a.rs`), cut to 60 columns, or, for a pane Switchboard opened, what it is: Notes, the diff (or the diff of uncommitted changes), the PR view, the ticket view, the link view, the AWS sign-in, Switchboard's setup. It closes only the pane, a tab with Notes brought beside its last pane keeping that pane with a fresh shell in it. In the tab's Claude pane, in a tab whose one pane it is, or on a tab's row in the sidebar, `M-w` closes the tab instead, asking "Close tab 3 …?" with the programs running in its panes listed, as `M-p`'s "Close the tab, after asking" does from anywhere; the prefix's `x` and `M-p`'s "Close the pane" close the pane alone wherever it is, the last pane closing its tab. Settings' "Ask before closing a pane" (`confirm_close`) turns both questions off, and "M-w closes the tab from Claude or a lone pane" (`close_tab_from_claude`) off makes `M-w` close the pane it is pressed in wherever that is, the last pane closing its tab.

To close several tabs at once, select them in the sidebar (`v`, see [Into the sidebar and its keys](#into-the-sidebar-and-its-keys)) and pick "Close these tabs" from their menu, which asks once, "Close 3 tabs?", listing each tab's number and name with the programs running in its panes, each by its name, Switchboard's own in a word or two (`uncommitted diff`, `PR view`).

## The sidebar

The sidebar runs down the left of every tab: the session's tabs, most in need of you first, what each one's Claude is doing, under them the first items of each plugin that asks for a place there (see "Plugins"), and the notifications. It is 34 columns wide, and a window narrower than 90 columns has none (both are set in `sidebar.json`, below); a zoomed pane covers it. Settings' "Sidebar" (`sidebar`) turns it off and on, as does "Turn the sidebar on or off" in `M-p`. It is drawn on a shade of its own, a step darker than the panes (in the canvas's own colors on a terminal that draws truecolor), with the chosen row lighter and the accent's `▌` at its left; Settings' "Shade the sidebar" (`sidebar_panel`) off draws it on the terminal's background.

At its top are the session's name, in a pill of the current tab's color, and its counts: failing builds first, in red (`✗ 2`), then the tabs waiting on you (`! 1`), finished (`✓ 3`) and working (`⋯ 4`). Below them are the tabs in their groups, each group under its heading with how many it holds.

On a screen too short for every tab, the session's line at the top and the notifications at the bottom stay put and only the groups and tabs scroll: the line under the session's says how many tabs are above (`▲ 3 more`) while no filter is typed there, and the list's last row how many are cut below (`▼ 2 more`). The list holds still as you go from tab to tab: it stays where it was while the tab in front is in sight there, else at the top while the tab is in sight there, else it scrolls to the heading of the tab's group.

### Filtered to a repository

`M-R`, or `M-p`'s "Filter the sidebar to a repository", picks one of the repositories the session's tabs are in, each with how many tabs it has and the one filtered to now marked `now`: the repositories filtered to most recently come first, then those never filtered to, by the tab of theirs in front most recently and then by name. "Every repository" is the first row while a filter is on, where it clears it, and the last, marked `now`, with none; the list opens on the first repository that isn't the one filtered to now, which is the repository filtered to before while it still has a tab (else the next in the list, or "Every repository" when no other repository has a tab), so `M-R` and `Enter` go back to it (once the box saying which repository the sidebar is filtered to has closed: as with any message, the first key pressed while it is up only closes it); a worktree counts as its main clone, so every worktree of one repository is in it. Every other repository's tabs go under Other repos at the bottom, whatever they are doing, but one whose reminder has come due, which goes under Reminders elsewhere, a group after the filtered repository's own and before Other repos, with its repository named on the line under it, so Needs you holds the filtered repository's tabs alone; once its reminder is done with it goes back under Other repos; and a box says "Sidebar: switchboard only". Other repos is folded to its heading, which carries ▸ and its tabs' marks, so one waiting on you still shows, and their count, with their repositories named on the line under it; `Enter`, `l` or a click on that line unfolds it, listing its tabs ordered by the group each would otherwise be in, and a click on its heading folds it again. `M-a` and `M-A` pass those tabs by, but `M-a` goes to the tabs under Reminders elsewhere after the filtered repository's Needs you and Build failing tabs, and their notifications are as before. The top line shows the filter as a chip, "switchboard ×", how many of the tabs are its ("7 of 9") and `/ filter` at its right, the count giving way and then the repository's name being cut where the line is short, so `/ filter` stays; a click on it clears the filter, as "Show every repository's tabs in the sidebar" in `M-p` does. Other repos folds and unfolds as any group does ([Folding a group](#folding-a-group)). The filter and whether Other repos is unfolded are kept with the layout, through a restart and an upgrade, until cleared; the order the repositories were filtered to is kept the same way, and clearing the filter leaves it as it is. `switchboard sidebar-repo <dir>` filters to the repository a directory is in from a script, and `--all` clears it. Settings' "Sidebar repository filter" (`sidebar_repo_filter`) off takes the commands away and puts every tab in its own group.

### Groups

Each tab is in one group, the same as its stage in mission control's Tabs view, and the groups come in this order. Within a group the tabs are in the order of their numbers, lowest first, so a tab keeps its place as what it is doing changes; Needs you, In merge queue and Landing order theirs as the table says, and Other repos lists its tabs by the group each would otherwise be in, each of those in its own order.

| Group | What puts a tab there |
| --- | --- |
| Needs you | Its Claude waits on you or has finished there (a turn that ends while only watches run, too, the watches named: "finished 2m ago · monitoring · CI on #12"), you pinned it, or its reminder came due. Pinned tabs and reminders come first, then waiting tabs, the longest-waiting first, then finished ones, the oldest first. |
| Build failing | Its open pull request has a failing check and isn't in a merge queue, and no `/ci-loop` is at work on it. The heading is in red. |
| Working | Claude is working, or its turn ended with background tasks still running, which Claude carries on from when they end, with none of the loops below. |
| Monitoring | Claude's only background work is watches, each named ("monitoring · CI on #12"): `Monitor` watches, and background shell commands that only wait (a `while` or `until` loop that sleeps, `gh run watch`, `tail -f`) and run nothing but a sleep and commands that look (`grep`, `kill -0`, `gh run view`, `git log` and the like), named by their description; one that builds or tests between its looks stays working. A turn that ends while the watches run shows under Needs you first, then here once seen. |
| Review loop | Its Claude works under `/review-loop`, from the moment you ask for it and between its rounds, or the loop runs in the background. |
| CI loop | The same for `/ci-loop`, a failing build's included. |
| Queue-and-clean | The same for `/queue-and-clean`, until its pull request is queued, landing or merged. |
| Loops | A `/loop` between its ticks: a self-paced one while the wakeup its last tick scheduled is pending, and for five minutes after it is due; one on a timer (`/loop 5m …`, or a cron job Claude created) until Claude deletes the job, the session ends, or seven days pass. |
| In merge queue | Its pull request is in the merge queue, in queue order, with Settings' "Show the landing queue" off. |
| Landing | It holds or waits for the landing lock of `/queue-and-clean`'s "Land on the default branch", in the landing queue's order, or its pull request is in the merge queue ("merging" at its head, else "2nd in line to merge"). |
| Testing Deploy | Its pull request merged and the tab is still at work on it: `/deploy-test` waiting for the deploy or recording its test, or Claude working under `/deploy-test` or the `/queue-and-clean` that follows the merge. |
| Merged | Its work landed: its pull request merged, or `/queue-and-clean` landed it straight on a branch ("landed on main · 1a2b3c4", "landed on main without asking"), while the tab is in that repository's main clone on the branch it had, where the landing leaves it, until a prompt there starts new work; only while its Claude does nothing: at work again, or watching something, it is in that work's group, its words still saying it merged. |
| Idle | A Claude session with nothing to do. |
| Reminders | A reminder still to come, ahead of Build failing, while Claude isn't working there. |
| Other | No Claude in it: Notes, the Slack relay, a shell. A Claude parked behind the diff, lazygit or a view in its pane's place keeps its tab in its own stage. |
| Reminders elsewhere | While the sidebar is filtered to a repository, a tab of any other whose reminder has come due, until you go to it (see [Filtered to a repository](#filtered-to-a-repository)). |
| Other repos | While the sidebar is filtered to a repository, a tab of any other, whatever it is doing but a reminder come due (see [Filtered to a repository](#filtered-to-a-repository)). |

### Folding a group

Any group folds to one line: `Enter` or `Space` on its heading in the sidebar, `z` on one of its tabs, or a click on its heading. Every group's heading is a stop for `j`, `k`, `g`, `G` and `Tab`, above its tabs, lit when the cursor is on it, where `Enter`, `Space` or `z` folds or unfolds the group; `M-a`, `M-A`, `M-1`…`M-9` and a click on a tab never stop on one, and a `/` search steps over the tabs alone. Folded, it is its heading with its count and, on the line under it, its tabs' marks, so one waiting on you still shows. Its heading is then the stop in its tabs' place; `Enter`, `l`, `z` or a click on it unfolds the group, the cursor onto its first tab without bringing it to the front, so the tab in front stays the one you were on until `j`, `k` or `Enter` moves, and a click on its heading folds it again. The notifications fold the same way, `z` on one of them or a click on their heading, to their heading and one line of their kinds ("3 new on Slack · 2 PR updates"); a click there or `n` unfolds them. A `/` search unfolds every group while it is on, and a tab gone to with `M-a`, `M-A`, `M-1`…`M-9` or a search's match unfolds the group it is in. What is folded is kept with the layout, through a restart and an upgrade; nothing is folded until you fold it.

Settings' "Reminders in the sidebar" (`sidebar_reminders`) off leaves the Reminders group out of the sidebar: the top line counts its tabs on a chip (`⏰ 3`), a click on which opens mission control's Reminders view, `M-1`…`M-9` still reach them, and a tab comes back under Needs you when its reminder comes due.

Needs you always wins: a tab whose Claude waits on you stays there even while its pull request is queued. After it, the landing queue and the pull request's place win over what Claude is doing, so a tab whose PR is queued stays under Landing while `/queue-and-clean` watches it; then a reminder still to come, while Claude isn't working there, so a tab you set one on waits whatever its checks say (its row still shows the failing checks in red); then a failing build, unless `/ci-loop` is at work on it; then the loop running; then the work running. A tab's pull request, its checks and whether it is queued or merged come from one GitHub query for every tab's PR (see "How often mission control looks"), so a group can lag GitHub by that long. A tab whose worktree has been removed, as `/queue-and-clean` leaves it once its PR merges, is found by the worktree's branch and keeps its PR (in a fork's clone, a PR from your fork's branch in the project `upstream` names counts, one in your fork winning).

Settings' "Build failing" (`build_failing`) off leaves such tabs in Working or Idle, the failing count still in their words. Settings' "Show the landing queue" (`landing_queue`) off leaves landing tabs where their work puts them, and a tab whose PR is queued under In merge queue; the queue still orders the landings.

### What each tab shows

A tab's row starts with a bar in its project's color (thicker on the chosen tab), its badge, its number (its own while it is open, for `M-1`…`M-9`) and its name as the tab bar shows it, wrapped to at most three lines. Under it is what it is doing and for how long, as its Claude's hooks record it: "waiting on you 4m · permission: Bash git push", "finished 2m ago", "working 2m · Run the test suite", "working in background · 2 tasks", "idle for 20m". In auto mode a permission request counts as waiting only once Claude Code shows it to you: a call auto mode allows by itself stays work. A request whose hook names no tool takes the call from the transcript ("permission: Bash: sleep 30"). A tab running a known loop names it from the moment you ask for it (by its bare name or Switchboard's plugin's, `/switchboard:review-loop`), the work between its rounds included: "review loop · round 2" while `codex-review` runs, two runs to a round; "CI loop · round 3" while `/ci-loop` watches the checks; "queue-and-clean · watching" while `/queue-and-clean` watches the merge queue, or the step it is at while it lands on the default branch ("queue-and-clean · waiting for the landing lock"). A loop between ticks says when the next one is and the loop's prompt ("next in 8m · /oncall"). The row adds the place of its pull request ("in merge queue at 3", "merged") or in the landing queue ("landing", "2nd in line to land"), the PR's number when the name doesn't already say it, and a failing check in bold red ("✗1 failing") whatever else the tab is doing. A tab with no Claude names the program in front.

| Badge | Means |
| --- | --- |
| `!` yellow | Claude is waiting on you |
| `✓` green | Claude finished and you haven't looked yet |
| spinner, blue | working |
| spinner, slate | working in the background |
| spinner, violet, teal or orange | a review loop, a CI loop, `/queue-and-clean` |
| spinner, magenta | testing the deploy |
| `◉` cyan | monitoring |
| `↻` blue | a loop between its ticks |
| `»` orange | idle, its PR in the merge queue |
| `◆` purple | its PR merged |
| `⇣` amber | landing, or in line to land |
| `✗` red | idle, its open PR has a failing check |
| `📌` yellow (`⚑` with UX 2.0) | pinned |
| `⏰` blue | a reminder still to come |
| `⏰` orange | a reminder come due |
| `○` faint | idle |

The states are the ones the tab bar's marks show, recorded by Switchboard's hooks for Claude Code.

### Notifications

The notifications sit at the bottom of the sidebar, under a double yellow rule and their own heading, so they never scroll away and never read as a tab. Each kind is one line, saying how many there are, with a line of detail from the first under it, so the tabs keep their room however many come in; the Notifications window lists each one. The kinds, in this order:

- the next meeting, as it is about to start ("Standup in 3m · ↵ joins");
- new Slack messages for you ("3 new on Slack"), until you have seen them in the Slack view or dismissed them;
- changes to today's calendar since you last looked at it ("Calendar: Standup moved to 3pm", or "2 calendar changes");
- notes written for you ("New note: Standup 2026-11-02", or "3 new notes"), until you open them in Notes;
- unread notifications of your Linear Inbox ("2 in your Linear inbox"), while the Inbox view is on;
- your PRs' news ("PR 212 merged, web-app", "PR 2019 approved, web-app", "Checks failed on PR 2027, web-app", or "3 PR updates"), found by comparing each answer of the PRs board's lookups with the one before, so nothing that happened before the workspace started is said; each until you dismiss it, the newest 20 kept (Settings' "PR notifications", `pr_notices`);
- a default branch whose CI fails ("main failing at abc1234: Tests"), while autopilot works on its repository or a tab waits to land on it and its landings test in CI, once for each failing commit, until you dismiss it or a later commit passes;
- each landing autopilot made without asking ("Landed <short hash>: <item>", or "Landed 2 changes without asking"), until you dismiss it or go to its tab, and each landed tab it closed before you saw it ("Closed <item>: landed <short hash>"), until you dismiss it, both only while Settings' "Notify when autopilot lands" (`autopilot_landing_notices`) is on;
- what autopilot has to say: that it stopped for the day's spend, or the note has nothing left and none of its tabs is open;
- suggestions from how you work.

"Your day", "How you use it" and "Autopilot", below, say more of each.

Moving past the last tab with `j`, `n` in the sidebar, or a click puts the cursor on them, and brings the Notifications window to the front, as moving onto a tab brings that tab: a tab of its own, named Notifications in the tab bar only while it is in front, listing every notification with its whole message (a Slack message as Slack draws it) and when it came, dim at the right of its title ("9:42 AM" today, "Yesterday 6:05 PM", "Mon 2:10 PM" within the week, "Nov 3, 2:10 PM" within the year, the meeting still to come "Starts 3:00 PM"; one with no time of its own, when the history first saw it), the one the cursor is on chosen. `j` and `k` in the sidebar step over them a kind at a time, as the sidebar shows one line a kind, the window on that kind's first, and up from the first kind goes back to the last tab, while down from the last kind goes round to the first tab (Settings' `sidebar_wrap`). Enter in the sidebar moves into the window, where `j` and `k` choose (the sidebar's cursor following), Enter, `l`, `S-F10`, a click on the chosen one or a right click on any opens its menu over it, and `m` or a right click where no notification is the view's own (dismissing every notification shown, or mission control's Notifications view), as in mission control's Notifications view, and `Esc`, `q` or `h`, or a click on "‹ tabs", go back to the tab it was brought in front of; with Settings' "Tab moves focus" on, Tab and Shift-Tab go round "‹ tabs", the notifications and the footer's "m view actions", which a click opens too. The window is no tab: the sidebar, mission control, `M-1`…`M-9`, the next and previous tab keys and `switchboard tabs` leave it out, it stays after every tab, and it isn't saved with the layout. Settings' "Notifications window" (`notice_list`) turned off leaves the cursor on them in the sidebar, a kind at a time, where Enter or a click opens mission control's Notifications view with that notification's menu over it, nothing picked yet: the sidebar only leads to the notifications, and their menus, dismissing and every other choice are the window's and the view's. Each one's menu, the same box in the Notifications window and mission control's Notifications view, is its item's menu in the view it came from with dismissing added (a Slack message's is the Slack view's menu, the message whole above it and dismissed until its thread gets a reply), led by "Show it in the <view> view" while that view is on, so a click shows the item in its view, and whatever is picked there dismisses it, since it was dealt with; a suggestion's menu has its own choices. Settings' "Notifications" (`sidebar_notifications`) turns the block, and that view, off.

### Into the sidebar and its keys

`M-s` moves into the sidebar, on the tab you are in, and back out; `M-Left` from the leftmost pane moves in too, and `M-Right` back out. While it has the focus, its cursor moves through the tabs and each comes to the front as the cursor reaches it, with the list held still so tabs don't move about as you look at them. Leaving keeps the tab you are on. A key that acts on "the pane" (a split, zoom) gives the focus back to your pane first and acts there, while `M-w` asks to close the tab under the cursor, keeping the focus in the sidebar; `M-a`, `M-A`, `M-1`…`M-9`, `M-f`, `M-y` and `M-p` keep it, and pressed from the sidebar `M-a`, `M-A` and `M-1`…`M-9` leave the cursor on the tab they went to.

| Key | In the sidebar |
| --- | --- |
| `j` `k` (arrows, `C-n` `C-p`) | the next or previous tab, brought to the front, or group's heading; on the notifications, the next or previous kind, the Notifications window on its first; from the last row round to the first and from the first to the last (Settings' "Up and down wrap around", `sidebar_wrap`; off, they stop at the ends) |
| `g` `G` | the first or last row, a group's heading or a tab |
| `z` | fold the chosen tab's group to one line, or the notifications on one of them; on a group's heading, fold or unfold it (see [Folding a group](#folding-a-group)) |
| `C-d` `C-u` | the list half a screen down or up, the cursor onto the tab there, stopping at the first and last tab, as in Vim (Settings' "Half-page scrolling", `sidebar_half_page`; off, they run in the chosen tab) |
| `Enter` `l` | into the chosen tab's pane; on a group's heading, `Enter` folds or unfolds it, and `l` unfolds a folded one; on a notification, into the Notifications window (with it off, what the notification leads to) |
| `h` `q` `Esc` `C-c` | back out to your pane (`Esc` clears a kept filter first) |
| `1`–`9` | the cursor onto that tab, brought to the front |
| `/` | find a tab by name: typing narrows the list, the arrows move, Enter goes, Esc clears |
| `Tab` `S-Tab` | the next or previous row, tabs then notifications, wrapping round (Settings' "Tab moves focus", `tab_focus`; off, `Tab` opens mission control) |
| `Space` | peek at the chosen tab; on a group's heading, fold or unfold it |
| `m` `S-F10` | the menu a right click on the chosen row opens, beside it: a tab's, a notification's, or on a group's heading the sidebar's own |
| `a` | answer the chosen tab's Claude, or send it a message, in mission control's box (see [Answering a tab](#answering-a-tab)) |
| `n` | to the notifications, unfolding them, or a box saying there are none |
| `t` | a new tab running Claude, as `M-t` opens one, from the chosen tab, so its directory is first in the list |
| `i` | the chosen tab's whole title in a box, as `M-p`'s "Show this tab's full title" shows it |
| `d` `L` | the chosen tab gone to, its diff (as `M-d`) or lazygit in its pane's place |
| `v` | select mode, the chosen tab selected (Settings' "Select several tabs", `sidebar_select`) |
| any other Alt or Ctrl key | goes to the chosen tab and runs there |
| `?` | these keys |

A click on a tab goes there, and the wheel scrolls half a screen, as `C-d` and `C-u` do, bringing the focus into the sidebar, the rest of one flick let go; on a notification, or going on past the first or last tab, it steps as `j` and `k` do, so the notifications stay in reach (with `sidebar_half_page` off, it always steps, as `j` and `k` do). A click on the `▲ more` or `▼ more` row scrolls half a screen, as `C-u` and `C-d` do, whether or not a filter is being typed (with `sidebar_half_page` on). A click on a group's heading, or the notifications', folds or unfolds it, and a click on a folded line unfolds it. A click anywhere else takes the focus back out. With Settings' "Highlight on hover" (`hover_highlight`) on, the row under the mouse is lit.

A right click opens a menu beside it: on a tab, the tab's menu as mission control's Tabs view has it, with peeking at it first and chosen, so a right click then Enter peeks; on a notification, its own menu, as in the Notifications window; anywhere else, the sidebar's own: on a group's heading or the notifications', folding or unfolding it first, then a new tab, filtering the sidebar to a repository (and showing every one again while it is filtered), going to the notifications and dismissing every one shown while there are any. With Settings' "Click in menus" (`menu_mouse`) off, a right click on a tab peeks at it and anywhere else does nothing. `m` or `S-F10` with the focus in the sidebar opens the menu a right click on the cursor's row would, beside that row, whatever that setting says, and with UX 2.0 the sidebar's footer shows `m menu` while the focus is in it.

`v` starts select mode, for closing several tabs at once: the chosen tab is selected, and a selected tab's row carries a `◆` in the accent before its mark. The top line says how many are selected, "3 selected", in a chip, with `↵ actions  esc` beside it. In select mode, `j`, `k` and the other moving keys move as ever, `Space` or `v` selects or unselects the tab under the cursor, and a click on a tab selects or unselects it rather than going there; a Shift-click on a tab, in a terminal that passes Shift-clicks on, starts select mode with it selected. `Enter`, `m` or a click on the chip opens a menu, "3 tabs selected", whose "Close these tabs" asks once, "Close 3 tabs?", listing each tab's number and name and the programs running in its panes; yes closes each, the tab in front last, so the focus goes where closing it alone would, leaves select mode and the sidebar, back to that tab's pane, and says "Closed 3 tabs.", and no keeps the selection. A `/` filter typed in select mode narrows the list as ever, and `Enter` keeps it and goes back to select mode's keys, the tabs still selected. `Esc` clears a kept filter first, then leaves select mode, unselecting every tab, and so does leaving the sidebar; a tab closed meanwhile drops out. `M-p`'s "Select several tabs in the sidebar" moves into the sidebar in select mode, the tab in front selected. Settings' "Select several tabs" (`sidebar_select`) off makes `v` do nothing and a Shift-click go to the tab as a click does, so the sidebar acts on one tab at a time.

### Peeking

A peek shows a tab without going there, in a box over most of the screen: its last turn in full (your prompt, all of Claude's reply, and the tool call it waits on), or, for a tab with no Claude, its screen with 200 lines of what scrolled off above it. `s` switches between the turn and the screen, `r` reads the screen again, `j` `k` scroll (`C-d` `C-u` half the box, `g` `G` the ends), Enter goes to the tab, and `Esc`, `q`, `C-c` or `Space` close it, so `Space` in the sidebar opens and closes it; with easy mode's ×, Tab moves onto it and Enter presses it. `Space` in the sidebar, a right click on a tab then Enter, and "Peek at its last turn" in a tab's menu in mission control open it.

### What needs you, one tab at a time

`M-a` goes to the next of what needs you, across every session, in the sidebar's order: the tabs under Needs you, pinned ones and reminders come due first, then those waiting on you, then those that finished, then the tabs under Build failing, whose PR has a failed check (Settings' "M-a visits Build failing tabs", `walk_build_failing`), then, in a sidebar filtered to a repository, the tabs under Reminders elsewhere, and last, while there are any, the notifications (Settings' "M-a goes to notifications", `needs_notices`), where it puts the sidebar's cursor on the first one. Each press goes on to the stop after the one you are at, wrapping round, so pressing it again and again walks the whole list, though a tab stays waiting until you answer it. A finished tab leaves the list once seen, so pressed again from there the walk carries on where it was. A Build failing tab stays on the list until its checks pass. From a tab that only finished or whose build fails, or one not on the list, a tab pinned or waiting on you comes first. With nothing to go to it says "Nothing needs you."

`M-A` goes to the next idle tab the same way: the tabs under Idle, in the sidebar's order, across every session, the one after the tab you are at, wrapping at the end, or the first from a tab that isn't idle. Only the sidebar's grouping can tell an idle Claude from one working in the background or looping, so those aren't on it, nor a tab with no Claude. It says "No idle tabs." when there are none, and "This is the only idle tab." from the only one. Settings' "M-A cycles idle tabs" (`idle_walk`) off hands `M-A` to the pane.

### Pinning and reminders

`M-f` pins the tab you are in: it goes to the top of Needs you with a yellow `📌` (with UX 2.0 a `⚑` in place of its state's mark), whatever its Claude is doing, and stays until `M-f` unpins it, since going to it doesn't clear it. A tab's menu in mission control pins and unpins it too.

"Remind me about this tab later" in `M-p`, or "Remind me about it later" in a tab's menu in mission control, asks when, in a list (in 20 minutes, in 1 hour, in 3 hours, tomorrow at 9:00, next Monday at 9:00) where a choice's number and Enter take it, or a time typed in (`45m`, `2h`, `1d`, `3pm`, `15:30`, `tomorrow 9am`, a clock time already past today being tomorrow's; a weekday, `tue 9am`; or a date, `nov 20`, `11/20`, `dec 1 3pm`, `2027-01-04 15:30`, at 9:00 with no time and a date already past this year being next year's), which wins over a row it only resembles, then what for, which may be left empty. With UX 2.0 on, one box asks both, titled "Change the reminder for <tab>" when one is set: When offers the five times, each with the clock time it means at the right ("2:35", "Sat 9:00"), or one typed in, read back as you type ("✓ Tuesday, November 10 at 9:00 AM · in 3 days 19 hours", or what it couldn't read); What for holds what it is for, the one set now to start with; at its foot the reminder set now. `↑` `↓` choose a time, typing takes over the chosen one, Tab moves to What for and the keys, Enter sets it from either field (a time it can't read keeping the box open), `M-x` clears it, Esc cancels; each time, field and key is a click too. Until then the tab waits under Reminders with a blue `⏰` and the time left ("remind in 1h 20m · check the deploy"), even while its build fails, unless something else places it: working, it is under Working (or Build failing), waiting on you or finished, under Needs you, and its PR landing, queued or merged, there, each with its own badge and the reminder's words beside it. When the time comes it moves to the top of Needs you with an orange `⏰`, and a box on the screen used last names it, with a desktop notification while Settings' "Push notifications" (`notifications`) is on. It is done with once you are at the tab. Asked for again on a tab with a reminder, the menu offers "Clear the reminder" first. Reminders are kept with the tabs and survive a restart. Settings' "Reminders" (`reminders`) off neither sets nor shows them.

### The Landing group

`/queue-and-clean` in a repository that lands straight on its default branch lands each worktree's work one at a time, behind a landing lock, the others waiting in a landing queue. The tab holding the lock is first under Landing, saying "landing" or the step it is at; the tabs waiting follow in the queue's order ("2nd in line to land"). A landing that must review its change again lets the lock go and waits first in line while it reviews, kept there without holding up those behind it, and lands next once its review is clean. A tab counts only while its worktree is there and the Claude session that took the lock or joined the queue still runs, wherever it started (a session started in the main clone that went into the worktree to land it included), or any Claude session runs in the worktree, so a landing that ended without a word leaves the group. The tab shown is the one whose pane took the lock or joined the queue, else the one whose Claude runs that session, so a landing run from a directory other tabs share files only its own tab; a lock taken by an older build, which names neither, goes to the tab in its worktree, and to none when several tabs are there. Enter on a waiting tab's card in mission control offers "Land it next", "Move it up in the landing queue" and "Move it down in the landing queue", which reorder the queue as `switchboard land-lock move` does; the landing tab can't be passed. A tab whose pull request is in the merge queue joins the group too, "merging" at the queue's head, else "2nd in line to merge".

### sidebar.json

`~/.config/switchboard/sidebar.json` sets the sidebar's size, mission control's cards and the plugins. Every key is optional, and a file that isn't JSON leaves every key at its default. It is read again whenever it changes, and mission control's Settings view has a row for each key.

```json
{
  "width": 34,
  "minWindowWidth": 90,
  "cardWidth": 56,
  "cardHeight": 13,
  "plugins": { "github": { "query": "org:your-org" } }
}
```

`width` is the columns the sidebar takes, `minWindowWidth` the narrowest window that gets one, `cardWidth` and `cardHeight` the size of mission control's cards, and `plugins` the plugins that load (see "Plugins").

## Mission control

`M-Space` opens mission control over the whole screen, and closes it again. Its first view is every tab of every session as a card; the views after it (Reopen, pull requests, CI, tickets, your calendar and Slack, Settings and more) are named in the bar across the top, each with how many items it has, beside the counts of tabs waiting, finished and working. The keys, or what the last action did, are at the bottom. The first time it opens, the bottom line says how to get around it.

### The cards

The cards are grouped in stages, the sidebar's groups: Needs you, Build failing, Working, Monitoring, Review loop, CI loop, Queue-and-clean, Loops, In merge queue, Landing, Testing Deploy, Merged, Idle, Reminders, then Other. A tab running a loop is in that loop's stage, from the moment you ask for it, while Claude works between its rounds too; a tab whose pull request is in the merge queue or landing is in that stage even while it runs a loop, and a merged one is under Merged only while it does nothing; and a tab with a failing build that runs `/ci-loop` stays under CI loop while the loop is at work. Each stage is a box in the stage's color, titled on its top edge, packed side by side so the screen fills: a stage that doesn't fit in the room left on a row starts the next, and one too big for a row takes several rows of cards. While a note is worked continuously, a line above the cards says so ("Autopilot", under Worktrees).

A card's top edge carries the tab's badge, number and name. Inside are what it is doing, its pull request's number, checks, review and size ("#12 · ✓12 ✗1 ⏳3 · approved · +340 −80"), and the session's name; then your last prompt, the end of Claude's last reply, and, when it waits on a permission prompt or a question, what it is asking. A tab with no Claude shows its directory.

With UX 2.0 (`ux2`) the Tabs view is board 3. Its header ends with how many tabs there are and how many need you ("11 tabs · 3 need you"). Two groups are cards, each a label with its count at the right edge and no frame round it: Needs you, a finished tab you haven't looked at among them, and Working, which a tab watching a monitor, running a review loop, a CI loop or queue-and-clean, or working while its build fails is part of here, in the tabs' numbers' order; a tab that is landing, in the merge queue or testing a deploy is a line on the shelf whatever its Claude is doing, and a merged one only while it does nothing: at work again, or watching something, it is in that work's group, its card's foot saying it merged ("#12 merged", "landed 1a2b3c4"). A card is a closed box with rounded corners, its edge in its group's tone, the chosen one's edge alone in the accent and the one under the mouse in its tab's color. The cards start under their label and share the width equally, as many side by side as fit at 40 columns each, up to four (three at 160 columns with the list of views beside them, two at 120), and a row of cards is as tall as its tallest holds, 20 rows at most. With more tabs than the screen holds and Settings' `scroll_bars` on, the scroll bar has the last column and the cards end a column before it. A card's top edge has the tab's mark, its number in its color and its name, cut only where the edge has no room for it, and at its right how long it has been in its state ("waiting 4m", "working 2m"); its foot has its branch, cut only where the card has no room for it, then its PR's checks in their state's color (✓ 412 passed, ◌ 3 of 5 run, ✕ 2 of 401 failed), or "no PR", and at the right edge how long the tab has been open (`opened 1h12m`, left out where the card is too narrow). What is inside depends on what the tab is doing:

- waiting on you: what it asks, word for word, under its kind ("Bash command" and the whole command, or "Question · <header>" and the question), its options, each with its description under it (a question's from Claude, a permission prompt's as Claude Code shows them), and under Before it asked what Claude said last before asking. Enter's menu on the card (and on its reminder) shows the request whole above its choices, when the screen has the room for it, and offers each option as "Answer: <option>" after "Go to tab", as "Answer it here" would answer it, only where what it answers is known whole: not on a request the card doesn't show all of (an edit, whose change it doesn't show, one longer than six lines, or, in a card's own menu, one whose words and options with their descriptions take more than the 15 rows inside a card), a question taking several choices, the option for an answer of your own, or an option whose screen shows more than its label, that doesn't fit the menu's row whole after "Answer: ", or whose line reaches the pane's edge, which "Answer it here" or the tab answers; a card whose request is longer than it shows says the tab shows all of it;
- working: under This turn what the turn has done (your prompt and Claude's latest words, or its summary), then under Now the tool it runs, its command, path or pattern, and for how long, and, with three cards across, chips for its phase, its review round and its diff's size;
- anything else (a finished tab, one you pinned, one whose reminder came due): under Last turn a sentence on what its last turn did, then its state in words.

"Show its screen in the card" in the chosen card's menu shows its screen in its place, until "Show its card again" or the cursor moves; a card of a tab with no Claude (one you pinned) shows its screen's last lines. Under the cards, the shelf: every other group as a label with its count (Build failing, Landing, In merge queue, Testing Deploy, Merged, Loops, Reminders, Idle, then Other, the tabs with no Claude such as a shell or Notes, Reminders elsewhere and Other repos), each tab a line with its mark, its number in its color, its name, how long it has been there or its place in line to land, and what its last turn did (where a tab with no Claude is, for one), which goes first on a narrow screen. With room for only one card across, the shelf's groups fold to their labels, and Enter on one shows its tabs.

What a last turn did is a sentence Haiku writes once the turn has ended, from the turn's prompt and Claude's last reply, kept in `~/.cache/switchboard/turn-summaries/<session>.json` so a turn is asked about once, held while you're away as the lookups are (`pause_when_away`); until it comes, or if it fails, a card shows the reply's first line. It sends the turn's words to Claude, so it waits for Settings' "Summaries of each turn" (`turn_summaries`), off by default; off, nothing is sent and the cards show the reply's first line. On a call asking several questions, a card follows the one its tab's screen shows.

### Keys

Mission control's own keys only move about. No view acts on an item by a key of its own: in every view, Enter opens the chosen card's or row's actions, what Enter did before them first, so Enter twice does it, and `m` opens the view's own actions. Every view's footer reads `Enter actions  m view actions  / filter` after its keys that move (in the view's own words where the canvas words them: the Inbox's "Enter actions, marks read", Resources' "Enter actions: go to the tab, end a process", Settings' "Enter its choices", Autopilot's "m view actions: its settings, pause"), each key a click and, with one view shown, a Tab stop (with several at once, Tab goes between the views).

| Key | In mission control |
| --- | --- |
| `h` `j` `k` `l` (arrows) | move between cards or rows; `j` `k` move a row of cards keeping the column, on a board `h` `l` move between its columns, and in the Calendar a day back or forward |
| `g` `G` | the first or last |
| `J` `K` | with UX 2.0, scroll the pane beside a board (a PR's or a ticket's description, the Autopilot plan) down or up a line; the wheel over the pane scrolls it three; it shows another item from its top |
| `C-w h` `C-w l` | move the focus between a view's panes, as Vim's windows: the Settings board's categories at the left, the list or board, and the pane beside it (a PR's, a ticket's, an Inbox item's, a Slack thread); `C-w w` and `C-w W` go round them (`C-w C-h`, `C-w C-l` and `C-w C-w` too). With UX 2.0 the list of views at the left is the leftmost stop: `C-w h` from the view's leftmost pane gives it the keys, where `j` and `k` move its chosen row (each view coming to the front, with `mission_nav_follow` on) and Enter opens it or moves into the view in front, and `C-w l` hands them back to the view. After `C-w` the footer shows it for a second, waiting for the second key; any other key is taken as itself. The focused pane takes `j` and `k`: in the pane beside they scroll it a line and `g` `G` go to its top and end, the rule before it in the accent, while `h` `l`, Enter and `m` keep what they do; a click in a pane gives it the focus too. The pane with the keys is the one drawn at full strength: every other pane of the view is greyed, as a tab's pane without the focus is, but a pane showing what it has chosen (the pane beside while the list has the keys, the list while the categories or sections over it do), the list of views with them while a view has the keys, and the whole view while the list of views does; with several views at once, every view but the one with the focus is greyed too. The footer names `C-w h l panes` where a view has more than one. `C-w j` and `C-w k` (`C-w C-j`, `C-w C-k` too) move the cursor to the first row of the next or last section a view stacks in its list, as Usage stacks its tabs, services and sessions, Settings its categories, Reopen its tabs, sessions and worktrees, the Work map its repositories and the Inbox its days, staying put with none that way; the footer names them while `C-w` waits, in a view of more than one section, and a view of one section, or a board whose sections stand side by side, takes them as themselves. Settings' "C-w then h, j, k or l moves between panes" (`ctrl_w_panes`) off leaves the list with the keys |
| `C-M-S-h` `C-M-S-l` | with UX 2.0, widen or narrow the pane beside the PRs, Tickets or Autopilot board five columns (the pane resize keys, whatever `keys.json` binds `resize-left-5` and `resize-right-5` to without the prefix, which the footer names beside `J K`); dragging the pane's left edge, lit under the mouse, does the same; each board keeps its width, between 30 columns and what leaves the board its narrowest, in the state directory's `mission-side.json`, so it outlives a restart; `C-M-S-j` and `C-M-S-k` do nothing there. Settings' "Resize the pane beside a board" (`resize_side_panes`) off keeps the pane at its share of the screen |
| `1`–`9` | choose that tab |
| `]` `[` | the next or previous view; with UX 2.0, round the views of the group in front |
| `M-]` `M-[` | with UX 2.0, the first view of the next or previous group |
| `M-{` `M-}` | go back or forward through the tabs and views visited, closing it onto a tab |
| `Tab` `S-Tab` | move the focus a section at a time, as on a webpage: each view's name (with UX 2.0 the list of views as one stop, while `mission_nav_follow` is on), the filter, a side panel (Settings' categories), then each section of the view at its first row (the Usage view's tabs, services and sessions, a grouped view's headings, a board's columns, the Keys board's groups; a list of one section is one stop, its rows reached with `j` and `k`), a pane beside it, then the footer's keys, wrapping round; Enter or Space presses it. With Settings' "Tab moves focus" (`tab_focus`) off, they switch views |
| `v` | go to a view by typing part of its name, lit in the bar as you type; Enter goes there |
| `/` | filter: the cards by name, number, PR or directory, a view's rows by their words, each found somewhere in the row in any order, as in the palette ("slack reply" finds "Reply from the Slack view"), not on the PR and ticket boards; Enter keeps the filter, Esc clears it |
| `Enter` | the chosen card's or row's actions, in every view |
| `S-F10` | what a right click opens: the chosen card's or row's actions, as Enter, or with nothing chosen the view's, as `m` |
| `m` | the view's own actions (in Notifications, a kind; in Usage, today or this week; in Resources, the sort; in the Calendar, a day; in Reminders, a reminder on the chosen day; in Keys, "Find a key by pressing it"; in Settings, its file; in Autopilot, its settings, pausing or resuming it, landing with or without asking, hands-free, taking the skipped items back and stopping it; in a plugin's view, asking it again), then "Several views at once…" (with UX 2.0, see [Several views at once](#several-views-at-once)) and "This view's settings", Settings filtered by the view's name |
| `Space` | on a row, opens its actions, as Enter does |
| `?` | these keys |
| `Esc` | clears a filter, else, with several views at once, goes back to the one with the focus, else closes mission control |
| `q` `C-c` `M-Space` | close it |
| `M-w` | close it, whatever is open over it |
| `M-1`–`M-9` | close it and go to that tab, as keys.json binds them; a number with no tab only closes it |
| `M-p` | the palette over it: a mission control view or Settings picked there shows in it, anything else closes it and runs in the pane it was opened over; Esc goes back to the view |

A click on a view's name goes to that view, on a group's name (with UX 2.0) to its first view, a click on the filter starts typing in it, and a click on a card or row chooses it, a second click doing what Enter does. A right click on a card or row (a notification, an event, a folded line) chooses it and opens its menu, as Enter does, also where a click would act at once; where nothing in a view takes a click, the pane beside included, it opens the view's menu, as `m` does; on a view shown at once with others but without the focus, it gives that view the focus and opens its menu; on the bar and the footer it does nothing. It follows Settings' "Click in menus" (`menu_mouse`). The wheel moves through the cards or rows. With Settings' "Highlight on hover" (`hover_highlight`) on, what a click would press is lit under the mouse. With UX 2.0, where the sidebar shows beside it, a click on the sidebar closes mission control and does what it does there (a tab's row goes to that tab, a right click opens its menu), while the wheel and the mouse moving over the sidebar scroll and light it with mission control left open.

### Menus

Enter on a card or row opens a menu of what can be done with it, its usual action first, so Enter twice does it. `j` `k` move, Enter or Space takes the chosen option and a number takes that option; its options have no letters of their own, as the tmux version's had none, so a letter never acts ahead of moving. Esc or `q` goes back. `/` and part of an option's label narrow the menu to the options that match. With Settings' "Click in menus" (`menu_mouse`) on, a click takes an option and a click outside closes the menu.
