An option that takes a moment (opening a link, asking Linear, removing a worktree) shows a box with a spinner until it is done (a link until the browser comes to the front, up to eight seconds, or a moment on Linux, where that can't be told), and what it did is said at the bottom of the screen.

On a tab, the menu offers:

- go to the tab;
- answer it here, or send it a message (below);
- peek at its last turn, or at its screen for a tab with no Claude;
- show its full title, as `M-p`'s "Show this tab's full title" does, with Copy the title;
- its diff, as `M-d` opens it, or lazygit (below), in its pane's place, going to the tab;
- the `M-p` palette in that tab;
- rename it (empty names it after its directory again);
- pin or unpin it;
- set a reminder, or change or clear one;
- for a tab waiting in the landing queue, land it next or move it up or down;
- close the tab, after asking;
- on a merged tab, close every Merged tab (each one idle), and remove their worktrees, after asking: a worktree with changes not committed is kept, and named.

### Answering a tab

"Answer it here, or send it a message" opens a box with the question or permission prompt the tab's Claude shows, read off its screen, and its options numbered. A number answers with that option, `s` submits a question that takes several answers, and `t` types an answer of your own where the question takes one. When the tab shows no prompt, what you type is sent to it as a message with Enter. After an answer the screen is read again, and the box comes back for the next question of several.

### Several views at once

With UX 2.0, "Several views at once…" in any view's `m` menu opens the "Views at once" box: every view under its group, Work and Code in one column and Day and System in the next, each ticked with its place number once picked, and beside them the layouts, `1` 2 × 2, `2` one beside two stacked (1 + 2) and `3` side by side, the one in use drawn small with each picked view in its cell. `Space` picks or unpicks the chosen view (up to four; a fifth is refused with "Up to four at once"), `J` and `K` move it a place later or earlier, `1` `2` `3` (left off the box's keys), or Tab to a layout's keycap and Space or a click, choose the layout, a click on a cell of the drawing gives that view the first place, `Enter` shows them and `Esc` goes back to one view. The first time, it has Slack, the Inbox, Usage and the Calendar picked, 2 × 2, those of them that are on; afterwards it starts from what you picked last, which is kept in `mission-grid.json` in the state directory, so a restart keeps it.

The views then share the screen under the bar, a rule between them, each under its name and its count at the right ("4 new" for Slack's messages not yet seen, the Inbox's unread, "$8.41 today" for Usage, "3 left" for the Calendar's events not yet over). The first picked has the focus, its name in the accent: it takes the keys and the clicks as the view in front does alone (j and k move in it, Enter opens the chosen row's actions and `m` its own, `/` filters it). `Tab` and `S-Tab` move the focus to the next or previous view, and a click on another view gives it the focus. Usage shows a row a tab that spent today, a meter of its share of the day in its color with the share and the dollars, then the 5-hour limit's meter; the Calendar shows the rest of the day as a list, each event's time, its title and a chip ("in 15m", "now", "moved"), then when the last one ends. The bar's first row lights every group a shown view is in, and its second names the shown views, the one with the focus underlined, with how many at its right. `Esc` goes back to the view with the focus alone, and a second closes mission control; "Several views at once…" again opens the box on what is shown. A layout whose cells would be under 34 columns or 9 rows gives way to 2 × 2, and a screen too small for that shows the view with the focus alone, Tab going to the next.

### The views

After the cards come Notifications, Routines (while Settings' "Routines", `routines`, is on), Calendar, Reminders (while Settings' "Reminders", `reminders`, is on), Slack, Reopen, PRs, CI, Landing (while something lands), Tickets, Inbox, Resources, Usage, Keys and Settings, then each plugin's view, each only while its setting is on. The ones that read a service ask it again when you go to them.

**Notifications** is every notification of the last two weeks, kept by day, newest first: under "Today", "Yesterday", then each weekday, a row each with its clock time ("9:42 AM"; the meeting still to come its start), its kind's mark, its title, what it led to and whether the sidebar still shows it (`shown`, `dismissed`, `read`, or `older` for one its source let go by itself, such as a Slack message seen in Slack). What it led to is recorded as it happens: the choice picked from its menu ("you went to tab 5", "a tab opened for it", "you chose yes", "marked read in Linear" once Linear answers, "opened in the browser", "you opened it at 9:02"), and for a landed tab autopilot closed, "tab 2 closed"; one with nothing recorded leaves it empty. With UX 2.0, a row of chips along its top counts the week's notifications of each kind ("All 23", "Landed 6", "PRs 5", "Slack 4", "Inbox 3", "Calendar 3", "Notes 2"), the one shown lit, with "23 this week · 5 in the sidebar" at its right; the column heads sit over a dashed rule, a title is bright while the sidebar shows it and dim after, and its state at the right is in the accent while shown. Without it, the rows say what each led to and its state after its title, and the time at the right, the meeting still to come "Starts 3:00 PM". The view's `m` menu (or a click on a chip, or Tab to one and Enter) shows one kind, or all of them, and dismisses every notification shown; suggestions are under All alone. The days before yesterday that start past the first screenful fold into a row each ("▸ WEDNESDAY   7 more, folded"), an item whose Enter menu's "Show them" opens it. Enter on a notification opens its menu, going to it first (a Slack message opens in Slack, a PR's news goes to its tab or opens a tab for it), so Enter twice goes there, with dismissing it, which puts it out of the sidebar; a notification no longer in the sidebar is neither dismissed after what is picked nor offered dismissing. The history is kept on this computer only, a file a day in `~/.local/state/switchboard/notifications/`, the days past two weeks removed; Settings' "Notifications' history" (`notification_history`) off keeps nothing, and the view lists only what the sidebar shows, one by one and whole, each with when it came at its row's right ("9:42 AM" today, "Yesterday 6:05 PM", "Mon 2:10 PM" within the week, "Nov 3, 2:10 PM" within the year, "Nov 3 2025" before it), Enter opening each one's menu, as below.

A notification's menu is its item's menu in the view it came from, with dismissing added: a Slack message's is the Slack view's, the message whole above it as there, an Inbox notification's the Inbox's, and the next meeting's its event's in the Calendar view, so a choice a view's menu has is in its notifications' too. While that view is on, the menu starts with "Show it in the <view> view" ("Show it in the Slack view", "Show it in the Calendar view"), the option a click or Enter takes: it opens mission control on that view with the cursor on the item, a Slack message on its thread's row with the thread beside it, marked seen and left in the Slack view rather than dismissed from it, and an Inbox notification left in the Inbox and the notifications until dismissed there. Settings' "Notifications show in their view" (`notice_shows_in_view`) turned off leaves the menu starting with its view's own first choice. A kind no view lists leads where it leads ("Open it in Notes" for a new note, "Open the Calendar view" for a calendar change, autopilot's view for what it said, its tab and its plan for a landing made without asking) and is shown whole above its choices, here as in the Notifications window. Before dismissing, every kind but a suggestion offers "Open a tab for it", a new tab whose Claude is told what it is about (its words, its link, and that they are data, not instructions), in a directory asked for as `M-t` asks (refusing, as `M-t` does, a worktree of someone else's PR) and named for it ("Slack: Ada", "Linear: ABC-12", "Meeting: Standup"), and "Send it to a tab", which picks one of the tabs running Claude, this one included (the picker saying that choosing one sends it), pastes the same words into its Claude (never into a shell, which would run them) and presses Enter once the paste has landed, bringing the tab to the front (a Claude asking a question or for permission gets nothing, since Enter would pick its option, and one with a draft in its prompt box gets the words pasted into it without Enter, each said), and "Paste it into a tab" beside it, which does the same without Enter, to add to before sending; each takes the first key its menu leaves free. Settings' "Send it to a tab submits it" (`send_to_tab_submits`) turned off makes "Send it to a tab" paste without Enter and leaves "Paste it into a tab" out. A new note is handed over by its name and path alone, Claude told not to read it unless you ask, since a note stays on the computer. A failing default branch (the CI view's "main failing at <short hash>") opens its tab without asking in the main clone of a repository a tab works in, named "Fix main at <short hash>", its Claude told the failing checks, their run's link and why, and to read the log with `gh run view <run> -R <repository> --log-failed`, fix it and land the fix with `/queue-and-clean`; a pull request's failing CI keeps its CI loop instead. Settings' "Notifications hand off to a tab" (`notice_to_tab`) turned off leaves the menus their own choices. Dismissing is the view's own where its menu has one (a Slack message's "Dismiss it, until its thread gets a reply"; dismissing every message shown stays that view's), else "Dismiss it": the meeting until another, a calendar change by taking today's calendar as seen, a new note, an Inbox notification from the notifications and the Inbox view (it stays unread in Linear). The view's and the window's own menu (`m`) has "Dismiss every notification shown", which puts away every one the sidebar shows, each as dismissing it alone would (a Slack message until its thread gets a reply), suggestions left to their own choices. Whatever is picked dismisses it too; a suggestion gets its own choices instead. It is there while the sidebar's notifications are on.

**Reminders** is every tab's reminder, in time order. With UX 2.0 on it is a month, the weekdays across and six weeks down, each day headed by a dashed rule with its number on it (today's red, the chosen day's lit, the days of the months around faint), each reminder in its day as a block in its tab's state's color with the tab's number in its tab color and its name; beside the month, the chosen day with its reminder (its time and how long until it, or a "due" chip once it came due, the tab's state, what it is for) and a keycap for Enter, whose menu (the tab's) goes there, changes it or clears it, or on a day with none one for `m`, each a click, then This month's reminders and the Later ones, each with its day, its tab and its time at the right, or the "due" chip in its place; under the month, the twelve months ahead, each counting its reminders, a click showing it. `h` `l` move a day, `j` `k` a week, `H` `L` a month, the month following the chosen day, and the view's `m` menu goes back to today; Enter opens the reminder's tab's menu, and the view's `m` menu asks which tab to remind you about on the chosen day, at 9:00, or goes back to today. The bar's right says how many there are and how many came due ("6 reminders · 1 came due"). On a screen narrower than 160 columns the months ahead drop from the end and each reminder in its day shows its tab's number alone; narrower than 140, the chosen day goes under the month and its lists behind `i`, as the view's `m` menu's "Show this month and later" does. Without UX 2.0 it is a list.

**Calendar**, **Reminders** and **Slack** are in "Your day"; **Reopen**, **Resources** and **Usage** are in "How you use it".

**PRs** is a board of your pull requests in the repositories your tabs are in, a fork's clone counting as in both its `origin` and the project its `upstream` remote names (where the pull requests from your fork are), in columns: Draft, In review, Approved, In merge queue, and Merged in the last 7 days, each with its checks, review and place in the queue, failing checks or changes requested marked as needing you. `h` `l` move between columns. Enter offers to go to the tab it is open in, work on it in a new tab (`wtpr`), read it here in a pane beside the one you were in, open it in the browser, or copy its link. Settings' "PR board" (`pr_board`).

**CI** is your open pull requests in the repositories your tabs are in (a fork's upstream among them, as on the PR board), under a heading each with their counts: Failing (a canceled check counts, since it never passed), each with the checks that failed and the line of the first failing job's log that says why; Running, with how many checks passed so far ("4 of 5 passed, 1 running"); and Passing ("9/9"). With UX 2.0 on, each failing PR is its mark, number, title and the tab it is open in ("tab 6", or "no tab"), a dim line of its repository, branch and how many checks failed ("2 of 9 failed"), then a line for each failed check with the line of its log that says why; a running or passing PR is one line. `j` and `k` move through the PRs and their failed checks' lines, and a click chooses one. The chosen check (a PR's first, on its own row) is a pane beside the list, or under it on a screen narrower than 140 columns: its name, the PR, run and how long it ran, then only the step of its log that failed (the one holding its last error), its lines numbered as in the whole log, the line that says why tinted red with `▶` and opened in sight, `J` and `K` scrolling the rest; under it each other failed check of the PR with its own line that says why. Each failed check's log is read once and kept, so the step costs nothing more. The footer's keys take a click too. Enter, on the chosen check, offers to start a CI loop on a failing one (typing `/ci-loop` into the tab already on it when its Claude is idle or finished, else opening a new tab on its worktree with `wtpr`), go to its tab, read that check's log in a pane beside yours (the log of each failing job is kept in `ci-logs/` in the state directory once the view has read why it failed, and goes when the job no longer fails; it opens at its failing step, else at its end, without the timestamps Actions puts on every line), open that check in the browser, read the pull request here in a pane beside the one you were in or open it in the browser, or copy its link. Above them, while one fails, is each default branch Switchboard watches (Settings' "Watch the default branch's CI", `main_ci_watch`: a repository whose landings test in CI, while autopilot works on it or a tab waits to land on it), "main failing at <short hash>: <job>" with the line of its log that says why, asked every two minutes; its menu reads the failing log or opens the failing run, and it stays while a later commit's CI still runs, going once one passes. Settings' "CI" (`view_ci`).

**Landing** (`M-p`, "Mission control: Landing") is there only while something lands in a repository a tab is in, or while it is in front, saying "Nothing is landing now" once the last landing ends until you leave it; a client stays on the view it was on when Landing comes or goes. It has a section per repository and branch landed on, headed "<repo> · straight on <branch>" for the default branch's landing queue, "<repo> → <branch>" for a target branch's (autopilot's "Land on:"), either with "lands without asking" when the tab holding the lock has autopilot's "Land without asking" on, or "<repo> · merge queue" ("<repo> · through a PR · GitHub merge queue" for a repository that lands through a PR, as repos.json or the last answer worked out says), its rows in queue order. With UX 2.0 it is a board of stage cells with the chosen worktree's detail and keys of its own, as "UX 2.0" above says. A landing-queue row is the tab landing or waiting, the one whose pane took the lock or joined the queue (the worktree's folder when no tab has it), with its step ("landing · pushing") or "waiting" ("reviewing again · keeps its turn" for one reviewing its change again first in line), how long since it took the lock or joined, and its tab; Enter offers to go to the tab, Land it next, move it up or down, take it out of the queue after asking (`switchboard land-lock leave`, which stops its wait), or copy its directory. A merge-queue row is every pull request in it, everyone's: its place, number and title, the repository, where it stands, about how long until it merges as GitHub estimates it, its author, and the tab it is open in; one of your tabs' that can't merge is marked as needing you. Enter offers to go to the tab, read the pull request here in a pane beside the one you were in, open it or the queue in the browser, or copy its link. The merge queues are asked of GitHub with the `github` setting on; the landing queues are read from files. Settings' "Landing view" (`view_landing`); a "Merge queues" turned off before is read as it turned off.

**Tickets** is a board of the Linear issues assigned to you, in columns: Todo (urgent first), In progress, In review (a started state whose name says review), and Done in the last 7 days, each with its state, priority, project and the tab working on it, an urgent one marked as needing you. `h` `l` move between columns. Enter offers to go to the tab it is open in, start work on it in a new tab (`wt`), shape it in a new tab (`shape`), close it in Linear (moving it to its team's first completed state, after asking), read it here in a pane beside the one you were in, open it in the browser, or copy its ID. A new tab starts in the directory of a tab working on a ticket of the same team, else your home directory. Settings' "Ticket board" (`ticket_board`).

**Inbox** is your unread Linear notifications that need you, newest first: a mention of you in a ticket or a comment, a new comment on a ticket of yours, a ticket newly assigned to you; status changes and the rest of Linear's inbox are left out. Each is in the sidebar's notifications too, and one dismissed there or here leaves both, staying unread in Linear. Enter offers what the Tickets board offers for its ticket, to mark it read in Linear, and, with Settings' "Notifications hand off to a tab" (`notice_to_tab`) on, to open a tab for it or send it to a tab, as a notification's menu does. Settings' "Inbox" (`view_inbox`).

With UX 2.0, the Inbox is a board. Its notifications are grouped by day ("Today", "Yesterday", then the weekday or the date) with each day's count at the right, each two rows beside the large photo of who acted (their Linear photo with Settings' "Photos" on, else their initial): its kind's mark (● assigned to you, ◇ a comment, @ a mention), the ticket, what happened ("assigned to you by Ana", "Ben commented", "Priya mentioned you") and when at the right, then the ticket's title under the ticket; under them, "Read, archived and snoozed ones stay in Linear." Beside the list, sharing the screen with it, is the chosen one in its ticket: its title, its state, a priority chip (Urgent and High in yellow), its project and "▲ tab N works on it" when a tab's name names the ticket; then the last three comments up to the notification's own, each with its author's photo and how long ago, the notification's own marked with a bar and a "new" chip (for an assignment or a mention in the description, the description's start instead); then **From your work**: the tab working on it with what it is doing, which a click goes to, and the newest commit on a default branch of the tabs' repositories whose message names the ticket ("✓ 3f2a91c landed 2h ago  Hold the lock  touches land_lock.rs"), found with local git alone, so a commit shows once a fetch or a landing has brought it. The ticket is read from Linear as you choose a notification, from the ticket view's copy at once when there is one, and again when the notification is newer than the copy or the copy is five minutes old. Enter opens the Tickets board's menu for its ticket (go to its tab, start work, shape it, close it, read it, open it in Linear, copy its ID), then "Dismiss it here: it stays unread in Linear", which takes it out of the view and the notifications; whatever else is picked, closing the menu marks it read in Linear, which takes it out of the view. Under 160 columns the commit's files go first, then the ticket's project, then what happened past who did it ("Ben commented" kept); under 140 the ticket isn't beside the list, and `i` (or the view's `m` menu's "Show its ticket") shows it in the list's place until `i` or Esc.

**Routines**, **Keys** and **Settings** are below; each plugin's view comes last (see "Plugins").

Settings' "GitHub" (`github`) off takes out the PRs and CI views and the Landing view's merge queues, and Settings' "Linear" (`linear`) off the Tickets and Inbox views.

### Routines

A routine is a prompt of yours run on a schedule, each run a headless Claude (`claude -p`) in its directory that opens no tab, allowed only the tools the routine names. `n` in mission control's Routines view, or "New routine" in `M-p`, opens its box (board 30): its **Name**; its **Directory**, where each run works, the pane's to start with, a directory of your tabs that starts with what you type shown dim after it, `→` taking it; **When** it runs; its **Allowed tools**, a chip each (Read, Glob and Grep to start), a click on a tool's × taking it off, Backspace with nothing typed taking off the last, and a tool typed there added with Enter or a click on "+ Add a tool" (as Claude Code's `--allowedTools` names one: `Bash(gh pr list:*)`, `Write(~/Notes/Todos/**)`, `mcp__claude_ai_Slack__slack_read_channel`); **If it's missed** while the computer slept, running when it wakes (up to an hour late) or skipping it; its **Prompt**, several lines, added to Claude's system prompt in each run; and its **Model**, empty for Claude Code's default. Tab and Shift-Tab move between the fields, Enter moves to the next one (and adds a line in Prompt), `←` `→` or Space change an option, `M-Enter` saves it from any field, `M-r` saves it and runs it now, and Esc cancels, asking first (a second Esc) once anything was typed; each field and option takes a click. What it couldn't save it says under the field.

When is read back as you type it ("✓ Monday to Friday at 8:45 AM", with its next three runs as chips under it, or what it couldn't read). It reads a schedule that repeats: `weekdays at 8:45`, `mondays 10:00`, `mon, wed and fri at 9`, `mon-thu 9:15`, `weekends 10am`, `daily 7:30` or `every day at 6pm`, `every 2h`, `every 30m 9-6 weekdays` (from 9:00 to 6:00 PM, both run at; over the whole day the interval has to divide it evenly, so `every 7h` is refused, and since each run is a turn of Claude, it runs every 15 minutes at most), and `the 1st at 9:00` (a day a month has none of, as the 31st, runs on its last); or a time to come once, as a reminder reads it: `nov 20 3pm`, `2027-01-04 9:00`, `tomorrow 9am`, `mon 9am` (a weekday alone is once; plural, several, or after "every", it repeats). A change of clocks leaves it at the time asked for.

A run is `claude -p` in the routine's directory, off the server's task, without Switchboard's plugin (so its hooks speak for no tab): its prompt added to the system prompt with a line naming the tools it may use, "Run <name>" as the turn, its model, and its session resumed from the run before (a session is resumed only in the directory it was made in, so a routine moved to another directory starts a new one, as does one whose session Claude Code no longer has). Only its tools are allowed, only the built-in tools its list names being there at all (`--tools`, so not even reading its own directory is allowed without `Read`), none of the allow rules in your Claude settings or the directory's applying (`--setting-sources ""`, which reads no settings file but managed ones), a rule naming `~/Notes/` or `~/.local/state/switchboard/` following `$NOTES_DIR` and `$SWITCHBOARD_STATE_DIR` where they are set, and any other tool is refused rather than asked about (`--permission-mode dontAsk`), since nobody is there to answer: a run that tried one failed, whatever it replied, and is recorded as "failed: needs Bash(ls)", the routine keeping what it needed for its menu's "Allow Bash(ls) and run it again…". What it needed is the call refused, not the whole tool: the exact command for Bash (`Bash(ls)`), the file for Read, Write or Edit (`Write(//tmp/out.md)`), an MCP tool by its full name; only a refusal that names neither, a command or path too long to read whole in a question, or another built-in tool, is the whole tool, which the menu says ("Allow all of Bash and run it again…"); at most three are kept from a run, the rest refused again on the next one. Allowing it asks first (`[Y/n]`), listing every rule whole, and warns when it would leave the routine reading Slack or Fathom, where anyone's message could ask its Claude for a call, and allowed all of Bash, Write or Edit, or an MCP tool that isn't plainly a read, whichever of the two it had before. A run records how long it took, what it cost (as Claude Code estimates it) and the reply's first line; one Claude ended in an error, or that didn't answer in two hours, failed. A directory holding someone else's PR is never run in, nor one that is gone. A run due while its last run still goes is skipped ("still running"). A run outlives the server: its output goes to files beside the routine (`run-<id>.out`, `.err` and `.live`), so after `switchboard upgrade`, a restart or the server ending, the next server takes it up and records it once its Claude ends, with Routines turned off meanwhile too. A run that failed or was skipped is said once in a box and stays in the notifications until put away, its menu the routine's. A run missed while the computer slept (a minute or more between two of the server's ticks) or no workspace ran runs when it is back, up to an hour late, and is said to have run late, or is recorded as missed, as the routine says; several missed count once. A routine to run once that has run stays, under Ran once.

The view lists Recurring, Once and Ran once. Without UX 2.0 a routine is a line: its name, its schedule and when it runs next. With UX 2.0, it is board 29: each routine two rows, its mark (`↻`, `◷` to run once, `‖` paused, `✕` its last run failed, the spinner while it runs), its name, its schedule dim and when it runs next at the right, then a chip for its allowed tools ("Read, Glob, Grep") and one for its last run in its outcome's color ("✕ Mon · failed: needs Bash"); under them This week, a row a routine and a column a day, each day's runs as their marks (`✓` ran, `◌` ran late on waking, `–` missed or still running and skipped, `✕` failed) with a count past one, today's day in red. Beside it the chosen routine: its schedule and directory, Enter's keycap for its menu, its prompt on a panel, its allowed tools, how it runs, today's runs (the time, the mark, the reply's first line or "failed: needs Bash" in red, how long and its cost), the earlier ones this week folded with their spend, and its next runs. The bar's right says how many there are and when the next runs ("5 routines · next at 2:30 PM"). Narrower than 160 columns each row's schedule goes, then the week's counts as the days narrow; narrower than 140, the chosen routine is behind `i` (or the view's `m` menu's "Show the chosen routine"), in the list's place until `i` or Esc. Enter opens a routine's menu (board 76): "Allow <what it needed> and run it again…" first while its last run needed something, "Open its last session in a tab" once it has one (a tab in its directory running `claude --resume <session>`, to read the run or carry on from it), "Run it now", "Edit it", "Pause it" or "Resume it", and "Delete it…", which asks first and leaves its sessions in Claude Code; `m` opens the view's, with "New routine…"; `n`, `r`, `e`, `p`, `o` and `x` do the same from the list, `x` after asking; a paused routine resumed counts from then, as does one whose schedule changed.

Each routine is a file in `~/.local/state/switchboard/routines/` (`<id>.json`), and its runs a line each in `runs-<id>.tsv` beside it. A routine written by a version that ran routines in tabs is given its tools the first time it is read (a starter its own, any other Read, Glob and Grep), its tab options ignored; a tab such a version opened for it is left as it is. Settings' "Routines" (`routines`) off runs none and takes the view away, keeping the routines.

#### Starter routines

The first time a server of this version reads the routines, it makes four of its own, from `assets/routines/`, each run in your home directory with the tools it needs (its connector's read tools, `Read`, the `switchboard` commands it runs, and writing only to its own folder of notes). From then on they are yours, as any routine is: the Routines view changes, pauses or deletes them, a starter deleted is never made again (`routines/.starters` records that they were made, and which is which), and no update writes over one. Each starts paused unless what it replaces, built into the workspace before, was on:

- **Today's todo note** (daily at 9 AM, Sonnet, a run passed over without starting Claude when the day's note is there): writes `Todos/Today's Todo <date>.md` in your notes, unless it is there already, from the unticked top-level checklist items of your notes (`.trash`, `Todos`, `Standups` and a section named Later, Deferred, Parked, Someday or Backlog left out), what mission control last read of today's calendar, your pull requests and their failing checks, and your Linear tickets and Inbox (`switchboard sources`, a view that hasn't answered today left out, the calendar through the Google Calendar connector when the view is on with no feed or saved day), the reviews asked of you with `gh` (with Settings' `github` on), and the Slack messages that need you through the Slack connector (with the Slack view on): a `## Meetings` section, `## First` with what most needs doing today and `## Also` with the rest, each a checklist line, naming a source it couldn't read as left out. Its prompt says what it sends: your notes' unticked items go to Claude, so it starts paused unless Settings' "Today's todo note" (`todo_note`) is on, at the hour Settings' "Todo note time" (`todo_note_time`) names, 9 AM unless it names another. A run due while you are away waits for your return; one missed while the computer slept runs as it wakes, up to an hour late. Its prompt has Claude run each command as a call of its own, which its allowed tools match one at a time, and look for the day's note with Glob. A routine made with an earlier prompt of the starter's, word for word, is given the new one as the routines load (one you edited is kept); one made before it ran daily keeps running every weekday hour until the day's note is there, so change its schedule in its form.
- **Meeting notes** (every 30 minutes from 7am to 9pm, Haiku): each meeting of yours that Fathom recorded, through Claude's Fathom connector, becomes a note, `Meetings/<title> <date>.md`, with its action items (yours first), its summary and its transcript, its timestamps linking back to the recording. A meeting is yours when you recorded it or were invited, by your Fathom email and name or your git name; one whose summary isn't ready yet waits for the next run, and one written (or whose note is already there) is recorded in `~/.local/state/switchboard/meetings.json` and never written again, with where the next run lists meetings from, so a meeting recorded while the computer was off isn't missed. It starts running when Settings' "Meeting notes" (`meeting_notes`) is on and Fathom had worked (its connector's last call, or a meeting note written before).
- **Slack** (every 15 minutes): follows the slack-updates skill (reading the file `switchboard skill-path slack-updates` prints, since a headless run has no plugin to run `/slack-updates` from), which keeps mission control's Slack view up to date while Settings' "Slack without a tab" (`slack_background`) is off. It starts running when that setting was off and the Slack view on, unless a Slack tab the workspace opened before was still looping it, which goes on as it was.
- **Calendar** (every 30 minutes): follows the today skill the same way, which keeps the Calendar view up to date without your calendar's iCal address. It starts running when the Calendar view was on with no address stored and a day /today saved, unless a Calendar tab the workspace opened before was still looping it.

### When a view isn't set up

A view that is on is always there to go to, and before what it reads from is set up it says what is missing and how to set it up. GitHub's views need a token and Linear's a key: "GitHub and Linear access", under "Reading beside your work", says where each is looked for and how to store one, and mission control picks a stored one up within two minutes. A view whose tabs are in no GitHub repository says to open a tab in one. A look that fails for another reason (the network, a rate limit) keeps what the view had, with the error above it.

### How often mission control looks

Every tab's pull request and the merge queues are asked about every minute, and the rest every two minutes; with Settings' "Look often" (`poll_fast`, off at first) on, GitHub and Linear are asked every 15 seconds, and the PR board every 30, about eight times as many requests, which can reach GitHub's 5,000 an hour. The merged column of the PR board is asked for only every ten minutes. A lookup still waiting on its answer when the next is due is skipped rather than started twice. The CI view's search reads each pull request's first hundred checks; a pull request with more has the rest read again only once its head commit changes or one of its checks changes state, kept from the last look in between. A tab's pull request for its name is asked for every five minutes while its branch has none, and at once when the PR board shows a new one of yours from that branch, and not at all while GitHub's lookups are slowed down (below).

Every answer from GitHub says how much of the hour's limit is left. Under 20%, GitHub's lookups wait four times as long between asks, a message says so once, and the health light shows `GitHub: 12% of the hour left`; under 5%, only the default branch's CI is asked about, until the limit resets. What you ask for (going to a view, `M-m`) is asked all the same, and Linear's lookups go on as before. Going to the Inbox, CI or Landing view asks again at once, and so does `M-m`, for the views that show pull requests, so they agree with what it says.

While you are away (no terminal attached, the screen locked, or 10 minutes with neither the computer's keyboard and mouse nor a key in Switchboard used; a key pressed in Switchboard from another computer, over SSH, counts even with the screen locked) the GitHub and Linear lookups, the calendar feed, the Slack look and the diff's stories told ahead pause, and all ask again when you are back. Settings' "Pause lookups while away" (`pause_when_away`) off keeps them going. Autopilot keeps opening tabs while you are away, whatever that setting says, as long as a terminal is attached, unless Settings' "Keep opening tabs while I'm away" (`autopilot_while_away`) is off.

### Finding a tab or a worktree

`M-j` opens a picker of the open tabs, a row each with its ticket and where its PR stands, and `M-o` the Reopen view (below), with no day's heading, whatever Settings' "Reopen" says, each with a filter already typing: the arrows, `C-n` `C-p` and Tab move, Tab going round past either end (stopping on easy mode's × on the way, which Enter closes), Enter opens the chosen row's menu, its usual action first, so Enter twice goes to the tab, or reopens a closed tab, resumes a past session or opens a tab in a worktree, and Esc closes the menu, then the picker. The filter types on while a menu is open over it. With Settings' "Search what panes show" (`tab_search_panes`) on, `M-j`'s filter finds a tab by what its panes show too: each pane's screen and the last 1000 lines of its scrollback ("Lines searched in each pane", `tab_search_lines`: 200, 1000 or all kept), read in the server's memory as you type and never written anywhere. The tabs found by name come first, then those found only by their panes, the most matching lines first; under each row found that way, up to two of its matching lines, cut around the match, the match in the accent, and how many more the tab holds. Its row's menu adds "Go to the match", which goes to the tab with its pane in copy mode on the last line that shows it, the match selected.

### The Keys view

`M-/` opens mission control on its Keys view: every key Switchboard binds, in one list. First the workspace's, the keys above and yours from `keys.json`, as `M-p` lists them, each under the first of its tags (panes, tabs, git and the rest), the biggest group first, a command with no key of its own shown as `M-p`, which reaches it; then the sidebar's and mission control's own (what their `?` shows), then a box's that takes typing ("Boxes"), then the diff's (`M-d`: its own keys, the cover while its story is told, and, with Settings' "Ask Claude about the diff" (`diff_ask`) on, the box `ga` opens and the answer it shows), then Notes' (a note's, its leader keys and, with Easy mode on, easy mode's Cmd keys, then the list's). Last come the commands with no key of their own, under "Through M-p", which reaches them. Each row is the key, what it does and its tags, with a "yours" chip on a key `keys.json` binds. `/` filters by a key, its words or any of its tags, leaving out the groups with nothing that matches, and Enter on one of the workspace's keys opens its menu, whose "Press it here" presses it in the pane mission control was opened over, as an `M-p` pick is; the diff's commands, which only the diff takes, and the other parts' keys are there to read, and Enter on one says which app takes it. Settings' "Keys" (`view_keys`), under Mission control, takes the view away and hands `M-/` back to the pane.

With UX 2.0 and mission control at least 100 columns wide and tall enough for its panel of groups, the view is a board, laid out from the screen's width: the table's column of what a key does and the chosen key's description take what the rest leave. Narrower, the table's tags column gives way first, then the chosen key beside the keyboard. At the left, its groups: the workspace's tags with how many keys each has, then each app's own keys (the sidebar, mission control, the boxes, the diff, Notes), then those only through `M-p`, the group in front on the highlight. At the top right, the keyboard as a heatmap: each letter and digit a key, filled in the accent by how often you pressed it with Alt this week (`M-j` and `M-J` both count for J), the keys the group in front binds outlined, a line under it saying which group that is. The counts are Settings' "Suggestions from how you work" (`habits`), kept on this computer only and read again about once a minute while the view is in front; with it off, the keyboard's place says to turn it on. Beside the keyboard, the chosen key: its keycap and what it does, a longer description, whether it is the default or yours, and how often it was pressed this week ("212 this week · your most used"). Under a rule, the group's keys as a table: the key, what it does, its tags, and "default" or a "yours" chip. `h` and `l` move between the groups, `j` and `k` between the group's keys, and a click on a group, a key of the keyboard or a row chooses it. Narrower or shorter, or with UX 2.0 off, it is the list.

"Find a key by pressing it" in the Keys view's `m` menu opens "Find a key": press any key, the way you would anywhere, and it comes up as a large keycap with what it does, its tags, whether it is the default or yours, how often it was pressed this week and its longer description, nothing pressed. A key an app takes for itself (`j`, the sidebar's) is named with its app; the prefix waits for the key after it; a key nothing is bound to says `keys.json` can bind it. Under a dashed rule is the key you looked up before it. Enter, or a click on its keycap, closes the box with the key chosen in the list, the filter cleared, where Enter's menu presses it; Esc closes it. `?` lists mission control's own keys.

### Settings

"Settings: turn the workspace's options on and off" in `M-p` opens mission control on its Settings view: every option under its category, with its value and what it does, each mission control view's options in a category of its own (`Mission control: Slack`), after the others. Enter or Space opens the chosen one's menu, whose first choice changes it (on and off, or the next of its choices), so Enter twice does, and the change takes effect at once; its file in your editor is its next. `/` filters by name, description, category, key or value, leaving out the categories with nothing that matches. What you change is kept in `~/.config/switchboard/settings.json`, only what differs from the defaults; a change made there by hand takes effect too, and the version before each change is kept beside it as `settings.json.bak`.

With UX 2.0 on and mission control at least 136 columns wide, Settings is a board in two panes. At the left, a panel lists the categories, each with how many options it has and, when some of them differ from their defaults, a chip with how many, then under a "Mission control" heading what belongs to mission control as a whole and a category for each of its views (Slack, Calendar, Autopilot and the rest, a view's on/off switch first among its options), then under "Your files" each file of yours with its settings. Beside it, the category in front: its name, how many options it has and how many changed, and a row per option, its name bold, a "changed" chip when it differs from its default, its key dim at the right, and a switch at the far right (green with its knob at the right when on, an image where the terminal draws them, kitty's or sixel, ` ━●` or `○━ ` elsewhere), or the value's words for an option of choices. The chosen option opens to its whole description, its default and what Enter does ("default on · Enter turns it back on"). `h` and `l` move to the category before or after, `j` and `k` between options; `C-w h` (or a click on a category) gives the panel the focus, the category in front on the chosen surface and the chosen option resting, `j` and `k` there walk the categories with their options showing, and `C-w l`, Enter or Space go back into the options; Enter and Space open the chosen one's menu, changing it first ("Turn it on", or for an option of choices every choice, the one it has marked "(now)", each one pick away), then opening its file in your editor; a click on a switch flips it and a click on a category goes there. `/` searches every category, the pane listing the matches under each category's name and the panel dimming the categories with none. The view's `m` menu opens the file in front in your editor in a tab of its own: `settings.json` for a category, the file itself for one of yours, `repos.json` for a repository's landing. With UX 2.0 on, the "Tabs and status bar" category is named "Tabs and header", since the status line is gone; `/` finds it by either name. Narrower, the view is the list above, with the "changed" chip on an option that differs from its default.

Below the options, each setting kept in a file of your own in `~/.config/switchboard` has a row under that file's name: where your Claude rules come from and the file in your rules repository that holds them (`claude.json`), your Linear workspace, team and labels (`linear.json`), the Chrome profile each site's links open in and the default profile (`browser.json`), the Slack view's channels (`slack-updates.json`), the Slack relay's owner (`slack-relay.json`), the on-call settings (`oncall.json`), the sidebar's sizes (`sidebar.json`), and the git repository your files are kept in and the folder in it (`files.json`). Enter on one types a value in, starting from what it is (empty unsets it); a Chrome profile is picked from the profiles Chrome lists. "Your CLAUDE.md" offers the local file (`~/.config/switchboard/CLAUDE.md`), another file or a git repository, the last two typed in; it writes `claude.json` and runs `switchboard setup claude-md`, which clones a repository named and writes `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` to import the rules, and says what it changed or why it couldn't. Saving "Rules file in the repo" runs that part too. Anything bigger (your CLAUDE.md rules, your key bindings in `keys.json`, your trusted directories, the plugins, the on-call channels and rules) opens the whole file in your editor (`$VISUAL`, else `$EDITOR`, else `vi`) in a tab of its own.

## Your day

Switchboard keeps your day beside your tabs: today's calendar, what is new for you on Slack, the next meeting, and notes it writes for you. The notes go in your notes directory (`$NOTES_DIR`, else `~/Notes`), in `Standups/`, `Todos/` and `Meetings/`. A note already there is never written over, so each is yours to edit once written.

### The Calendar view

Mission control's Calendar view is today, laid out as Google Calendar's Day view lays it out: the date and when it was read at the top, the all-day events as chips in a row of their own, then the hours down the left, each event a blue block from its start to its end with its title and times, events at the same time side by side, a red line where it is now, events already over faded, and one you haven't said yes to drawn in outline. Two meetings back to back in the same place take different shades, so they read as two. The day runs from 8 AM (or the first event, or now) to 6 PM (or the last event), as many rows to an hour as the screen has room for, up to eight; a day too long for the screen starts an hour before now. With [UX 2.0](#ux-20) on it is the board's timeline instead, from 8:00 to 7:00, the chosen event's pane beside it (above, under UX 2.0). `j` and `k` go through the events (all-day ones first, then by start), the chosen one in a lighter blue, and a click chooses one. It reads your Google Calendar's iCal feed itself, every five minutes and when you go to the view, once its secret address is stored: in Google Calendar, open Settings, your calendar, Integrate calendar, and copy "Secret address in iCal format", then run

```sh
switchboard platform secret-set google-calendar-ical
```

paste it and press `Ctrl-D`. It is stored as the Linear key is (see "GitHub and Linear access").

Without the address, the view shows the day the `/today` skill last saved, which reads your calendar through Claude's Google Calendar connector (connected with `/mcp` in Claude Code); run `/today` once in any Claude tab to start. A day saved before today, or over 45 minutes ago, is marked out of date, and while no tab named Calendar runs Claude and none loops `/today`, mission control opens one in the background, in your home directory, running `/loop 30m /today`, at most every ten minutes. If the feed can't be read for 15 minutes (no network, Google down, the address revoked), the view falls back to `/today`'s day in the same way, and goes back to the feed once it reads again. Settings' "Calendar" (`view_calendar`).

`j` and `k` walk every event in turn, side by side ones included, and `h` and `l` move a day back or forward: the feed is read a week back and two weeks ahead, each day drawn as today is, with no line at now; past those days, or with `/today`'s day alone (no feed), the view says why it can't. The view's `m` menu moves a day too, or back to today. Enter on an event offers, moved with ↑↓ or `/` and taken with Enter or a click, its keys left off its rows (board 54) but still taking them: join the Meet (`o`) first, so Enter twice joins, Going (`y`), Not going (`n`) and Maybe (`m`) as it takes them, open it in Google Calendar, copy the Meet link (`c`), read each ticket and pull request its description links to in a pane beside the one you were in, and, with `notice_to_tab` on, open a tab for it or send it to a tab, as a notification's menu does. An answer goes through Claude's Google Calendar connector, as Google's own buttons answer it, so the organizer is told. Answering needs the feed, since only its events carry their Google ID (Settings' "RSVP from the Calendar", `calendar_rsvp`).

What changed on today's calendar since you last looked (an event added, moved, renamed or gone) is in the sidebar's notifications ("Calendar: Standup moved to 3pm", or "2 calendar changes", each one in the Notifications window). A key pressed with the Calendar view in front and unfiltered, or dismissing one, takes the day as seen and clears them. They need the Calendar view on (Settings' "Calendar changes", `calendar_changes`).

From five minutes before a meeting until ten minutes after it starts, the notifications show it with the tickets and pull requests it names, and Enter there joins its call (Settings' "Next meeting", `next_meeting`).

### The Slack view

Mission control's Slack view is what is new for you on Slack in the last day, newest first: direct messages and mentions, marked as needing you, then replies to threads you started and everything in the channels you list in `~/.config/switchboard/slack-updates.json`:

```json
{ "channels": ["C0123456789"] }
```

With Settings' "Slack without a tab" (`slack_background`) on, mission control looks itself while you are at the computer, as often as Settings' "Check Slack for new messages every" (`slack_look_every`, 30 seconds; 15 seconds, 1, 2 or 5 minutes) says but at most once a minute through the connector, since each look there is a model call, and when you go to the view: one search through Claude's Slack connector (connected with `/mcp` in Claude Code), run by a headless Haiku allowed that one tool, reading only and keeping no session, or, with a Slack sign-in of your own and "Read Slack through your own token" on, through your token (below). The first look goes back an hour. When the connector isn't connected, the view says so first. With it off, the `/slack-updates` skill looks instead, run every 15 minutes by the Slack starter routine (under Routines), which going to the view runs at once when its last look is over a minute old, unless the routine is paused or Routines are off; a look over 20 minutes old is marked out of date. The Slack relay's own messages are never in it. Settings' "Slack" (`view_slack`).

Slack's connector can't say what you have read, so the view counts as read anything before a message of your own in the same DM or thread, and remembers what you have seen in it: leaving the view marks what its look brought as seen (with UX 2.0, whose board shows one section at a time, only the messages of the sections it showed until you have looked at every one), and picking anything from a message's menu marks that message seen. Seen messages sit below the new ones, faded. New messages are in the sidebar's notifications too, one each. Enter shows a message whole above its menu, which offers to open it in Slack, open a tab for it (in a directory you pick, as `M-t` asks, at the end of the tabs, Claude told to read the thread through the Slack connector and say what it asks of you, drafting no reply unless you ask), send it to a tab (with `notice_to_tab` on, as a notification's menu does), copy its link, or dismiss it, or every message shown, until something newer comes in its thread.

With UX 2.0 on, the view is drawn as board 21: at the left a column of sections as Slack's own sidebar lists them, Mentions and Threads first, then DIRECT MESSAGES (each with its person's photo and name) and CHANNELS (each `# name`), the direct messages and the channels each in the order of their newest message, every section with its count at the right, bold in the accent while it holds something new; beside it the chosen section one row per thread, so a thread with several new replies is one row and nothing shows twice beside the thread pane, headed by its name, how many threads have new replies, how many messages are new and "newest first", the rows ordered by when each thread last moved, new and seen together (the seen dim). A row shows who started its thread with their photo (with Settings' "Photos"), "started Tue" when that was before the look (its first message read with the thread), where it was in Mentions and Threads, and when it last moved at the right, in the accent while something in it is new; its first message on one line, with `@names` in the accent; and for a thread, who is in it as small photos, its reply count, an "N new" chip and "↩ replied" once you have written after it; a message with no replies is the first two lines alone, and the chosen row shows its message's pictures. The list ends "Older messages stay in Slack.", and a section's count is the messages it holds. `h` and `l` move the focus between the sections, the messages and the thread (as `C-w` and a direction do); `j` and `k` in the sections choose a section, its newest row chosen, and in the messages move within the section; a click on a section chooses it, and the wheel over them walks them. Under 100 columns the sections fold into a line above the messages ("@ Mentions 2  ◇ Threads 1  Ben 1  # web-app 3 … +4"), the chosen one lit, where `h` and `l` or a click change the section, as `h` and `l` do at any width with Settings' "C-w then h, j, k or l moves between panes" (`ctrl_w_panes`) off. Beside the messages, from 120 columns, the chosen row's whole thread: where it was, how many replies and how many of them are new ("thread · 7 replies, 2 new"), or for a message with no replies what brought it ("opened from your mention"), each message with its sender's photo, how long ago and its text, the replies since you last looked marked with a bar and a "new" chip, its pictures, and a video's first frame (on macOS, by Quick Look's thumbnail) with "a click or Enter's menu plays it" under it; then "From your work": each ticket the thread names with its title and state from the Tickets board and the tab working on it ("no tab yet"), and today's events it names with how soon they start. On a narrower screen `i` shows the thread in the list's place, `i` or Esc going back. Enter opens its menu, opening it in Slack first, then a tab for it, sending it to a tab, copying its link, dismissing it until its thread gets a reply and dismissing every message shown, whatever is picked but playing its picture or video marking it seen (a thread's row, every message it stands for); with a picture or video in its thread, its menu's "Play its video or show its picture" (after opening it in Slack, and in its notification's menu too, where playing it leaves the notification in place) plays it or shows it with Quick Look, or the desktop's viewer on Linux (picked again, the thread's next one; only a file whose bytes are the PNG, JPEG, GIF, WebP, MP4 or MOV its type says is kept, under that type's extension; one Quick Look or the viewer can't be started for says why on mission control's footer while it is open and as any message is said (a box, or with Settings' `message_boxes` off the status line where there is one), and in the server's log), a click on a picture in the thread showing that one, and `/` filters.

Replying is off by default, since it sends your words to Slack: with Settings' "Reply from the Slack view" (`slack_reply`) on, a message's menu offers, after "Open it in Slack", the replies that apply, each naming where it goes (board 107): for a message in a channel, "Reply in <name>'s thread" (the thread it is in, or the one it starts) and "Reply in #<channel>"; for a direct message with one person, "Reply to <name>" (a group DM offers none, since the look can't say for sure who reads one). Either opens the reply box (board 108): where it goes, the message it answers quoted, a box to type in, and Send and Cancel. It types as every box does (the keys under "Boxes" in the Keys view: the cursor keys, wrapping, C-z), and Enter there sends, doing what the Send button does (nothing on an empty reply), while S-Enter and M-Enter add a new line; Tab still moves to Send and Cancel, which Enter, Space or a click press; Esc cancels. Nothing is written for you: your words go exactly as typed, to the message's own conversation and thread.

How they leave is Settings' "Slack messages go out as" (`slack_send_as`), which covers every message Switchboard sends as you. By default they leave as a draft in Slack, since a message Claude's Slack connector sends carries a "Sent using Claude" line that Slack adds, and a draft you send from Slack carries none. Send makes the draft with the connector's draft tool alone, asking nothing first since nothing is sent, then opens the conversation in Slack, on the thread for a thread's reply, so the draft is in front of you in its composer (it is in Slack's Drafts & Sent too). A box stays behind (board 109), saying where it is, "Your reply is a draft in #<channel>, in <name>'s thread. Send it from Slack.", with "OK" and "Take me to the draft", which opens it again. Nothing counts as read until the look finds the message sent. Slack keeps one draft a conversation: with one unsent there already, the box says so (board 110) and offers "Take me to it", and the reply box comes back with what you typed, as it does when Slack can't make the draft for another reason (you aren't in the channel, it can't be found), said in plain words.

With the setting at "sent at once", Send asks first, naming the conversation and who can read it, `[Y/n]`; no comes back to the box with what you typed. Only a yes sends, through the connector's send tool alone, as you, with Slack's line under it. Once it is sent, what came before it in that thread or DM counts as read, as a reply of yours always does, and the next look brings the reply into the thread beside the list (with "Slack without a tab" on, a look starts at once).

Two things the connector can't do come from a Slack sign-in of your own instead: your reminders, which arrive in your DM with Slackbot where the connector neither searches nor reads, and marking what you open read in Slack. `switchboard setup slack-user` sets it up: it says which User Token Scopes to add to a Slack app of yours (the relay's works: `reminders:read` and `reminders:write` for the reminders; `channels:read`, `groups:read`, `im:read`, `mpim:read` and `channels:write`, `groups:write`, `im:write`, `mpim:write` for the read marks; `channels:history`, `groups:history`, `im:history`, `mpim:history`, `search:read` and `users:read` for the reads below, and `files:read` for pictures), has you reinstall the app, takes its User OAuth Token (`xoxp-…`) without showing it, asks Slack who it is for, and stores it as the secret `slack-user-token`, in the Keychain on a Mac or the desktop's keyring on Linux (where there is no keyring, or it is locked, in `~/.config/switchboard/secrets.json`, which only you can read), in no file of its own; `SWITCHBOARD_SLACK_USER_TOKEN` is read before it. Who it is for is kept in `~/.local/state/switchboard/slack-user.json`; signing in again, as another account too, lets go of the reminders kept, which the next look reads again. Its requests go to Slack's Web API alone, each counted in the Usage view's requests. Without a sign-in, while either of its settings is on, the view says on one dim line under its header how to make one, and everything else works as before.

With the sign-in, Settings' "Read Slack through your own token" (`slack_token_reads`, on) has the view read Slack with it rather than a connector call each, a headless Haiku that costs a model call: the look's search (`search.messages`, everyone's messages since the last look and your own a day back the first time, as often as "Check Slack for new messages every" says while you are here; Slack's search gives one of several messages that land close together, so a message right beside another can be missed), a thread (`conversations.replies`), people's names (`users.info`, kept while the server runs), a thread's pictures and videos (`files.info` and the file's private address, which needs `files:read`), and search everything's Slack results. The token is read from the Keychain at most every 15 minutes, never on the server's task. A read Slack refuses (the token revoked, a scope missing, the rate limit spent) or that can't reach Slack is read through the connector instead, and the view says why on its dim line under the header until a read through the token works again; when the look's search finds Slack's rate limit spent, the look waits as long as Slack's `Retry-After` asks and then looks half as often for its next ten looks, the dim line saying Slack asked it to slow down; a picture read without `files:read` says to add it, and is read through the connector meanwhile. Off, or with no sign-in, every read goes through the connector as before.

With the sign-in, Settings' "Slack reminders in the Slack view" (`slack_reminders`, on) lists your reminders that are due and not complete, read every two minutes while you are at the computer and when you go to the view, kept in `~/.local/state/switchboard/slack-reminders.json`: each with what it says and how long ago it was due, new until you have seen it in the view, with its notification among the view's; with UX 2.0 they are a section of their own, Reminders, after Threads. A reminder's menu offers "Mark it complete", which asks first and then completes it in Slack, "Open it in Slack", which opens Slack's Later list, and "Dismiss it", which takes it off the view while Slack keeps it. A recurring reminder is never listed, since Slack gives it no time of its own.

Settings' "Mark what you open read in Slack" (`slack_mark_read`) is off by default, since it changes what Slack shows you as unread. On, a message whose thread you open in the view, by Enter on it or by its thread showing beside the list for two seconds, is marked read in Slack up to that message, after asking Slack how far you have read there, so a mark never moves back; a message only listed, or dismissed unopened, marks nothing. A mark that fails is tried once more, and then its row says "not marked read", with why beside it.

Slack's search names no files and no reply counts, so both come from reading the thread: with UX 2.0 on, each look reads, through the connector's read-thread tool (or your token, below), the threads its messages are replies in that have something newer than what is kept, at most eight a look (`/slack-updates` reads them too and passes them to its save), and choosing a message whose thread isn't kept reads it then, the pane saying "Reading the thread…" meanwhile. They are kept in `~/.local/state/switchboard/slack-threads.json` while their messages are in the view. The chosen thread's pictures and videos are read once each, through the connector's read-file tool (10 MB at most), and kept for a week in `~/.cache/switchboard/slack-files/`. Settings' "Slack threads and pictures" (`slack_threads`) turned off reads none of it, and the view lists the messages alone, a row each.

### The Slack relay view

Mission control's Slack relay view (board 53, in the Day group after Slack) chooses which of the Slack relay's messages reach you. Its top line says whether the relay is running, in which tab and for how long (from the tab the server holds, never a process), how many messages it sent today by itself and how many notified you (answers to what you sent a tab from Slack aren't counted), with Start or Stop. Below are the three presets, the one the settings match lit (none, "Custom", when they match none); a row for each kind of message with today's count, a switch of Notify, Thread and Off, and a "changed" chip where it differs from its default, the chosen row opening to its whole description, today's count by tab (an autopilot tab named so) and its default; then autopilot's tabs and quiet hours. Enter or Space opens a row's menu (its other choices, a preset applied, the relay started, stopped or gone to), and a click on a switch's part sets it at once; each change is said and taken up with the relay's next message. Without UX 2.0, or narrower than 80 columns, the rows are a plain list. "What reaches you" under the Slack relay says what each choice does. Settings' "Slack relay" (`view_relay`).

### The standup note

With Settings' "Standup note" (`standup_note`) on, each weekday, once Switchboard runs after 6am, it writes `Standups/Standup <date>.md` from the last working day: the pull requests you merged, opened or worked on and the ones you reviewed, the Linear tickets assigned to you or made by you that changed state, and the commits you made in the repositories your tabs are in; then what is in progress today, the reviews asked of you, and an empty Blockers list for you to fill. GitHub's part needs GitHub on, and Linear's, Linear. With Settings' "Standup summary" (`standup_summary`) on, a few sentences over the list sum it up, written by Claude (Haiku, no tools) from the list alone; the note is written without them when Claude can't. A note that fails is tried again every 15 minutes.

### New notes in the notifications

Each note written for you (the standup, and the todo note and meeting notes their starter routines write, which run `switchboard new-note <note> [what wrote it]` once a note is saved) is in the sidebar's notifications until Notes shows it, its file is gone, or its menu in the Notifications view dismisses it. Enter on one opens it in Notes, through the running app's socket (or as the note it opens with when it isn't running), and goes to Notes in its own tab, or opens it in one (Settings' "New notes", `new_notes`). Notes beside a tab's pane goes home first, as `M-e` pressed again there takes it, so that tab keeps its work and its own note.

## How you use it

### Suggestions from how you work

Switchboard sends nothing about how you use it anywhere. With Settings' "Suggestions from how you work" (`habits`) on, it counts on this computer the keys you press (the ones pressed alone, but `C-w`), the commands you pick in `M-p` (a command with a key counting as that key pressed) and the mission control views you bring to the front, written once a minute to a file a day in `~/.local/state/switchboard/habits/` and kept 60 days. From the counts it suggests, in the sidebar's notifications:

- a key, for a command you picked from `M-p` three times in 14 days;
- turning off a view you haven't opened in 30 days (once 30 days are counted), which for the views that ask GitHub or Linear also stops what they look up;
- turning on a feature that is off, when what you did this week would use it: the second status line after you looked up a tab's PR or ticket 10 times, Routines after you set a reminder 5 times.

A suggestion's menu in mission control's Notifications view makes the change ("Turn it on", "Turn it off", or "Got it" for a key), puts it off for 14 days ("Not now"), or stops suggesting it ("Don't suggest it again"). Turning the setting off removes what was counted. `M-p`'s "How you use Switchboard" shows the counts themselves, and the suggestions now, in a box.

### Resources

Mission control's Resources view is how much CPU and memory each tab uses, counting every process under its panes (Claude, a dev server, an editor and its language servers, a test run), as a table: a row per tab with its % CPU (100% is one core), resident memory and number of processes, and a row for all the tabs together at the foot. Tabs are sorted by CPU, and the view's `m` menu sorts them by memory instead, and back; the sorted column's name is lit. The chosen row adds its three heaviest kinds of process, which `/` filters by too. Enter offers to go to the tab, and to end each of its processes using more than 1% CPU or 100 MB, up to five, heaviest first: asked first ("End node in tab 3? [Y/n]"), and sent SIGTERM only once it is checked to have the same name and still be under the tab's panes, so a pid handed to another process is never signaled. It is read from the kernel every three seconds while the view is in front, and never otherwise. Settings' "Resources" (`view_resources`).

With UX 2.0 and a screen 136 columns wide, it is a board. At the top is the whole computer: its CPU as a share of all its cores with a sparkline of the last two minutes, its memory used of what is installed with a meter (on macOS app memory, wired and compressed, as Activity Monitor's "Memory Used" counts it), and Switchboard itself, its server and the lookups it runs outside your panes. Under a rule each tab is two lines: its number in its color and its name, its CPU, memory and processes, each red past a core or 4 GB, and two sparklines of its last two minutes at the right, CPU (to 160%, red past 100%) and memory (to 6 GB, red past 4 GB); on the line under, when it is heavy, a red ✕ chip saying why ("a core pegged", "over 4 GB"), then its heaviest processes ("node 96% · lua-language-server 12%"). The chosen row opens to its four heaviest processes as a tree, indented as they were started, each with its CPU and memory, then how many more and their names. A note at the foot says memory is resident, so memory two processes share counts in both, and 100% CPU is one core, as `top` counts it. The history starts over when the view has been away, and is drawn as text where the terminal takes no images.

### Usage

The Usage view is what Claude has cost today, at API prices as Claude Code estimates it, across the interactive sessions that ran today (a headless `claude -p` isn't counted; a session resumed in a new Claude process, by the handoff, a restart or `claude --resume`, adds only what it costs from then on, even one the view has no record of, whose earlier cost is left out rather than counted as today's), and how much of each rate limit is used (the 5-hour and weekly limits, or a spend limit, and any other window Claude Code reports, named from its key, so a limit of a model's own shows once Claude Code gives one; the view has no limit Claude Code doesn't report) with when it resets, one at 80% or more marked as needing you, a row for each model that spent today (Fable 5.2, Opus 5.5, Haiku and so on) with its dollars and its share of the day, and a row for each tab that spent today or this week. Under Sessions it then lists each Claude tab's session with how full its context is, its model, how long it has run and how long since it last did anything, the fullest first, one at 80% or more marked as needing you: the ones to compact or close; Enter offers to go to the tab. Settings' "Usage" (`usage`).

With UX 2.0 on and mission control at least 136 columns wide, the Usage view is a board of charts. Its heading is today's spend. Under it, the spend by quarter hour since the hour of the day's first spend (four hours of the day at least), its bars sharing the width up to the tabs, each stacked by the tabs that spent in it, each in its tab's color, with the amounts at its left, the hours under it and a red line at "now"; beside it, keeping its share of a wider screen, each tab's number in its color, its name, its spend and its share of the day, and under them the chosen tab's rate since its first spend ("tab 7 since 11:00 AM: $1.21 an hour"). A tab that has closed keeps its number, name and color; spend no tab is known for is "sessions outside a tab". Then the limits, a meter each, with how much is used, when it resets and, from 15 minutes into a window, where it ends up at the pace it has filled so far ("at this pace, 58% by then"), the 5-hour meter marking that pace with a tick; then By model, today's spend a model, the most spent first, each with a meter of its share of the day, the percentage and its dollars, or "nothing spent yet today"; then the request audit (below), two lines a service: its name, its protocols, its requests by hour as a sparkline two rows tall, a meter of its share of the busiest service and its count at the right edge, and under its name what made them (the PR board, the tab names, CI, ...); the sparklines and the meters stretch to fill the width, and on a narrow screen the protocols move to the second line, then the meters go; then the Sessions, a row each with its context as a meter. A service with requests this week but none today has no row today, and a service written in two cases ("github" and "GitHub") is one. j and k move through the tabs, then the services, then the sessions, and the chart outlines the chosen tab's part of each bar; Enter on a tab opens its menu, going to it first, or says it has closed; the view's `m` menu shows this week by day in place of today, and again today; `/` filters the tabs and the services; a click on a tab, a service or a bar chooses it. Narrower, or with UX 2.0 off, it is the list.

Claude Code gives the context, the model, the cost and the limits only to its statusline, which is `switchboard statusline` in Switchboard's panes: drawn there by Switchboard's plugin, or the old way merged into your settings by `switchboard setup`. It writes them to `~/.local/state/switchboard/context/` and `~/.local/state/switchboard/usage/` as each session draws, so a session shows in these views once its statusline has drawn, and the limits are as the last request answered left them: a session that hasn't spent since it last wrote them leaves them alone, so an idle session redrawing never brings back a week the account has moved past. The statusline also appends what each draw adds to the day's spend, with the time, the session and the model it ran, to `usage/spent-<day>.tsv`, which places the spend in time on the board and splits it by model though a session changes model through the day (an amount logged before the model was, or missing from the log, counts under the model its session runs now), and the workspace keeps which tab ran each session in `usage/sessions-tabs.tsv`, so a tab closed since still has its name and color there; both are kept 8 days and stay on this computer.

### The request audit

Every request Switchboard makes to a service outside this computer is counted as it is made, a line each (when, the service, the protocol and what made it, tab-separated) in `~/.local/state/switchboard/requests/<day>.tsv`, a file a day, kept 14 days. Below its spend and limits, the Usage view lists today's: the total by protocol (GraphQL and REST over HTTPS, WebSocket, iCal, MCP, `claude -p`, git, plain HTTPS), then each service (GitHub, Linear, Slack, Google Calendar, Anthropic, Fathom and the rest) with its own protocols, busiest first, GitHub's with how much of its hour's limit its last answer said was left. A Claude connector call counts twice, as the MCP call to its service and as the `claude -p` run that makes it; each Haiku run that sums up a tab's turn for mission control's cards counts as `claude -p` to Anthropic, its source "turn summary". What Claude Code sessions reach themselves (their model and connectors) and skills' scripts aren't counted. Settings' "Requests in Usage" (`request_audit`) leaves them out of the view.

### Reopen

Mission control's Reopen view is the tabs closed and the Claude sessions of the last two weeks no tab runs, one row per Claude conversation, newest first under "Today", "Earlier this week" and "Older", then the worktrees no tab has open under "Worktrees with no tab". A closed tab is listed once per conversation, so several closed in the same repository are each there, leaving out a conversation a tab has open again; a tab with no Claude is listed once per directory no tab is open in, and a tab that came and went within ten seconds isn't listed. A past session is one `switchboard wt-revive --sessions` finds (read in the background while the view or `M-o`'s picker is shown), leaving out one a tab runs, one a closed tab listed has, and one that ended in someone else's PR's worktree.

The worktrees no tab has open are those of the repositories your tabs are in and every one a Claude session worked in over the last two weeks (`switchboard wt-revive`), read with git about once a minute while the view or `M-o`'s picker is shown, leaving out someone else's PR's, most recently used first, a line each: its name, its repository, a chip for where its PR stands ("PR 2019 · approved", "PR 88 · ✕ 1 failing", "no PR", its PR looked up by its branch while GitHub is on), with UX 2.0 a chip for its commits once its branch is counted, as the chosen one's is ("2 commits to push", "4 commits ahead", "nothing to push"), and when it was last used. Those whose PR merged fold under one line, "▸ 3 merged worktrees, folded", whose menu shows them (or hides them again) or removes them all after asking, naming each. With UX 2.0 the chosen worktree's pane says where it is, its branch and how far ahead of the default branch it is, and the last prompt given there.

With UX 2.0 each row is two lines: a chip of its kind (`tab` or `session`), its name, its directory, a chip of what became of its work (`✓ landed <hash>` once it landed straight on its branch, `✕ tests failed` with its PR's checks failing, `✕ changes asked` with changes asked for on its PR, `● untrusted · resumes sealed` for a sealed review) and how long ago at the right edge; then its last prompt. Under the list, after a rule, the chosen row's pane: its name, when it closed, its panes and its Claude session, what reopening it brings back, and its last screen in a box: its last lines as text, those that are only a frame or a rule left out, with when it was opened and closed beside it, and how many prompts and slash commands it had while its conversation is one of the last two weeks, which are what that count is read from. Below 160 columns the facts go under the box, the directory goes, then the outcome chip's words past its mark; under 140 the pane is behind `i`, which shows it over the list until `i` or Esc, as the view's `m` menu's "Show what reopening it brings back" does. With UX 2.0 off it is a list, a line a row.

Enter's menu (Space, a click or a right click open it too) is the row's, its first option chosen, and what removes or forgets comes last and asks first. On a closed tab: reopen it where it was, with the name it had and its Claude resumed (`claude --resume`), or a shell when it had none; open its note (the tab's `@tab_note` as it closed), copy its directory, and "Forget it…", which takes it off the list until that conversation is closed again and keeps its directory and session. On a past session: resume it in a new tab in the directory it ended in, open its diff in a tab, open its PR here (in a pane beside, while its worktree's PR is known), copy its session ID or its directory, and, when it ended in a worktree no tab has open, "Remove its worktree…". On a worktree: open a tab there and resume its last Claude session, or a new one (as `switchboard claude-here` does), open its diff or lazygit in a tab of their own there, copy its directory, and "Remove the worktree…". Removing runs `git worktree remove` from its main clone, which keeps a worktree with changes not committed and says so. The view's `m` menu shows only tabs, only sessions (either without the worktrees), or everything again.
