A sealed review, the tab `/review-pr` marked once it started the PR's sealed try (`switchboard set @sealed_review <worktree>`), reopens in the main clone with its Claude resumed, and `switchboard try --sealed --detached <worktree>` typed into a shell beside it; on Linux, or with the worktree gone, only its Claude is resumed, which is said.

The newest 200 closings are kept in `~/.local/state/switchboard/closed.jsonl`, and, while Settings' "Save what panes show" (`save_contents`) is on, each one's last screen (its Claude's pane, else its active one, as it closed) in `closed-screens/` beside it, private, taken out with its closing; none of it is sent anywhere. Settings' "Reopen" (`view_reopen`, which an earlier "Closed tabs", `view_closed`, turned off carries over to); off, `M-o` opens it still and nothing else lists them.

### Plugins

Anything with an API can be a view of its own in mission control: a plugin fetches a list of items, and Enter on one opens its link. A plugin is an executable in any language (no longer a JavaScript module), `~/.config/switchboard/sidebar-plugins/<name>` (a name of lowercase letters, digits and dashes), and only the plugins named under `plugins` in `~/.config/switchboard/sidebar.json` load, each with the options given under its name (`true` for none, `false` to leave it out):

```json
{
  "plugins": {
    "deploys": { "url": "https://deploys.example.com/api", "intervalSeconds": 120 },
    "github": { "query": "org:your-org" }
  }
}
```

It is run every `intervalSeconds` (five minutes when nothing says), never through a shell, with its options as JSON on its input, and prints its items as JSON: a list, or an object with `items` and, if it likes, `title`, `intervalSeconds` and `sidebar`, which the config's own win over. `sidebar` is how many of its items the sidebar lists under the tabs (none when nothing says): its title in capitals with how many items it has and how many need you ("DEPLOYS 3 ! 1"), its error when its last run failed, and its first items, each marked by its state. Each item has a `title`, and may have an `id`, a `detail` line, a `state` (`attention` marks it as needing you, `active`, or `idle`), a `url` (an `http` or `https` link, which Enter opens) and a `time` in milliseconds:

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
import json, subprocess, sys, urllib.request

options = json.load(sys.stdin)
token = subprocess.run(["switchboard", "platform", "secret", "deploys-token"],
                       capture_output=True, text=True).stdout.strip()
request = urllib.request.Request(options["url"], headers={"Authorization": f"Bearer {token}"})
deploys = json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(request))
print(json.dumps({"title": "Deploys", "items": [
    {"id": d["id"], "title": d["service"], "detail": d["status"],
     "state": "attention" if d["status"] == "failed" else "active",
     "url": d["link"]} for d in deploys]}))
```

`switchboard platform secret <service>` prints a secret stored with `switchboard platform secret-set <service>`, so no token sits in the config. A run that takes over 30 seconds is killed, and one that fails keeps the items the last good one gave, with its error's first line above them. `r` in a plugin's view asks it again at once. The config is read again whenever it changes.

`github`, built in, lists your open pull requests and the ones waiting on your review, with the GitHub token mission control uses, marking as needing you a review requested of you, or a pull request of yours whose checks failed or that has changes requested; drafts are idle. Its `query` option adds search terms to both searches, such as `org:your-org`.

## Reading beside your work

The branch's pull request, its Linear ticket and any link can be read in a pane split off to the right of the one you are in, rather than in the browser; `M-v` and `M-i` close their view again from anywhere in the tab. Each view is a program of Switchboard's own (`switchboard pr-view`, `ticket-view`, `link-view`), drawn and paged the same way, so each can also be run by hand.

### The PR description

`M-v` shows the branch's pull request in a pane to the right, taking 45% of the width: its title and number, a line saying whose branch goes into which and whether it is open, a draft, merged or closed, then its description. The PR is found from the pane's branch as the tab's name finds it (in origin and, in a fork's clone, the project `upstream` names; a branch checked out from someone's fork by the branch it tracks; a `wtpr` worktree by its number), and a merged or closed one still counts. The copy last read for the branch shows at once, and the PR is read again in the background; when it has changed, the view is laid out again where you were. Left open, the view follows the PR as the workspace's lookups see it change: when the tab's PR lookup answers with a different state, checks, review decision or size, the view reads the PR again and shows it where you were, so its checks, reviewers and comments are never those of the moment it opened. It has no timer of its own, so while the lookups are paused (you're away, or GitHub is off) it stays as it is, and once its copy is five minutes old the bottom line says so at its right end ("read 12m ago"). `r` reads it again at once, and so does coming back to the view after five minutes or more in another pane, tab or app (the focus as the workspace tells a pane of it: moving between panes, tabs and apps, not a tool such as the diff opened in the view's place, nor a split closed beside it, after which `r` reads); a read that fails leaves the copy as it is, to be read again when the lookup next answers, and one `r` asked for says why. `M-v` again closes it. With no PR to show, the view says why: not a git repository, no `origin` on GitHub, no GitHub token, or no PR for the branch yet ("Push it and open one, then M-v again").

With [UX 2.0](#ux-20) on, the PR shows in Claude's place, laid out as GitHub lays out its page: a chip saying whether it is open, a draft, merged or closed, its number and title, a line starting with the author's small photo saying who wants to merge how many commits from which branch (without its owner) into which and the lines added and removed, then the author's large photo with "opened this" and when, then the description and the PR's comments, each with its author's photo. Beside them, a panel lists the checks (the failing ones first, then those still running, with how many have passed), the reviewers and what each said or that they were asked, the review threads still open (who started each, on which file and line, what they said, and how many replies with the last one), and the files changed with their lines added and removed, a long list ending in "N more". The panel stays put while the description scrolls; in a pane under 100 columns wide it follows the description instead, and so does any of its sections that doesn't fit beside it in the pane's height. The footer names the keys that work from there. A photo is drawn where the view draws pictures, with Settings' `photos` on, and is the person's initial on a color of their own until then and elsewhere; one that failed to download is tried again every 15 minutes while the view stays open.

`M-p`'s "View the branch's PR description as an image" draws the description as GitHub draws it (its HTML, GitHub's stylesheet, Mermaid diagrams drawn) and shows it a page at a time as pictures in place of the pane: `j`/`k` or Space between pages, "Page 2 of 3" and "↓ more" in the corner, `q` (or the command again) giving the pane back. "View the branch's Linear ticket as an image" does the same for the ticket. Both need Chrome or Chromium, which draws the page (the view says so when neither is installed), and a terminal that shows kitty's pictures (Ghostty or kitty), or one that gets sixel by Settings' "Image protocol", as Windows Terminal does by default ([Pictures in Windows Terminal](#pictures-in-windows-terminal)).

Both keep what they draw on this computer (Settings' "Cache PR and ticket images", `image_cache`), so one opened again shows at once. What the page loads from the web (GitHub's stylesheet, Mermaid and the Markdown scripts, and the PR's or ticket's pictures) is fetched once and kept in `~/.cache/switchboard/assets/`, under its link without the query for GitHub's private pictures and Linear's uploads, whose links carry a new signature on every read; a pinned file is used only while its hash is the one the page pins. The pages drawn are kept in `~/.cache/switchboard/pr-image/pages/`, under the text (those signatures left out) and the pane's size, so the same PR or ticket at the same size shows from there without starting Chrome, which runs only once something needs drawing: a size not drawn before, or a description that changed when it was read again. Pages in which something failed to load are shown but not kept, and are drawn again once the PR or ticket has been read again. The two together are held to 300 MB, the least recently used going first, and pages not opened for 30 days are dropped. `M-p`'s "Clear the PR and ticket image cache" removes it all, the text last read included, after a yes. With the setting off, everything is fetched and drawn each time. The page loads nothing from the web but its pinned files (its policy refuses the rest), and a picture only from the copy kept here, so a PR's or ticket's picture reaches no server as it draws, and one that couldn't be fetched, or any with the setting off, is left out.

### The Linear ticket

`M-i` does the same for the branch's Linear ticket. The ticket's ID comes from the pane's branch, the first thing in it shaped like a ticket ID in any case (`abc-123-fix-login` gives ABC-123); with none there, from the tab's name (a "Shaping ABC-123: ..." tab before there is a branch, or a name you gave it). The view shows the ticket's title, the facts from its side panel (state, priority, assignee, project, cycle, estimate, labels), who opened it and when, when it was last updated and its parent, then the description, sub-issues, related tickets and links, and the comments, oldest first, each reply under the comment it answers. A bare link to another ticket reads as that ticket's ID. As with the PR, the copy last read shows at once while the ticket is read again, and the view left open follows the ticket: it reads it again when the Tickets lookup sees its state or its last change differ (a ticket assigned to you, which is what that lookup reads), on `r`, and when you come back to it after five minutes or more, saying how old its copy is once that is five minutes. With no ticket in the branch or the tab's name, `M-i` says so.

With [UX 2.0](#ux-20) on, the ticket shows in Claude's place, as its page in Linear: its ID and title, who opened it and when with their large photo, the description, the sub-issues each with its state's mark and how many are done, and the comment threads, each with its author's large photo, each reply under the comment it answers with its photo where that comment's words start. Beside them, as the PR's, a panel holds its facts (its state as a chip, priority, assignee, project, cycle, estimate, labels and parent), the pull requests linked to it with their state where Linear keeps it, the related tickets, and its other links.

### In the pager

Every view draws its Markdown for the terminal: headings without their `#`s, tables, task lists and code blocks, a link as its text alone, an image as a line naming it, and the HTML a PR template leaves read for what it says. A click on a link, or an image's line, opens it in the browser. A line too wide for the pane is cut at its edge rather than wrapped, so a drawing keeps its shape; Left and Right scroll across. Resizing the pane lays the text out again at the new width, opened at the line that was at the top.

Mermaid diagrams are drawn as text: flowcharts (`flowchart` or `graph`, in any direction), each subgraph a rounded box round its nodes with its title on its top edge, laid out in its own `direction` when it names one, and sequence diagrams. A left-to-right flowchart wider than the pane is drawn top to bottom instead, and one wider still is left whole, for Left and Right to scroll. Any other kind of diagram keeps its source, with a line saying where it is drawn properly (`M-r` on GitHub, `M-l` in Linear, the page in the browser).

In a terminal that shows kitty's pictures (Ghostty or kitty), with Settings' "Pictures and diagrams in the views" (`view_images`) on, a picture on a line of its own and every Mermaid diagram, of any kind, are drawn as pictures instead, inside a rounded border, as large as they are but no wider than the text and no taller than the pane. Each shows as text until it is ready, and stays text when it can't be had. A picture is fetched from its link, a GitHub attachment with your GitHub token and a Linear upload with your Linear key, each sent to that host alone, and none of GitHub's or Linear's pictures is fetched while that integration is off; a note or a file of yours (`switchboard view`) has its diagrams drawn but no picture fetched, since a link in it is your words; a diagram is drawn by Mermaid in Chrome or Chromium, as the image view draws one, and stays text without either. Both are kept in the image views' cache while `image_cache` is on, so a view opened again shows them at once. The view's pane passes on the graphics commands that draw them while it runs, and the view leaves out any control character the text holds, so a PR or ticket can't send your terminal commands of its own.

The Markdown, and a board's column (the PR and the ticket with UX 2.0, its panel staying put), has a cursor, a yellow `›` in the margin of the line it is on, with Settings' "A cursor in the views" (`view_cursor`) on. The keys below move it rather than the screen, which scrolls only to keep it in sight; it passes over blank lines, and over a picture or diagram in one step, its `›` beside every row of it. `Enter` or a click on a picture or diagram shows it full size over the view, drawn by the workspace as a note's picture is, `Esc`, `q` or `Enter` going back; the prompt line says so while the cursor is on one. Where the workspace won't draw it (outside Switchboard, or with Settings' "Images in the workspace", `server_images`, or "Enter shows a picture full size", `picture_full_size`, off), it opens in Quick Look on macOS, or in the desktop's viewer on Linux. In a terminal that doesn't draw the views' pictures, a picture stays its line of text, and a click on it opens its link in the browser. A click elsewhere moves the cursor to the line, and opens a link under it. With the setting off, and for a log or a plain text file, the keys scroll as less's do and `Enter` is a line down.

| Key | What it does |
|---|---|
| `j` `k`, `↓` `↑`, `Enter`, `e` `y`, `Ctrl-n` `Ctrl-p` | A line down or up (`Enter` on a picture or diagram shows it full size) |
| `Space` `f` `b`, `PageDown` `PageUp`, `Ctrl-f` `Ctrl-b` | A screen down or up |
| `d` `u`, `Ctrl-d` `Ctrl-u` | Half a screen down or up |
| `g` `G`, `Home` `End`, `<` `>` | The top or the end ("(END)" shows at the bottom there) |
| `←` `→` | Half a screen across |
| `/` | Search, ignoring case unless what you type has a capital; `Enter` finds it, `Esc` cancels |
| `n` `N` | The next match, or the one before |
| `o` | The page being read, in the browser (the link view) |
| `q`, `Esc` | Close (`Ctrl-c` does nothing, as in less, so one meant for the pane beside it is lost harmlessly) |

The wheel scrolls three lines at a time.

### Opening the PR and the ticket in the browser

`M-r` opens the branch's pull request in the browser. A branch with no PR yet opens its repository instead, and a box says why. `M-p`'s "Open the repository in the browser" opens the repository whatever the branch. When the PR or the repository can't be opened, a box says why: the directory isn't in a git repository, the repository has no `origin` remote or one that isn't on GitHub, there is no GitHub token or GitHub refused it, or GitHub doesn't have the repository or won't show it to your token.

`M-l` opens the branch's Linear ticket, found as `M-i` finds it. With your workspace written in `linear.json` (below), the ticket's address is made from it at once; without, Linear is asked where the ticket is. A box says when there is no ticket in the branch or tab name, when Linear has no such ticket, or why Linear couldn't be asked.

With GitHub turned off in Settings (Integrations, `github`), `M-v`, `M-r`, `M-m` and "Open the repository in the browser" do nothing but show a tip saying so, the first time; with Linear off (`linear`), `M-i` and `M-l` do the same.

### Whether the PR can merge

`M-m` asks GitHub whether the branch's PR is ready to merge and answers in a box, "Reaching out to GitHub..." until it knows. A merged or closed PR says so and stops there, and so does one in the merge queue, with its place and where it stands (waiting on the queue's checks, passed and waiting its turn, merging now, or unable to merge and about to be removed). Otherwise the verdict is GitHub's own, so it agrees with the merge button: ready to merge (noting merge hooks, or optional checks not passing), or not ready because the branch is behind its base, a required review or check blocks it, it has conflicts, or it is still a draft. When GitHub hasn't worked out yet whether it can merge, it is asked again, up to three times a second apart. Under the verdict come the lines that explain it: the required checks, all checks counted by outcome, the review, conflicts, whether it is a draft, and auto-merge.

A branch with no PR (one pushed before its PR is opened, the default branch of a repository that lands straight on it) gets the CI of the commit GitHub has for it instead, in the repository it is pushed to: "No PR · CI on <branch> at <commit>", then whether its checks are passing, failing (how many of all), running (how many of all), canceled (how many of all) or there are none, with all of them counted by outcome. When this tab's commit isn't the one GitHub checked, a line says the tab is that many commits ahead of it or behind it, or that its commit isn't the one GitHub checked. A branch GitHub doesn't have says so. With a check failing, "See the failing checks" is offered as for a PR; there are no conflicts or review lines, which only a PR has.

The box ends with what can be done about the answer. With a check failing, "See the failing checks" opens a pane beside yours listing each one with its description and link and, for a GitHub Actions job, the last 25 lines of its log. With conflicts, "Type /resolve-conflicts into this tab's Claude" types it into the tab's Claude session and moves you there to press Enter, since what is in its prompt already can't be seen from the box. `j` and `k` (or the arrows, Tab, the wheel) move between them, Enter, Space or a click takes one, and Esc or `q` closes the box; with nothing to do, any key closes it. Each `M-m` also has mission control's PR views look again, so they agree with what it said.

### Search everything

`M-F` (Alt-Shift-F) opens one box: what to search for, a chip for each source under it, and the results under those. Enter searches the open tabs, your notes, Linear, GitHub and Slack at once for what you typed. Each source's results appear under its name as it answers, up to 20 each, its chip showing how many it found (`⋯` while it is still looking) and the field the count so far, and the rows stay in the sources' order, so the list doesn't shift under the cursor when a slow one arrives. Change what is typed and Enter searches again; with the results of what is typed shown, ↓ goes from the field to the chips and from the chips to the results, ↑↓ move through them (↑ on the first goes back to the chips, and from there to the field), PageUp and PageDown move through them from anywhere, and Enter reads the chosen one beside the pane you searched from, in the link view: a note as its Markdown, a pull request as `M-v` lays one out, a ticket as `M-i` does. A Slack message can't be read there, so Enter opens it in the browser. Ctrl-O opens the chosen one in the browser instead, whatever it is but a note. A tab is gone to with Enter, and Ctrl-O goes to it with its pane in copy mode on the last line that shows what you searched for, the match selected, as `M-j`'s "Go to the match" does.

- **Tabs**: every open tab, the one you searched from included, by its name; with Settings' "Search what panes show" (`tab_search_panes`) on, by what its panes show, read as `M-j`'s filter reads them; and with "Search tabs' conversations" (`tab_search_conversation`) on, by what was said in its Claude conversation, your prompts and Claude's replies from the session's start (not its tool calls), read from the transcript Claude Code keeps on the computer, a transcript over 50 MB from its last 50 MB. All of it is read in the server's memory and sent nowhere, and listed first since it answers at once. Each shows its number and name, then the last line that holds what you typed (for a tab found only in its conversation, after "said" and how long ago, as in "said 3h 0m ago: …"), or "by name"; the tabs found by name come first, then those found by their panes, the most matching lines first, then those found only in their conversation, the latest first, and in each the tab you searched from comes last, marked "this tab". Enter on a tab found in its conversation goes to it with its history (`M-h`) open at the moment it was said, and Ctrl-O goes to the tab alone.
- **Notes**: every Markdown note in `$NOTES_DIR`, else `~/Notes`, that holds every word you typed, newest first, with the line where the first word appears. Hidden folders, the trash among them, are skipped. The notes are read on the computer and sent nowhere.
- **Linear**: Linear's issue search.
- **GitHub**: issues and pull requests in the repositories your tabs are in.
- **Slack**: Slack's own search, through your own Slack sign-in while "Read Slack through your own token" is on, else through Claude's connector (below), arriving about 15 seconds after the rest.

The chips choose which sources are asked: one for each source Settings has on (Tabs, Notes, Linear, GitHub, Slack), lit while it is searched, and All, which lights every one again. ↓ from the field reaches the chip you were on last (Tabs the first time), ← and → move along the chips, wrapping from the last to the first and back, and typing on a chip or a result puts it in the field; Settings' "Arrow keys reach the search chips" (`search_arrows`, under Integrations) turns that off, leaving ↑↓ to the results alone. A click on a chip, or Space or Enter on it after Tab or the arrows have reached it, turns its source on or off for the search: a source turned off loses its rows and is sent nothing, and one turned on after a search is asked for the same thing at once. The last chip lit stays on. The chips as you leave them are kept for the next `M-F`, in `~/.local/state/switchboard/search.json`, and the field's prompt names what is searched when it isn't every source ("Search notes, Slack: "). The box shows no keys, so the results have its room.

The chosen result is previewed beside the results (under them when the box is narrower than 100 columns; the box takes four fifths of the screen's width for it): where it was found on its first line (a note's title and the heading above the match, a tab and its pane or its conversation, a ticket, an issue or PR, a Slack channel and who wrote it), with the match's line number for a note, what it is under that, and then up to five lines before and five after the match, the match's line lit and the words you searched for in the accent. It follows the cursor as you move it, from what each source answered: a note's lines and a pane's from what was searched, a conversation's from its transcript, a ticket's, issue's or PR's from its description, and a Slack message's from the messages around it in Slack's answer. With the results focused, J and K scroll it, as the wheel over it does, and, with the preview beside the results, the pane resize keys and a drag of the line between the two move that line. Settings' "Preview the chosen search result" (`search_preview`, under Integrations) turns it off, leaving the box as it was.

A source's name and a colon at the start of what you type does the same as its chip: `tab:`, `note:`, `linear:`, `gh:` (or `github:`) and `slack:`, in any case, one or more (`note: slack: deploy` searches notes and Slack for "deploy"). The prefix leaves the field as you type it and lights those chips alone, for this search only. A word before a colon that names no source (`fix:`) is searched for like the rest. `M-p`'s "Search the open tabs", "Search notes", "Search Linear", "Search GitHub" and "Search Slack" open the box with that source's prefix typed.

A source that fails shows a row saying why, which reads nothing when chosen. GitHub and Linear are left out while their settings are off, with no chip, even when a prefix names them. Settings' "Slack in search everything" (`search_slack`) leaves Slack out, and "Search everything" (`search_everything`), both under Integrations, turns the key off.

### Reading a link

`M-u` (`M-p`'s "Read a link in a pane beside this one, typed or one this pane shows") lists the links the pane shows and has scrolled past, newest first, each once; a link a program wrote into its text (an OSC 8 hyperlink, as Claude Code writes them) shows that text before its address. The links in what you sent the pane's Claude that the pane doesn't show are listed too, marked "you gave it": Claude Code shows a long paste as "[Pasted text #1 +12 lines]", so a link inside one is never on the screen. The newest 500 are kept, on this computer alone, however many links its replies name, until the pane's Claude starts a new session or the pane closes, and through a restart that keeps every pane (Settings' "Links you gave Claude", `prompt_links`; off, the commands list only what the pane shows). With the notes pane open in the tab (`M-e`), the links of the note it shows are listed too, each marked "in the note", so a link in the note can be read from Claude's pane beside it; in the notes pane itself the note's links are listed with those of the tab's other panes, since Notes shows a link's text alone. The whole list is newest first, across the pane, what you sent its Claude and the note: a link by when you last sent it to Claude or Claude last named it in a turn's final message, or when the note was saved with it, if it was added after the note was first read since the server started; the links with no such time follow, the pane's from its bottom up, then the note's from its top down. A note open in another tab adds nothing, and Settings' "Pane commands read the tab's note" (`pane_commands_read_note`) turns this off. Type to narrow the list, or type or paste a link of your own; `M-Enter` takes what you typed even when a listed link matches it. Enter reads the link in a pane to the right, half the width, in place of any link read in that tab before, and `Ctrl-O` opens it in the browser instead. What the link is decides how it is read:

- a GitHub pull request, as `M-v` lays one out;
- a Linear ticket, as `M-i` lays one out;
- a Notion page, or a Google Doc or Slides deck, through Claude's connectors, as the connector gives it, in about 15 seconds. Settings' "Docs in the link view" (`link_view_docs`, Integrations) turns this off, and such a link is then read as any other page, which a private one won't allow;
- a note, a `file://` link that search everything gives, read only from the notes directory;
- a Slack message, which can't be read without Slack's connector, so its pane offers only to open it: `o` opens it, any other key closes;
- any other web page, fetched, its main text turned into Markdown under its title and address.

`o` in the link view opens the page in the browser, and `M-p`'s "Open the link being read beside this pane in the browser" does the same from any pane in the tab. `q` closes it. A page that can't be read says why: `o` then opens it in the browser and closes the pane, and any other key closes it.

The same list does two more things. "Open a link from this pane in the browser, or read it in a pane beside" opens the chosen link in the browser, `Ctrl-O` reading it beside instead; "Copy a link from this pane to the clipboard, or open it in the browser" copies it (to your terminal's clipboard and the computer's, as a selection is), `Ctrl-O` opening it. With no links in the pane or the note open in its tab, each says so.

"Go to a tab this pane mentions, or copy its number" and "Copy the number of a tab this pane mentions, or go to it" read the same text, the pane's then the note open in its tab (the note's first in the notes pane), for the tabs it names by number: "tab 15", "Tab #3" or "tabs 3, 5 and 7", any case, as whole words, so "table 4" names none. A tab keeps its number while it is open, so the list holds the open tabs named, newest first, each with its name and what its Claude is doing, the pane's own tab left out. Going to one switches session when it is another's, and with one tab named it goes there at once; copying puts the number on the clipboard. `Ctrl-O` does the other, and with no open tab named, each says so.

"Open an image or video from this pane in its app, or show it in its folder" lists the pictures and videos the pane names, newest first: a file's path (absolute, from `~`, or from the pane's directory, listed only when the file is there, one a program broke across lines put back together; png, jpg, gif, webp, heic, svg, bmp, tiff, mp4, mov, webm, mkv, m4v and avi), then the links among the pane's whose address ends in one, those you gave its Claude included (a file's path in a prompt is not listed); after the pane's files come those the note open in the tab names (from the note's own folder, where a picture pasted into it is kept, from `~`, or absolute), and the note's links are among the links; from the notes pane, the note's files come first, then those the tab's other panes name, each from its own pane's directory. Last come the files the last turns of the tab's Claude named that the screen doesn't show (its messages, questions and tools' output, from its transcript), marked "from the conversation" (Settings' "Pictures and videos Claude named", `conversation_media`; off, only the pane's and the note's). Enter opens a file in the app the desktop opens it with (Preview or QuickTime on a Mac) and a link in the browser; `Ctrl-O` shows a file in Finder, or opens its folder on Linux.

Mission control's menus read a link beside your work too: "Read it here" on a pull request or ticket on the boards and in the Inbox, "Read the PR here" in the CI and Landing views, and an event's linked tickets and pull requests in the Calendar view, each in a pane beside the one you were in when mission control opened.

### The views by hand

```
switchboard view notes.md               # page a Markdown file, drawn as the views draw one
switchboard view --log ci.log           # page a CI job's log from its end
switchboard view --log +40 ci.log       # page it from line 40
switchboard view +42 src/main.rs        # page any file as plain text from line 42
switchboard pr-view [dir]               # the branch's PR description
switchboard pr-view --checks [dir]      # the PR's failing checks (the branch's, with no PR), as M-m shows them
switchboard ticket-view [dir | ABC-123] # a ticket, by ID or from a directory's branch
switchboard pr-image [dir]              # the branch's PR description as GitHub draws it, as images
switchboard pr-image --ticket ABC-123   # a Linear ticket the same way
switchboard link-view <url>             # what a link holds
```

Each runs in the terminal it is started in, the directory defaulting to the one you are in, and takes the pager's keys. `pr-image` has Chrome draw the page and shows it a page at a time as pictures (`j`, `k`, Space and `b` page, `g` and `G` go to the ends, `r` draws it again, `q` quits), through kitty's graphics protocol, so it needs Chrome and a terminal that draws pictures (Ghostty, kitty, or inside the workspace one that gets sixel by Settings' "Image protocol", Windows Terminal by default); inside the workspace only a pane allowed to send pictures shows them, which is why `M-p`'s "View the branch's PR description as an image" and "View the branch's Linear ticket as an image" open it in the pane's place.

### Where links open

Every link Switchboard opens, from mission control, the views, `M-r` or `M-l`, goes to the Chrome profile `~/.config/switchboard/browser.json` gives its site, else in that file's default profile, else in your default browser. The file is yours and is never in a repository:

```json
{ "hosts": { "linear.app": "Profile 2", "slack.com": "Profile 2" }, "default": "Profile 1" }
```

A site in `hosts` matches itself and its subdomains, the longest match winning. A profile is written as the folder Chrome keeps it in, as `chrome://version` shows it ("Profile 2"), or as the name Chrome shows for it ("Work"). Settings lists `hosts` and `default` under "Browser (browser.json)", as Chrome profiles and Default Chrome profile, the default picked from Chrome's own profiles. On macOS the link opens with `open -na "Google Chrome"`; on Linux the first of `google-chrome` and Chromium that is installed is run, with the profile. Where neither is installed, the link opens in the default browser (through `xdg-open` on Linux). Settings' "Chrome profiles" (`chrome_profiles`, Integrations) turned off opens every link in the default browser.

While a link opens, a box says where it is going ("Opening github.com in Chrome · Work…", or "in your browser") until the browser comes to the front; then it closes by itself, says the browser hasn't shown it yet when it hasn't after 8 seconds, or says why it couldn't open. A link in a chosen profile can take seconds: naming a profile starts a new Chrome process, which hands the link to the Chrome already running, and macOS (and any security agent) checks that process before it runs, slowest while something else keeps it busy. A link with no profile goes to the running browser at once. Mission control says the same in its busy box, and the notes app and the PR and ticket views on their message lines. Settings' "Opening-link message" (`link_opening_message`) turned off opens links with nothing said.

`switchboard open-url <url>` opens a link the same way, for scripts and skills, and `switchboard open-url --as <site> <url>` in the profile `<site>` would get (as the AWS login opens AWS's pages in `awsapps.com`'s). Every pane's environment has `GH_BROWSER` set to `switchboard open-url`, so gh's `--web` (`gh pr view --web` and the rest) opens the same way from any pane; outside the workspace, set it yourself.

### GitHub and Linear access

Switchboard talks to GitHub and Linear over their own APIs. Each finds its credential where you already keep one:

- **GitHub**: the token of GitHub's CLI, `gh auth token`, and nowhere else, so install [gh](https://cli.github.com) and sign in with `gh auth login`. Every GitHub request Switchboard makes, the update check's too, signs in as the account gh is signed in to, and shares its rate limit of 5,000 requests an hour; gh decides which token that is, its own `GH_TOKEN` included. The update check, which reads a public repository's releases, still looks without a token when gh has none, GitHub refuses gh's or says your account's limit is spent, or the `github` setting is off, from the 60 requests an hour GitHub allows every program behind the same address.
- **Linear**: `LINEAR_API_KEY`, the stored secret `linear-api-key`, else `linearApiKey` in `~/.config/switchboard/linear.json`. Make a key in Linear under Settings, Security & access, Personal API keys.

Slack is reached through Claude's Slack connector, but for your reminders and read marks in the Slack view, and its reads while "Read Slack through your own token" is on, which use a user token of your own: `SWITCHBOARD_SLACK_USER_TOKEN`, else the stored secret `slack-user-token`, which `switchboard setup slack-user` stores ("The Slack view").

A credential from `gh`, a secret or the file is kept for ten minutes and forgotten as soon as the service refuses it, so a new one is used on the next request. GitHub's is looked for once at a time, and a look that finds nothing (a `gh` signed out, or too slow on a busy computer) is made again only after a minute, the token found before still used meanwhile. To store a secret, run the command and paste it, then press `Ctrl-d`:

```
switchboard platform secret-set linear-api-key
```

It reads the secret from its input, never from an argument, which other programs could see. On macOS it goes in the Keychain, handed to `security` on its input too (so a secret of up to about 2000 bytes), as a generic password whose service is `linear-api-key` (one you added there yourself under that service is read too). On Linux it goes in the desktop's keyring through the Secret Service (GNOME Keyring, KWallet), as an item whose `service` attribute is that name; on a computer with no keyring, such as a server or WSL, it goes in `~/.config/switchboard/secrets.json`, which only you can read. `switchboard platform secret <name>` prints one back.

`~/.config/switchboard/linear.json` also holds what Switchboard and its skills need to know about your Linear, each listed in Settings under "Linear (linear.json)":

```json
{ "linearWorkspace": "your-workspace", "linearTeam": "ABC", "linearLabels": ["bug"] }
```

`linearWorkspace` is your workspace's name in its addresses (`linear.app/your-workspace`), which `M-l` makes the ticket's link from (`LINEAR_WORKSPACE` in the environment wins over it). `linearTeam` and `linearLabels` are the team key and the labels `/shape` and `/note-tickets` file new tickets with.

### Claude's connectors

What Switchboard can only reach through Claude's connectors (a Notion page or Google Doc in the link view, search everything's Slack) it asks of Claude Code: one headless Haiku, allowed only the one connector tool it needs, keeping no session, its answer taken from the tool's own result rather than from what Haiku writes. Claude Code must be installed and signed in, with the connector connected to your Claude account. A connector that isn't connected, or whose sign-in has lapsed, fails with a message saying what to do ("Notion isn't connected in Claude: run /mcp in Claude and sign in to Notion."), shown where the feature shows (the link view, search's Slack row), and the health light in the status bar names it ("Notion not connected") for two hours after, or until the connector answers again (Settings' "Health light", `health_light`). A run whose Haiku never called the tool isn't taken for that alone, since a connector can still be connecting as a headless run starts (it waits for them first): Claude Code is asked whether the connector is connected (`claude mcp get`, with no model), and when it is, the call is made once more two seconds later, a second miss failing as "Slack's tool wasn't ready in Claude; it will be tried again" without calling the connector down. How each connector last answered is kept in `~/.local/state/switchboard/connectors.json`. While one is down, the looks nobody is waiting on (the Slack view's look) don't run a Haiku to find it still isn't there: every 15 minutes they ask Claude Code whether it is connected now (`claude mcp get`, which checks that one connector with no model, in about a second), and look again once it is. A reconnect in `/mcp` is noticed sooner: it writes Claude Code's config (`~/.claude.json`), so while a connector is down a change to that file has Switchboard ask about each one down within seconds (at most every 10 seconds), and while the Slack view shows Slack missing it asks every minute; the Slack view's `m` menu has "Check Slack again" to ask at once. One found connected says so ("Slack is connected again."), leaves the health light and the view, and the Slack view looks at once. A search, a link or anything else you start tries at once too.

## The diff

`M-d` shows the branch's changes, those not yet committed too, in place of the pane you are in, as GitHub's "Files changed" shows them: the changed files in reading order on the left, the chosen file side by side on the right. The rest of the tab stays as it is. The pane you were in waits, its program still running, and takes its place back when the diff closes: `M-d` again, `q`, or `M-w` on the diff's pane. The first time, a tip says so. Beside a lone pane narrowed by "Widest a lone pane gets", with at least 80 columns spare at its right, the diff opens beside it instead, taking three quarters of the width beside the sidebar (or the spare columns, when they give it more), the pane narrowed at its left while the diff is open and given its own width back when it closes, which it does the same ways (Settings' "Open views beside a narrow pane", and "Diff's share beside a narrow pane" for half, two thirds or the spare columns alone). Dragging the line between them resizes for that diff alone. Outside a git repository, `M-d` says so instead.

### What it compares

The diff runs from where the branch left its PR's base branch (the merge base) to the working tree, clean or not: the branch's commits and its changes not yet committed together, staged, unstaged and files git doesn't know yet, those listed as added. Each file with changes not yet committed is marked with a yellow ● in the list and "● uncommitted" in its bar (see "The story" for where it goes in the list). `R` reads the change again, so edits made while the diff is open come in, and the file you are on stays chosen. With Settings' "Uncommitted changes in M-d" (`diff_uncommitted`, under Diff) off, the diff is the branch's committed changes alone (`git diff <merge base> HEAD`), as GitHub's "Files changed" shows them.

The base is the PR's base branch, else `origin`'s default branch for a branch with no PR (or with GitHub off), else `main`. On the default branch itself that is its own copy on `origin`, so the diff is its commits not yet pushed and what isn't committed. A branch with no PR whose upstream is another branch (one cut from a feature branch, `git checkout -b over --track origin/under`) compares with that branch instead, from where it left it; an upstream that is the branch's own copy on a remote, as `git push -u` leaves it, doesn't count. A PR GitHub has for that other branch is that branch's, not this one's, so the diff doesn't take its base; this branch's own PR is found once it tracks its own copy (`git push -u`), and its base is the diff's from then on. With UX 2.0, the heading over the diff says which: `against main (PR #123)` for a PR's base, `against origin/main (no PR)` (or the upstream's name) for a branch with none, and `against origin/main · 3 unpushed` on the default branch. It is fetched from `origin` first, saying so on the diff's screen, so a branch rebased onto newer work isn't shown that work as its own; a branch still at the commit it was last compared at, with the same upstream, skips the fetch, since its merge base can't have moved; one compared as a branch with no PR is asked about again once that is ten minutes old (while GitHub is on), so a PR opened since is named and its base taken. When the fetch fails, the diff compares with the base as last fetched and says so. A branch with no commits past its base and nothing uncommitted (with the setting off, no commits past its base) has nothing to show: a box says so ("Nothing to show: no commits past origin/main and no uncommitted changes", or on the default branch "Nothing here that origin's main lacks: no unpushed commits and no uncommitted changes") and the pane stays as it was.

`M-p`'s "Open the changes not yet committed, staged and not, in place of this pane, or close them" shows the working tree's changes instead, in the same place and with the same keys: the changes not staged under "Changes", a file git doesn't know yet among them as added, and the staged ones under "Staged changes" below, as diffview listed them, a file staged and changed again since in both. A file not staged has the index on its left and the file on its right; a staged one has HEAD on its left and the index on its right. Its story reads the staged changes and then the rest, with no commit messages, and `yl` links to HEAD for a side GitHub can't have. `M-d` stays on the branch's diff, and closes either.

### The files and their changes

Each file in the list shows its status (A added, M modified, D deleted, R renamed), its name, its folder faintly, and its lines added and removed. The chosen file's old text is on the left and its new text on the right, each line beside the line it became and a line only one side has facing a gap. Lines are matched as a person reads them, so fewer look changed than a plain diff shows, and within a changed line only the words that changed are marked, in a stronger shade of git's green or red. The text has its language's syntax colours, in your terminal's own palette (comments its bright black, strings green, functions blue, keywords magenta, types yellow), which come a moment after a file opens. A long line wraps within its side, so a paragraph of Markdown on one line reads whole. Unchanged runs more than three lines from a change are folded into a "⋯ N unchanged lines" line. Each side numbers its lines at its left, faintly, unless it is too narrow to spare the columns. An added file is shown alone in a single pane, every line numbered and tinted green as added, and so is a deleted one, as it was, tinted red. Each file opens with the cursor on its first changed word. The bar over the new side names the file's path from the repository's root; the old side's names one only for a renamed file, which is where it was.

Settings' "Files in reading order" (`diff_order`, under Diff) lists the files flat in the order a reviewer reads them: documentation first (Markdown, `docs/`, READMEs, changelogs), then the database's changes (migrations, SQL, schema files), then the server's side of a web app (Next.js server actions, API routes, `*.server.ts`, `server/`), then the rest of the code, then tests, then generated files. Generated files are lockfiles, migration and test snapshots, minified files, source maps, and built or generated output; the diff opens on the first file that isn't one. When no story is told, Haiku orders the server's and the rest of the code's files from the most abstract to the most concrete, from their paths and line counts alone; until it answers, or if it can't, they are A to Z. Its answer is kept, so the same diff opens in that order at once. With the setting off, the list is A to Z.

### The story

With Settings' "Diff told as a story" (`diff_story`, which needs Files in reading order on) the list is told as a story. Haiku reads the whole diff, up to about 160,000 characters (files that fit kept whole, the rest sharing what is left, every change still named), and the commits' messages, and groups the changes into two to eight pages, each one thing the change does, from its core to its edges, tests right after the code they test, generated files last whatever it says. With Settings' "Diff story reads the tab's plan" (`diff_story_plan`, off by default) on, it is given the tab's plan note too (an autopilot item's plan, the first 4,000 characters), which sends that note's words to Claude. This sends the diff's text and the commit messages to Claude. It needs Claude Code installed; a diff of one file, or of generated files only, has no story.

The story is the commits' own, the one told ahead for the branch's commit (below): what isn't committed yet is never sent to Haiku, so editing tells no new story. The files changed only since the last commit go in a last page, "Not committed yet", with no why; a file changed both in a commit and since stays in its committed page, the last page showing it too, and its notes are left out, being for the lines as committed. When the commits have no story (one file, Claude Code not installed, Haiku failing), they go first in a "Committed" page, so the uncommitted one stays last; with nothing committed yet, the "Not committed yet" page stands alone, with no cover and nothing asked of Haiku. `R` puts what is uncommitted now in its page, keeping the story; after a new commit, the new commits' story is told, with no cover over the diff already open.

While Haiku reads, the diff waits under a cover, blank for its first moment so a story already known shows with no words flashing past. Then the cover shows how many files and lines it is reading, the time taken against about how long it should take, and each page as Haiku names it. `t` shows Haiku's answer itself as it comes, and `t` again the pages; with Settings' "Diff story progress" (`diff_story_progress`) off, the cover shows only a spinner. `Esc` or `q` takes the cover down and shows the diff at once, in the kinds' order, with "telling the story" under the list's heading until the story comes; the list then takes the story's order and keeps the file you are on.

Each page heads its first file in the list with its number, title and a why of two or three sentences, which presents and defends the page to the colleagues reviewing it: the problem it solves, the approach, and the alternative passed over and why, only where the commits or the plan give one. Under the why, a ⚠ line says what a skeptical reviewer should watch for (an edge case, a changed behavior, a missing test), when anything stands out; with UX 2.0 it sits under the why in the page's heading over the diff, and `yS` and `switchboard diff-story` include it. The bar over the diff names the file's page before its path; in a list narrower than about 48 columns the heading keeps only the why's first sentence, and the whole why follows the path in that bar. A file sits in the page that holds most of its changed lines. With Settings' "Diff story notes on changes" (`diff_story_notes`) on, Haiku's note sits over each change worth one, saying why it is needed, and a change belonging to another page than its file's is headed with that page's number and title.

In the list, `]c` and `[c` go to the next page and the one before, and in a story told as a map to the next chapter and the one before (below). `]p` opens the first page alone, a diff of its own holding only the files that page touches, as if it were a commit, the list headed "Page 1 of N"; `]p` and `[p` turn the pages, and `[p` from the first goes back to the whole diff. On a page, the changes of other pages in its files are dimmed. Settings' "Diff story a page at a time" (`diff_story_pages`) off makes `]p` and `[p` do nothing. With no story, `]c` in the list, `]p` and `[p` say why in a box: it is still being told, it is off in Settings, the diff changes only one file, a merge conflict is unresolved, or what went wrong when Haiku was asked. When Haiku's answer named changes the diff doesn't have, a box says the story may be thin.

### The story as a map

With Settings' "Tell the diff's story as a map" (`diff_story_map`) on, Haiku tells the change as a map of what builds on what rather than as one list. It tells one story in chapters, each made of pages: the first chapter is what the change exists for; another chapter for each part of the change that hangs together and that nothing in the first needs; and "Side work", for cleanup, renames, formatting and drive-by fixes. Each page says which pages it builds on, each chapter marks the page it exists for (its goal), a change is listed again on a second page that can't be read without it, and each page lists the things in the code it adds or changes (functions, types, tables, components, settings) with what each uses. Pages are counted within their chapter: a page of the same chapter is named by its number alone ("needed by pages 2 and 5"), one in another chapter by both ("chapter 2, page 1"). A story told as a list before the setting was turned on is told again the first time, since what Haiku is asked is part of what a story is kept under; one told before chapters and pages had these names reads as it did.

The links are Haiku's: nothing in the code is read to check them, since what a name or a line means is each language's own rule, so read the map as Haiku's account of the change. A thing Haiku named stays on its chapter's map with the change it named for it, and an arrow between two things only where both are listed.

The diff then has two modes. Map takes the diff's whole width, with no list beside it (boards 134 and 135). Across the top, the chapters' strip names each chapter with its number and how many of its pages are read ("1 Show signups on the map  3 of 5 read"), the one shown lit; at its right, what the change is compared with and its files, and an accent "In the files only · N ›" chip for the files no page tells (what isn't committed yet, generated files, changes Haiku left out), which opens Files mode on them. Under it is the shown chapter's map: a box for each page with its number, title and how many files and changes it has, the goal at the top of chapter 1, an arrow from each page down to the pages it builds on, dashed where the link is a guess, a ✓ on the pages you have read, and "next" on the first one not read yet; then a legend row, and the chosen page: "Page n of m" and its title, what needs it, its files and changes at the right, and its why. `j` and `k` move down a level to what the page builds on and up to what needs it, `h` and `l` across a level, and `Enter` or a second click on a box opens the page; the wheel reads the chapter from the goal down. `]c` and `[c` show the next chapter and the one before, as does a click on the strip, and `Tab` gives the strip the keys, one stop, `h` and `l` moving between its chapters and on to the chip, `Enter` on the chip opening its files, `Tab` or `Esc` handing the keys back to the map. The footer names `]c [c` while the story has more than one chapter. `]p` and `[p` turn to the next page and the one before, read from the goal down, past a chapter's last page into the next chapter's first (`]p` on the map opens the chosen page, `[p` from chapter 1's first goes back to the map).

An open page shows alone, as `]p` shows one, with its own map over the diff: the things it adds or changes as boxes, an arrow from each to what it uses. `Tab` from the list reaches that map, `j` and `k` move between its things, and `Enter` or a click on one goes to the change that holds it. Under the page's files the list names what it builds on and what needs it. A change shown from another page sits under a dim "also in page N" line ("also in chapter 2, page 1" from another chapter), its file marked "also N" in the list; `Enter` or a click on that line goes to the page where the change has its note. `Esc` in the list goes back to the map. A page is read once it has been opened, and the pages read are kept with the story.

Files mode lists the change by chapter, then page, then file, as board 136 draws it: each chapter's number, name and pages read, its pages under it counted in the chapter, their files under them, and the pages no chapter holds under In the files only; over the diff, the shown file's place ("Chapter 1 · page 4 of 5" and its title). `]c` and `[c` in the list go to the next chapter's first file and the one before's. `g m` shows the map from anywhere, `g f` shows the chosen page's first file in Files mode, and `m` or a right click opens a menu of the two (`/` searches it), which goes back to where each mode was left. With easy mode on, a Map / Files switch leads the row of buttons. The diff opens in the mode used last, Map the first time a story is told as one. With the setting off there is no Map mode, and the story is one list of pages.

`yS` copies the story as Markdown, to share: the story under `#`, each chapter under `##` with its map as a Mermaid block, each page under `###` with its number in its chapter, title and why, its "Builds on" line, its files, and each change's note under its file as `path:line`; `switchboard diff-story` prints the same. `M-p`'s "Copy the diff's story (M-d) as Markdown, to share it" presses it, and is listed only while the diff is open.

A story is kept in Switchboard's cache (`~/.cache/switchboard/diff-story/`) under the diff's text, the commits' messages and the version of what Haiku is asked, so the same diff opens on its story at once and any change to it is a new one. Two diffs asked for the same story at once share one telling.

With Settings' "Diff story told ahead" (`diff_story_ahead`) on, when a tab's branch has a commit whose story isn't told yet, it is told in the background, so `M-d` opens on it at once; an `M-d` opened meanwhile follows that telling on its cover rather than asking again. It tells one branch at a time and each commit once, tries a failed one again after half an hour, and counts the default branch too, whose commits not yet pushed are its diff. It runs only with Files in reading order, Diff told as a story and GitHub on, and Claude Code installed, and sends each new commit's diff and messages to Haiku, a run taking up to five and a half minutes.

`M-p`'s "Clear this tab's diff cache (M-d): the story, file order and PR base kept for its branch" removes, after a yes, what the diff keeps of the tab's branch: its story, Haiku's order of its files (of its commits, and of the diff to the working tree as it is now) and the PR base kept for the worktree, so the next `M-d` asks again. It works from any pane of the tab, the diff open or not, in the directory the tab is named from, so Notes beside it clears the tab's work, and a box says how many files went. Nothing is told again until `M-d` opens. A story being told right now is kept, since its telling would keep it again, and the box says so; the base is found as `M-d` finds it, never asked of GitHub with GitHub off, and when the change can't be read (no base, a diff git won't give) the box says so and why rather than reporting the story and order cleared.

### The branch's commits one at a time

`c` goes through the branch's commits one at a time, oldest first, so the branch reads in the order it was written: each commit's own change against the commit before it, in the diff's place, the list headed with "Commit 2 of 5", its hash and its subject. `]p` goes to the next commit and `[p` to the one before; `c`, `q` or `Esc` in the list go back to the whole branch, where you were. A commit has no story of its own, and `ga` asks only about the whole branch (in a commit it says so). A working tree's changes have no commits to go through.

### Asking about the change

`ga`, in the list or the diff, asks Claude a question about the change (Settings' "Ask Claude about the diff", `diff_ask`). A box opens in the middle of the diff to type it in: `Enter` asks, `Shift-Enter` or `Ctrl-j` starts a new line, a question pasted in keeps its line breaks, `←` `→`, `Home` `End` (`Ctrl-a` `Ctrl-e`), `Backspace` and `Delete` move and edit in it, `Ctrl-u` clears it, and `Esc`, `Ctrl-c`, Cancel or a click outside closes it. The answer streams into a window on the right half, titled with the question, each file Claude opens named on a line of its own. When the diff is open in place of a pane running Claude, the question goes to a copy of that tab's own session (Settings' "Ask in the tab's session", `diff_ask_session`, on by default), resumed and forked from the directory the session started in (`claude -p --resume <id> --fork-session`), on the session's own model and with its own instructions, so it knows the conversation that made the change: why it was built so, what was tried, what the plan said. Its answer's first line says so ("Asked in the tab's session (Opus)"). It is given the story, the diff, where your cursor is and the question; the tab's conversation is left as it was, and the copy is deleted once it has answered. With the setting off, a pane with no Claude, or a session that can't be resumed (the answer then saying "Asked a fresh Haiku: this tab's session couldn't be resumed" first), Haiku is asked instead, the answer's first line saying so: it is given the story, the diff as the story read it, where your cursor is (the file, the line and its change), and the question, and keeps no session, each question standing alone. Either runs with only the tools to read, search and list files, so it can read the repository to answer and change nothing. This sends the diff and your question to Claude. `M-p`'s "Ask Claude about the diff (M-d), with its story, reading the repository as needed" presses `ga`, and is listed only while the diff is open.

| Key, in the answer | What it does |
|---|---|
| `j` `k`, `↓` `↑` | The line cursor down or up |
| `Ctrl-d` `Ctrl-u` | Half the window down or up |
| `gg` `G`, `Home` `End` | The first or last line |
| `gf` | The `path:line` on the cursor's line, in the diff, when the change has that file; a file of the repository the diff doesn't change opens in a pane below the diff at that line, in your `$VISUAL` or `$EDITOR` (given `+<line>`), else as plain text (`switchboard view`); a click on a `path:line` does the same |
| `ga` | A new question |
| `v`, then `y` | Select lines of the answer from the cursor, and copy them |
| `yy` | Copy the answer |
| `yS` | Copy the story |
| `q`, `Esc` | Close it, stopping Haiku if it is still answering |

Its Close, Ask again and Copy buttons do the same, the wheel scrolls it (the line cursor kept in sight), and a click outside it closes it.

### The pane beside its diff

`M-p`'s "Show the pane a tool stands in for (the diff, lazygit) beside it, on its left" brings the pane the diff stands in for back on the diff's left, half the width, so a Claude session and its diff sit side by side, and "Show a tool in place of a pane alone again, full width" puts it away again. Both are listed only where a tool stands in for a pane. `M-d` in either pane closes the diff, and the pane takes back its whole place. A restart saves the pane in the diff's place, so a Claude session waiting behind it is resumed.

### lazygit in a pane's place

`M-p`'s "Open lazygit in place of this pane, or quit it; quitting brings the pane back" runs [lazygit](https://github.com/jesseduffield/lazygit) in the pane's directory in its place, as `M-d` puts the diff there, the pane waiting behind it until lazygit quits. The command again, in lazygit or in the pane beside it, sends lazygit `Ctrl-c`, its own quit, rather than `q`, which would be typed into a commit message. lazygit is a program of its own, not part of Switchboard: where it isn't installed, the command says so. Mission control's menu on a tab offers it too. Esc quits it at its top level, as q and Ctrl-c do: Switchboard passes lazygit its own settings as it starts it (`quitOnTopLevelReturn: true`, kept in `~/.local/share/switchboard/lazygit.yml`), naming Switchboard's and then your config in `LG_CONFIG_FILE`, so lazygit takes them as defaults for that run alone, what your config says winning (a `quitOnTopLevelReturn: false` of yours keeps Esc as it was), and your config file is never written. lazygit you start yourself in a shell reads only your own config.

### The file list

Settings' "Diff file list wide" (`diff_list_wide`) opens the list wide enough for its longest path, at most half the screen; `E` narrows it, and widens it again. `/` in the list filters it by path: a line at its top takes what you type, leaving only the files whose path holds every word typed, case aside, a word starting with `!` leaving out the paths that hold it (`src !tests/`), with how many are left of how many (`2 of 5 files`), the cursor on the first file left and its diff shown; `↑` `↓` move while you type, `Enter` keeps the filter and gives the keys back to the list, where `j`, `k`, `]c` and `[c` move only through the files left (`]c` saying so when no later chapter has one), and `Esc` while typing, or `/` then `Esc`, clears it, as `Backspace` widens it. A click on its line types into it again. The filter stays while the diff is open, `R` included; the next `M-d` starts with none. From the list, `J` and `K` scroll the chosen file without leaving the list, stopping at its start and end, where the diff itself reads the files as one scroll with UX 2.0. `yp` copies the cursor's `path:line` (on the side it is in, the new one from the list), and `yl` a GitHub link to that line at that side's commit, when `origin` is on GitHub; a file marked ● has no link from its new side, the file on disk, whose lines GitHub doesn't have yet, and `yl` says so. In a side of the diff, `v` or `V` starts selecting lines from the cursor, lit as the cursor moves; `y` then copies their text, whole lines without their numbers, and `yp` and `yl` the lines' place (`path:12-20`, `#L12-L20`), each ending the selection, as does `Esc` or `v` again. `v` selects in the file list too, where `y` copies the files' paths with the chapters' titles between them, and in an answer, where it copies the answer's lines. A drag of the mouse selects any text on the screen, by characters, within the part it starts in (the list, a side of the diff, the heading over it, an answer): a side's selection leaves out its line numbers and the other side, a line wrapped is copied as one, and the selection stays on its text while the wheel scrolls, the lines scrolled out of sight copied with the rest. A double click selects a word and a triple click a line. Letting the button go copies what is selected, saying how many lines, and `y` copies it again while it is lit; `Esc`, a click or any other key clears it, but a press on a scroll bar, which scrolls under it as the wheel does. A click on a file shows it, a click in the diff puts the cursor there (opening a fold it lands on), and the wheel scrolls the list or the diff under it, the list past the file chosen, which a key brings back in sight. The list and the diff each have a scroll bar at their right while they run past the screen (Settings' `scroll_bars`), a click on its track paging and a drag on its thumb scrolling; with UX 2.0's one scroll of files, a file not read yet counts by its changed lines until it is.

With Settings' "Easy mode" (`easy_keys`, under Help) on, the diff has a row of buttons at the bottom, each pressing its keys in the list: ◀ Chapter, Chapter ▶, ◀ Page, Page ▶, Widen, Copy link, Refresh, Ask (with Ask Claude about the diff on) and Close, followed, with "Key help line" (`key_help_line`) on, by a line naming the keys. The buttons are drawn as Switchboard's own are: bold in the accent on the panel's shade between rounded ends where the terminal draws them, on the chosen row's shade under the mouse with "Highlight on hover" (`hover_highlight`) on. Both take effect when the diff next opens. `m` in the file list, or a right click, opens the diff's menu, with or without the row: "Settings are in Switchboard's Settings (Diff)", which opens mission control's Settings, then what each button does.

### Keys in the diff

The keys depend on where the cursor is: in the file list, or in the diff on its right.

| Key | In the file list | In the diff |
|---|---|---|
| `j` `k`, `↓` `↑` | The next or previous file | The next or previous line, stopping at the file's first and last |
| `Ctrl-d` `Ctrl-u` | Half a screen of files down or up | Half a screen down or up |
| `gg` `G`, `Home` `End` | The first or last file | The first or last line |
| `]c` `[c` | The next or previous chapter (in a story told as a list, page) | The next or previous change |
| `]f` `[f` | The next or previous file | The next or previous file, the cursor on its first change |
| `Enter` | Into the diff | Open the fold under the cursor |
| `l`, `o`, `→` | Into the diff | `→`: from the old side to the new |
| `←` | | From the new side to the old, from the old to the list |
| `J` `K` | Scroll the chosen file five lines down or up, stopping at its start and end | |
| `m` | The diff's menu: its settings, then what the row of buttons does | |
| `Shift-F10` | The diff's menu | The diff's menu |
| `v` `V` | Select files from the cursor, for `y`; again to stop | Select lines from the cursor, for `y`, `yp` and `yl`; again to stop |
| `Esc` | End a selection, else close the diff | End a selection, else back to the list |

These work wherever the cursor is:

| Key | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Ctrl-f` `Ctrl-b`, `PageDown` `PageUp` | The diff a screen down or up |
| `Ctrl-e` `Ctrl-y` | The diff scrolled a line down or up, the cursor kept in sight while its file is |
| `zo`, `za`, `zv` | Open the fold under the diff's cursor |
| `zR` `zM` | Open every fold of the file, or fold them again |
| `Tab`, `Shift-Tab` | Between the list and the diff |
| `Ctrl-w h` `j` `k` `l`, `M-Left` `M-Down` `M-Up` `M-Right` | The list or side of the diff that way, else the workspace's pane on that side (the sidebar past the list), as `M-Left` and the rest move between panes |
| `Ctrl-w w` | The next window of the diff |
| `]p` `[p` | The next or previous page of the story, or commit of the branch's |
| `g m` `g f` | The story's map, and the chosen page's file in Files mode |
| `m` | The view's menu: the map or the files |
| `]c` `[c` | In Map mode, the next chapter's map and the one before; in Files mode's list, the next chapter's first file and the one before's |
| `c` | The branch's commits one at a time, and back to the whole branch |
| `R` | Read the change again, edits made since it opened included |
| `E` | Widen or narrow the list |
| `y` | Copy the text selected, by `v` or with the mouse |
| `yp` | Copy the cursor's `path:line`, or the lines selected (`path:12-20`) |
| `yl` | Copy a GitHub link to the cursor's line, or to the lines selected |
| `yS` | Copy the story as Markdown |
| `ga` | Ask Claude about the change |
| `q`, `Ctrl-c` | Close the diff |
| A number | Repeats the move that follows (`5j`) |

Under the cover, `Esc` or `q` shows the diff and `t` switches between the pages and Haiku's answer. While the change is still being read, `q` or `Esc` closes the diff, and when it can't be read, any key does. A message box in the diff ("Copied …") closes on the next key or click, which goes on to do what it does (`Esc` only closes it), or by itself after five seconds.

### The diff by hand

`M-d` runs `switchboard diff` in the pane's place, and it runs in any terminal too:

```
switchboard diff [dir]                  # the branch in dir (else here) against its PR's base, as M-d shows it
switchboard diff --base origin/develop  # against another branch or commit, used as given, nothing fetched
switchboard diff --working [dir]        # the changes not yet committed, staged and not
switchboard diff --ahead [--plan <note>] [dir]  # tell the story and keep it for M-d, drawing nothing
```

With `--ahead` it compares with the base `M-d` would (`--plan` naming the tab's plan note, a path in the notes directory, which the server passes with Diff story reads the tab's plan on), and exits 0 once the story is told (or there was none to tell), or 2 with why on its error output; a second run on the same commit does nothing.

`switchboard diff-story [--path] [dir]` prints the story `M-d` tells of the branch's change as Markdown, as `yS` copies it, so the tab's Claude can read the story you are looking at; Switchboard's instructions to Claude name it, and Claude Code may run it without asking. Without a directory it reads the pane's, as `M-d` would, else the current one. It only reads what the diff kept: the base `M-d` last compared the branch's commit with, never fetched, and the story kept under the change's key, never asked of Haiku, so with neither it prints "No story told for this change yet: open M-d and press its story key." and exits 0; while Settings' "Diff told as a story" or "Files in reading order" is off, it says `M-d` tells no story. With "Uncommitted changes in M-d" on, the files changed only since the last commit follow in a last "Not committed yet" chapter, as `M-d` shows them. `--path` prints the story's file in `~/.cache/switchboard/diff-story/` instead.

### The diff alone

The diff is also an app of its own, `switchboard-diff`, for a terminal with no Switchboard running: the same program and the same keys, in any git repository.

```
curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/cquinones100/switchboard/main/install.sh | sh -s -- --diff
switchboard-diff [dir]                  # the branch in dir (else here) against the default branch, or the branch it was cut from
switchboard-diff --base origin/develop  # against another branch or commit
switchboard-diff --working [dir]        # the changes not yet committed
```

It has a version and releases of its own, apart from Switchboard's (tagged `diff-v<version>` in the same repository): each holds `switchboard-diff-<version>-<target>.tar.gz` with its checksum and its signature by the same release key, which the install line checks before it puts `switchboard-diff` in `~/.local/bin`. `switchboard-diff update` installs a newer one (below). Alone, it knows no pull request, so it compares with the default branch, or with the branch its own tracks when that is another one, tells its story through `claude -p` directly (and without Claude Code lists the files by kind and sends nothing), follows its own settings, kept in `~/.config/switchboard-diff/settings.json` apart from Switchboard's, and keeps its stories in `~/.cache/switchboard-diff`. It has no list of trusted directories, so it reads any repository as your own code: open someone else's pull request with `wtpr` and `M-d` instead. [Its README](../crates/diff/README.md) has the rest.

Alone, the diff heads its screen with a line of its own (its name, the repository and what the branch is compared with, and its version at the right) and always has the row of buttons at the bottom, with a Settings button before Close. `M-m`, that button, or "Settings" in the menu `m` opens shows its settings view in the diff's place: each of the diff's own options a row with its name, its key and whether it is on, the chosen one opened to its whole description and its default, the same names and words as Settings' Diff rows in Switchboard, which a test keeps the same. `j` `k` or the arrows move, `Enter`, `Space` or a click on the chosen row turns it on or off, and `Esc`, `q` or `M-m` goes back to the diff. A change is written to the diff's own `~/.config/switchboard-diff/settings.json` at once (only what differs from its default, the version before kept as `settings.json.bak`), never to Switchboard's, and the diff follows it at once: the story dropped and no longer told with "Diff told as a story" off, the change read again for "Uncommitted changes in M-d". The options that are about a workspace (the story told ahead, the tab's plan and session, the share beside a narrow pane) are left out. In Switchboard there is no such view and no header, and `M-m` stays the workspace's.

Once a day (an hour after a run closed before GitHub answered), after it has drawn, `switchboard-diff` alone asks GitHub for its newest release, keeping the answer in `~/.cache/switchboard-diff/update.json`; the request names nothing of yours. When one is out, the header says so at its right end (`0.2.0 is out · switchboard-diff update`) and the settings view has a row that installs it. "Check for a newer switchboard-diff" (`diff_update_check`, in that view alone) turns the look off. Only a release build looks; one built from a clone doesn't. `switchboard-diff update` downloads the newest release's archive for this computer, refuses it unless it matches the SHA-256 published beside it and is signed with Switchboard's release key, runs the new binary once and puts it in the old one's place; `--check` only says what is out, and a binary built from a clone is replaced only with `--force`. In Switchboard none of this runs: `switchboard update` covers the diff built into it.

`switchboard diff` and `M-d` are this same program, loaded by the workspace as its first plugin (the plugin API, `crates/plugin-api`), which answers what it asks: the pull request's base, your settings, the request audit, the pane beside it. The notes app is the second ("Notes alone").

## The notes app

The notes app keeps your notes as Markdown files and shows them in the terminal the way the macOS Notes app does, edited with Vim's keys.

```
switchboard notes
```

On the left is the sidebar, a list of folders, tags and notes; on the right, the note you had open when you last quit, or the newest when that one is gone. A note is a Markdown file, drawn the way Notes shows it: markup is hidden (it shows only on the line you are typing in), a title stands out from headings and subheadings, list markers show as • and –, checklist items as ☐ and ☑, a quote with a bar, a table with box lines, and pictures under their lines. The first run makes `~/Notes` with a welcome note showing everything a note can do.

Notes are kept in `$NOTES_DIR`, or `~/Notes`, one Markdown file each, in folders that are directories, nested as deep as you like. A note's title is its first line, and its file is named for it and renamed when the title changes. A title emptied or deleted, in Notes or by a write from outside, is put back as the note saves (a title being typed over is left until you leave Insert mode), so a note is never written without its title nor renamed after its first item; a title retyped, as a heading or as plain words, is the new title. A note saves itself a moment after each change and whenever you leave it. Pinned notes and the note open last are listed in `.notes.json` there, and deleted notes go to `.trash/`, the Recently Deleted folder. Nothing else is stored, so any editor can open them.

Each note's last line is its ID, `<!-- note-id: n-… -->`, which the app never shows, keeps last and puts back if it goes; it gives a note one when it makes it or first opens it, without changing when the note was last changed, and a copy of a note's file, which carries the original's, one of its own, saying so once (of two notes holding one ID, the one changed later is the copy). The ID survives a rename or move anywhere (`mv`, Finder, git, a Claude editing the note), so what keeps a note by it, `wtn`'s worktrees and autopilot, still finds it; `wtn` and autopilot give a note one too when it has none, and another editor shows it as an HTML comment. Titles, previews and searches leave it out.

Notes save themselves as you type, so two apps editing one note would write over each other. With Settings' "A Notes app in each tab" (`notes_per_tab`) on, any number of apps run on a notes directory, and each note is open for editing in one at a time: the app that opens it first holds its lock (`.locks/<the note's ID>` in the notes directory, with its process ID and pane), and another app opening the same note shows it read-only, its last line dim, saying "Open in tab 3, read-only here · Enter or a click: go there"; Enter, or a click on that line, goes to that tab, and once that app opens another note or quits, the note becomes editable here without opening it again. Trying to edit it there (typing, a paste, a key that changes the text) changes nothing and opens a box saying "Plan is open in tab 3, so it's read-only here", with "Go to tab 3" chosen as it opens, "Take it over", and "Keep reading" (Esc); it opens again on a later try.
