"Take it over" asks tab 3's app, over its socket, to save the note and let it go, so it shows read-only there, naming this tab, and the note is yours to edit here at once ("Took it over from tab 3"); when that app doesn't answer within 2 seconds, Notes says so and the note stays read-only. Changes saved in one app reach the others as changes made from outside, merged as any are, and `.notes.json` (the pins, the recent notes, the jump list) is read and written again under a lock of a moment, so no app's write drops another's. With it off, one app runs on a notes directory at a time, holding `.notes.lock` there with its process ID; a second one says the notes are open already and closes with `q`. A lock left by an app that has gone is taken over either way.

A note changed from outside the app, by a skill, a script or another editor, shows the change while it is open, without a save or a keypress: a watch on the notes directory brings it in. If you have typed something not yet saved, the two are merged line by line, so changes to different lines, even next to each other, both stay; where both changed the same line, both versions are kept, yours first, and the app says so. An item ticked from outside moves under the ones left to do, as ticking it in the app does. A note renamed or moved outside the app while open (`mv`, Finder, `git mv`: its file moved, not copied) is followed by its stable ID: the note window and the sidebar go on with it at its new path, its pin and the tabs linked to it follow, and typing not yet saved is merged into it there; its file is named for its title from then on, as every note's is, so a rename that left the title as it was is undone at the next save. A note deleted outside the app while open (gone, or moved to Recently Deleted) isn't written back: what is on screen stays, unsaved, and the app says so.

Nothing in a note is sent anywhere. Squashing notes, after you say yes, is the one exception in the app, and it sends no note's ID. The question of which note is a tab's ranks the notes on the computer, and a note's new tab tells Claude only the note's title and file. The `/note-tickets` and `/note-watch` skills read notes only when you start them, and only the notes you name or confirm. `M-d`'s story reads the tab's plan note only with Settings' "Diff story reads the tab's plan" (`diff_story_plan`) turned on.

### In the sidebar

| Key | Does |
| --- | --- |
| Enter, or a click | open a note, or show a folder's notes; on Recent's "all 20 ›", every note opened lately, newest first, in Ctrl-F's box ("Open a recent note" in the palette, from a note too) |
| `j` `k`, the arrows, `gg` `G`, Ctrl-D Ctrl-U | move, half the sidebar at a time with Ctrl-D and Ctrl-U |
| `n` / `N` | new note in the folder shown, its title ready to type / new folder |
| `dd` or `D` | delete, to Recently Deleted; from there, for good, after a question |
| `r` | restore, in Recently Deleted |
| `p` | pin or unpin |
| `m` | move to a folder |
| `R` | rename a note (its title, so its file, as typing a new title on its first line does) or a folder |
| `s` / `S` | mark notes with a ✓ / squash the marked ones into one |
| `t` | the note's tab (see below) |
| `/` | search every note, by every word you type; each note shows the line that matched |
| `x` | clear the search or tag |
| Ctrl-F | find a note (in a note too) |
| Ctrl-B | put the sidebar away, for the note to have the room; Ctrl-B in a note shows it again |
| Ctrl-Shift-P | every command, in a palette (in a note too); in the workspace, the workspace's palette, as `M-p` |
| Tab, or Ctrl-W `l`, or `M-Right` | go to the note; Ctrl-W `h` or `M-Left` goes on to the pane on the sidebar's left, Ctrl-W `j` `k` or `M-Down` `M-Up` to the pane below or above |
| `q` / `?` | quit / these keys |

The sidebar has Recent, the last 8 notes you opened, newest first, each with its folder dim at the right (Settings' "Recent notes in the sidebar", `notes_recent`), then Folders (Notes for the top folder, which it opens on, your folders, Recently Deleted, each with its count), then the notes in what is chosen: pinned first, then newest first, each with when it changed and how it starts, and in a search or a tag's notes, which folder it is in. `.notes.json` keeps the last 20 notes opened, a deleted one left out. There is no list of every note or of the tags: `/` searches every note, and a `#tag` followed in a note, or typed into `/`, lists the notes that have it. The wheel scrolls it.

**Squashing.** Mark two or more notes with `s`, then `S` merges them into one. After you say yes, a headless Claude (Sonnet, with no tools and no session kept) merges them, keeping every item, box and link, the note changed last winning where two disagree and nothing says which is right. A box shows it at work with the seconds so far; Esc in it cancels, leaving the notes as they were. The new note goes in the first one's folder, with an ID of its own, the originals go to Recently Deleted, and every tab linked to one of them links to the new one; autopilot, on one of them, its skipped items and its tabs take the new note's ID in place of theirs. It needs Claude Code.

**Renaming and moving.** A note renamed with `R`, its title typed over, moved with `m`, squashed, or in a folder renamed takes the tabs linked to it along, and a tab named after the note takes its new title.

### In a note

The note is edited with Vim's keys, with the settings a note had before: Space is the leader, which the app takes for its styles; indenting is two spaces; search ignores case unless you type a capital; and a yank or delete reaches the system clipboard, which `p` reads back.

| | Keys |
| --- | --- |
| Modes | `i` `a` `I` `A` `o` `O`, `R`, `v` `V` Ctrl-V, `gv`, `gi`, Esc |
| Motions, each with a count | `h` `j` `k` `l` and the arrows, `w` `W` `b` `B` `e` `E` `ge` `gE`, `0` `^` `$` `g_`, Home, End, `gg` `G`, `f` `F` `t` `T` `;` `,`, `{` `}`, `%`, `H` `M` `L`, `+` `-` `_`, `gj` `gk`, Enter where there is nothing to tick, fold or follow, Shift-Left and Shift-Right by words |
| Operators, with a count, a motion or a text object | `d` `c` `y` `>` `<` `g~` `gu` `gU`, doubled for whole lines (`dd`, `cc`, `yy`, `>>`, `<<`, `g~~`, `guu`, `gUU`) |
| Text objects, after `i` or `a` | `w` `W`, `"` `'` `` ` ``, `(` `)` `b`, `[` `]`, `{` `}` `B`, `<` `>`, `p` |
| Changes | `x` `X` `s` `S` `C` `D` `Y` `p` `P` `J` `gJ` `r` `~`; `Ctrl-A` and `Ctrl-X` add to and subtract from the number under or after the cursor, with a count; `.` repeats the last change, a count before it replacing the change's count (`3.` after `dw` deletes three words, and `.` after that three again) |
| Undo | `u`, Ctrl-R; each note keeps its history, and its folds, while the app runs, going to another note and back included, a change made to its file meanwhile one more step to undo |
| Changes visited | `g;` back and `g,` forward through the places the note's changes left the cursor, with a count; a change on the same line as the newest takes its place |
| Marks | `m` and a letter `a`–`z` sets a mark, `` ` `` and the letter goes back to it and `'` to its line, as a motion too (`d'a`, `` y`a ``, a selection grown to it); ``` `` ``` and `''` go back to where the cursor last jumped from; each note keeps its marks while the app runs, each moving with its line and gone with it |
| Jumps | Ctrl-O goes back and Ctrl-I or Tab forward, with a count, through the places the cursor jumped from: a search, `n` `N` `*` `#`, `gg` `G`, `:<line>`, `%`, `{` `}`, `H` `M` `L`, a mark, a link followed and a note opened from another (from the list, the finder, a link or the workspace), across notes; the list, and where Notes ended, is kept in the notes directory's `.notes.json`, so Notes opened again (in another tab, after a restart) goes back through it, a note since deleted passed over; past the oldest or the newest, nothing moves |
| Search | `/` `?` `n` `N` `*` `#` |
| Scrolling | Ctrl-D Ctrl-U, PageDown PageUp, Ctrl-E Ctrl-Y, `zz` `zt` `zb` |
| Folds | `za` `zo` `zc` `zM` `zR` |
| Commands | `:<line>`, `:s/a/b/g` and `:%s/a/b/g`, `:w`, `:q`, `:wq` or `:x`, `:noh`; `ZZ`, `ZQ` |
| Insert mode | Ctrl-W, Ctrl-U, Ctrl-H, Backspace, Delete, the arrows, Home, End; Ctrl-R then `"` `0` `+` or `*` types the register (which is the clipboard) and Ctrl-R `/` the last search; Ctrl-O runs one Normal mode command and goes on typing; Ctrl-T and Ctrl-D indent and outdent the line; Ctrl-V then a key types the key itself |
| Replace mode | typing replaces the character under the cursor, Backspace puts back what was there, Enter still breaks the line, Esc ends it; a count (`3R`) types what was typed again |
| Visual mode | the motions and text objects grow the selection; `d` `x` `c` `s` `y` `>` `<` `~` `u` `U` `J` `p` `r` `o` act on it, `v`, `V` and Ctrl-V switch between characters, lines and a block, Ctrl-C copies it. On a block (Ctrl-V) they act on the rectangle, `>` and `<` shifting at its column; `I` and `A` type before or after it on every line, `$` stretches it to each line's end, `O` goes to the other corner on the same row, and `p` puts a block cut or yanked as a column |
| Work | `gw` on an unticked checklist item at the top of its list starts work on it in a new tab, as `M-I` does (see [Starting work from the workspace](#starting-work-from-the-workspace)); with UX 2.0, Enter on an item's `Plan: [[…]]` line shows its plan beside the note and, again, starts it, `M-e` in Notes' own tab shows or puts away the plan of the item under the cursor, `o` goes to its tab, and `ga` adds an item (see [UX 2.0](#ux-20)); `M-p` lists "Autopilot: work “<note>”" first (see [The palette](#the-palette)) |
| Windows | Ctrl-W `h` or `M-Left` (typing too) goes to the sidebar, saving the note and leaving Insert mode; Ctrl-W `l` or `M-Right` goes on to the pane on the note's right, and Ctrl-W `j` `k`, `M-Down` `M-Up` to the pane below or above, as the Alt-arrows move between panes |

Macros and named registers aren't there: `"+y` and the like yank to the one register, which is the clipboard. A drag with the mouse selects; a click puts the cursor there; the wheel scrolls. A note longer than the screen, and the sidebar, have a scroll bar at their right (Settings' `scroll_bars`), measured in the note's lines, a closed fold's left out; a click on its track pages and dragging its thumb scrolls. Ctrl-F and Ctrl-B take the place of Vim's page down and page up in a note; PageDown and PageUp still page.

**The Format menu.** Everything a note can do is in the Format menu, Ctrl-P, which acts on the selection when opened from Visual mode. A key, Enter or a click picks an item; Esc or `Q` closes it. A style picked there leaves you typing in it, unless it was for a selection; opened while typing, the menu goes by where you were typing. The common styles also have a leader key (Space, then the key), which styles in place:

| Key | Style | Written as |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `1` `2` `3` `0` | Title, Heading, Subheading, Body | `# ` `## ` `### ` |
| `m` | Monospaced block | between ```` ``` ```` fences |
| `8` `-` `7` | Bulleted, dashed, numbered list | `* ` `- ` `1. ` |
| `l` | Checklist | `- [ ] `, `- [x] ` once ticked |
| `q` | Block quote | `> ` |
| `b` `i` `U` `x` `h` | Bold, italic, underline, strikethrough, highlight | `**b**` `*i*` `<u>u</u>` `~~x~~` `==h==` |
| `` ` `` | Monospaced text | `` `code` `` |
| `k` | Link | `[text](https://…)` |
| `t` | Table | rows of cells between bars |

The menu also has, under their own keys there, the checklist's Tick or untick (`c`), Move checked to bottom (`s`), Uncheck all (`u`), Delete checked items (`D`) and Start work on the item in a new tab (`w`, as `gw`); Paste image (`v`); and a table's Add row (`r`), Add column (`C`), Delete row (`R`) and Delete column (`X`); and, with no key, the note's Attach this note to a tab (see "A note's tab" below).

A line style applied again turns the line back to body text, as in Notes. A character style works as it does in Notes too: on the selection; off again, for the whole run, in text that has it already; on the word under the cursor; and anywhere else (a space, an empty line, right after a word you typed) on what you type next, starting Insert mode between its markers, which go again if you type nothing. Underline and highlight, which Markdown has no syntax for, are written the way Markdown tools usually write them.

- **Checklists.** Click a ☐, or press Enter on its line (but on a web link, an image or a `#tag` there, which Enter follows), to tick it. A ticked item is dimmed and struck through with everything indented under it, and moves under the ones left to do, with its details and sub-items, a blank line between, so a list leads with what is left; sub-items sort the same way under their item.
- **Lists.** Enter in Insert mode carries a list on (a checklist item comes unticked, a number counts up) and ends it on an empty item; Tab and Shift-Tab indent and outdent an item.
- **Tables.** In Insert mode, Tab moves to the next cell, past the last one adding a row, and Shift-Tab back. A table lines up again whenever you leave Insert mode or change it, measured as it shows, so bold markup doesn't throw a column out.
- **Collapsible sections.** Enter on a title, heading or subheading, or a click on its ▾, folds what is under it, up to the next heading of the same or a higher level; a folded one shows ▸ and how many lines it hides. Vim's `z` commands work too.
- **Enter.** In Normal mode, Enter does the first of these that applies: a web link, an image (under the cursor or just right of it) or a `#tag` under the cursor is followed, on a folded line too; a closed fold opens; a checklist item is ticked or unticked; with UX 2.0, on an item's `Plan: [[…]]` line, its plan shows beside the note, and Enter there again while it shows starts work on the item; any other `[[Note title]]` link is followed; a heading's section folds or unfolds; else the cursor moves down a line. A click keeps its own rules: the ☐ ticks, the ▾ folds, and a link is followed.
- **Links.** Enter, or a click, opens a web link in the browser (in its site's Chrome profile, as every link Switchboard opens), a `[[Note title]]` link in the note with that title (made if there is none; drawn as missing until then), and a `#tag` in the sidebar. Typing `[[` offers every note's title.
- **Tags.** Any `#word` after a space, not in code, is a tag. Following one lists the notes that have it under its name, and `x` in the sidebar goes back to the folder shown.
- **Line numbers.** The note's lines are numbered from its first line, a folded section's hidden lines counted as in Vim. With Settings' "Relative line numbers" (`notes_relative_numbers`) on, they are numbered by their distance from the cursor's line instead, the cursor's line showing its own, as Vim's `relativenumber`, for jumps like `5j`.

**Diagrams.** A ```` ```mermaid ```` block (a flowchart, with its subgraphs, or a sequence diagram) is drawn as its diagram in characters while the cursor is outside it, taking the block's place as a closed fold takes its section's, and shows its code while the cursor is in it, so you always edit the code; `j` onto it from above goes to its first line, and a click on it goes there too. A diagram wider than the note, even drawn top to bottom, is cut at the note's edge with a `›` on each row cut and a dim line under it. A kind it can't draw (a pie chart, a class or state diagram, a Gantt chart) keeps its code, with a dim line under it saying so. The Format menu's Show diagram code (in `⋯` and in the palette) shows the note's blocks as code until its Show diagrams, while Notes runs. With Settings' "Draw Mermaid diagrams" (`notes_mermaid`) off, every block shows as its code.

**Pictures.** Ctrl-V while typing pastes the image on the clipboard into the note, as in Claude Code (Settings' "Ctrl-V pastes an image", `notes_ctrl_v_image`), and so does the Format menu's Paste image; with no image there, Ctrl-V does what it always does (the terminal's own paste key pastes only text). The image is saved beside the note in `.attachments/<note>/` in its folder, which the sidebar leaves out, and written as `![name](path)` on a line of its own below the cursor's, indented under the item when the cursor is on a list or checklist item: the image goes with the item, and Claude, working the note's items, reads it with them. On Linux, pasting an image needs `wl-paste` (Wayland) or `xclip` (X); under WSL it takes the picture on Windows' clipboard (a screenshot, an image copied in a browser, or a picture file copied in Explorer, read from the file) through Windows PowerShell. Windows Terminal keeps Ctrl+V as its own paste, which pastes text (a copied file's path), so there use the Format menu's Paste image. The picture is drawn in the note under its line (Settings' "Images drawn in a note", `notes_images`) in a terminal that speaks the kitty graphics protocol with Unicode placeholders, Ghostty or kitty, or in one that gets sixel by Settings' "Image protocol", Windows Terminal by default ([Pictures in Windows Terminal](#pictures-in-windows-terminal)); elsewhere it shows as its name. The multiplexer places the picture like text, so it scrolls with the note and a box over it covers it, and passes the picture's graphics commands to your terminal (or, with sixel, reads them and draws the picture itself) from the notes app's pane alone, since one can have the terminal, or the workspace, read any of your files. A picture other than a PNG is made one in Switchboard's cache first. Enter or a click on it shows it full size over the notes pane, drawn by the workspace with kitty graphics (or as sixel, with sixel chosen), Esc, q or Enter going back (Settings' "Enter shows a picture full size", `picture_full_size`); where the terminal doesn't draw the workspace's images ("Images in the workspace", `server_images`), or with the setting off, it opens in Quick Look on macOS, or in the desktop's viewer on Linux.

**The palette.** In the workspace, Ctrl-Shift-P opens the workspace's palette, as `M-p` does, which with Notes in front lists Notes' commands first (see [The palette](#the-palette)). Run alone, Ctrl-Shift-P, in a note or the sidebar, lists every command Notes has, each with its key, the ones picked last first: the sidebar's (on the note open, from a note), the Format menu's, folding, and Switchboard's for Notes: moving Notes beside your work (`M-e`), the note's tab, starting work on the checklist item under the cursor in a new tab (`gw`), with UX 2.0 the item keys (its plan, its tab, Add an item) and, while a plan shows beside the note, "Show the note beside its plan", choosing a tab's note, reading a link from the note beside it, searching the tabs, notes, Linear, GitHub and Slack (`M-F`), and "Shape the note's checklist items as Linear tickets", which opens a tab running `/shape` for each unticked item. Settings' "Notes command palette" (`notes_palette`) turns it off.

**The finder.** Ctrl-F, in a note or the sidebar, finds a note: every note but those in Recently Deleted, pinned first and then newest, each as its title, its folder and how it starts, any of which you can type to match, with the note shown beside the list. Enter opens it; Esc closes the finder.

**The toolbar.** Over each note is a row of buttons for the common styles (bold, italic, strikethrough, monospaced, title, heading, bulleted list, checklist, table, link, image) and `⋯` for the whole Format menu. A click runs one on the selection, or else on the word or line the cursor is on, and the button under the mouse lights up (Settings' "Highlight on hover", `hover_highlight`). In a narrow window the last buttons drop off, `⋯` always kept. Settings' "Toolbar" (`notes_toolbar`) hides it.

**Easy mode.** Settings' "Easy mode" (`easy_keys`, off at first) is for anyone who doesn't know Vim's keys. Shift with an arrow, Home or End selects, and so does a drag with the mouse; typing replaces the selection, and Backspace deletes it. Cmd-S saves, Cmd-Z undoes, Cmd-Shift-Z redoes and Cmd-X cuts, and Ctrl-C copies the selection. A line at the bottom names the keys that work in the mode you are in (Settings' "Key help line", `key_help_line`), and the Format menu, the palette, the finder and the app's questions get a × in their top-right corner that a click closes. Vim's keys keep working beside them, and the setting takes effect when Notes next opens.

The Cmd keys need your terminal to send them as Ctrl+Alt+Shift keys in the kitty keyboard form (`CSI <code>;8u`), since a terminal otherwise reads a Cmd key as its own or as Alt: Cmd-S as `CSI 115;8u`, Cmd-Z as `CSI 122;8u`, Cmd-Shift-Z as `CSI 121;8u` and Cmd-X as `CSI 120;8u`. In Ghostty, let `switchboard setup terminal` add them, or add to its config:

```
keybind = super+s=csi:115;8u
keybind = super+z=csi:122;8u
keybind = super+shift+z=csi:121;8u
keybind = super+x=csi:120;8u
```

The multiplexer passes those four keys only to the notes app's pane while it runs in easy mode, and drops them anywhere else, so no other program reads them as Ctrl keys.

### An item's plan beside the note

With UX 2.0 on, Enter on an unticked item's plan link (its `Plan: [[…]]` line), `M-e` in Notes' own tab on such an item, or a click on its "plan" chip shows the plan in a panel beside the note, as board 8 draws it, rather than opening the plan note in the note's place. The sidebar is put away while it shows and the note narrows, its items keeping their chips (a tab's mark and number, "needs a choice", "next in the queue" for an item autopilot has put next); the plan takes the rest, its header "Plan <name>" with its folder at the right, then its sections as text (`Today` and the rest), an open choice (`## Decision needed`) as a card for each option side by side, the recommended one's edge in the accent with "recommended" at its right, the option's mock inside (a fenced ```` ```mock ```` block under the option, which `/plan-item` writes when the options differ in how something looks) and its words under it, and a keycap to choose it, then the steps.

Ctrl-W and the M-arrows move the keys between the note and the plan, as between Notes' other windows, and past the plan to the workspace's pane beside Notes. While the plan has the keys, the terminal's cursor is in it, on the line it was on last (its first the first time), and the note shows none: `j`/`k` and the arrows move it a line, Ctrl-D and Ctrl-U half the panel, `g` and `G` to the top and the bottom, the plan scrolling to keep it in sight (the wheel over the plan scrolls it, taking the cursor along), and a click on a line of the plan puts it there; Ctrl-W back to the note puts the cursor back where it was there. In the plan, the option's letter (`a`, `b`) or a click on its card chooses it, and only there: in the note, the letter is Vim's. The cursor on a card lights it, Tab moves between the cards, and Enter on one chooses it, `e` opens the plan note in the note's place, and Esc closes the panel. Choosing an option ticks it in the plan, renames `## Decision needed` to `## Decision` and the steps' heading to `## Steps (<letter>)` (saying, when the steps were written for another option, that they need rewriting before work starts), and turns the item's "(needs a choice: …)" into "(chose <letter>)". Enter on the plan link of the item whose plan shows, or in the plan with no card chosen, starts work on it as `gw` does, through the "Work on this item" box (see [Starting work from the workspace](#starting-work-from-the-workspace)). Esc in the note closes the panel too, and `M-e` again puts it away (on a line with no plan it says "No plan for this line"); beside another tab `M-e` still sends Notes home. The footer names the keys of the side that has them, leaving the letters and `e` off it: in the plan, Enter (`choose A` on a card, else `start the item`), Esc and `C-w h note`; in the note, `C-w l plan`.

The note and the plan share the width as board 8 draws them, the plan never narrower than 34 columns, its text wrapped at 100. As Notes narrows (board 45), the plan's header drops its folder first, then the cards drop their mocks, then they stack; narrower still, the plan takes the whole width, and "Show the note beside its plan" in the palette (and `M-p` while Notes is in front) shows the note in its place, the same command or Esc showing the plan again. It has no setting of its own: with UX 2.0 off, Enter on an item ticks it as it always did, and the plan note opens from the sidebar or its link.

### Notes in the workspace

Each tab has a Notes app of its own (Settings' "A Notes app in each tab", `notes_per_tab`, on by default). `M-e` starts one in a pane to the right of the one you are in, showing that tab's note, and pressed again in that tab closes it; every other tab's Notes stays open where it is, so switching tabs never takes a note away. `M-N` goes to the app in Notes' own tab, or opens one there, and a note opened from a notification opens in that one. Each app listens on its own pane's socket (`notes-<pane>.sock` in the state directory), and a note open in one tab shows read-only in another (see [The notes app](#the-notes-app)). When the last of a tab's own panes closes, its Notes closes with it.

With the setting off, there is only ever one Notes in the workspace, and the rest of this section describes it.

`M-N` (Alt-Shift-N) goes to Notes wherever it is open, or opens it in a tab of its own, named Notes. `M-e` brings that same Notes into a pane to the right of the one you are in, or starts it there; pressed again in that tab, or `M-w` on Notes there, it goes back to a tab of its own rather than closing. While it is away, its own tab shows which tab has it ("Notes is beside tab 3"), so the tab stays in the sidebar: Enter there goes to that tab, and `M-e` brings Notes back. When the last of a tab's own panes closes, Notes goes back to its own tab rather than stay there alone. A note opened from a notification, or from anywhere but `M-e`, opens in Notes' own tab, Notes going home first when it was beside a tab. While Notes is beside a tab's pane, that tab says "✎ Notes here" in the sidebar and on its mission control card.

Beside another tab's pane, Notes' sidebar is put away, so the note has the room (Ctrl-F still finds a note), and it comes back in Notes' own tab (Settings' "Hide the sidebar beside a tab", `notes_hide_sidebar`).

**A tab's note.** Beside a tab, Notes shows that tab's own note, the first of these whose file is there: the note you linked to the tab yourself (the question below, M-p's "Choose this tab's note", or a note's tab picked or opened from Notes' `t`); on a tab autopilot opened, the plan note its item links (`Plan: [[…]]`, found once as the tab opens); on a tab autopilot opened, the note autopilot is working through, while it is on; then the one its Claude works from (`/note-watch`, `/queue-and-clean` and a tab started on a note's item record it), else the last one opened beside that tab (a note Notes only carried over from the tab before isn't taken as this one's). Opening another note while beside the tab records it as the last one there, which doesn't outrank the first three: choosing the tab's note is how a tab gets one above them. Notes says which it showed on its message line ("The note linked to this tab", "This tab's plan", "Autopilot's note"), so a note shown by that order isn't taken for one you chose. Settings' "M-e opens the tab's plan or autopilot's note" (`note_by_precedence`) turns the first three off, back to the tab's note or the last one shown there. A tab with no note of its own is asked which it is: every note, the most recently changed first, each with how long ago it changed at the right (`2h`, `3d`), and what you type keeps the matches newest first rather than best match first, so the note you changed today stays above an old one whose name matches more tightly: the notes whose names hold the words you typed as written, newest first, then those matched only as letters scattered through the name. Above them, under "Suggested" (the list itself under "Every note", with how many there are; UX 2.0 board 126), are at most three notes the tab is likely about, so Enter takes the first: the note last shown beside it, then notes that mention its ticket or PR number, those whose titles share words with the tab's name, and those naming its repository; each is in its place in the list below too, and typing leaves the suggestions out. The other lists of notes ("Start work on a note's checklist items", shaping a note's checklist, autopilot's "Work which note continuously?") keep their order as you type the same way. "Keep whatever is open", last, stops it asking for that tab, and Esc leaves the question for next time. M-p's "Choose this tab's note, the one M-e shows beside it" asks again any time, and Settings' "Ask for a tab's note" (`ask_tab_note`) turns the question off. Each Claude session that starts in a tab with a note of its own is told the note's path and to read it before its first reply (by the plugin's `switchboard claude-context`, below, so only while Switchboard loads as a plugin), so a standing rule kept in the note reaches every session in the tab, whatever repository it works in; Settings' "Point Claude at the tab's note" (`tab_note_context`) turns that off.

**A note's tab.** `t` in the sidebar, or M-p's "Open the tab for the note in Notes" (for the note Notes shows), goes to the tab linked to the note. With none, it asks which tab is the note's: one of the open tabs, linked to the note then, or a new one, in a directory you pick as `M-t`'s does, linked to the note and named after it. The new tab's Claude starts knowing which note it is for: its title and file are added to its system prompt, so no turn is spent on it, and the note itself is read when you ask, or before the first reply while "Point Claude at the tab's note" is on (Settings' "Tell a note's new tab its note", `note_tab_prompt`). Either way Notes moves beside the tab's Claude showing the note, as `M-e` puts it there, the cursor left in Claude's pane (Settings' "Notes beside a note's tab", `note_tab_beside`).

"Attach this note to a tab", in Notes' palette (Ctrl-Shift-P), its Format menu (Ctrl-P, under Note) and `M-p` ("Attach the note in Notes to a tab"), makes the same link without going anywhere, whatever tab the note has: it lists every open tab with its number and name and, dim at the right, the note it has now (`note: <name>`), the tab this note is attached to marked `attached`; `/` or typing filters it. A tab with no note takes the note at once ("Attached Plan to tab 3"). A tab that has another note asks first, "Tab 3 has Ideas. Attach Plan to it in its place? [Y/n]", and no leaves both as they are. A note is one tab's: attached to another tab before, it leaves that one, which the question says under it ("Plan will no longer be tab 1's") and the message after ("Attached Plan to tab 3, no longer tab 1's"). Picking the tab it is already attached to says so and changes nothing. With no tab linked, `t`'s list is headed with the command's name, since picking a tab there attaches the note too, but replaces a tab's note without asking and goes there.

These need the workspace: in a plain terminal `switchboard notes` runs alone, and its palette leaves the workspace's commands out (a key for one says it needs the workspace).

The workspace reaches the running app through a socket, `~/.local/state/switchboard/notes.sock` (or `$NOTES_SOCKET`), which you can use from a shell too:

```
switchboard notes open <note>       show a note, by its path in the notes directory
                                    (with no app running, the note it opens with)
switchboard notes sidebar on|off    show or put away the sidebar
switchboard notes shown             the note shown, as its path
```

### Notes alone

The notes app is also an app of its own, `switchboard-notes`, for a terminal with no Switchboard running: the same program and the same keys, on the same notes (`$NOTES_DIR`, else `~/Notes`).

```
curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/cquinones100/switchboard/main/install.sh | sh -s -- --notes
switchboard-notes                  # the app, on the note open last
switchboard-notes open <note>      # show a note in the running app
switchboard-notes sidebar on|off   # show or put away the running app's list
switchboard-notes shown            # print the note the running app shows
```

It has a version and releases of its own, apart from Switchboard's (tagged `notes-v<version>` in the same repository): each holds `switchboard-notes-<version>-<target>.tar.gz` with its checksum and its signature by the same release key, which the install line checks before it puts `switchboard-notes` in `~/.local/bin`. Run the line again for a newer one. Alone, it has no workspace to ask, so what needs one is left out: there is no tab's note, no tab to go to, no work started on an item (`gw` says it needs the workspace) and no chips for the tabs working on one, and `Ctrl-Shift-P` lists none of those commands; a link followed says it can't be opened; pictures stay the lines that name them; and what you copy goes to the terminal's own clipboard, while `p` puts only what was yanked in the app. It follows what you set for Notes in `~/.config/switchboard/settings.json` when you have one, and keeps its socket and the palette's picks in `~/.local/state/switchboard-notes`. Squashing notes still asks Claude through `claude -p`, after a yes. [Its README](../crates/notes/README.md) has the rest.

`switchboard notes` and `M-e` are this same program, loaded by the workspace as a plugin (the plugin API, `crates/plugin-api`), which answers what it asks: your settings, the server's commands, the desktop's clipboard and viewer, a link opened in its site's browser, how a picture reaches your terminal, and which line of a note work can be started on.

## Setting Claude Code up

Switchboard learns what each Claude Code session is doing from Claude Code's own hooks, and gives it a statusline, its own instructions (how to read the tabs, the notes app) and a set of skills. `switchboard setup` puts them in place. The install script runs it once the binary is in place, `switchboard update` runs it after installing a release, and `M-p`'s "Run Switchboard's setup" runs it in a tab of its own, to put in place what isn't or to answer a question you said no to.

```
switchboard setup                  the guided setup
switchboard setup --check          say what would change, and change nothing
switchboard setup claude skills    only those parts, asking nothing
switchboard setup --parts          list the parts
switchboard setup --refresh        bring the parts already set up up to date, quietly
switchboard setup --move           move Switchboard into its plugin or back into ~/.claude, as the setting says
switchboard setup slack-user       store a Slack user token of your own, for the Slack view's reminders, read marks and reads
```

### As a plugin, or in ~/.claude

Switchboard's hooks, instructions and skills are a Claude Code plugin, `~/.local/share/switchboard/claude/plugin`, which only the Claude in Switchboard's panes loads: every pane's environment names it in `CLAUDE_CODE_PLUGIN_DIRS`, which Claude Code 2.1.280 and later read, so Claude started anywhere else has your own hooks, rules and skills and none of Switchboard's. The plugin holds every skill below, a `hooks/hooks.json` with the hooks under [Claude Code's settings](#claude-codes-settings), and a hook as each session starts, resumes, clears or compacts that runs `switchboard claude-context`, which gives an interactive session Switchboard's instructions and the path of its tab's note to read (and a headless `claude -p` nothing, by Claude Code's `CLAUDE_CODE_ENTRYPOINT` and the session's file). The server writes the plugin as it starts (but one with a state directory of its own, `SWITCHBOARD_STATE_DIR`, which shares your home) and `switchboard setup` in every mode, each file only when it differs, so a new build's arrives with its first start. Switchboard's skills are then `/switchboard:<name>`, and `/<name>` too while no skill of yours or the project's has that name. Its statusline comes from the plugin's hooks module, `hooks/switchboard.js`, which `hooks/hooks.json` lists under `modules` (what Claude Code runs as a mod; it runs one a plugin, so this one imports the statusline's file, `hooks/statusline.js`, and the permissions', `hooks/permissions.js`): as Claude Code reads its settings, the module puts `switchboard statusline` over what it read, in memory, so a session in Switchboard's panes draws Switchboard's statusline whoever starts it, every other Claude draws yours, and no settings file changes. Settings' "Switchboard's statusline in its panes" (`pane_statusline`) off has the module leave the statusline alone, so the panes draw yours too, and mission control's Usage view gets nothing from them; the module asks `switchboard setting pane_statusline` as Claude Code reads its settings, so a Claude started after the change takes it. Claude Code runs an administrator's managed plugins before yours, and one that answers its settings without passing them on skips the module, so the pane draws no statusline: when a pane's Claude finishes a turn and its statusline still hasn't drawn, Switchboard says so once, in a box naming the tab, and `switchboard setup` reports it while the server knows of one; turning off "Load Switchboard as a plugin" brings the statusline back, since the old way puts it in `~/.claude/settings.json`, which such a plugin doesn't skip. Its permissions reach the panes without a settings file too ([Claude Code's settings](#claude-codes-settings)): each skill allows the commands it runs itself, in its `allowed-tools`, for the turn that runs it, a subagent it starts and each tick of a `/loop` running it, and the plugin's module, from a second file, `hooks/permissions.js`, hooks Claude Code's permission decision to allow the few of Switchboard's own commands its instructions have Claude run on any turn and to refuse reading `.env` files and `git pull`. Settings' "Switchboard's permissions in its panes" (`pane_permissions`) off has it leave every call to Claude Code, asked once in each session; the skills' own allow their commands either way. The headless `claude -p` runs Switchboard starts for itself from a pane (the diff's story and questions, the connectors, Notes' squash, today's todo note, the Slack relay's summaries, `codex-review`'s Claude reviewer) leave the plugin out of the variable, keeping any other directories you set, so its hooks don't speak for the tab.

Settings' "Load Switchboard as a plugin" (`claude_plugin`) is on for a new install. Off, setup puts Switchboard in `~/.claude` as earlier versions did, for every Claude of yours: the hooks in your settings, an import of its instructions in your CLAUDE.md, and its skills in `~/.claude/skills`, and the panes name no plugin, which would run the hooks twice. Changing the setting in Settings (or with `switchboard setting claude_plugin on`) runs `switchboard setup --move`, which moves them: into the plugin, it takes out of `~/.claude` exactly what Switchboard put there, each part only when there is something of it to take out, and back, it puts all three in. A pane started after that follows the setting; a Claude already running keeps what it loaded. A tab already open keeps the environment its shell started with, so a `claude` started again there has none of Switchboard's hooks, skills or instructions after a move into the plugin, and runs every hook twice after a move back, until the tab is reopened or the workspace restarts (`switchboard kill-server`, then `switchboard`); what setup says after a move ends by saying so. Editing the setting by hand moves nothing until setup runs.

An install an earlier version made has Switchboard's hooks, import or skills in `~/.claude` and no choice in the setting. The guided setup asks it once, "Load Switchboard as a plugin in its panes?": yes moves it over, and no keeps it there, recording the setting off. The first run of a new build (below) never moves anything by itself: it turns the setting off for such an install, so the panes don't load the plugin on top of the hooks still in your settings, and notes that you haven't been asked (`~/.local/state/switchboard/claude-plugin-unasked`), so the guided setup still asks, once Claude Code can load the plugin; your answer there, or changing the setting yourself, takes the note away. Claude Code older than 2.1.280 can't load the plugin, so with one setup keeps Switchboard in `~/.claude` whatever the setting says, turns the setting off and says why; once Claude Code is updated (`claude update`), turn it on in Settings. It does the same where Claude Code's managed settings, which an organization puts on the computer (`/etc/claude-code/managed-settings.json` on Linux, `/Library/Application Support/ClaudeCode/managed-settings.json` on a Mac), set `disableSideloadFlags`: with that, Claude refuses to start when its environment names a plugin directory, as every pane's would; if the policy is lifted, turn the setting on in Settings. Mission control's suggestions may offer it too, once you use the Claude tabs a lot.

As a plugin, a Claude session outside Switchboard runs none of Switchboard's hooks, so the Slack relay hears only of sessions in the workspace, not of one in a terminal of its own; with the setting off, every session's hooks tell it, as before.

### The guided setup

Alone, it is the guided setup. When the tmux version of Switchboard left anything behind (below), it offers to tidy it first. It checks that `switchboard` is on your PATH, since Claude Code's hooks and statusline run it by name, and that git is there for the worktree commands, the diff and the tabs' branches, writes the plugin, which is Switchboard's own and never asked about, and checks that Claude Code is installed; without Claude Code it says how to install it and goes on to the terminal. With it, it says whether Claude Code is too old for the plugin or its managed settings keep one from loading, asks an earlier install whether to load Switchboard as a plugin, and takes each part of that way in turn. The first time, it asks before the part changes anything of yours (`[Y/n]`: Enter is yes, and a closed input is no, so nothing piped or unattended says yes); once a part is set up, it brings it up to date without asking, and taking out what an earlier version put in `~/.claude` isn't asked about either. As a plugin it adds nothing to your `~/.claude/settings.json`: it says that the plugin draws Switchboard's statusline in its panes (or that Settings turns it off), and takes out the hooks and statusline an earlier version added there, keeping a statusline of yours. A `~/.claude/settings.json` that links to a file that isn't there leaves Claude Code none of your settings, so it is said as left to do, with `switchboard setup claude` when it is the tmux version's link into its clone's `claude/settings.json`, which that part makes a file of your own from what it held. After Claude Code's parts it looks at the terminal (Option as Alt, below) and, where lazygit is installed, says that it gets Switchboard's settings at launch (below), and then says which optional parts are there, each for what it names, with how to set up the ones that aren't: the GitHub sign-in (`M-v`, the PR views, PR numbers in tab names), the Linear key (`M-i`, the Tickets and Inbox views, ticket titles) and the Linear team `/shape` files tickets in (`linearTeam` in `linear.json`), your calendar's address (the Calendar view), the Slack relay's tokens and owner (`M-S`), Chrome (the PR and ticket views as images, `/walkthrough`'s videos), ffmpeg and Node (`/walkthrough`, `/deploy-test`, `/preview-test`), git-delta, Node and Chrome (`/diff-screenshots`), and Codex (`/review-loop`'s reviewer; Claude reviews without it). GitHub and Linear are left out while Settings turns them off. It ends with the optional parts not there named on one line, then what is left to do, every ✗ again, or "Ready." Running it again changes only what isn't in place yet, and each part says what it changed or that it was up to date. With parts named, it does only those, asking nothing, the way the setting says.

| Part | What it does |
|---|---|
| `plugin` | Writes Switchboard's plugin, which the Claude in its panes loads, to `~/.local/share/switchboard/claude/plugin` |
| `claude` | With the plugin off, adds Switchboard's hooks, statusline (in its panes, before a statusline of yours that runs everywhere else) and permissions to `~/.claude/settings.json`; with it on, which carries the hooks, statusline and permissions itself, adds nothing and takes out the hooks and statusline an earlier version added |
| `claude-md` | Writes `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` to import your rules, and Switchboard's part with the plugin off; with it on, takes out the import an earlier version added |
| `skills` | Writes Switchboard's skills into `~/.claude/skills` with the plugin off; with it on, takes out the ones an earlier version wrote. Either way, links `skill-path` and `codex-review` into `~/.local/bin` |
| `terminal` | Makes Option act as Alt in a Mac's terminal: Ghostty's config, Terminal's or iTerm2's default profile; in Ghostty's config, also sends Cmd-S, Cmd-Z, Cmd-Shift-Z and Cmd-X to Notes' easy mode |
| `lazygit` | Writes Switchboard's lazygit settings (`quitOnTopLevelReturn: true`) to `~/.local/share/switchboard/lazygit.yml`, which lazygit in a pane's place layers under your config, so Esc quits it there unless yours says otherwise; where lazygit is installed. Your config is never written |
| `leftovers` | Removes the tmux version's links that lead nowhere, putting back from its clone's history the files Claude Code still uses and the configs setup.sh linked, takes out its worktree lines in `~/.zshrc`, and takes out a `switchboard` function or alias that starts tmux |

### Option as Alt, lazygit, and the tmux version's leftovers

Every `M-` key needs a Mac's terminal to send Option as Alt ([Option as Alt on a Mac](#option-as-alt-on-a-mac)). The `terminal` part sets it where it can: a Ghostty config of yours (`~/.config/ghostty/config`, or the one in `~/Library/Application Support/com.mitchellh.ghostty/`) gets `macos-option-as-alt = true` unless it already sets `macos-option-as-alt`, and the four `keybind` lines Notes' easy mode needs ([Easy mode](#in-a-note)) unless it already binds any of Cmd-S, Cmd-Z, Cmd-Shift-Z or Cmd-X itself or includes another config (`config-file`) or a theme of its own by its path, either of which may, and one is made when you run it in Ghostty with none; and when it runs in Terminal or iTerm2, that app's default profile is changed through macOS's own preferences (`defaults export` and `import`): "Use Option as Meta key" in Terminal, both Option keys sending Esc+ in iTerm2. Open a new window, or the app again, for it to take. The guided setup asks first, and for another terminal it says the line to add to its config. On Linux a terminal sends Alt as it is, so the part changes nothing there; under xterm the guided setup says to add `XTerm*metaSendsEscape: true` to `~/.Xresources`, and otherwise reminds you that a key your desktop takes for itself can be moved in `keys.json`.

lazygit in a pane's place is given `LG_CONFIG_FILE` naming Switchboard's copy of its settings and then your config, so yours wins where both say something. Your config is the files `LG_CONFIG_FILE` already names, else the first lazygit would read: `$CONFIG_DIR/config.yml`, then `lazygit/config.yml` in `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, which on a Mac without it is `~/Library/Application Support`, and in `~/.config`. Switchboard's copy is written as lazygit opens when it is missing or out of date, and by the `lazygit` part; a config an earlier version of the part wrote where lazygit reads its own is yours and left as it is. Switchboard offers no editor config: what it needs from a terminal is all setup writes of another program's, and lazygit gets its settings at launch.

A clone of the tmux version pulled past the rewrite leaves links into files it no longer has: the scripts setup.sh linked into `~/.local/bin` and `~/.config/tmux`, and `~/.claude/commands`, `~/.claude/hooks/*`, `~/.claude/statusline-command.sh` and `~/.claude/otel-headers.sh`. Its `~/.zshrc` lines, `eval "$(worktree-from-ticket init zsh)"` and the rest, are no longer needed, and fail on every new shell once its scripts are gone. The `leftovers` part removes each such link that leads nowhere into a Switchboard clone (a link that still leads somewhere, or anywhere else, is left alone). One Claude Code still uses, a link `~/.claude/settings.json` names (a hook's command, `otelHeadersHelper`; not the statusline, which the `claude` part replaces, with `switchboard statusline` the old way and with none as a plugin) or `~/.claude/commands`, which Claude reads by itself, is replaced with a copy of what it led to from the clone's git history instead, and the part says so; one the history doesn't have is left alone, and the part says that too. The configs setup.sh linked into the clone, which the rewrite removed (`~/.config/ghostty/config`, `~/.config/karabiner/karabiner.json`, `~/.pgclirc`, lazygit's `config.yml`, the `~/.config/nvim` folder), and any other link leading nowhere into a clone directly in `~`, in `~/.config` or `~/Library/Application Support` or a folder in either, or directly in `~/.claude`, are each replaced with what they last held in the clone's history, a folder as it last was, and the part names each; Ghostty's `command`, which attached to tmux, starts the `switchboard` that ran setup instead, named by its full path. A link into the clone's older name, `~/editor-configs`, is looked up in the history of the `switchboard` clone beside it, and removed when that history doesn't have it. It takes each of those lines out of `~/.zshrc` or `~/.bashrc`, with the comment right above it that names the function it defined, since every tab, menu and skill that runs `wt`, `wtn` and the rest defines the function itself; a line through the binary (`eval "$(switchboard worktree-from-ticket init zsh)"`) is yours, and left alone. The tmux version's `tmux_server` setting ran it on a server of its own, opened with `tmux -L switchboard new-session -A -s main`; a `switchboard` function or alias on one line that runs tmux, as that command was wrapped, would run in the binary's place in every new shell, so the part takes it out, with a comment right above it that names Switchboard and tmux (one that runs anything else is yours, and left alone). The file is changed through a link to it, with a copy as it was left beside it as `<name>.bak.<time>`. The guided setup offers it only when there is something to tidy.

### Claude Code's settings

With the plugin off, the `claude` part merges Switchboard's settings into your own `~/.claude/settings.json`; as a plugin it only takes out what an earlier version merged there. The file stays yours, never a link: Claude Code writes `/model` and "always allow" there, and reads no settings file after it. What is already there is kept, and a copy of the file as it was is left beside it as `settings.json.bak.<time>`. A file that isn't a JSON object is left alone, and the part says so.

- **Hooks**: one for each Claude Code event the workspace follows (`SessionStart`, `UserPromptSubmit`, `PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse`, `PostToolUseFailure`, `PermissionRequest`, `Notification`, `Stop`, `PreCompact`, `PostCompact` and `SessionEnd`), each running `switchboard hook <Event>`. They are how a tab knows its Claude is working, waiting on you or done; without them every tab shows idle and `M-a` has nothing to go to. As a plugin they are the plugin's, and the part takes out of your file any hook running one of their commands, or one the tmux version of Switchboard added, keeping your other hooks; with the plugin off it adds them, replacing a hook of yours running the same command and the tmux version's.
- **Statusline**: `switchboard statusline`, which shows the model in the project's accent color, the directory and branch when the session runs outside the workspace, and how full the context is, inside the workspace as outside it. With Settings' "Context in Claude's status line" (`statusline_context`) off, UX 2.0 on and mission control's Usage view on, the statusline under Claude's prompt leaves the context out inside the workspace, where the Usage view's Sessions and the tab's header show it. It also keeps each session's context use and spend for mission control's Usage view. As a plugin, the plugin draws it in its panes (above), and your statusline, whatever it is, is drawn everywhere else; the part takes Switchboard's out of your file, and the tmux version's (below), and takes the line before your command that the old way added off again. With the plugin off, a statusline of your own is kept for every Claude outside Switchboard's panes, `~/.claude/statusline-command.sh` included, the script Claude Code's `/statusline` writes, as a file or a link into your dotfiles: setup puts `if [ -n "$SWITCHBOARD" ] && command -v switchboard >/dev/null; then exec switchboard statusline; fi; ` before your command, so the panes run Switchboard's and the Usage view gets what it records, and a command that already runs `switchboard statusline` in them is left as it is. Only the tmux version's gives way to Switchboard's: a command running `~/.claude/statusline-command.sh` while that is still the link its setup made into a Switchboard clone's `claude/`, or, once the link is gone, the command its settings had, `bash ~/.claude/statusline-command.sh`, which then runs nothing.
- **Permissions**: what Switchboard's skills and loops need to run unattended (the `switchboard` commands they call, read-only `gh`, `git status` and `git fetch` alone or from `origin`, and the read-only tools of the Slack, Calendar and Linear connectors), and the denials: reading `.env` files, `git pull`, and the options that make `git log`, `git diff` or `git fetch` write a file, run a program or fetch from an address of its choosing (`--output`, `--ext-diff`, `--upload-pack`, `--multiple`). Anything that writes outside the computer is left to ask, and so is every script in the plugin's skills, since each takes a path or a file Claude writes. `git log` and `git diff` have no rule: Claude Code runs git's read-only forms without one, by its own checks, where a rule with a `*` would also allow their options that write a file or run a program. With the plugin off they are added to yours. As a plugin none are added, and those an earlier version added stay, since one of them can't be told from the same rule of yours (Claude Code's "always allow" writes them there too). Either way, the allow rules an earlier version added and Switchboard dropped (the tmux version's, `node` running any skill script, `git log`, `git diff` and `git fetch` with any arguments) are taken out of your file, each only as it was written, a new build's first run included; the panes get them through Claude Code's own permission decision instead, never a file:
  - Each skill carries the rules for what it runs in its `allowed-tools`, which Claude Code applies as it applies a rule of yours for the turn that runs the skill, a subagent the skill starts and every tick of a `/loop` running it included. A deny or ask rule of yours still wins.
  - The plugin's hooks module, from its file `hooks/permissions.js`, allows the few of Switchboard's own commands its instructions have Claude run on any turn (`switchboard tabs`, `show`, `set`, `setting relay_*`, `slack-relay sends`, `diff-story`, `skill-path`), only each alone, with no other command, redirect or expansion, and only when no rule of yours, hook or check of Claude Code's decided the call, so auto mode's classifier isn't asked about them either. It refuses the denials whatever decided: a `.env` file read with Read, Grep or Glob or by a shell command that shows a file (`cat`, `head`, `grep` and the like, or `< .env`), but not one copied or written to, `git pull` anywhere in a command, and `git log` or `git diff` given `--output` or `--ext-diff`, or `git fetch` given `--upload-pack` or `--multiple` (or the start of either, which git takes for it), anywhere in a word, and any of those three with an expansion (a `$` or backtick outside single quotes, an unquoted brace list, glob or tilde starting a word), whose words can't be read before it runs. Settings' "Switchboard's permissions in its panes" (`pane_permissions`) off has it leave every call to Claude Code.

Once all of it is there, running the part again changes nothing. A permission or hook Switchboard stops shipping isn't taken out of your file.

### Your CLAUDE.md

Switchboard's part of Claude's instructions is what every user's Claude needs to know about the workspace (the notes app and which note is the tab's own, `switchboard tabs` for what the other tabs are doing, how to talk to another tab and name one, a repository's own skills). As a plugin, the plugin's start-of-session hook gives it to the Claude in Switchboard's panes, and your CLAUDE.md doesn't import it. With the plugin off, the `claude-md` part writes it to `~/.local/share/switchboard/switchboard.md` and your `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` imports it with a line of its own, which Claude Code follows (`@path`). Either way a new version takes effect with the next session.

Your own rules come from where `~/.config/switchboard/claude.json` says, a git repository or a file, which Settings' "Your CLAUDE.md" row writes for you (and then runs this part):

```json
{"repo": "https://github.com/you/claude-rules", "file": "CLAUDE.md"}
```

```json
{"path": "~/somewhere/CLAUDE.md"}
```

A `repo` is cloned into `~/.local/share/switchboard/claude-md` and pulled, fast forward only, each time the part runs. On GitHub or a gist, gh's sign-in is added as the clone's credential helper when gh is signed in, so a private repository works. Without `file`, your rules are the repository's `CLAUDE.md` at its root, else the one nested least deep; none, or several at that depth, is an error that says so. Changing `repo` moves the old clone aside. With neither `repo` nor `path`, `~/.config/switchboard/CLAUDE.md` is used when there is one.

With rules named, `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` becomes a short file written by setup that imports your rules (and then, with the plugin off, Switchboard's part); edit your rules where they live, not there. A CLAUDE.md of your own in its way is moved aside to `CLAUDE.md.bak.<time>` first, but never by the move that follows a change of "Load Switchboard as a plugin", which asks nothing: that only adds Switchboard's import line to a CLAUDE.md of yours, or takes it out, and leaves a link of yours as it is. A rules file named that isn't there stops the part, naming the path. With no rules named, your own `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` stays as it is: with the plugin off it gets one line added, `@~/.local/share/switchboard/switchboard.md`, and with none, one is made holding that line. A `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` that linked into a clone of the tmux version has its rules copied to `~/.config/switchboard/CLAUDE.md`.

Moving into the plugin, the part takes the import line out of your CLAUDE.md, with the blank line setup put before it when it added it to the end of yours, and nothing else; a CLAUDE.md setup made to hold only that line would be left empty, so it is removed, as it was before setup made it. Then `~/.local/share/switchboard/switchboard.md` goes too, unless your CLAUDE.md is a link to a file that still imports it.

### Skills

As a plugin, the skills are the plugin's `skills/<name>`, which only the Claude in Switchboard's panes loads, as `/switchboard:<name>`, and `/<name>` while no skill of yours or the project's has that name. The `skills` part takes out of `~/.claude/skills` each skill an earlier version wrote there (in a folder holding `.switchboard-skill`, the files that list names, the list and the packages the skill installed beside them, such as `node_modules`; or a link into a Switchboard clone), so it doesn't take the bare name everywhere. A folder still holding a file you added there is left with it, which the part says: without its `SKILL.md` it is no skill, and with the plugin off Switchboard's is written into it again beside your files. The part keeps a skill of your own by the same name; the guided setup lists those, since they take the bare name over Switchboard's.

A build from a clone takes the skills from the clone's `assets/skills` rather than what it carries, as [Staying up to date](#staying-up-to-date) says, the server bringing the plugin's copies up to date within seconds of a change there.

With the plugin off, the `skills` part writes each skill the binary carries into `~/.claude/skills/<name>`, where Claude Code finds a personal skill. A later run brings each one up to date, removes a file a newer version no longer ships, and leaves alone what a skill made for itself, such as `node_modules` beside its scripts. Each folder Switchboard wrote holds `.switchboard-skill`, the list of the files it wrote. A folder without that list, or a link to somewhere else, is a skill of your own by the same name: it is kept, and the guided setup lists the ones it kept. An edit to a skill Switchboard wrote is written over on the next run, so to change one, delete its `.switchboard-skill` and it is yours. Once any skill is installed, the guided setup writes a new one without asking.

Either way, the skills' scripts and the files they share are run from the plugin, which is always there, and the part links `skill-path` and `codex-review` in `~/.local/bin` to the binary, which runs the command a link is named after, so a repository's own copy of a skill written for the tmux version, which runs them by those names, still finds them; a file of your own by either name is left alone, and a link leading nowhere (into a clone of the tmux version) is replaced.

A repository may have its own skill by one of these names, and Claude Code may run Switchboard's over it (a personal skill over a project's, the plugin's when it is named), so each of Switchboard's starts by running `switchboard skill-path <name>` and follows the repository's copy when there is one (`.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md` in the worktree, else in its main clone). A skill that hands off to another reads the file `skill-path` prints in the same way. Without a repository's copy, `skill-path` prints Switchboard's own, never a skill of yours that only shares its name, so `/switchboard:<name>` and a hand-off reach Switchboard's while yours keeps the bare name; a copy of Switchboard's you made yours, which still opens by running `skill-path`, counts as Switchboard's.

The ones marked "you" run only when you type them; Claude may also pick up the others on its own when what you ask fits.

| Skill | What it does | Started by |
|---|---|---|
| `/ci-loop` | Watches the branch's PR checks in the background and fixes what fails, pushing each fix, until every check passes; stops and asks when a fix isn't the branch's to make | you |
| `/clean-worktree` | Removes a worktree and its local branch once nothing in them would be lost (no uncommitted changes, every commit on GitHub), then prunes deleted remote branches | you |
| `/deploy-test` | Waits for a merged PR to deploy, tests its feature on the deployed site in a real browser, records a captioned video of the test and asks you to approve it | you |
| `/diff-screenshots` | Renders a branch's or PR's diff with git delta as side-by-side PNG screenshots, one set per file, core code first and tests last, and opens them (in Preview on a Mac); it needs git-delta, Node and Chrome | you or Claude |
| `/note-tickets` | Reads the open checklist items in the notes you name, checks Linear for tickets that already cover each, and suggests tickets for the rest, creating only the ones you pick | you |
| `/note-watch` | Watches one note for new checklist items while the session runs, and works each as it appears, ticking it when done | you |
| `/plan-item` | Adds a checklist item to the tab's note, its first line a title under 90 characters, `BUG: ` and what is broken or `FEAT: ` and what to build, and writes its plan note, which holds the detail, in the plans folder beside it, after checking the item isn't there already and reading the code it touches, in the shape `/wtn` and autopilot build from; Notes' M-Enter in "Add an item" starts it in a new tab | you, or Claude when you ask for an item |
| `/oncall` | Watches your team's Slack alert channels while you're on call, and reaches you through the Slack relay only about what needs you | you or Claude |
| `/preview-test` | `/deploy-test` before the merge: tests an open PR's feature on its preview deploy and records it | you |
| `/project-status` | Says where the session's project stands, from Linear's tickets, milestones and status updates and what the team said on Slack | you or Claude |
| `/queue-and-clean` | Queues the branch's PR for merge, fixes what stops it merging, tests the deploy, closes out the ticket and the note's item, and removes the worktree; in a repository that commits straight to its default branch, lands the branch there instead | you |
| `/resolve-conflicts` | Rebases the branch onto the remote's main and resolves the conflicts, carrying a rebase, merge or cherry-pick through to the end | you or Claude |
| `/review-loop` | Reviews the branch over and over with Codex (Claude where Codex isn't there), fixing what each round finds, until two rounds in a row come back clean | you or Claude |
| `/shape` | Shapes a Linear ticket with you: reads the code, checks for tickets that already cover the work, drafts the ticket and writes it once you approve | you |
| `/slack-updates` | Gathers what is new for you on Slack since the last look and saves it for mission control's Slack view, run every 15 minutes by the Slack starter routine | you or Claude |
| `/today` | Today's calendar as a table, with times, length, where to join and your reply, and saves it for mission control's Calendar view | you or Claude |
| `/walkthrough` | Records a captioned video of a feature in a real browser, or in Switchboard's demo with each key pressed shown, reached the way a person would reach it, and hands back an MP4 | you or Claude |
| `/wt` | Hands this tab over to `wt` for a ticket: the session ends, and the ticket's worktree opens in the same pane with a new Claude working on it | you |
| `/wtn` | The same for one checklist item of a note, through `wtn` | you |

Codex is used for one thing, reviews. `switchboard codex-review`, which `/review-loop` runs each round, has Codex review the branch when the `codex` command is installed and signed in (`codex login`); without it, Claude Code reviews in its place with the same prompt and the same JSON (see "Reviews" below).

## Worktrees

Each piece of work runs in a git worktree of its own, on a branch of its own, with Claude started in it: a Linear ticket, a checklist item of a note, a branch or a pull request. Five commands make or find one, and each prints a shell function for your shell's startup file:

| Function | Command | For |
|---|---|---|
| `wt` | `switchboard worktree-from-ticket` | a Linear ticket |
| `wtn` | `switchboard worktree-from-note` | a checklist item of a note |
| `wtb` | `switchboard worktree-from-branch` | any branch, new or existing |
| `wtpr` | `switchboard worktree-from-pr` | a GitHub pull request |
| `wtr` | `switchboard worktree-resume` | a worktree one of the others made, its Claude resumed |

### The shell functions

No program can change the directory of the shell that started it, so the commands alone make the worktree and start Claude in it but leave your shell where it was. A function runs its command, `cd`s into the worktree the command names, and starts Claude there itself, so your shell sits in the worktree for the whole session and after Claude exits, and a pane split from it opens there too. Nothing needs them in your shell: the workspace's keys, mission control's menus, autopilot, `/wt` and `/wtn` each define the function in the tab they open. Only to type them at a prompt yourself, add them to `~/.zshrc`:

```sh
eval "$(switchboard worktree-from-ticket init zsh)"
eval "$(switchboard worktree-from-note init zsh)"
eval "$(switchboard worktree-from-branch init zsh)"
eval "$(switchboard worktree-from-pr init zsh)"
eval "$(switchboard worktree-resume init zsh)"
```

For bash, put the same lines with `init bash` in `~/.bashrc`; zsh and bash are the shells `init` writes for. `worktree-from-ticket`'s `init` also defines `claude`, which in one of Switchboard's panes starts Claude with Switchboard's plugin as Settings' "Load Switchboard as a plugin" says now (`switchboard claude-plugin-dirs`), so a Claude typed in a shell opened before that setting changed loads it, or doesn't, as a new tab would; outside the workspace it is plain `claude`. Setup doesn't add these lines.

### Where the worktrees go

Each worktree goes in its repository's `.claude/worktrees/`, under the main clone, so one made from inside another worktree goes beside it rather than inside it. Add `.claude/worktrees/` to the repository's `.gitignore`. A different parent directory can be given first, relative to the main clone (`wt .worktrees ABC-123`). The main clone's `.claude/settings.local.json`, which git doesn't copy since it is ignored, is linked into each new worktree, so your local permissions apply there too; a worktree with its own is left alone.

Run again on the same work, a command reopens it: the worktree is reused if it is there, and the branch if it is there. `wt` and `wtn` refuse while Claude is already running in the worktree, naming that session, its title and the tab it runs in, rather than start a second one editing the same files and pushing the same branch. `wtb` and `wtpr` stop when the branch is checked out in your main clone, since git allows a branch in one worktree at a time, and use the worktree that already has it when there is one.


A worktree there removes itself once its work has landed and it is left alone: everything on its branch is on the default branch as origin last had it (its last commit there, as after a rebase and a push, or merging it changing nothing, byte for byte, as after a squash), nothing in it is uncommitted or untracked, nothing git ignores in it is of its own (a tool's output such as `node_modules`, a directory marked as a cache as cargo marks `target`, a link, or a file the main clone holds the same go with it; a local database or an `.env` of its own keeps it), no file is marked for git status to pass over (assume-unchanged or skip-worktree), no tab has it open, and git hasn't changed its index or HEAD for an hour (a commit, a checkout, a staged file; a read such as git log doesn't count). The workspace looks when a tab in one of a repository's worktrees closes, and every hour for each repository a tab has been in, after fetching its default branch (while Settings' GitHub is on). Removal is `git worktree remove` without `--force`, so nothing git would lose goes, and the branch always stays; a message names the worktrees removed. One on a detached `HEAD`, locked, or someone else's PR checked out untrusted is never removed. Settings' "Remove landed worktrees" (`sweep_landed_worktrees`) turns it off, and Reopen's menu still removes one by hand.

A new branch never starts from your main clone as it is on disk, which may be behind origin, on another branch, or carrying commits you haven't pushed, which would then land with the new work. `wt` names the branch as Linear does: a local branch of that name is the ticket's own work, reused as it is; one only origin has, from someone who started the ticket before or another computer, is fetched and checked out tracking it; otherwise the branch starts from origin's default branch as it is now, fetched first, with no upstream until its first push, as `wtn`'s does (below). A fetch of the default branch that fails is tried twice more, 5 then 15 seconds apart ("Fetching origin's main again"), as `wtn`'s is; when every try fails, the branch starts from origin's default branch as last fetched, said with when a fetch last moved it ("Starting from origin/main, last updated 2 hours ago: couldn't reach origin"), and the tab's Claude is told to fetch before its first commit and ask you before building on the ticket's branch should origin have one, else rebase onto the default branch; when the clone has a copy of the ticket's branch that origin couldn't be asked about, `wt` stops instead, so an outage neither hides work already started nor checks out commits whose authors couldn't be checked; with no copy of the default branch fetched before, `wt` stops rather than start from the clone. A repository with no origin starts from the clone's checked-out commit.

`wt`, `wtpr`, `worktree-done`, `wt-jump`, `codex-review`, `wait-ci` and `wait-deploy` are programs you run, so they ask GitHub and Linear whatever the `github` and `linear` settings say, as the tmux version's scripts did; the settings turn off what the workspace does by itself (its lookups, views and keys). `wt` and `worktree-done` read Linear with the key the rest of Switchboard uses: `LINEAR_API_KEY`, else the stored secret `linear-api-key`, else `linearApiKey` in `~/.config/switchboard/linear.json`. Make a personal API key in Linear (Settings, Security & access) and store it with `switchboard platform secret-set linear-api-key`, which reads it from stdin and keeps it in the Keychain on a Mac, or the desktop's keyring on Linux (with no keyring, in `~/.config/switchboard/secrets.json`, which only you can read). The same `linear.json` holds `linearTeam` and `linearLabels`, the team and labels `/shape` files new tickets with. `wtpr` reads GitHub with gh's token, `gh auth token`, as the rest of Switchboard does.

### Starting work from the workspace

| Key | What it does |
|---|---|
| `M-t`, `M-n` | New tab, running Claude, in a directory you pick |
| `M-W` | Start work on tickets in new tabs (`wt`) |
| `M-B` | Start work on a branch, new or existing, in a new tab (`wtb`) |
| `M-p`, "Start work on a PR in a new tab (wtpr)" | A PR, in a new tab running `wtpr` |
| `M-I` | A note's items, a tab each running `wtn` |
| `gw` in Notes, on a checklist item | That item, in a new tab running `wtn` |
| `M-p`, "Autopilot: work a note" | A note's items, a few tabs at a time, the next opened as each lands (below); while autopilot is on, listed as "Autopilot: work a note: show its board for <note> (m for its settings…)" |
| `M-p`, "Continue shaping Linear tickets, each in a new tab" | Tickets, a tab each running `shape --continue` |
| `M-p`, "Review a contributor's PR in a new tab (/review-pr)" | A fork's open PR, checked out untrusted, in a new tab about its worktree whose Claude runs `/review-pr` from the main clone; listed only in a repository with that skill |
| `M-o` | Reopen a closed tab or resume a past Claude session |
| `M-j` | Search and jump to a tab |

Each of the first six asks which directory first, in a list: the directories picked in any of them before, the most recent first (the last 50, kept in `~/.local/state/switchboard/work-recent.json`), then the one you are in, so with nothing picked yet Enter takes it, then every open tab's, then those Claude sessions ran in, most recent first, then your home, with worktrees left out but the one you are in. For the keys that make a worktree, each entry is its repository's main clone, listed once, and a directory in no repository is left out. Typing narrows the list; a path typed in is taken when nothing matches it, or at once with Alt-Enter (`~` is your home). Then:

- `M-t` opens the tab there running Claude, from an interactive shell of yours, which is left in the tab when Claude exits. Then a menu asks what to call it, which you may skip with Esc: `n` a name typed in, `t` its ticket and the ticket's title, or `s` "Shaping: " and a ticket, the form `/shape` gives a tab. Each ticket is the one the branch names, else asked for; `t` on a ticket's own branch leaves the tab to show its ticket and title by itself (Settings' "Name new tabs", `name_new_tabs`).
- `M-W` asks for the tickets, IDs or Linear URLs separated by commas or spaces, filled in from the clipboard when it holds only tickets. Every one is checked before any tab opens, so a typo leaves none half-started. The tabs go at the end of the tab bar in the order given, so no open tab is renumbered, and the first comes to the front.
- `M-B` lists the repository's branches, local and remote, the most recently committed first. Enter works on the one picked; a name no branch matches is a new branch, and Alt-Enter takes the name typed even when some branch matches it.
- "Start work on a PR" asks for its number or URL, filled in from the clipboard when it holds one. A URL names its repository, which `wtpr` finds a clone of, so it asks for no directory; a bare number asks which repository it is a PR of.
- `M-I` asks for the repository, then the note, the most recently changed first, then which of its unticked items to work on. Items under a section for parked work (`## Later`, `## Deferred`) and items a worktree already works on are left out, one whose plan still needs a choice is marked so, and one held by an open item its `After:` line names is marked "(waits on <item>)"; with more than one, the first row takes them all, and Tab marks a few (a `+` before each, Shift-Tab marking going up), which Enter then takes together, then which branch to land on ("Land on:", the same question autopilot asks, sharing its order: the branches picked there before in this repository, the most recent first, the default branch among them by when it was picked, then the default branch if it wasn't, so with nothing picked yet Enter lands there, then the repository's other local branches by name, then "Another branch…", a branch typed there counting as picked; a branch other than the default one runs `wtn --target <branch>`, as autopilot's tabs do). In a repository that lands through pull requests, which go to the default branch, a branch other than the default one is refused and no tab opens: Switchboard says "<branch> can't be landed on" and to land on the default branch, or to set `"landing": "direct"` for the repository in `~/.config/switchboard/repos.json`. The lower half of the list shows the item under the cursor whole, its full text and what is indented under it, since a row cuts it to one line (Settings' "Show a note's item whole in wtn's list", `wtn_item_preview`). Each item gets a tab, named after it and linked to the note: the tab bar shows the item's first 50 characters, cut at a word and ending in …, and the full title (`M-p`'s "Show this tab's full title"), the sidebar and mission control its whole first line, while the tab keeps that name; renamed since, it shows its new name.
- `gw` in Notes, on an unticked checklist item at the top of its list (the Format menu's "Start work on the item in a new tab" and Notes' palette have it too), starts that item the same way without asking for the note or the item: Notes saves the note, so the line it names is the one on screen, then the workspace asks for the repository, the one this note's items were last started in first (kept by the note's ID in `work-recent.json`, so Enter alone takes it), then "Land on:", and opens the item's tab in front.
