On a ticked item, one under another, one in a section for parked work or a line that is no item, Notes says why; an item a tab is already linked to asks whether to go to that tab, and one a worktree of the repository picked already works on is refused. A branch other than the default one is refused in a repository that lands through pull requests, as for `M-I`.

With UX 2.0 on, `gw` (and Enter on the plan link of an item whose plan shows beside the note) opens one box, "Work on this item", in place of those questions: the item with its plan's chip ("plan settled", "needs a choice" or "no plan"); the repositories as radio rows, the note's last one marked "the note's" and chosen first, then "Another directory…", which asks for one as `gw` did before (and is all the box asks when none is offered), scrolling with the focus or the wheel when they don't all fit; "Lands on", holding the branch last picked for the repository (empty for its default branch), its suggestion dim after what is typed and `→` taking it, with how it lands under it (straight on the branch through queue-and-clean, or through a PR); what Start opens (the tab's number, the worktree `wtn` makes, `wtn` on the item and its plan), or that a worktree of the repository has the item already; then "▶ Start it", the row it opens on, so Enter alone starts it, and, while autopilot works the note, "Next in autopilot's queue", which puts the item first among the items autopilot takes next until it lands (`switchboard autopilot-next <note> <line>` does the same from a script; an item that needs a choice isn't taken). `↑`/`↓` and Tab move between the rows, Enter or a click chooses one, and Esc cancels. With a tab on the item already, the box says so with the tab's mark, number and name, its one row "Go to it".
- "Continue shaping Linear tickets" asks for the tickets, then the directory, which must be in a repository so `shape` has code to read.
- "Review a contributor's PR" is listed only when the pane you press `M-p` in is in a repository whose main clone has a project skill named review-pr (`.claude/skills/review-pr/SKILL.md`, as Switchboard's own repository has), and asks for no directory: it lists that repository's open PRs from forks, the most recently updated first, each with its author, the drafts after the rest, dimmed, marked "· draft" and not picked (the moving keys pass over them to a PR that can be, Enter on one does nothing, and a draft's number typed, listed or not, says it can be reviewed once it is ready), a number typed taken too, then checks the PR out untrusted, as `wtpr --untrusted` does (in `~/.cache/switchboard/pr-review/<owner>-<repo>/<number>`, again on a later review), and opens a tab in front in the main clone, named `#<number> Review PR <title>` (the PR's title without control characters, or just `Review PR` for a number typed that isn't in the list), running Claude with `/review-pr <number>`. Its Claude stays in the main clone, never loading the PR's `CLAUDE.md`, settings, hooks or skills, while the tab is about the PR's worktree (its `@tab_dir` option): named, colored and given its PR from it, so `M-v`, `M-m` and `M-d` there show the PR's, `switchboard tabs` names the worktree, and the diff reads its files as text whatever its `.gitattributes` say. lazygit, which runs the repository's own commands, isn't opened there, and the diff's `ga` asks no question there, since Claude would start in the worktree. A split or new tab from it still starts in the main clone, and handing the tab to other work (`/wt`, `/wtn`) forgets the worktree. With `github` off it is left out, as the other PR commands are.

`M-p`'s "Shape the note's checklist items" opens a tab running `/shape` for each unticked checklist item of a note you pick.

### Autopilot

`M-p`'s "Autopilot: work a note", asked from a pane in the note's repository (a worktree of it will do), keeps tabs working through a note's checklist unattended. It opens mission control on its tabs and asks which note, listing those holding an unticked item, the one it last worked first, then the newest, with the rest behind "More notes…" (all of them at once when none holds one); which branch to land on ("Land on:": the branches picked there before, here or in "Start work on a note's checklist items", the most recent first, then the repository's default branch if it isn't among them, so with nothing picked yet Enter lands there, then its other local branches by name, then "Another branch…" to type one, a branch only origin has made a local one tracking it); how many of its tabs work at once (3); the most of them open at once, those waiting on you included (5); and a spend limit in dollars, empty for none. Each box starts with what it was last time, so Enter keeps it. From then on Switchboard opens the note's next item whenever fewer than that many of its tabs work and fewer than the most are open, one at a time and a few seconds apart, in the background, in the session you used last: a tab running `wtn`, named after the item and linked to the note, as "Start work on a note's checklist items" opens one, in the repository's main clone, its worktree cut from that branch (`wtn --target <branch>`, which records it as `branch.<branch>.wtnTarget`), and `/queue-and-clean` lands it straight onto that branch, as `switchboard landing`'s `target` says: rebased onto it, fast-forwarded, and pushed only when it tracks a remote branch, a local-only one just moving. Each branch landed on has a landing lock and queue of its own (`land-lock` reads the branch from `wtnTarget`, or `--target <branch>`), so tabs landing on different branches never wait on each other. A repository that lands through a pull request (repos.json's `landing` `pr`, or GitHub's rules) is refused: autopilot lands only straight onto a branch for now, and so is a branch other than the default one for an item started by hand there, since its pull request would go to the default branch. The items are taken in this order: those whose `Plan: [[…]]` links a settled plan note first (one with no "## Decision needed", or whose choice was made: a ticked option or a `Chose …` line in each such section, or the item saying `(chose …)`), then those with no plan, each in the note's order; never one whose plan needs a choice (hands-free, below, takes those after the settled ones), one whose open choice waits on your review of a design board (an option naming a board, `board 21` or `boards 69 and 70`, or saying it waits until something is reviewed), in either mode, listed in Next up after the free ones, dim, with "waits on your review of board 21", one under a section for parked work, one a worktree already works on, one `wtn` started in the last half hour (each item it starts is kept a day in `~/.local/state/switchboard/wtn-claims.tsv`, so one whose worktree is gone a moment before its item is ticked isn't taken again, however its tab was opened), one held by an item it builds on, or one you skipped. An item builds on another when an indented `After: <the other item's first line>` line is under it (one a line, `/plan-item` writing it when the new item needs the other's code first): it is held while that item is unticked in the note, parked or not, matched by its first line exactly or else, when no item of the note, ticked or not, has that first line, by the line being part of exactly one open item's first line, ignoring case, so a ticked item, or a line naming none or several, holds nothing; a landed item not yet ticked still holds. Autopilot opens the next free item in its place, takes it once the other is ticked, and its view's Next up lists it after the free ones, dim, with "waits on <item>". There is no setting: an item is held only when its note says so. When `wtn` refuses an item in one of its tabs (its branch claimed by another item), the item is skipped, its notification says why ("Skipped <item>: wtn refused it, <why>"), and the tab closes, so no slot waits at a shell; taking the skipped items back offers it again. A fetch of origin that fails as `wtn` cuts an item's branch is tried twice more, 5 and 15 seconds apart ("Fetching origin's main again"), then the branch is cut from origin's default branch as last fetched, saying when a fetch last moved it ("Starting from origin/main, last updated 12 minutes ago: couldn't reach origin"), since the landing rebases onto the newest one anyway; only a clone that never fetched it stops there. When `wtn` stops before making the item's worktree in one of its tabs (`@autopilot_failed`, the error's first line; a failure after it, the item claimed, waits on you in the tab as before), the tab closes at once, freeing its slot, and the item stays in Next up, its card saying "start failed: <why>", opened again no sooner than 5 minutes later; the third failure within an hour skips it ("Skipped <item>: its start failed 3 times in an hour, <why>"), and taking it back gives it its tries again.

With UX 2.0 on (Settings' `ux2`), its settings are one box in place of that chain of questions and of the menu's options that each change one: "Autopilot: work a note" (and Notes' `M-p`, with the note it was pressed on) opens it as "Work a note" while the mode is off, with the note last worked (else the first holding an unticked item), the pane's repository and the counts as they were, and `M-↵` starts it; while it is on, "Autopilot's settings…" in the Autopilot view's own menu (`m`), Enter on its heading or a click on its button open it as "Autopilot · <note>", and `M-↵` saves what changed at once, saying it ("Autopilot: 4 tabs at once, lands on team/next"). A status line says whether it is working, paused or off, how many of its tabs work and are open, and today's Claude spend against the limit. Under it, a row a setting: Note (Enter picks another), Repository and Lands on (each typed, with a suggestion dim after what is typed, from repos.json, the mode's repository and the branches picked before, the default one and the rest, which → takes; empty lands on the default branch, and how the repository lands shows under it), Takes from (where its work comes from, ← and → or a click on ‹ › choosing: the note, GitHub issues, or the note, then GitHub issues; shown while Settings' "Autopilot can take GitHub issues", `autopilot_github_issues`, is on) and, with issues, Issue label (typed, `autopilot` by default), Tabs at once and Most open (each a stepper: − and +, or ← and →, the most open following the slots up and the slots following it down), Spend limit (dollars, empty for none, with a meter of today's spend against it and the dollars spent at the right), Weekly limit (a stepper of percents, off, then 50 to 100 by 5, with a meter of the week's use of Claude Code's weekly rate limit against it and the percent used at the right), and Planned only, Land without asking and Hands-free (the last only while Settings' `autopilot_handsfree` is on), each a switch Space flips; then the note's skipped items, `u` or a click on its "Take it back" keycap taking one back (an item skipped meanwhile, as when `wtn` refuses one, stays skipped), and the items left out for needing a choice, each with a "needs a choice" chip. Turning Land without asking or Hands-free on asks the same question as the menu before it saves, a no going back to the box. A repository that isn't one, lands through a PR, or differs from the one its tabs work in while it is on (stop it first), a limit that isn't dollars or a branch name git wouldn't take is said in the box, which stays open. Its keys are under a rule: the `M-↵` Save keycap (Start while off), while on the Pause (Resume) and Stop buttons, acting at once, and More… for the menu's other options (skipping the next item, closing landed tabs), which `p`, `s` and `m` take too without showing them, and the `esc` Cancel keycap; each of those but Save asks first (`[Y/n]`, with Yes and No a click takes) when anything changed, since it leaves the changes unsaved. On a screen too short for it, the box drops its extras, shows its items in a window and scrolls with the focus, its keys always in sight; Tab and Shift-Tab, or ↑ and ↓, move between the fields, and every field, stepper, switch, item and keycap takes a click. The menu's "Change its note, slots, cap or spend limit…", "Change the target branch…" and count options open the box on their field. Under 160 columns the steppers' words, the line about the suggestion and each switch's second line go, and the skipped items fold to a line with their count, Enter showing them.

Only the tabs it opened count, each marked `auto` in the sidebar. A tab waiting on you (a question, a turn ended) frees its slot but still counts toward the most open, as does one whose `wtn` never reached its worktree without saying why (one whose start failed closes, above), and one out of its worktree but not back in the main clone, as while `/queue-and-clean` removes the worktree it is in, which keeps its item held; a tab `/queue-and-clean` has landed, back in the main clone, leaves the mode, and the next item opens in its place. Once its Claude's pane is back in a main clone, idle, it says `landed` in the sidebar instead of `auto` (`landed without asking` when it did, below), and closes as Settings' "Close a worked note's landed tabs" (`autopilot_close`) says: when seen (the default), once it has been in front for a second and you leave it, or half an hour after it landed if you never look; at once, as soon as it lands; after 5 minutes, or sooner once seen and left; or never, left open for you to close. Whatever the choice, it is never closed while in front or while a tool stands in for one of its panes, a prompt typed in it after it landed makes it yours (`@autopilot_yours`), out of the mode, so it stays open, a plain tab in the sidebar, and its item stays under the Autopilot view's Landed; with "never", a landed tab is yours as soon as it is marked landed, and with Notes moved beside it only its own panes close, so Notes goes back to its own tab. A tab closed without your having seen it leaves a notification, "Closed <item>: landed <short hash>", which Enter dismisses, while Settings' "Notify when autopilot lands" (`autopilot_landing_notices`) is on; it is off by default, and then the closed tab is in the Autopilot view's Landed column and the Notifications view's history, as dismissed, but never in the sidebar. A setting saved as on before reads as when seen, and off as never. What marks them is kept with the tabs, so it carries on after a restart. It keeps opening tabs while you're away, so it works through the note overnight, its spend limit still the cap, but only with a terminal attached, since a tab opens in one; with Settings' "Keep opening tabs while I'm away" (`autopilot_while_away`) off it opens nothing while you're away (whatever "Pause lookups while away" says) and finishes only the tabs already open. It opens nothing once today's Claude spend in the Usage view passes its limit, or while the note changed in the last minute, since an item being typed is saved as it is typed. It says so in the notifications when it stops for the spend, when the note has nothing left to take and none of its tabs is still open (while some are, the Autopilot view's heading says "nothing more to start" instead), or when the note is gone; its notification's menu goes to its line, or puts it away.

With GitHub issues among its sources, autopilot also takes work from the repository's issues: the open ones assigned to you and labeled with the issue label, each only when you opened it or the label was added by you or someone with write access to the repository (asked of GitHub's collaborators' permissions), so nobody outside it can queue work for your tabs by opening an issue. They are read in the background through the `github` setting, at most every two minutes (kept in `autopilot-issues.json` in the state directory), and counted in the request audit; with the note too, its items come first and then the issues, oldest first, the same slots, most open, spend limit and pause holding for both. An issue is ready as a planned item is, its body its plan: its card shows `<owner>/<repo>#<number>: <title>`, and its tab runs `wtn --issue <number>`, which asks GitHub for it again, makes a worktree on `issue/<number>-<slug>` (kept as the issue's in `branch.<branch>.wtnIssue`, so it isn't offered again while the worktree is there), and starts Claude on the issue's title and body, given as data with its link, landing it with `/queue-and-clean` as an item lands. Once its tab's landing has pushed (`@autopilot_pushed`, or `@autopilot_autoland` for one made without asking), autopilot comments on the issue with that commit and closes it; once its tab finds the issue a duplicate, autopilot comments that it found the same work elsewhere (never what the tab named, which may be a note's words) and leaves it open. Before closing one itself, autopilot checks the commit its landing pushed is on the repository's default branch on GitHub (an item landing on another target leaves its issue open, saying so). Started by hand (`wtn --issue <number>` in a tab of your own), the issue is yours to comment on and close. A landed issue joins the skipped items, so it isn't taken again whatever becomes of its comment. Each comment asks first in a box, unless the tab landed without asking (a duplicate's comment always asks); with nobody attached to ask, it writes nothing and says so.

Every tab autopilot opens, from the note or an issue, is told first to look for the same work started or done elsewhere (the open tabs, the repository's open and merged PRs and its issues while the `github` setting is on, and for a note's item the note's other items while Settings' `tab_note_context` is on; an issue's tab is pointed at no note), and on finding it to stop without changing anything and run `switchboard autopilot-duplicate '<which>'`, which marks its own tab `@autopilot_refused` with "Duplicate of <which>" (a mark `switchboard set` typed in a pane may not set): autopilot skips the item with that reason, says it in the notifications, and moves on.

While it is on, its line leads mission control's Tabs view, above the cards: `AUTO`, the note, the branch it lands on when it isn't the default one ("→ team/next"), how many of its tabs work and are open against those counts, the item it takes next, and how many are skipped. `k` from the first row of cards, a click or Tab reaches it, and Enter or `m` there opens its menu: pause it (and resume), skip the next item (and take the skipped ones again), take only items with a settled plan (and those without one too), "Close landed tabs: <choice>", which cycles that setting, land without asking (and ask before landing again, below), hands-free (and ask me again, below), change the target branch (for the tabs it opens from then on; those open land where they were cut from), "Tabs working at once: <n>…", "Most tabs open: <n>…", "Spend limit: <$n a day, or none>…" and "Weekly limit: <n%, or none>…" (a percent from 1 to 100, typed with or without `%`), each naming its value and asking that one alone, written as you press Enter with the rest of the mode kept (slots past the most open raise it, and a most open typed below the slots is taken as the slots, which it says), "Change its note…", which asks the note and the branch to land on and keeps the counts, or stop it; the tabs open carry on either way. The weekly limit stops it opening tabs once the week's use of Claude Code's weekly rate limit reaches that percent, as Claude Code last reported it to Switchboard's statusline (`switchboard statusline`, the `limits` file the Usage view reads, so it needs that statusline in its panes): it says "Stopped opening tabs: weekly limit 85% reached, resets Mon 9:00 AM" in the notifications and the view's heading, the tabs at work carry on, and it opens tabs again by itself once the week resets or the limit changes; with no current reading it holds nothing. Off until set, past the spend limit, which is said first when both are reached. While it is on, the palette lists "Autopilot: work a note" as "Autopilot: work a note: show its board for <note> (m for its settings: its tabs at once, the most open, the spend and weekly limits, the branch it lands on, pause or stop)", the same command under the same key and recent picks, typing its name still finding it; picked, it opens mission control on the mode with nothing over it, leaving the menu (and with UX 2.0 the settings box above) to `m`, so the board shows first. The menu is a box at most 72 columns wide, or two thirds of the screen, an option longer than that (the skip option's item) cut with …. What it keeps is in `~/.local/state/switchboard/autopilot.json`: the note by its path and its ID (given one as the mode is set up, when it has none), its skipped items, today's landings for the Autopilot view, and the items it holds for its tabs, each by its note's ID and first line, so an item skipped in one note never hides one worded the same in another; each tab carries its note's ID in `@autopilot_note`, one opened before taking that of the note it was opened for. Notes takes a renamed or moved note along, and the mode follows one renamed or moved outside Notes too, by its ID, wherever it went, at most every 30 seconds while it looks, writing its new path back; a note at its path rewritten without its ID is given it back, and with the ID nowhere, or another note at its path, it takes nothing and says it can't find the note. Settings' "Autopilot" (`autopilot`) off allows none of it.

Its menu's "Land without asking", after a yes to a question saying what it skips and what it keeps, has its tabs land straight on the default branch without asking before the push: each of its tabs not landed yet is marked `@autopilot_land` (and unmarked by "Ask before landing", which asks nothing), which `/queue-and-clean` reads at its push question and takes as yes, and its push, `switchboard land-lock push`, reads again as it pushes, and `/review-loop` reads to fix medium findings without asking, as it does high ones, leaving low ones as known gaps. The tests, two clean review rounds of this very change (the pre-push hook's check), the landing lock and `switchboard leak-check` all still run, and anything `leak-check` finds, an unclear conflict, a test failure the branch didn't cause or the review loop's round cap still asks, as does a repository whose landing runs no tests or no review loop; `land-lock push` reads the mark again and checks the rest in code as it pushes, so "Ask before landing" chosen meanwhile asks after all. After each landing without asking, with Settings' "Notify when autopilot lands" (`autopilot_landing_notices`) on, the notifications say "Landed <short hash>: <item>" (its menu, or Enter with the Notifications window off, goes to its tab while it is open) and the Slack relay sends the same while it runs; off, as it starts, the landing goes into the Notifications view's history as dismissed and nowhere else that notifies, a failed or stopped landing still telling you. Either way the tab says `landed without asking` in the sidebar once landed, and the note's `Done:` line says so. While it is on, the mode's line in mission control says `lands on its own`. It covers landings straight on the default branch alone: a landing through a PR still waits for you to approve its test. In Claude Code's auto mode, which judges each command itself and may refuse a push to the default branch, the landing's push goes through because each of autopilot's tabs starts its Claude with the landing's push command allowed for its session (`claude --settings`, with `Bash(switchboard land-lock push)`, exactly so), never a `git push`: the rule is in no file, no other session has it, and any other push (forced, another branch, a tag) is judged as before. The command pushes without asking only when its own gate passes, and the push after your yes (`land-lock push --asked`) is allowed by no rule, so Claude Code asks it, and the server asks it too outside a tab that lands without asking: with "Land without asking" off, and in any tab not autopilot's, the server answers its PreToolUse hook with an ask, so Claude Code asks it after the landing's question too, and Claude can't push without you. Since "Land without asking" is turned on and off while tabs work, the rule is given to every tab and the mark read at each push. Settings' "Autopilot's push allowed in auto mode" (`autopilot_push_rule`) turns it off; a tab `wtn` or `/wtn` opens by hand, and a repository landing through a PR, never get it. The marks are kept with the tabs, so they carry on after a restart, and they are the tab's whatever tool stands in for Claude's pane.

Its menu's "Hands-free: decide for me", after a yes to a question saying what it decides and what still stops, has its tabs take the recommended option of anything they would ask you, and turns "Land without asking" on with it ("Ask me again: hands-free off" leaves that as it was, while "Ask before landing" turns hands-free off too). Each of its tabs not landed yet is marked `@autopilot_handsfree`, a new one as it opens, and the server answers its Claude's hooks: a question box (`AskUserQuestion`) is denied, the server choosing each question's option marked (Recommended), else its first, naming the choices in its reason for Claude to go on with, and writing `Decided hands-free: <question> → <choice>` itself at the end of the item's plan note (with no plan, indented under the item in the note), its modified time kept, which an open Notes takes in as any change made from outside, so the tab never waits in Needs you and the record of what was decided is the server's rather than Claude's (a question Claude asks again headed `[always ask]`, having found it must be asked after all, takes its line back, and one with nowhere to go, its item renamed in a note with no plan, is said in a message); a turn that ends on a question is sent back to carry on with what Claude recommends, at most three times a tab (`@autopilot_nudged`), after which it waits on you as usual, never once it has pushed (`@autopilot_pushed`, which the landing sets) or landed, and never when Claude says `[always ask]` in it, as it is told to for a question a rule or skill says must be asked; and the queue takes the items whose plan needs a choice too, after the settled ones, `wtn` telling Claude to pick the recommended option, tick it in the plan with a `Decided hands-free` line, and build it, never one whose choice approves a design board, which waits on your review (above). Once the tab has pushed, it asks as usual again. Its `/review-loop` runs up to 20 rounds rather than 5, so a branch that takes many rounds to come clean doesn't stop at the cap to wait for you. What your rules or a skill say must be asked still asks: anything your or the repository's `CLAUDE.md` says to ask about, a decision that approves a design board (in a tab started on such an item with `/wtn`), a question a skill says to stop and ask, which the shipped skills head `[always ask]` (the landing's push question when it doesn't land without asking, an unclear conflict, a test failure the branch didn't cause, `/review-loop`'s untracked files of someone else's and whether to keep going at its round cap, deploy-test's approval), auto mode's permission prompts, and the spend limit, which pauses autopilot as before. After a landing without asking, the notification counts the decisions ("Landed <short hash>: <item> (2 decided hands-free)") and its menu opens the plan in Notes, and the note's `Done:` line names them. An item a hands-free tab finds already done on the branch it lands on is ticked with a `Done:` line naming the commit that did it (`switchboard item-done --already-on`), and the tab cleans up and goes back to the main clone, where autopilot closes it, rather than stopping to tell you (one that no longer applies still stops); and a hands-free tab's cleanup removes its worktree and branch without asking when nothing would be lost, asking only about what would be. A hands-free tab doesn't ask after landing what the change needs to take effect ("Offer to apply a landed change"): it applies none of it, and a landing made without asking adds it to its notification ("Landed <short hash>: <item> (not applied: Build Switchboard and put it in place of the one running)"), and the note's `Done:` line says what it takes effect once. While it is on, the mode's line says `hands-free`. Settings' "Autopilot's hands-free mode" (`autopilot_handsfree`) off takes it out of the menu and every tab asks as usual.

An item can be put next in its queue from Notes' "Work on this item" box ("Next in autopilot's queue", while autopilot works the note; see [Starting work from the workspace](#starting-work-from-the-workspace)) or with `switchboard autopilot-next <note> <line>`: kept in `autopilot.json`'s `next` by the note's ID and the item's first line, it is taken before the note's other items, the last one put there first, until it lands, and its item in Notes carries a "next in the queue" chip. An item whose plan still needs a choice isn't put next.

### `wt`: a Linear ticket

```
wt ABC-123
wt https://linear.app/your-workspace/issue/ABC-123/some-title
wt ABC-123 --prompt "start with the migration"
wt .worktrees ABC-123
```

`wt` reads the ticket from Linear, makes the worktree on the branch Linear names for it (else `<id>-<title, slugged>`), and starts Claude with a prompt that carries the ticket's state. A ticket in a closed state (completed, canceled or duplicate, whatever the team calls it) is raised first, with the question whether to reopen it. Then Claude checks the ticket against the code (is it still relevant and needed, is its approach sound), builds what the code needs where the ticket gets a detail wrong and updates the ticket to match, and stops to ask only about a change of scope. It works on the ticket without running tests or checks, runs `/review-loop`, and pushes and opens a PR. Its last message holds only a ticket check ("Unchanged." or the edits it made), "PR pushed" or "PR not pushed" with why, a word when the review loop stopped at its round cap, and "Preview:" and "Prod:", a few steps each for trying the work. `--prompt` adds your own words at the end of the prompt.

`/wt` in a Claude session hands its tab to `wt`: the session ends, and in the same pane `wt` opens the ticket's worktree with a new session working on it. The ticket comes from the session (one it created or saved, the one it was about, else the branch's), or from `/wt ABC-123`; any other words go into the new prompt (`/wt start with the migration`). It first lists what the session still runs in the background and asks whether to stop it, and it clears a tab name `shape` or you set, so the tab takes the ticket's name.

### `wtn`: a checklist item of a note

```
wtn --note "Project notes" --item 12
wtn --note "Project notes" --item 12 --prompt "keep the old flag"
wtn --note "Project notes" --item 12 --target team/next
```

`--note` is the note's path within your notes directory (`$NOTES_DIR`, else `~/Notes`) or an absolute one, `.md` optional; `--item` is the line number of an unticked `- [ ]` item at the note's top level. A ticked item, a line that isn't one, a note outside the notes directory or in its trash, and an item worded the same as another are refused. An item held by one its `After:` line names, still open, asks "<item> waits on <other>, still open. Open it anyway? [Y/n]" first, no terminal to answer being no; autopilot never opens a held item. The branch is `note/<the item's first line, slugged>` and the worktree `note-<slug>`, made from origin's default branch as it is now, fetched first, the default branch being the one `switchboard landing --json` names as `defaultBranch` (GitHub's answer, which points the clone's `origin/HEAD` at it when that names another), so the branch starts where `/queue-and-clean` lands it and autopilot's push rule allows that push; the tab is named from that line too, which is why `/plan-item` keeps it short. With `--target <branch>`, it is made from that branch instead (one only origin has made a local branch tracking it): from the remote branch it tracks as it is now, fetched first, and refused while the local branch has commits that remote branch lacks, since the landing would never push them; a target tracking nothing is made from the local branch. The target is recorded as `branch.<branch>.wtnTarget`, which `/queue-and-clean` lands it on, and when the item started as `branch.<branch>.wtnStarted` (kept when a rerun reuses the worktree), from which its `Done:` line says how long it took. `--target` is refused in a repository that lands through pull requests, since its pull request would go to the default branch carrying the target's commits, except naming the default branch itself, which starts the item as if no target were given: start the item without it, or set `"landing": "direct"` for the repository in `~/.config/switchboard/repos.json`. Which item a branch is for is kept in the repository's git config (`branch.<branch>.wtnItem`, the note by its ID and the item by its first line), so another item that slugs the same is refused rather than started in this one's worktree, the other item named by its words and its note's title. The same first line in another note is the same item, copied there: `wtn` takes its worktree over, commits and all, moves the claim to this note and says "Taking over <branch>, started from <note>", adding "it is still open in <note> too" while the other note still has it unticked. While its worktree is there, "Start work on a note's checklist items" and autopilot leave the item out of both notes. A note renamed or moved, in Notes or outside it, keeps its items' worktrees: "Start work on a note's checklist items" and autopilot still leave them out, and `wtn` on one reuses its worktree. The note is given an ID first when it has none. Claims from before notes had IDs, by the note's path, are rewritten once in each repository, the note found at that path, else by its title, else as the one note holding the item, each only when it holds the item; one it can't place is listed once and still read by path, and taken as its item's once its note is back there.

Claude is given the item as written, with what is indented under it. It is told to read the plan a `Plan: [[…]]` line links first and ask about any choice the plan still needs (with no plan, to read the code and ask before building what the item leaves open), to compare the item and its plan with the code first and, when it is already done or no longer applies, to say so with the evidence and stop without ticking it (autopilot's tabs, given the same prompt, then wait on you), to build what the code needs where the plan gets a detail wrong and correct the plan to match, stopping only for a change of scope, to commit on the branch, and to land the work as `/queue-and-clean` does once it is done. `--prompt` adds to the prompt. In a tab autopilot opened, in a repository landing straight on a branch, Claude starts with the landing's one push allowed for its session, as "Land without asking" above says.

`/wtn` does for a note's item what `/wt` does for a ticket: it settles which note, lists its unticked items to pick from, names the tab after the item and hands the pane to `wtn`. A branch after the item, `/wtn <item> → <branch>` or `--target <branch>`, is passed on as `wtn --target`, so the item lands there; with none, on the default branch.

### `wtb`: any branch

```
wtb fix-login-redirect
wtb feature/login
wtb --trusted their-branch   a branch with someone else's commits, as your own
```

A branch that exists here or on the remote is checked out as it is, tracking the remote's; a local one behind the remote's is fast-forwarded, and one that has diverged is left alone with a note. A branch that exists nowhere is made off the remote's default branch, fetched first, and gets an upstream on its first push; with no remote, it is made off `HEAD`. The worktree is named after the branch with its slashes made dashes (`feature-login`). Claude starts with no prompt. `wtb` is plain git: it needs no Linear key and no GitHub.

A branch with a commit beyond the default branch (origin's, else the local `main` or `master`) that someone else wrote or committed, a teammate's say, holds their `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/settings.json`, hooks and skills, so `wtb` makes its worktree but links nothing into it, leaves your shell where it is and starts no Claude, naming who wrote them. Read them, then `wtb --trusted <branch>` opens it as your own; a worktree listed in `~/.config/switchboard/trusted-dirs` opens without asking. Your own commits are those under git's email for you (`user.email`, or `GIT_AUTHOR_EMAIL`), and a merge GitHub committed for you on the web counts as yours. `wt` does the same for a ticket's branch a teammate started, opened with `wt --trusted <ticket>`.

### `wtpr`: a pull request

```
wtpr 123
wtpr https://github.com/owner/repo/pull/123
wtpr --untrusted 123       a worktree that runs none of its code (the default for a fork's PR, or one with someone else's commits)
wtpr --trusted 123         someone else's PR as your own
```

`wtpr` makes the worktree on the PR's head branch, in `pr-<number>-<branch>`. Claude starts by reading the PR (its description, diff, unresolved review threads, failing checks and its Linear ticket), says where it stands and what it would do next, and changes nothing until you say; on someone else's PR it pushes nothing unless you ask. Settings' "wtpr asks where the PR stands" (`wtpr_prompt`) turned off starts Claude with no prompt. `WTPR_PROMPT` in the environment replaces the prompt, as mission control's "Start a CI loop on it in a new tab" does with `/ci-loop`.

A bare number is a PR of the repository you are in. A URL of another repository, or one given from outside any, is worked on in a clone of it found among every open tab's repository, every one a Claude session ran in lately and the directories right under your home; with none, it says so. Any PR works, whatever its state: one whose branch the remote has deleted opens from the commits GitHub keeps for it, on a local branch with no upstream.

A PR from a fork is someone else's code, and so is one of the repository's own with a commit beyond the default branch that someone else wrote or committed (a stacked PR's base included) (read from the commits GitHub keeps for it, as `wtb` reads a branch's), so either opens **untrusted** unless you pass `--trusted` (any PR opens so with `--untrusted`): in `~/.cache/switchboard/pr-review/<owner>-<repo>/<number>`, outside the clone, on a local branch `pr-review/<number>` made from the commits GitHub keeps for it, with no remote added. That worktree's own git config, which no file in it can change, marks it (`switchboard.untrusted`) and turns off git's hooks, every filter your config names (a checkout runs the smudge filter its `.gitattributes` picks, git-lfs's say), the file-system monitor and submodule recursion (which would fetch from wherever its `.gitmodules` says), so no git command there runs the PR's hooks or yours. Nothing is linked into it, your shell stays where it is, and no Claude is started, since its `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/settings.json`, hooks and skills are its author's. It prints the author and how long their account has been on GitHub, the PR's size and `switchboard pr-risk`'s list of what in it runs without being asked, then, in a block of its own, the command that opens it as your own (`switchboard worktree-from-pr --trusted <number>`, naming the PR by its URL when it was opened by one, only for a PR whose author you trust, since its hooks, settings and scripts then run), then how to go on from your main clone: `/review-pr <number>` in Switchboard's own repository, or `switchboard pr-risk`, `switchboard codex-review -C` and `switchboard try --sealed` by hand (see [Someone else's PR](#someone-elses-pr)). Its title isn't printed, since its author wrote it and a terminal acts on escape sequences in it. Run again, it moves the worktree to the PR's newest commit.

A PR from a fork opened `--trusted` (a collaborator's you know) is set up as `gh pr checkout` sets one up: the fork becomes a remote named after its owner, and the branch, `<owner>-<branch>`, tracks the fork's, so a push updates the PR. It says so when the fork's owner hasn't let maintainers push.

### `wtr` and `claude-here`: back into a worktree

```
wtr ABC-123
wtr 123
wtr https://github.com/owner/repo/pull/123
wtr fix-login-redirect
```

`wtr` takes what the others take, finds the worktree they made for it in `git worktree list` (a ticket's ID or `pr-<number>` in its directory or branch, or a branch by its whole name), moves your shell there and resumes its Claude with `claude-here`. Nothing is made or fetched, so it needs no Linear key, no GitHub and no network. Several matches, such as a ticket's worktree and one made later from its PR, are listed by number, the most recently touched first, and Enter takes the first. A worktree git still tracks whose directory is gone is said to be so, with the `wt`, `wtpr` or `wtb` that makes it again.

`switchboard claude-here` resumes the Claude session of the repository you are in (its top level, or the directory you are in outside one), from the directory the session started in. With one session it resumes it at once. With several it lists them by when each was last active, with the last thing you typed in it, to pick with `j`/`k` or the arrows and Enter (Esc, `q` or `C-c` cancels), or by number when its input is a pipe. `--print` prints them instead, a line each with the session's ID, when it was last active and its transcript; any other argument is passed to `claude`.

### Finding a tab or a worktree again

`switchboard wt-jump` goes to the tab working on a ticket, a PR or a branch:

```
switchboard wt-jump ABC-123      the ticket's tab
switchboard wt-jump 123          the PR's tab
switchboard wt-jump theme-slot   any tab whose branch, directory or name contains that
switchboard wt-jump --list       print the tabs, and jump to none
```

Tabs are matched on each pane's directory and the branch checked out there, and on the tab's name, whose `#<number>` front is its PR; you land on the pane that matched. A ticket is matched whole, so `ABC-12` never finds `ABC-123`. A bare number is looked for as a `pr-<number>` worktree first, and only then is GitHub asked which branch the PR is on. One match jumps outright; several are listed by number, the tabs waiting on you first; none prints the `wt` or `wtpr` that would open one. A tab in another session is made the current tab there, and the terminal you used last moves to that session, as tmux's did. To pick a tab by looking, `M-j` opens mission control's Work map as a picker of the open tabs.

`switchboard wt-revive` lists the worktrees no tab has open, in the repository you are in (or `<dir>`'s), every open tab's and every one a Claude session ran in over the last two weeks: each one's repository and branch, how long since it was used and the last prompt Claude was given there, most recent first. `--json` prints a line of JSON for each. What each transcript says is cached in `~/.cache/wt-revive/index.json`, so it reads only the transcripts that changed. Mission control's Reopen view shows these rows under "Worktrees with no tab", and Enter on one offers "Open a tab here and resume its Claude", which runs `claude-here` there as `wtr` does. `--sessions` lists the Claude sessions of the last two weeks instead, a subagent's left out, each with its id, the directory it ended in, its last prompt and how many prompts it had, newest first (`--json` a line of JSON each, which the Reopen view reads, with the folder `claude --resume` finds it from), leaving out one that started or ended in someone else's PR's worktree, and one whose folder is gone, which Claude can't find to resume.

### Several at once

```
switchboard wt-batch ABC-123 ABC-124
switchboard wt-batch "ABC-123, https://linear.app/your-workspace/issue/ABC-124/some-title"
switchboard shape-batch "phase label is unclear" "results load slowly" ABC-123
switchboard shape-batch --continue "ABC-123, ABC-124"
switchboard shape-batch < ideas.txt
```

`wt-batch` opens a tab per ticket, running `wt` in each. Tickets are separated by commas, spaces or both, and every one is checked before any tab opens. `shape-batch` does the same with `shape`. Each of its arguments is one thing to shape, since an idea is free text, while an argument made only of ticket IDs or Linear URLs is split as `wt-batch` splits it; with no arguments, it reads ideas from stdin, one a line, and `--continue` is passed on to each `shape`. Both run only in a Switchboard pane. They open their tabs at the end of the tab bar in the order given while focus stays where it is, start each in the directory they were run from (so run them from the repository the work belongs to), and open anything given twice once. Each tab is left a shell when Claude exits.

### Shaping a ticket

```
switchboard shape "people can't tell which phase is active"
switchboard shape ABC-123
switchboard shape ABC-123 split out the API part
switchboard shape --continue ABC-123
```

`shape` is the step before `wt`. It starts Claude with `/shape` in the repository's main checkout, even when run from a worktree, since a ticket being shaped has no branch yet. It fetches first and warns when the local branch is behind its upstream, since Claude reads the files on disk, and it names the tab "Shaping: <first words>". The skill does the rest: it reads the code, checks Linear for a ticket that already covers the idea, drafts the ticket in the form the team's recent tickets use, writes it to Linear only once you approve, reviews what it wrote with the repository's `/review-loop` in ticket mode (Codex reading the ticket against the code, its edits saved to Linear only once you say so), and names the tab "Shaping <ID>: <title>" once there is a ticket. Words after a ticket are your direction for the shaping. Given a ticket, it asks whether to run the repository's review loop on it or keep shaping; `--continue` keeps shaping without asking. `/wt` afterwards hands the same tab to the ticket's worktree. `/shape` works the same in a session you already have.

### Cleaning up

`switchboard worktree-done` looks up the Linear ticket each worktree of the repository is named after (from its branch, else its directory, with a `pr-<number>-` front left out) and offers the ones whose ticket is finished, in Linear's completed or canceled state whatever the team calls it, to remove. On a terminal it lists them by number: type numbers or ranges (`1 3-5`), `a` for all, or Enter for none, and taking all of them asks again first. With no terminal it prints them instead. Removal is `git worktree remove` without `--force`, so a worktree holding uncommitted work refuses to go, and the branch always stays.

```
switchboard worktree-done            pick finished worktrees to remove
switchboard worktree-done --list     print them, removing nothing
switchboard worktree-done --all      print every worktree by its ticket's state
switchboard worktree-done --paths    print only the finished ones' paths
```

`switchboard update-main [<dir>]` brings the repository's local default branch up to date with origin's from wherever you are, a worktree on another branch included. It switches nothing and only ever fast-forwards, so a default branch with commits of its own is left as it is. Where the branch is checked out, it is fast-forwarded there, unless that checkout has uncommitted changes, which it says; checked out nowhere, the branch alone moves.

### Another tab's last reply

`switchboard claude-reply <tab>` prints the last reply Claude gave in a tab, the final text of its last turn, read from its session's transcript; `<tab>` is the tab's number in your session or its id (`@12`). `--pane <tab>` prints the pane to type into instead: the tab's Claude pane, or its active pane when it runs no Claude. It exits 1, saying why, when the tab has no Claude or no reply yet. `M-p`'s "Send Claude's last reply to another tab" does it in the workspace (see [More in the palette](#more-in-the-palette)).

## Landing and reviewing

### Reviews: `/review-loop` and `codex-review`

`/review-loop` runs the branch through rounds of skeptical, read-only review, fixing and committing the findings it agrees with, until two rounds in a row come back clean, five rounds at most, or twenty in a tab of autopilot's hands-free mode, which still asks you whether to keep going when it reaches the twentieth with findings open. Before the first round it runs Claude Code's `/security-review` once, its findings fixed or asked about as the rounds' are (Settings' "Security review in the review loop", `review_security`). It keeps a notes file the reviewer reads every round, one per branch, whose fixes a later loop on the branch in the same session picks up, holding each finding a round fixed and what the fix changed, each finding you dispute and each gap you accept, with a reason for each; the reviewer is told a fixed finding is settled while the failure no longer happens, and a finding that only restates a fixed or disputed one is disputed at once rather than fixed again. It pushes once, at the end, after asking. `/review-loop all` asks about low-severity findings too, `/review-loop norules` leaves the rules out, and `/review-loop <worktree> [<base>]` reviews someone else's PR checked out untrusted from outside its worktree, reporting only ([Someone else's PR](#someone-elses-pr)).

Each round is `switchboard codex-review`. It reviews committed changes only, and refuses a tree with uncommitted ones. The PR is looked for as the tab's name finds it: in the repository `gh repo set-default` named, else in origin and the project an `upstream` remote names, origin's winning when both have one, and only a PR from where the branch is pushed counts, never a stranger's with the same branch name. The base is the PR's base on the remote that points at the PR's repository (upstream, in a fork's clone), else origin's default branch; on the default branch with no PR, working straight on `main`, it reviews the commits origin doesn't have yet, fetched first. It prints one JSON object: `summary`; `findings`, each with a severity (`critical`, `high`, `medium` or `low`), a category, a file and line, a title and the failure it found; then `reviewer`, `branch`, `base`, `reviewedCommit`, `mergeBase`, `patchId` and `warnings`, which lists the findings that mention a path outside the repository, to read before acting on them.

```
switchboard codex-review                       the branch against its PR's base (on main: the unpushed commits)
switchboard codex-review --base upstream/main  against another base, used as given when the ref exists
switchboard codex-review -C <dir> --pr <n> --base origin/main
                                               someone else's PR's untrusted worktree, from your main
                                               clone, with its description
switchboard codex-review --notes notes.md      fixed and disputed findings and accepted gaps, each with a reason
switchboard codex-review --include-low         report low-severity findings too
switchboard codex-review --round start         a loop's first round, counted, then --round next for each after it
switchboard codex-review --no-rules            leave your rules and the repository's out
switchboard codex-review --reviewer claude     have Claude review even with Codex there
switchboard codex-review --model <name>        the reviewer's model, the one option passed on to it
switchboard codex-review --dry-run             print the prompt and the reviewer's command, and run nothing
switchboard codex-review --patch-id A B        print the change from A to B as one hash, and review nothing
switchboard codex-review --recorded            print the clean review recorded for a pre-push hook
switchboard codex-review --record-squash C     record C in place of the reviewed commit, when it is the same change
```

Codex runs in its read-only sandbox, the commands it runs with no network and no way to ask for more, without your `~/.codex/config.toml` or the repository's `AGENTS.md`, and with the commit messages and the PR description given to it as data, not instructions. Without Codex, Claude Code reviews with the same prompt and JSON, with only its Read, Grep and Glob tools, so it runs nothing, and with no settings, MCP servers, skills or CLAUDE.md. The change is given as text (`git diff --text --no-textconv --no-ext-diff`), whatever the branch's `.gitattributes` say, since a `-diff` there would hide a file's lines. `-C <dir>` reviews the repository at `<dir>` rather than the one you are in, and `--pr <number|url>` names the PR whose description goes in the prompt when the branch's name doesn't lead to it, as with someone else's PR checked out untrusted; a PR named that isn't there stops the review. `CODEX_REVIEW_VERBOSE=1` shows Codex's progress on stderr.

The reviewer is given the rules you work under, and reports a change that breaks one (category `rules`): your `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` with what it imports, and the repository's `CLAUDE.md` and `AGENTS.md` as the base has them, which win where the two disagree. Settings' "Rules in reviews" (`review_rules`) turned off leaves them out, so your rules aren't sent to the reviewer.

`patchId` is the reviewed change as one hash, context lines included, so the same change rebased onto a newer base has the same one. It is `git patch-id --verbatim`'s hash (whitespace kept), worked out by `codex-review` itself with git's diff options fixed, so a git older than 2.39, which hasn't `--verbatim`, or a git config of yours gives the same one; `switchboard codex-review --patch-id <from> <to>` prints it for any two commits, as the pre-push hook asks for it. `codex-review` itself records a clean review, for a pre-push hook that asks for one: a round with no findings is noted in the clone's `review-loop-streak`, a round with findings clears it and the record, and a round with `--include-low` (the loop's confirmation round) that finds nothing on the same commit, base and patch as the clean round before it writes the reviewed commit, its base and that hash to `review-loop-clean` (both at `git rev-parse --git-path`), which `--recorded` prints. A review of another repository with `-C` records nothing. `--record-squash <commit>` moves the record to a commit with the reviewed commit's tree making the reviewed change on the reviewed base, as `/review-loop`'s squash does, and refuses any other. Switchboard's own repository has one for `main`, which Settings' "Review before pushing" (`review_before_push`) turns off.

`/review-loop` runs each round with `--round` (`start` for the first, `next` for each after it), and `codex-review` counts the rounds and applies the stop rule from the same streak the record comes from, so the loop and the record can't disagree on how many clean rounds there were. It keeps the loop's place in the clone's `review-loop-round`, runs the round after a clean one as the confirmation round with `--include-low` whether or not it was given, caps the loop at 5 rounds, or 20 in a tab of autopilot's hands-free mode, and keeps the Landing view's record of each round. Its JSON then ends with `round`, `cap`, `cleanRounds` and `next`, which says what the loop does now: `triage` the findings, `confirm` a first clean round with another, `done` after two clean rounds, the record written, or `cap`. Each finding carries an `action` from its severity: `fix` (critical and high, and medium in a tab that lands without asking), `ask` (medium, and low in a round that reports lows), `gap` (any low in a tab that lands without asking) or `report` (a low no one asked for); whether the finding is real, and how to fix it, stays the loop's. `--round next` with no loop under way, or with `-C` naming another repository, refuses.

### Someone else's PR

A pull request from someone you don't know is reviewed before any of its code runs on your computer. `wtpr` opens a fork's PR untrusted (above, [`wtpr`: a pull request](#wtpr-a-pull-request)): in a worktree under `~/.cache/switchboard/pr-review/` where no git hook, filter or Claude session of its own runs. From your main clone, then:

```
switchboard pr-risk <worktree> <base>                              what in it runs without being asked
switchboard codex-review -C <worktree> --pr <number> --base <base>  a review round, from outside it
/review-loop <worktree> <base>                                     the review loop, reporting only
switchboard try --sealed <worktree>                                its code, in a sandbox (macOS)
switchboard try --sealed <worktree> --run cargo test               its tests, in the sandbox
```

`/review-loop` given the worktree's path reviews it from the session you are in, never one started there, with `codex-review -C` every round, and fixes, commits and pushes nothing: each finding it agrees with goes in its notes file and in a report worded for the PR's author. Its code runs only in a sealed `switchboard try` ([Sealed](#sealed)), which Linux hasn't. In Switchboard's own repository, `/review-pr <number>`, a skill of the repository's own that no one else is given, runs all of it and drafts the review for you to post; `M-p`'s "Review a contributor's PR" picks the PR and starts it in a tab of its own. `switchboard pr-risk` is the first look at it: what it changes that runs without being asked, each file with its added lines, read from git alone (no hook, external diff, textconv or file-system monitor, and as text whatever its `.gitattributes` say), so nothing of the PR's runs.

```
switchboard pr-risk <worktree> <base>          the report
switchboard pr-risk --files <worktree> <base>  the files alone, a line each
switchboard pr-risk --json <worktree> <base>   all of it as JSON
```

It lists, under a heading each: what git runs or reads in the worktree (`.githooks/`, `.husky/`, hook managers' files, `.gitattributes`, which picks among the filters and diff drivers your config names, and `.gitmodules`); what runs on install and setup (`install.sh`, `src/setup/`); what a Claude session loads or runs (`.claude/`, `CLAUDE.md` and `AGENTS.md` anywhere, `.mcp.json`, and the plugin's settings, skills and instructions in `assets/`); what runs as it builds (`build.rs`, `Cargo.toml` and `Cargo.lock`, `.cargo/` and `rust-toolchain.toml`, anywhere: a build script or a proc macro runs as cargo builds, checks or tests, and as an editor's rust-analyzer opens the folder); CI (`.github/workflows/`, `.github/actions/`); and packages (manifests, lockfiles and package managers' settings anywhere). Then every added line, in any file, that fetches, evaluates, runs a shell, decodes, runs AppleScript, asks the Keychain, reaches for credentials or SSH keys, opens a socket or changes what git runs (its hooks path among them), and the hosts the PR names that the base names nowhere.

A worktree of someone else's PR checked out untrusted, in `~/.cache/switchboard/pr-review/` or marked `switchboard.untrusted` in its own git config, is left alone, since its `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/settings.json`, hooks and skills are its author's: `claude-here`, `wtr` and `wtb` refuse it; `M-t` opens no Claude tab there; `wt-revive`, the Reopen view's worktrees and the Slack relay's places never list it, nor the Reopen view a session that ended in it (a sealed review is listed as the tab `/review-pr` ran in, from the main clone), and the relay refuses one named; `switchboard skill-path` passes over its skills for its main clone's, so no PR can rewrite the review loop that reviews it; and `switchboard apply install` never builds it, whatever `trusted-dirs` says.

### Landing: `/queue-and-clean`

`/queue-and-clean` takes a branch from finished to gone. With a PR, it queues it for merge (the merge queue, or auto-merge), watches it in the background, and hands a failing check, a conflict or a drop from the queue to `/ci-loop` to fix, three times at most. Once it merges, it waits for the deploy and tests it as `/deploy-test` does. Once you approve the video, it marks the Linear ticket done, ticks the item in your note, removes the worktree and its local branch, and starts the tab's pane again in the main clone with the same conversation resumed, keeping the tab's name. With no PR, it checks whether the default branch already holds the work, a squash included, and cleans up if so. If not, it offers to open a PR, or, in a repository that lands straight on its default branch, as `switchboard landing` says (below), to land it there. A branch `/wtn` opened asks neither: its prompt names the note, and, in such a repository, settles that it lands on the default branch (the branch's `wtnItem` in git's config marks it).

`switchboard autopilot-duplicate '<which>'`, run by the Claude in a tab autopilot opened, marks the tab's item a duplicate of the work it names (another item, issue, PR or tab): it sets its own tab's `@autopilot_refused` to "Duplicate of <which>", and autopilot skips the item with that reason and closes the tab. Outside such a tab it refuses.

`switchboard item-done --says '<what it does now>' [--hash <commit>] [--already-on <branch>] [--merged-pr-head <commit>] [--checked-in <branch>] [--without-asking] [<worktree>]` is the landing's last step, run from the worktree (or given its path) once its work has landed: it checks nothing would be lost (no uncommitted changes, and every commit on a branch of the remote as it is now, fetched and pruned first, the work in the branch it lands on, a squash included, the worktree's HEAD being the head of the merged PR, `--merged-pr-head`, or every change found in a branch by reading it, `--checked-in`), ticks the item the branch was started for (`wtn` keeps it as `branch.<branch>.wtnItem`, by the note's ID, so a note renamed since is still found) with a `Done: <hash>, <took>[, landed without asking]. <says>` line under it (`Done: <hash>, already on <branch>. <says>` for an item found already done there), moving it under the unticked items as Notes does, then removes the worktree, deletes its branch and prunes the remote's deleted branches. The tick comes before the removal, so autopilot never finds an item unticked with its worktree gone and takes it again; a step that fails stops it there, saying why, and an item it can't find leaves the worktree as it is. A branch `wtn` didn't start has no item, which it says (`no item: …`) and goes on. `--hash` is the commit the work landed as (the worktree's HEAD by default), and `--without-asking` marks a landing made without asking. It prints a line a step, the main clone's path last, for the pane's move there.

`switchboard landing [--json] [--refresh] [<dir>]` says how the repository at `<dir>` (the current directory by default) lands its work: `pr` (through a pull request) or `direct` (straight on its default branch) on the first line, then a direct landing's steps as JSON, `test`, `testIn`, `squash`, `review` and `beforePush`, with where the answer came from (`source`) and why, and, for a branch `wtn` started, when it started its item (`started`, seconds, from `branch.<branch>.wtnStarted`) and how long ago that was (`took`, `1h12m`), both null otherwise, which the landing puts on the item's `Done:` line (`Done: <short hash>, 1h12m. …`); `defaultBranch`, the repository's default branch as GitHub has it (the clone's `origin/HEAD` with the `github` setting off or GitHub not answering); `note` and `item`, the path of the note and the first line of the item `wtn` started the branch for (null otherwise); `ciBranch`, the `ci/land-<id>` branch a landing whose tests run in CI pushes to, the branch's name as a hash; and `alreadyLanded`, whether the branch's commits are already on what it lands on as last fetched, or merging it there changes nothing (null when that isn't fetched); `--json` prints both as one object, and it fails when `repos.json` can't be read. The answer is the repository's entry in `~/.config/switchboard/repos.json`, by its origin's `owner/name`:

```json
{ "owner/name": { "landing": "direct", "test": "npm test", "testIn": "here", "squash": true, "review": "review-loop", "beforePush": "" } }
```

`landing` is `pr` or `direct`; the rest is a direct landing's steps: `test` runs before the review and again after the last rebase (empty, none; not set, the tests the repository's `CLAUDE.md` names), `testIn` is where they run, `here` (in the worktree, the default) or `ci` (in the repository's CI, below), `squash` lands the branch as one commit (off, its commits land as they are), `review` is `review-loop` or `none`, `beforePush` must pass before the push (empty, none), and `afterRebase` runs in place of the tests after the last rebase when that rebase had no conflict and the default branch's CI isn't failing (empty or not set, the tests run again; `cargo clippy --locked --all-targets -- -D warnings` for a Rust repository, say). With no `landing` set, it works one out: a pull request when GitHub says the default branch needs one (a ruleset, branch protection, or a merge queue), else straight on it when the repository's `CLAUDE.md` says its work goes there with no PR, as a headless Haiku reads that file alone (never one that links out of the repository), else a pull request. That answer is kept a day in `~/.local/state/switchboard/repos-landing.json`, `--refresh` asking again; off the network, or with Settings' "GitHub" off, the last answer stands, and with none it is a pull request. Settings lists each repository a tab is in under "Landing (repos.json)", with what was worked out for it (going to Settings works out those not known yet, in the background), and Enter changes its entry: how it lands (automatic, through a PR, straight on the default branch), and, landing straight, its tests, where they run, squash, review, command before the push and check after a clean rebase.

Landing on the default branch makes the branch one commit on top of the default branch as it is now (`switchboard land-lock squash --message-file <f> --onto <base>`, which refuses with anything uncommitted or a commit made after the last clean review, keeps a `backup/pre-squash-<hash>` branch, restores it when the commit fails or the squashed tree differs, and moves a clean review's record to the squashed commit; `--drop-backup` deletes the backups once the push lands; review-loop's squash uses it too), runs the tests, runs `/review-loop` on it, runs `switchboard leak-check` on what it would push and asks before pushing, showing what `leak-check` found, then pushes with `switchboard land-lock push`, all in each worktree at once, each step only as the repository's steps say (a tab of autopilot with "Land without asking" on takes the question as yes when `leak-check` finds nothing, the tests ran and the review loop ended on two clean rounds of this very change, above). A `beforePush` command must pass before the push. Only the push waits its turn: in one command, `switchboard land-lock settle`, the worktree takes the landing lock, rebases onto what landed meanwhile and, when anything had, runs the tests again (with `afterRebase` set, a rebase with no conflict onto a default branch whose CI isn't failing runs that check instead, since the tests have passed on each side, which keeps the lock held for about a minute rather than a second CI run), and pushes with no second review while its change keeps the review's `patchId`, reviewing and asking again when it doesn't. A landing that reviews or asks again lets the lock go meanwhile (`switchboard land-lock requeue`), waiting first in the landing queue marked reviewing, which the next landing passes over, so a review loop of an hour holds up no one; once its review is clean it takes the lock back in its turn and rebases again before it pushes. Then it fast-forwards the main clone, ticks the note's item and cleans up as above.

With `testIn` `ci`, a landing's tests run in the repository's CI rather than on your computer, both times. The landing runs `switchboard leak-check` on its commit, asking before anything leaves when it finds something, pushes the commit to a branch of its own, `ci/land-<id>` (the id a hash of the worktree's branch name, so the name itself, which may hold a note item's words, isn't published), forced since each fix rewrites it, and waits for the CI that runs on it as `switchboard wait-ci` does, all in one command, `switchboard land-lock test`, which deletes the branch again once the run ends, whichever way, an interrupt included; a failure is set beside the default branch's run at the commit the change sits on (`wait-ci --same-as`): one that is the change's is read from the run's log and fixed as a local one is, and when the default branch fails the same tests, the landing lets the lock go, waits for a tip of the default branch that passes and starts again from the rebase, fixing nothing. A commit tested again keeps its earlier runs' jobs, so the command asks for the commit's runs before it pushes: when they passed (each workflow's latest run that wasn't canceled, a failed job you ran again included, and the commit's other checks and statuses agreeing) it says `passed in CI before, run <id>` and passes with nothing pushed, and otherwise it waits for the run its own push started, leaving the earlier runs' jobs out; there a canceled job is neither passed nor failed, and a run with nothing failed and something canceled is `canceled in CI`, which the landing runs once more; when GitHub doesn't say which runs the commit already has, the tests run in the worktree. The setting is your standing answer for that push, so it isn't asked about; the push to the default branch still is. A repository with no CI, or whose CI doesn't run on such a branch, has its tests run in the worktree, as `here` does. `ci` takes whatever CI runs on a pushed branch to be the repository's tests, so set it only where CI runs them on every branch.

`switchboard wait-ci` prints one line once the commit's check runs and commit statuses settle: `passed <sha>` when every one has finished with none failing and one at least succeeding, `failed <sha> <run id> <job names>` as soon as one fails (the run's id for `gh run view <run id> --log-failed`, `-` when the check isn't GitHub Actions'), `none` when no check run that ran anything has appeared within `--grace` minutes (3 by default; check runs all skipped count as none), or `timeout` after `--timeout` minutes (45 by default). With `--now` it asks once and says what that look sees, without waiting: `passed <sha>`, `failed <sha> <run id> <job names>`, `running <sha>` or `none`; it exits 1 when GitHub can't be asked. With `--same-as <commit>` (the commit the change sits on, on the default branch), a failure is set beside that commit's run and named by whose it is in place of `failed`: `base-failing` when every test the failed jobs name failed there too, in the same job or another partition of it (a test moves between partitions as tests are added; another platform's job doesn't count), `change-failing` when none did (a test its log shows passed there, every job of its family passing there, or a job naming no test whose whole family passed there), and `mixed` for some of each or what it can't tell (a job that failed on both without naming a test or with a build or lint error beside its tests, two jobs of one name, a log it can't read, the job missing, skipped or still running there), and `base-failing` waits for the landing's other jobs to finish (`mixed` with `--now` while they run). The tests are read from cargo's and nextest's lines in each failed job's log.

```
switchboard wait-ci --repo owner/name --sha <commit>
switchboard wait-ci --repo owner/name --sha <commit> --timeout 60 --grace 5
switchboard wait-ci --now --repo owner/name --sha "$(git rev-parse origin/main)"
switchboard wait-ci --repo owner/name --sha <commit> --same-as "$(git merge-base HEAD origin/main)"
```

Once the main clone has the landed change (after a PR's merge too, which fast-forwards it first when it has the base branch checked out), it asks which of what the change needs to take effect to do, each named with the file that calls for it, and does the ones you pick; what only you can do it names in the question, and the note's item's `Done:` line says what was applied or when it takes effect. The list comes from `switchboard apply-actions <repo> <from> <to>`, which prints one action a line, `<key>`, `<label>`, `<why>` and `<command>` tab-separated, an empty command being yours to do, and each listed once however many files call for it:

- In your own clone of Switchboard, `~/switchboard` by its real path, a change to its code (`src/`, `assets/`, `build.rs`, `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock`, `rust-toolchain.toml`) is built and put in place of the binary running (`switchboard apply install <clone>`: `cargo build --release --locked`, only while its commits are yours as `switchboard try` asks and it has no change no commit holds, or it is listed in `trusted-dirs`, the build written beside the binary, run once and renamed over it, as `switchboard update` puts a release, then its `setup --refresh`, so Claude started from then on loads its skills and hooks), the Slack relay restarted while it runs and its code changed (`switchboard apply relay`, which waits for it to stop before starting it again in its tab), and the workspace restarted, which is yours to do, since the server, Notes, `M-d`'s diff and the views run the build they started with: `switchboard upgrade` restarts the server on it keeping every tab and what runs in it ([Restarting on a new build](#restarting-on-a-new-build)), and Notes, the diff and the views take it when next opened. A release build lists none of it, since releases keep it current.
- In another repository, the `apply` list of its entry in `~/.config/switchboard/repos.json`, by its origin's `owner/name`, each `{ "paths": ["db/**"], "label": "Reload the dev server", "command": "make reload" }` run in the clone when one of its `paths` (`*` within a folder, `**` any number of them) changed. Without one: a changed lockfile installs with its package manager (pnpm, npm, Yarn, Bun, Bundler, Poetry, uv) where packages are installed, Yarn's Plug'n'Play included; new migration files run the package's migrate script (`db:migrate`, `migrate`, …); and a changed `.env.example` asks you to copy what's new. An install, a migrate script or a command of yours runs the clone's own code, so each is offered only in a clone listed in `~/.config/switchboard/trusted-dirs`, and named for you to run elsewhere.

A tab in autopilot's hands-free mode asks nothing here and applies nothing, leaving it to you: the note's `Done:` line says what wasn't applied, and so does the landing's notification when it landed without asking. Settings' "Offer to apply a landed change" (`apply_after_landing`) turns the offer off.

`switchboard leak-check <range>` looks for anything identifying you or your employer in what a range of commits adds, before it goes to a public repository: each line any of its commits adds (a line a later commit removes, a merged side branch's and a file the repository marks binary included, read without its textconv drivers) and each commit message. It finds the words in `~/.config/switchboard/leak-check.txt` (your organization's and company's names, usernames, internal hosts, one a line, `#` lines left out; Settings' "Identifying words" opens it), as whole words in any case; Linear-style ticket IDs and PR numbers written with a `#`, outside tests, whose IDs are made up (and `ABC-…`, `XYZ-…`, `#123` and `#12345` anywhere, the made-up ones the docs use, and standards such as `UTF-8`); home directory paths; email addresses but noreply and example ones; and the shapes of GitHub, OpenAI-style, Slack and Linear tokens. A hit in a file is left out when the range's base (the merge base of its two ends) already has the same text in its copy of that file, so a line a change moves or rewrites isn't reported again, while one brought into another file, or a new one, still is; a commit message's hits always count. It prints each hit as `<file>:<line>: <kind>: <text>` or `commit <hash>: <kind>: <text>`, then on stderr how many it left out (`--all` prints those too, marked `(on <base>)`), and exits 1 on any hit kept, 0 on none and 2 when it can't look, a word list there but unreadable included, so a landing asks rather than pushing unchecked.

**A target branch follows the default branch.** Work cut from a target branch (autopilot's "Land on", `wtn --target`) lands on that branch, never on the default one, so the target would drift behind the default branch. With Settings' "Target branches follow the default branch" (`target_follows_default`) on, every direct landing on the default branch (`land-lock landed <hash> <default branch>`) opens a tab in the background, in the main clone and named `<branch> ← <default branch>`, for each branch items land on: autopilot's target while the mode is on that repository, and each hand-started item's `wtnTarget` while its worktree is there. A branch whose run hasn't ended, or ended leaving work for you, gets no second tab. The tab runs `switchboard update-target <branch> [<dir>]`, which works in a detached worktree of its own (`.claude/worktrees/update-<branch>`, its `/` written `%2F`): it takes the branch's landing lock, waiting in its queue like any landing (the tab shows in the Landing view while it holds it), fetches, merges origin's default branch into the remote branch the target tracks (else origin's branch of its name; `Merge <default> into <branch>`, a fast-forward when the branch has nothing of its own), runs `test` and `beforePush` from repos.json when they are set (only where the merge holds nothing but your own commits and origin's, or the main clone is in `trusted-dirs`, since they run the repository's code unasked), merging and testing again when the default branch moved on while they ran, pushes, never forced, fast-forwards the main clone's local branch, lets the lock go and removes its worktree. A conflict, or a step that fails, stops it with exit 3: the lock let go, the merge (or the merged commit) left in its worktree, the phase `merge conflict <files>` or `tests failed …` drawn red. Resolve the conflict and `git add` the files there (or commit a fix), then run `switchboard update-target <branch>` again, which finishes the merge or takes the commit as it is and goes on from the tests. `M-p`'s "Bring a target branch up to date with the default branch, under its landing lock" runs it by hand.

The lock, and the queue of worktrees waiting for it, live in the main clone's git directory, shared by every worktree of it, one of each per branch landed on: the default branch's `land.lock` and `land.queue`, and a target branch's `land.lock.<branch>` and `land.queue.<branch>` (its `/` written `%2F`). Each command takes the branch from the worktree's `branch.<branch>.wtnTarget`, or from `--target <branch>` given before it, else the default branch. `switchboard land-lock` keeps them:

| Command | What it does |
|---|---|
| `land-lock take` | Takes the lock for this worktree: exits 0 when taken (or held here already), or 3 when another holds it or others wait ahead, printing which, and joins the queue |
| `land-lock wait` | Takes it, waiting in the queue for this worktree's turn; exits 4 when taken out of the queue meanwhile (mission control's Landing view), or, after a `requeue`, while it reviewed |
