| `land-lock release` | Lets it go when this worktree holds it, and leaves the queue |
| `land-lock requeue` | Lets it go when this worktree holds it and puts this worktree first in the queue, marked reviewing, in one step: a take passes over it until its next `take` or `wait` drops the mark, so a landing reviewing its change again keeps its turn without holding up the others |
| `land-lock leave` | Leaves the queue |
| `land-lock holder` | Prints which worktree holds it, nothing when none does |
| `land-lock queue` | Prints the holder and each waiter, a line each: place, path, since when |
| `land-lock move <path> up\|down\|first` | Moves a waiter in the queue |
| `land-lock phase [words]` | Shows the landing's step in the tab; no words clear it |
| `land-lock record clear \| <tests\|review\|leak> <json> \| found <json> \| output <tests\|review> <file>` | Keeps what the landing has done, for the Landing view's cells and detail, in `landing.json` in the worktree's own git directory, sent nowhere: `tests` (`ok`, `passed`, `failed`, `total`, the failing `file`, a failure with no counts drawn as "✕ failed"), `review` (`round`, `reviewer`, `clean_rounds`, `findings`, `summary`) and `leak` (`found`) each replace the last, stamped with when; `found` adds an earlier round's finding (`round`, `title`, `fixed_by`); `output` keeps a file whole beside it (`landing-output.log`) and its last 40 lines in the record; `clear` starts it again. queue-and-clean and the review loop record as they go |
| `land-lock push [--asked]` | The landing's push, with this worktree holding the lock: fetches, exits 5 when the branch landed on moved past the commit's base, and without `--asked` goes on only when the tab lands without asking, `leak-check` finds nothing, the landing's record holds a passing run of its tests, it ran the review loop and the review's recorded patch is the change pushed, exiting 6 with the subject, why and leak-check's hits otherwise; `--asked` is the push after your yes. Then it runs `beforePush` (only in a worktree of your own commits or a clone in `trusted-dirs`), which must leave the commit and tree as they were. Pushes with `git push <remote> HEAD:<branch>`, never forced and with the repository's hooks, then sets `@autopilot_pushed`, runs `landed` (and `autolanded` with the tab's item and its plan, for a push its gate let through), deletes the landing's `ci/land-<id>`, fast-forwards the main clone and releases the lock, which a failure releases too; exit 3 when the lock isn't this worktree's |
| `land-lock settle [--command <cmd>] [--patch <id>]` | The rebase, retest and compare before the push, under the lock: takes it (or waits its turn, exit 4 when taken out of the queue), fetches the branch landed on, rebases onto it unless it is already under the commit, then, unless that very commit already passed its tests or check, runs `afterRebase` after a clean rebase onto a branch whose own CI isn't failing at one look (its output in `landing-check.log`), else the tests as `land-lock test --again` does (with `--command` before anything else, as `land-lock test` takes it), and compares the change with the review's recorded patch (`--patch`, the one asked about, for a landing with no review). Exits 0 `ready <short hash>`, holding the lock for `land-lock push`; 10 on a conflict, left in the worktree to resolve and continue, after which the next run tests it in full and has it reviewed again; 11 when the tests or the check failed, their last line last (`check failed <log>` for the check); 12 `changed` when it isn't the change reviewed, having run `requeue`. Every exit but 0 and 12 lets the lock go |
| `land-lock test [--leak-ok] [--command <cmd>] [--base <ref>] [--again] [--here]` | Runs the landing's tests and records them: repos.json's `test` in the worktree (its output in `landing-test.log` in the worktree's git directory, its counts read from cargo, nextest, jest, vitest or pytest), or, with `testIn` `ci`, `leak-check <base>..HEAD`, a forced push to `ci/land-<id>`, a wait for its CI beside the default branch's at the change's base (`wait-ci --same-as`) and the branch deleted again (the run its own push started, a commit's earlier run that passed answering with no push, `passed in CI before, run <id>` above `passed`), falling back to the worktree when no CI answers, and keeping a CI run's failed jobs' logs as its output. `--command` and `--leak-ok` come before the other arguments, so the permission that lets Claude run it unasked never covers a command of its choosing or a push past leak-check. Its last line: `passed`, `no tests`, `failed <n of m> <log>`, the change's failures as `failed in CI <sha> <run id> <jobs>` (`mixed in CI …` when some may be the default branch's, `base-failing in CI …`, exit 4, when all are), `canceled in CI <sha> <run id> <jobs>` (exit 8, nothing failed and the run canceled), `leak-check found <n>` (exit 3, `--leak-ok` to push anyway, `--here` to run them here), `not set` (exit 5, `--command` to give the one `CLAUDE.md` names), `untrusted` (exit 7, the worktree holds code that isn't yours and its main clone isn't in `trusted-dirs`) or `timeout ci/land-<id>` (exit 6) |
| `land-lock landed <short hash> <branch>` | Says the tab's work landed straight on `<branch>`: marks it `@tab_landed`, which files it under Merged as "landed on <branch>" while the tab is in the repository's main clone on the branch it had |
| `land-lock autolanded <short hash> [--plan <file>] [--apply-range <from> <to>] [item]` | Says the tab landed without asking, as `land-lock push` does once its gate let a push through: marks it `@autopilot_autoland` with the hash and adds "Landed <short hash>: <item>" to the notifications (`~/.local/state/switchboard/autolanded.json`; with `autopilot_landing_notices` off, to their history alone, as dismissed), the item the tab's own when not given, with the count of `Decided hands-free` lines in its plan (the tab's when not given), or under the item in the tab's note when it has none, what a hands-free tab left for you to apply (the actions `apply-actions` lists for `<from>..<to>` in the main clone) and a way to open it, and, said by `land-lock push` with `autopilot_landing_notices` on, sends it through the Slack relay, which an update never does; said again for the same hash, it updates that notification, keeping what it doesn't say |

The lock never expires with time, since a landing may wait on you while it holds it. It is taken over only from a landing whose worktree is gone, or whose Claude session has ended with none other running in the worktree, and a waiter gone the same way leaves the queue. The lock and each place in the queue name the Claude session that took it (Claude Code's `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID`) and its pane, so a landing run by a session started in the main clone keeps its lock, and a `take` from the same worktree by another session names that one instead; one taken by a build from before names no session and is kept by a session in its worktree alone. The sidebar and mission control show the landing tab and those waiting under Landing, in the queue's order ("landing", "2nd in line to land"), and Enter on a waiting tab offers "Land it next", "Move it up in the landing queue" and "Move it down in the landing queue" (Settings' "Show the landing queue", `landing_queue`; off, they show as Working, and the queue still orders the landings).

### Deploys and videos

`/deploy-test` waits for a merged PR to deploy, tests the feature it adds on the deployed site in a real browser, records a captioned video of the test, and asks you to approve it. `/queue-and-clean` runs it before removing a worktree, and you can run it on any merged PR. It changes nothing real on the site unless the environment is a test one or you say so. `/preview-test` does the same before the merge, on an open PR's preview deploy, found from the branch's GitHub deployments, a bot's comment or the repository's docs.

Both wait with `switchboard wait-deploy`, which prints one line once the deploy settles: `deployed <sha> <url>` (`deployed <sha> -` when it watched a deploy workflow, which has no address), `failed <url>` or `timeout`.

```
switchboard wait-deploy --repo owner/name --sha <commit> --environment production
switchboard wait-deploy --repo owner/name --sha <commit> --environment Preview --ref <branch> --timeout 20
switchboard wait-deploy --repo owner/name --sha <commit> --workflow Deploy --branch main
```

`--environment` watches GitHub deployments to that environment (what Vercel, Netlify, Render and most deploy actions report), and `--ref` only those of one branch; `--workflow` watches a deploy workflow's runs on `--branch`. A deploy counts when its commit is the one given or a later one, since a merge queue often deploys a commit that includes it. It looks every 30 seconds, for `--timeout` minutes, 45 by default.

Both record with `/walkthrough`'s recorder. `/walkthrough` records a feature as a video to keep or share: from the site's home page through its own navigation, each step captioned, each click shown with a cursor, and the page's address above it. For a change to Switchboard itself, or given `terminal`, it records Switchboard's demo instead: a demo of its own, under a folder of its own in `/tmp`, run on the worktree's build and walked with a headless client (`switchboard headless`), each step captioned and each key pressed shown as a keycap over the screen; it opens no window and touches neither your workspace nor a demo you run. It records at your workspace's size (the screen of the session it runs in, 160x45 outside Switchboard), never narrower or shorter than `switchboard headless --min-size`, so no view stacks its panes; `--size` records at another, and a frame wider than 2560 pixels is scaled down to that width in the video. You look it over and keep it, record it again or drop it; a video you keep goes in `~/Movies/Walkthroughs/`. Claude may start it when you ask for a video, a demo or a walkthrough, since it sends nothing anywhere and asks before keeping one. The recorders are Node programs using Playwright, kept with the skill, so it needs Node, the system's Chrome and `ffmpeg`, and its packages installed in the skill's scripts folder in Switchboard's plugin, which the skill does itself when they are missing:

```
npm ci --prefix ~/.local/share/switchboard/claude/plugin/skills/walkthrough/scripts
```

Each site keeps a Chrome profile of its own in `~/.cache/deploy-test/profiles/<host>`. When a recording lands on a sign-in page, the skill asks you to sign in and opens a window for it; previews keep one profile per repository, since their hosts change with every PR. Claude never types a password.

### Watching a note: `/note-watch`

`/note-watch` keeps an eye on one note while the session runs, and works each checklist item you add to it, ticking it when done. It confirms the note first and reads no other. It watches with `switchboard watch-note <note file>`, which prints `NEW: <the item's text>` for each item added after it starts, once the item's text has stayed the same for twenty seconds, and `GONE: …` if the note is moved or deleted.

### On call: `/oncall`

`/oncall` watches your team's Slack alert channels while you're on call, started as `/loop /oncall`. It reaches you only through the Slack relay (`switchboard slack-relay notify`, so Slack notifies you of the relay bot's message): an urgent alert at once, the rest only if they are still a problem after about 30 minutes with nobody on them. It reads Slack through the Slack connector and posts nothing there. It needs the relay running and `~/.config/switchboard/oncall.json`, which is yours and never in a repository; `config.example.json` beside the skill shows its shape:

- `channels`: each alert channel's `id` and `name`, with an optional `watch` (a sentence saying which posts in it count) and `urgent` (a sentence saying what in it is urgent);
- `rules`: optional, the path of a Markdown file of your team's own rules, followed over the general ones;
- `repo`: optional, the GitHub repository (`owner/name`) runbooks and code are looked up in;
- `from`: optional, the name shown above each notice (`oncall` by default);
- `addressed` and `taking`: optional, your team's own reaction names for an alert that is handled and one someone is on.

### What the skills run

`switchboard skill-path <name> [<dir>]` prints the `SKILL.md` to follow for a skill in the repository at `<dir>`, the current directory by default: the worktree's own `.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`, else the main clone's (a branch cut before the repository added the skill has none of its own), else Switchboard's own: your personal `~/.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md` when it is Switchboard's (written there with the plugin off, or a copy of it you made your own by deleting its `.switchboard-skill`, which still opens by running `skill-path`), else the plugin's, `~/.local/share/switchboard/claude/plugin/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`. A skill of yours that only shares a name with one of Switchboard's is never printed, so Switchboard's, run as `/switchboard:<name>` or handed off to by another, follows its own text. It prints nothing and exits 1 when there is none. When the skill it prints names in its `allowed-tools` a `switchboard` command or subcommand this build doesn't have, as a skill read from a clone newer than the installed binary can, it says so on stderr in a line starting `skill-path: build behind:` naming them, and queue-and-clean stops a landing there, saying to build and install Switchboard from main and land again.

`/today` and `/slack-updates` save what they read for mission control's Calendar and Slack views, which can't reach Claude's connectors themselves. `switchboard today-save` takes the day as JSON on stdin and keeps it in `~/.local/state/switchboard/calendar.json`. `switchboard slack-updates-save --since` prints when to look back to (ten minutes before the last look, or an hour ago the first time) as three fields: that time in ISO 8601, the same in seconds, and how far back to look for your own messages, in seconds, and `switchboard slack-updates-save` takes what the look found as JSON on stdin and adds it to `~/.local/state/switchboard/slack.json`. `switchboard setting <key>` prints one of the workspace's settings as Settings reads it (`on`, `off` or a choice's value), which is how `/review-loop` reads `review_security`. `switchboard new-note <note> [what wrote it]` puts a note just written, a path in your notes, in the notifications until you read it, as the todo note and meeting notes starter routines do; it refuses a file outside your notes.

## The Slack relay

The Slack relay connects a Slack app's DM to the Claude sessions in your tabs, for when you are away from the computer. Each Claude tab gets a thread in the DM. What you reply there is typed into that tab, and the relay posts back when the tab finishes, waits on you, may be stuck, commits or pushes. The app's Home tab lists every tab, and from it you can jump to a tab's thread, or open its screen to message, answer, stop, rename or close it.

It runs in a tab of its own, named Slack relay. `M-S` opens that tab, or goes to it when the relay is already running, and M-p's "Stop the Slack relay" stops it. From a shell:

```
switchboard slack-relay [start]   open the relay in its tab, or go to that tab
switchboard slack-relay stop      stop it
switchboard slack-relay status    whether it runs, its process and its tab
switchboard slack-relay notify [--from <name>] [<text>]
                                  post a notice in the DM, from <text> or stdin
switchboard slack-relay sends <needs-me|everything|quiet>
                                  choose what reaches you by a preset, and say what changed
```

It needs the workspace's server running, and works from anywhere that server can be reached (a Claude job, ssh): a new tab goes in the session you used last. The tab runs `switchboard slack-relay run`, which starts the relay again if it crashes, inside an interactive shell so your shell's setup puts `claude` on the PATH for its Haiku calls. Stopping the relay on purpose (M-p, `slack-relay stop`, `stop` in the DM, Ctrl-C) closes its tab once it has said so in the DM; when it crashes past its restarts or refuses to start, the tab is left at that shell with the error, and `M-S` starts the relay there again. Settings' "Close the relay's tab when it stops" (`relay_close_tab`) off leaves the tab at its shell however the relay ends; a tab opened before the setting existed keeps the old way until the relay is next started. It also needs Switchboard's hooks for Claude Code, since that is how it hears what each session does: as a plugin, only the sessions in the workspace's panes run them, so the relay hears of those alone; with Settings' "Load Switchboard as a plugin" off, every session runs them from your `~/.claude/settings.json`, one in a terminal of its own too.

`switchboard slack-relay notify` is how something else on the computer reaches you through the relay, such as the `oncall` skill. The notice goes out as the app's bot, under a 🔔 and the `--from` name (`notice` when none is given; up to 40 letters, digits, spaces and `. : _ -`), so Slack notifies you, which it never does for anything posted as you. The running relay posts it within a second, whether or not you are at the computer, and `notify` waits up to 45 seconds for it: it exits 0 once it is posted and 1 if it wasn't (the relay isn't running, or Slack refused), so a caller knows whether you were reached. It only ever posts in the DM.

When it starts, the relay says so in the DM with buttons for what you would otherwise type: **Home** (a link to the app's Home tab, added once Slack has sent the relay an event, since that is the only way it learns the app's ID), Status, Threads, Progress, New tab, Help, and Stop relay, which asks first. Stopping on purpose edits that message to say the relay stopped and when, and starting again within a day edits it back, so restarts don't fill the DM; a first start, an older message or a crash posts a new one. You stop it on purpose with `stop` in the DM, Stop relay, `switchboard slack-relay stop`, M-p's "Stop the Slack relay", Ctrl-C in its tab, or closing its tab.

### Threads and notices

Each Claude tab's thread is started the first time there is something to say about it, or when you open its screen from the Home tab, and holds everything about that tab: its finished turns, its waits, its milestones, notes that it may be stuck, and the answers to what you sent. A finished turn is posted when you started it (typing in the pane or replying from Slack) and it wrote a reply; a turn another tab's message or a background task started, or one with no reply, is left out, while a tab waiting on you is posted whoever started its turn. Settings' "Turns another tab or a task started" (`relay_send_other_turns`, off at first) posts every finished turn. The thread starts with a header of two lines, the tab ("Tab 3, work") and its state's mark with its state and since when ("🔴 Waiting on you since 3:04 PM"; 🔵 working, ✅ finished, ⚪ idle), and Progress, Skill, Rename and Close buttons. Since the header names the tab, what is posted in the thread calls it Claude ("Claude has finished", "Committed `abc1234`…") rather than repeating its number and name, which a line in the main DM still gives. The header is edited whenever the tab is renamed or its state changes, so Slack's list of threads shows how every tab stands. While the tab lands straight on its branch through queue-and-clean (holding the landing lock or waiting for it), its landing takes the state's place: "🟣 Landing since 2:12 PM · 1st in the queue" (or "Waiting to land", "Reviewing again" for one waiting first in line while its change is reviewed again), its PR's checks and review when it has one ("PR #57 · ✓ 9 checks · approved"), and, once it holds the lock, its steps as the Landing view's cells, "✓ rebased · ✓ tests · ✓ review, 2 rounds clean · ✓ leak-check · 🟡 push" (✓ done, 🔵 under way, 🟡 waiting on you, ✕ failed, ⚪ still to come). queue-and-clean's question before pushing comes to the thread as "Claude asks: **Push to main?**", Claude's question, what the review and leak-check found, and two buttons, **Yes, push it** and **No**: as at the desktop's [Y/n], no answer is no, so nothing pushes until you press one. Once the tab closes, or starts another Claude session, the header is one line, "⚫ Tab 3, work, closed at 5:12 PM", and offers **Delete thread**. A thread belongs to its tab for as long as the tab is open, and a new tab gets a new one.

What needs you or went wrong gets a one-line notice: a tab waiting on you, a dialog, a tab that may be stuck, a failed commit, push or pull request. Each is posted in the tab's thread with a mention of you, which is what makes Slack notify you of a reply in a thread you don't follow; nothing in a tab's thread is also sent to the main DM. A notice for a prompt carries the prompt's option buttons, so you can answer from the notice itself. By default a finished turn gets no notice, since it is already in the thread; which kinds get a notice, go to the thread alone or aren't sent at all is yours to choose ("What reaches you", below).

A tab can hold other panes beside Claude. The relay keeps each tab to its own Claude pane: the first one it sees in the tab (or the one `new:` started) is recorded on the tab and stays its pane while the tab is open, through `/clear` or Claude being started again in it. While that pane isn't running Claude, another Claude pane in the tab stands in.

What it posts by itself:

- **Finished turns and waits**, from the states Claude Code's hooks record. An alert carries the turn (a finished one reads "Finished." and its reply, the thread already naming the tab), and for a tab that is waiting, the end of its screen, since a permission prompt and its numbered options are only there. A turn that another tab's message started (Claude's `SendMessage`) is posted under that message, cut short, with the tab that sent it ("Tab 3, design sent: …"). A turn that ends in the tab you are looking at isn't marked finished in the workspace, since you saw it, but its thread gets it all the same. A tab that loops a skill (a Calendar or Slack tab) finishes a turn on every tick, so it alerts only when it waits on you.
- **Dialogs before a session starts** ("trust this folder", "new MCP server found"), which no hook reports, read off the screen, with the dialog's options as buttons.
- **Stuck tabs.** A tab marked working whose transcript hasn't changed for ten minutes gets a note in its thread (what it did last, and its screen) with **Stop** and **Progress** buttons, and a line there mentioning you. A long test run looks the same, and the screen usually tells the two apart. Each stall is noted once.
- **Milestones.** One line in the tab's thread when a session commits, pushes or opens a pull request, mentioning you when one of those fails. A push counts only when git says where it went and that is not a local folder. Test runs are not reported, failing or not, since one failing is routine while Claude works, and neither is CI, which GitHub already notifies you of.
- **A full context.** Each turn posted ends with how full the tab's context is and its model, as do the tab's line on the Home tab and its screen. Once a session passes 90%, its thread says so once, with a **Compact** button that opens the Skill form with `compact` picked; it says so again only after the context has dropped below 90% and passed it again.
- **Offline.** The relay can't tell Slack anything when the computer is off, asleep or offline, so it leaves the warning with Slack ahead of time: a message saying the relay is offline, scheduled in the DM about ten minutes ahead and renewed every two minutes. If the relay goes quiet, nothing pushes it back and Slack sends it, which notifies you. When the relay is back it says so, and for how long it was gone. A clean stop cancels it.
- **Crashes.** A relay that crashes is started again after a wait that grows each time (2 seconds, then 10, 30, 60 and 120), and posts that it restarted and why. After five crashes in ten minutes it posts that it is down and gives up.

Everything it posts is standard Markdown, the way Claude writes, so headings, **bold**, links, lists, tables and code show as they do in the terminal. An answer longer than one Slack message goes out as several.

### What reaches you

Each kind of message the relay posts by itself has a setting in Settings' "Slack relay" category, and a row in mission control's Slack relay view: Notify me (the tab's thread, with a line there mentioning you, which is what Slack notifies you for), Thread only (the thread alone, which Slack doesn't notify you for), or Don't send. The tab's thread keeps a record only of what is sent. The first message sent about a tab starts its thread, whose header is a message in the DM of its own, once per tab, whatever its kind says.

| Kind | Setting | Default |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Prompts and questions | `relay_send_prompts` | Notify me (no Don't send: only Slack may be there to answer one) |
| Dialogs before a session starts | `relay_send_dialogs` | Notify me (no Don't send) |
| Finished turns | `relay_send_finished` | Thread only |
| Tabs that may be stuck | `relay_send_stuck` | Notify me |
| Commits, pushes and pull requests | `relay_send_milestones` | Thread only |
| Failed commits, pushes and pull requests | `relay_send_failures` | Notify me |
| Full context | `relay_send_context` | Thread only |
| Notices from other programs (`slack-relay notify`) | `relay_send_notices` | Notify me (no Thread only: a notice is a message of its own; Don't send refuses it, and `notify` exits 1 saying so) |

Finished turns are only those you started that wrote a reply: "Turns another tab or a task started" (`relay_send_other_turns`, off at first), a row under them, sends the rest too, as Finished turns says. A kind turned off still counts as reported, so turning it back on doesn't post what happened meanwhile. The settings are read as each message goes out, so a change takes effect with the next one, the relay running or not.

Three presets set them all at once, from the view or as `switchboard slack-relay sends <preset>`, which prints what it changed: **Only what needs me** (`needs-me`: prompts, dialogs, stuck tabs, failures and notices notify, the rest isn't sent, and autopilot's tabs send only landings and failures), **Everything** (`everything`: every kind notifies) and **Quiet** (`quiet`: everything goes to its thread alone, notices still notifying). `switchboard setting relay_send_<kind> <notify|thread|off>` sets one kind, and `switchboard setting relay_send_<kind>` reads it; Claude in the workspace's panes may run both without asking.

**Autopilot's tabs.** "Autopilot's tabs" (`relay_send_autopilot`) at Landings and failures only sends a tab autopilot opened only its prompts, dialogs and failed commits, pushes and pull requests, each as its kind says, leaving out its finished turns, stuck notes, milestones and full context; at Like any tab (the default) it follows the kinds.

**Quiet hours.** With "Quiet hours" (`relay_quiet_hours`, off at first) on, between "Quiet hours from" and "Quiet hours to" (`relay_quiet_from`, `relay_quiet_to`, 10pm to 7am at first) by the computer's clock, a kind set to Notify me goes to its thread alone, but for prompts and dialogs while "Prompts notify in quiet hours" (`relay_quiet_prompts`) is on, and for notices.

What always reaches you: offline and crash messages, which say the relay can't reach you, and the answer to a message you sent a tab from Slack, posted where you sent it whatever the settings say.

### What you can send it

A message reaches a tab only as a reply in that tab's thread, or through a button or form made for it. A message never names a tab, so nothing at the top level starts work or reaches a tab by accident. A thread stays its tab's only while that tab runs the session it had when the thread began: once the tab closes, a reply there is refused ("Tab 3, work has closed since, so I didn't send this"), even when a new tab has taken its place. What is typed into a tab loses its control characters but line breaks and tabs, so nothing in a message acts as a key.

| You send | It does |
| --- | --- |
| a reply in a tab's thread | types it into that tab's Claude |
| `?` in a tab's thread | shows the tab's last turn: your last prompt and Claude's reply since, from its transcript (the screen if there is none) |
| `progress` in a tab's thread | what that tab is doing, without typing anything into it: its state, when the turn began, when it last did anything and how many tools have run, then a few bullets from a Haiku that read the turn's activity and the screen |
| `esc` in a tab's thread | presses Escape there, which stops Claude mid-turn or declines the prompt it waits on; a tab that isn't working or waiting is left alone, since a second Escape opens the rewind menu |
| `rename <name>` in a tab's thread | names the tab, as "Rename it" in its menu in mission control does; `rename` alone gives it back its own name |
| `status` or `?` | every Claude tab, its state, for how long, how full its context is, and a link to its thread |
| `threads` | a link to each Claude tab's thread; a tab with no thread yet says so |
| `progress` or `progress all` | the same as `progress`, for every tab at once: one line each, most urgent first |
| `new: <prompt>` | a new Claude tab with that prompt; its confirmation is its thread |
| `new <place>: <prompt>` | the same, in a place: a repository's name for its main checkout, a ticket ID or any part of a branch name for the worktree that matches, or a path starting `~` or `/` |
| `cleanup` | offers to delete the threads of closed tabs |
| `help` | the list of commands |
| `stop` | stops the relay |

`new <place>:` looks names up among the repositories your open tabs use and those a Claude session has run in over the last two weeks. A name that matches several places, or none, starts nothing and says so, and the reply names the directory it picked.

Anything else at the top level is not acted on, so a typo never starts work: the reply has a button per tab, and tapping one sends the message there, the conversation going on in that tab's thread.

When the relay types your message into a tab, it posts nothing. Your message's reaction says where it is: ⏳ while it waits behind a turn already running, 👀 once the tab's Claude takes it and while it works on it; once the answer is posted, the reaction comes off, the answer beside it saying how it went. Meanwhile a "Working on it" message with **Progress** and **Stop** sits where you asked, and is deleted once the answer is posted. If a tab that isn't busy hasn't taken your message after 30 seconds, the relay posts the tab's screen, since a dialog is usually what is in the way. It knows a tab has answered from the events Claude Code's hooks append while the relay runs, so it posts the turn within a couple of seconds; a session started before the hooks were set up is taken as done once its screen has stayed still for 20 seconds.

**An unsent draft.** Text typed in a tab's prompt box and not sent would go with your message as one prompt, so before typing a message the relay reads the prompt box, and when it holds a draft, it types nothing and asks in the thread, quoting the draft, with three buttons: **Replace it** clears the draft (Ctrl-U) and sends your message, **Send after it** types your message where the prompt's cursor is, after the draft unless you moved the cursor back into it, and sends both, and **Keep it** leaves the draft and drops your message. Only what is typed counts: Claude Code's dimmed placeholder and suggested next prompt don't. A second message for the same tab while one waits takes its place and asks again, and the buttons of the first then do nothing. A button pressed once the draft has gone (sent or cleared at the computer) sends your message as it is and says so. The message waits only while the relay runs. Settings' "Ask before typing over a draft" (`relay_draft_check`) off types the message after whatever is there, as before.

**Prompts and questions.** When a tab waits on a permission prompt or a question, the alert shows Claude's reply and the prompt's question, read from the screen, without your own prompt quoted back, and its options as buttons alone, each with its number, under one line on how to answer it; only an option too long for its button is written out in full. Tap one, or reply with its number (the start of an option works too). Only the number is pressed, with no Enter after it, since an Enter could accept a second prompt that follows. Anything else is refused while the choice is showing, rather than typed, since it would end with Enter and accept whichever option is selected; the refusal carries the option buttons. A button answers only the prompt it was made for, while that same wait is on. Answering in the DM, by a button on one of these messages or a typed reply, edits every message showing the prompt to say what was chosen ("Chose 1. Yes.") in place of its buttons, and posts nothing more (an answer from the Home tab or a tab's screen is said in the thread first, as what you sent); answering at the computer clears their buttons too. A dialog before a session starts is answered the same way, the relay moving to your option with the arrow keys and pressing Enter.

A question Claude asks you itself (AskUserQuestion) works the same, with more to it. When it asks several at once, Claude Code shows them one at a time in the same wait, so after each answer the relay looks at the screen again and posts the next question, and then the review of your answers, each with its buttons. A question that takes several answers shows its options ticked ☑ or not ☐: a tap or a number ticks or unticks one, `1 3` does two, and **Submit choices** (or `submit`) moves on. Any other reply to a question Claude asked is typed in as its "Type something" option, your own answer.

**Quick replies.** A finished turn has a **Skill** button. When Claude ended by asking, the replies you would likely tap are added a few seconds later: **Yes, go ahead** when it asks whether to go on with what it proposed, and a button per option when it asks you to pick one of a few it named. A question mark alone can't tell those from a question only your own words answer, so a Haiku reads Claude's last message (only one with a question mark near its end), and anything else gets no buttons. A tap types the reply into the tab, exactly as its button shows it, and is refused once Claude has said more since.

**Skill.** Slack takes any message starting with `/` for one of its own commands and never sends it, so `/review-loop` can't be typed into a thread as it is at the keyboard. Skill opens a form instead: a menu of the skills that tab can run, and an Arguments field for what follows the name, such as a ticket ID. Picking a skill redraws the form to say what it takes, from the `argument-hint` in its file. The menu is the tab's own: the skills its session lists, plus those only you can invoke, from `~/.claude/skills` and `~/.claude/commands` and from `.claude` in the tab's directory and the ones above it, and Switchboard's from its plugin while Settings loads it as one (by their bare names, or `switchboard:<name>` where one of yours or the project's has the bare one). It is grouped frequently used (the eight you use most, from the counts Claude Code keeps, each use counting half as much every 30 days), yours, Switchboard's, the project's, Claude Code's own commands that finish without asking anything (`compact`, `clear`, `context`, `cost`, `usage`), then plugins and built-in skills. A Slack menu holds 100 options, so a longer list is cut from the end, and a field takes any name the menu left out. A thread reply that starts with a space also gets past Slack.

### The Home tab and a tab's screen

The app's Home tab opens with when the computer last checked in, a time Slack itself renders and keeps counting ("checked in 47 minutes ago"); the relay publishes it every minute while it runs, so anything older than two means it is offline. The same line carries today's spend and how much of the 5-hour and weekly limits are used ("· today $8.41 · 5-hour 42% · week 31%"), as the statusline last wrote them, each left out until it has. **Refresh** beside it publishes at once. **Next** comes first under it, naming the Claude tab `M-a` would go to (the first Claude tab under **Needs you**: pinned tabs, reminders come due, then each tab waiting on you or finished) and opening that tab's screen, and is left out while nothing needs you. **New tab** asks where and for the prompt, as `new <place>:` does: Where is a menu of every checkout a Claude session has run in that still exists, and every open tab's, newest first, grouped by repository, with a field beside it for anything else. **Delete N closed threads** appears while there are some.

**Autopilot**, there while autopilot is allowed (Settings' `autopilot`) and off, opens its settings as a form whose **Start** starts it, as its settings box does on the computer (UX 2.0 board 52): the note it works, where it lands (the repository's default branch, then the branches picked before and its local ones, each "through queue-and-clean"), the tabs at once (up to the most tabs open, which the form keeps), the spend limit a day, and its switches, Planned only, Hands-free (offered only while Settings' `autopilot_handsfree` allows it, or it is on already, as the settings box offers it) and Land without asking, each starting at autopilot's settings as they are. Another branch, one only origin has included, is typed in the field under Lands on, and a note past the hundred options a Slack menu holds in the field under its menu. Start is the yes the box asks before Hands-free or Land without asking. The repository, the most tabs open and the skipped items stay as they were, so the form needs autopilot set up once on the computer, which picks the repository; until then it says so. A note's name is yours, so the form names notes only with Settings' "Notes in Slack" (`notes_in_slack`, off at first), and then only their names, never their items; off, it names none, starts on the note autopilot last worked, and never says its name. The relay says in the DM whether it started, or why not (the note gone, a repository that lands through PRs, its checks taking over four minutes), and the workspace says so too. The server's `autopilot-start` does the starting, and refuses to be run by hand.

While autopilot works, the Home tab shows its progress between the Next buttons and **Needs you** (UX 2.0 board 81): 🤖 **Autopilot**, the note it works (its name only with "Notes in Slack" on, else "your note"), its tabs at once and when its ready items should be done ("Done by about 6:40 PM · 5 left"), how many items stand in each column of its board (Next up, Landing, Working, Landed today, Waiting on you, and Skipped while any is), today's spend against its limit with a meter, and its tabs in flight, each by its tab's name (never the item's words), waiting on you first, then landing (with its place in the landing queue), then working, each with how long and a **thread ›** link to its thread. **Pause** pauses it as its menu's Pause does, and **Settings** opens the form above, whose **Save** changes its settings as the settings box's Save does and says in the DM what changed. Paused by you, the heading says so with the time, and **Resume** takes Pause's place; stopped by its spend limit, a red line says so, the meter is full and **Raise the limit** opens the form; either way it says it opens no new tab while the open ones go on to their end, and gives no finish time. The section is read from the server at most once a minute (at once when you open the Home tab or press one of its buttons), so the Home tab is edited in place no more than that for it and nothing new is posted: a landing or an item waiting on you still reaches you as its own message, as the settings below say. It is left out while the Home holds so many tabs that it would pass the 100 blocks Slack takes, so the tabs still reach you. Settings' "Autopilot in Slack" (`relay_autopilot`, on at first) leaves it out.

Then every Claude tab, in the sidebar's groups and order (**Needs you**, **Review loop**, **Merged**, **Idle** and the rest), so the Home and the sidebar never place a tab apart, each state in the desktop's colors, which its group's header wears too: 🟡 waiting on you, 🔵 working, 🟣 landing, ⚪ idle, ⏰ waiting on a reminder. A tab under the sidebar's Reminders is left out, with no Haiku line written for it, until its reminder comes due, and the check-in line ends with how many are ("· 2 tabs waiting on a reminder"); Settings' "Reminder tabs on the Slack Home" (`relay_home_reminders`, off at first) lists them under **Reminders**, last, each saying when ("remind in 2h"). A server too old to say a tab's stage gets the Home's own three groups instead: **Needs you** (waiting on you, then finished and not yet looked at, the longest first), **Working** and **Idle**. Each has its state, for how long, its last activity, and one line from Haiku on what it is doing or, once it has stopped, where it left off, written again when the tab has done something or changed state, at most once a minute. A tab's name, and the **Thread ›** button beside it, open its thread in the DM at its newest message, a link Slack opens itself with no round trip to the computer; a tab with no thread yet has neither, and says "No thread yet: it starts with its first message." **Screen** under it opens the tab's screen. A tab waiting on a prompt shows the prompt's options beside Screen, which answer it from there. The relay looks every 10 seconds (every minute with Settings' "Look often", `poll_fast`, off) and publishes when anything changed, at least once a minute, and whenever you open the tab. The bottom says what the Haiku calls have cost today, from what `claude -p` reports for each. When the relay stops on purpose, the Home tab says so, since nothing in it is current any more.

**Screen** on a tab shows its screen: its state, its last prompt, the end of its last reply, the options of a prompt it waits on as buttons, and these buttons. Opening a tab that finished, or whose reminder came due, counts as looking at it, as being at it on the computer does (with Settings' `done_clears` at "prompt", a finished mark stays until the next prompt there too), so it leaves Needs you, on the Home and in the sidebar, and `M-a`'s walk; a tab waiting on you stays until you answer it.

| Button | Does |
| --- | --- |
| Message | a field whose text is typed into the tab, handled as a reply in its thread would be (asking first when its prompt holds an unsent draft); the answer goes in the thread |
| Skill | the Skill form |
| Go to thread | the tab's thread, started when the screen opens if the tab has none yet |
| Progress | `progress`, shown on the screen |
| Rename | a field holding its name; cleared, the tab gets its own name back |
| Stop | Escape, only while it works or waits |
| Close | closes the tab after Slack asks, ending its Claude session; `switchboard claude-here` in its directory resumes it |

An open screen is drawn again whenever its tab's state changes, so a prompt answered at the computer leaves it. Every button names the tab and its Claude's process, so a screen or a Close button left from a tab that has closed can't act on a tab that has taken its place.

Slack doesn't let you delete the app's messages in its DM, only your own, so the relay deletes a closed tab's thread for you: **Delete thread** on its header, or **Delete N closed threads** on the Home tab and under `cleanup`, each confirmed by Slack first. It deletes the header and every message the app posted in the thread, with the files it uploaded. Your own replies stay, since Slack doesn't let an app delete them. Only threads of tabs that have closed can be deleted, and nothing deleted can be brought back.

Settings' "Haiku summaries" (`haiku_summaries`) turns off every Haiku call the relay makes, each a paid one: the Home tab keeps the lines it has, `progress` says why it has no summary, and finished turns get no quick replies. Each Haiku runs with no tools, MCP servers or settings and no session kept, so nothing in a transcript can make it act.

### Images and videos

An image attached to a message you send, such as a screenshot from your phone, goes to the tab with your text: the relay downloads it to `~/.local/state/slack-relay/files/`, readable only by you, and adds `[Image: <path>]` to what it types, which Claude Code reads as the picture. Only PNG, JPEG, GIF and WebP up to 20 MB, only from Slack's own file host, and only on a message you typed; anything else is answered, not fetched. A video (MP4, MOV or WebM, up to 200 MB) is downloaded the same way, and since Claude can't watch one, the relay takes eight stills spread through it with `ffmpeg`, at most 1280 pixels wide, and types `[Video: <path>]` and then the stills. Without `ffmpeg`, or for a file it can't read, the tab gets only the path and you are told. Downloads are deleted after a week.

Images and videos a tab makes come back the same way. When a turn is posted, the files Claude's own messages in it name (a Playwright screenshot or recording, a chart it saved) are uploaded under it, but not ones only its tool calls name; a path with spaces in it counts when it is written whole between backticks or double quotes. Only files that really are PNG, JPEG, GIF, WebP, MP4, MOV or WebM by their contents, written during that turn, up to 20 MB for an image and 200 MB for a video, up to 20 a turn, ten to a message, and never one you sent. A PNG more than twice as tall as it is wide, such as a long diff's screenshot, is shared as slices about as tall as they are wide, in order, since Slack shrinks an image by its longest side until a tall one's text is unreadable.

### While you're at the computer

The tabs' threads always get everything at once, so each is a complete record whether you were at the computer or not. What notifies you is the one-line notice mentioning you, and those wait while you are at the computer, since the workspace already shows them, and go out in the order they happened once you have been away 30 seconds. You count as away once nothing has been typed or clicked for 30 seconds, at the computer or in Switchboard from another one over SSH, or at once when the screen is locked, unless a key is then pressed in Switchboard from another computer; where the computer can't say (a Linux with no desktop session), the keys pressed in Switchboard alone count. Writing to the relay from Slack counts as being away for five minutes from your latest message, and sends what was held, unless the keyboard or mouse was used, or a key pressed in Switchboard, in the five seconds before it arrived, which means you wrote it in Slack where you work, not on your phone. Replies to what you send, and the results of requests you made from Slack, never wait. Held notices are kept in memory, so stopping the relay drops them.

Slack pushes each message to the relay the moment you send it, so it answers in about a second and costs nothing while you are quiet. Messages sent while its connection to Slack was down are read back from the DM and answered. One sent more than a minute before the relay started is answered with a note that it was missed, so you can send it again if it still stands. The relay keeps the computer from sleeping while it runs, but not its displays. A MacBook with its lid closed and no display connected sleeps anyway.

A lock keeps it to one relay at a time. Its files are in `~/.local/state/slack-relay/`, readable only by you, where earlier versions kept them, so a relay carried over keeps its threads: `state.json`, the hooks' `events.jsonl` (removed when the relay stops on purpose, so turns that end while it restarts are not lost), `relay.lock`, the `outbox/` that `notify` writes to, the `files/` you sent, `haiku.json` with today's Haiku cost, and `relay.log`. The log keeps what the tab shows: where each post went and whether Slack notified you of it, when you count as at the computer or away and why, each notice held, and how long each message waited to be handled. The same failure again within ten minutes is counted, not written, and three old logs are kept beside it, `relay.log.1` to `.3`.

### The Slack app, and keeping it yours

The relay is a Slack app of your own, which you talk to in its Messages tab. Create it at api.slack.com/apps, from this manifest:

```yaml
display_information:
  name: Slack relay
features:
  app_home:
    home_tab_enabled: true
    messages_tab_enabled: true
    messages_tab_read_only_enabled: false
  bot_user:
    display_name: Slack relay
    always_online: false
oauth_config:
  scopes:
    bot:
      - chat:write
      - im:history
      - im:read
      - im:write
      - reactions:write
      - files:read
      - files:write
      - users:read
      - users:read.email
settings:
  event_subscriptions:
    bot_events:
      - app_home_opened
      - message.im
  interactivity:
    is_enabled: true
  socket_mode_enabled: true
```

That is Socket Mode on, so Slack connects through a connection the relay opens and nothing on the computer listens for connections; the bot scopes for posting, reading and opening its DM, reacting to your messages, fetching and uploading files, and reading people's profiles for their photos (`users:read`, and `users:read.email` to find a calendar attendee by their address; an app made before them needs the scopes added and the app reinstalled); only the `message.im` and `app_home_opened` events; Interactivity for the buttons; and the Home and Messages tabs, with messages allowed from the Messages tab. Install it to your workspace, then make an app-level token with the `connections:write` scope (Basic Information, App-Level Tokens).

Store both tokens as secrets, never in a file of your own. Each command reads the token from stdin, so it never appears on a command line: run it, paste the token, and press Ctrl-D.

```sh
switchboard platform secret-set slack-relay-bot-token    # xoxb-…
switchboard platform secret-set slack-relay-app-token    # xapp-…
```

On macOS they go in the Keychain, where the earlier relay's tokens are found as they are. On Linux they go in the desktop's keyring (GNOME Keyring, KWallet) when it is there and unlocked, else in `~/.config/switchboard/secrets.json`, readable only by you.

Anyone in the workspace can open a DM with an app, so the relay acts only on messages typed by one member, named in `~/.config/switchboard/slack-relay.json`:

```json
{ "owner": "<your Slack member ID: Profile → ⋯ → Copy member ID>" }
```

Without the owner or the tokens, the relay doesn't start, and its log says what to set up. Everything else is ignored and logged, with no reply, so the app doesn't even say it is listening: other members, other conversations, other workspaces, bots (the relay's own posts included), edits and deletions. A message has to have been typed, too: other apps can post as you (anything holding your Slack user token, the Slack connector your Claude sessions use included), so a message counts only if it carries the `client_msg_id` Slack's own apps give what you type, which the Web API doesn't let an app set, and no `app_id`. Otherwise a Claude session that read something malicious could post as you in a tab's thread and drive that tab. For the same reason reactions control nothing: Slack can't say whether you added one or an app did as you. Buttons are trusted the same way: a press counts only if it is yours, on a message in that DM, or on the Home tab or a tab's screen, which take only their own buttons, and Slack only reports a press a person made, so no app can press one as you. A form (Message, Skill, Rename, New tab, Autopilot) counts only if you sent it from one of the relay's own screens. The owner comes from the file, never from a message. Everything the relay writes is refused before it leaves the computer unless it is for the app's DM with you, or its Home tab as you see it, whatever asked for it. The app subscribes to no channel events and has no scopes to read channels, so adding it to one shows it nothing.

## Staying up to date

`switchboard update` downloads the newest release of Switchboard for your computer, checks it against the SHA-256 published beside it and against its signature, which only Switchboard's release key makes (its public half is in the binary, so a release whose files were changed after it was published is refused even with its checksum changed to match), runs it once to see that it starts, and only then puts it in place of the binary you ran (a link to the binary stays a link). Anything that fails before that leaves your binary as it was. Then it runs the guided `switchboard setup`, for what the new version brings. `switchboard update --check` says whether there is a newer release, and what it brings, and changes nothing.

The running server goes on with the old version until it restarts on the new one. Once the release is in place, `switchboard update` asks whether to do that now, keeping every tab and what runs in it ([Restarting on a new build](#restarting-on-a-new-build)); Enter is yes, and with no terminal to answer (piped, or run by a script) it only says how. Settings' "Offer to restart after an update" (`upgrade_after_update`, on) off, it never asks, and says how.

A release build of Switchboard looks for a new release by itself: each time a terminal attaches, and every hour. When there is one, a box lists the releases newer than yours, newest first, each with its age and notes, over two options. "Update now" opens a tab that runs the update and its setup, then offers the restart, and stays until you press Enter. "Not now", or Esc, puts it off: the hourly look doesn't ask about that release again, only about a newer one, while the look as a terminal attaches asks whenever something newer is out. A box left unanswered is asked about again the next hour, and a newer one takes its place. Settings' "Update check" (`update_check`) chooses when it looks: "open, hourly" (`hourly`, the default), "open only" (`open`), or "off" (`off`).

`M-p`'s "Update Switchboard" looks now, whatever that setting says, and says so when there is nothing newer or it can't look. `M-p`'s "Run Switchboard's setup" opens a tab running the guided `switchboard setup`, to put in place what isn't, or to say yes to a part you said no to before; "Check Switchboard's setup" and "Run a part of Switchboard's setup" are in [More in the palette](#more-in-the-palette).

When `~/.config/switchboard/claude.json` names a git repository for your Claude rules, each look the setting allows (as a terminal attaches, every hour, or asked for) also pulls that repository's clone, fast-forward only, with `switchboard setup claude-md`, which writes `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` again if it must. This happens whether or not your build looks for releases, since your rules change apart from Switchboard. It never asks for a password: git's prompt and askpass are off, and so is ssh's askpass, so a pull that needs one fails quietly and is tried again at the next look.

A release's files are downloaded over HTTPS only, every redirect included. A release published before releases were signed (0.7.0 and earlier) has no signature, and is never offered.

A `switchboard` you built from source with cargo is kept current by git and cargo: its server never looks for releases, and `switchboard update` (and `M-p`'s "Update Switchboard", which says what is out and how to take it) leaves it alone unless you add `--force`. After each build, run `switchboard setup`: the skills, hooks, Claude Code's settings and Switchboard's part of CLAUDE.md are compiled into the binary and reach the plugin and `~/.claude` only through the setup (the plugin also as the build's server starts). The first time a new build attaches (`switchboard`), it does this by itself, quietly, for the parts you have set up, and records the build in `~/.local/share/switchboard/setup-build` so it does it once; a part you said no to is left for the guided setup to ask about, and it moves nothing between the plugin and `~/.claude` (an install an earlier version made in `~/.claude` stays there, the setting recorded off, until the guided setup asks). A run with a state directory of its own (`SWITCHBOARD_STATE_DIR`) leaves `~/.claude` and the plugin as they are, its server too. `switchboard setup --refresh` does the same by hand. With the new build in place of the one the server started from, `switchboard upgrade` restarts the server on it.

A build from a clone also reads those assets from the clone it was built in while it is there (its main clone once the worktree it was built in is gone): the skills, Claude Code's settings, Switchboard's part of CLAUDE.md and the list of settings in its `assets/` folder, falling back to what the binary carries for any file the clone lacks or, for a JSON file, that doesn't parse. So a skill or an instruction landed on the clone's branch is live without a rebuild: the server looks at the clone every 10 seconds and, when a skill, Claude's settings or the part changed there, brings the plugin and the parts you set up up to date as a new build's first run does (in `~/.claude` too, the old way), for the Claude sessions started after it, and each `switchboard claude-context` reads the part as it is. A clone holding someone else's PR is never read from, and a release build has no clone. `switchboard --version` names the folder it reads, and `switchboard setup` says where the plugin's skills and hooks came from. Settings' "Skills and instructions from the clone" (`assets_from_checkout`, on) off, every build uses what it carries.

### Restarting on a new build

`switchboard upgrade`, or `M-p`'s "Restart Switchboard on the latest main", restarts the server on the binary at the path it was started from, as that file is now, without ending anything: every pane's program goes on running, a Claude session mid-turn, a build or a shell alike, and every terminal attached stays attached. The server replaces itself with the new build in the same process, so its programs stay its own and never see their terminal close, and hands it every pane's terminal, every attached terminal's connection, its socket and what each pane showed; the new build takes them up as they are, shows each pane as it was (its history, colours, the screen of a full-screen program, where its cursor was), tells each program its size again, so a full-screen one draws itself again, and redraws every screen. Tabs, splits, names, options, pane ids and what each Claude was doing are the same after; what was open on top (a menu, mission control, copy mode) is closed. `switchboard upgrade` prints how it went, and the key says so in a box.

A build made from a clone knows the commit it was built from, and counts the commits on its main clone's default branch (as origin last answered) that it lacks: as the server starts, every hour, and as soon as a tab lands (`land-lock landed`), with no fetch of its own. While main is ahead, one notification says so, "Switchboard's main has 3 landings this build lacks", its count updated as more land rather than one a landing, and its menu's first option is "Restart Switchboard on the latest main". Dismissed, it stays away until the next landing; once the server restarts on a build at or past main, it is gone. Settings' "Say when main is ahead of this build" (`build_behind_notice`) turns it off.

While `M-p`'s restart builds, nothing waits on it: keys still reach the tab, and with UX 2.0 the sidebar's second line (under the tab count) is its build row, the spinner, the step it is on (Updating, Building, Installing), a meter of the crates built over how many the last build of that checkout counted (none the first time) and the time so far at the right edge, then "Restarting  every pane kept". With UX 2.0 off the same words follow the ☕ on the status line, and with the sidebar hidden a box says the build has begun, as before. A click on the row, or pressing the restart again while it builds, opens its steps beside it: where the checkout is ("main at 1a2b3c4"), each step with its mark (✓ Fetched with how many commits it brought and how long, Building with the crates so far, Install with where it goes, Restart), and the meter. The box takes no keys but `M-Esc` and dims nothing, so what you type still reaches the tab; `M-Esc`, a click anywhere, pressing the restart once more or `M-p`'s "Hide the restart's progress" closes it, the build going on, and it closes itself when the build ends. A failed build leaves "✕ Build failed · click for why" in red on the row ("Not restarted" when it stopped before cargo, on an unclean clone say) until you click it, which shows the failing step and cargo's error lines; with UX 2.0 off it is said in a box. After the restart the row says "✓ Restarted on <commit>" for ten seconds, and nothing is added to the notifications. The crates each checkout's last build counted are kept in `restart-build.json` in the state directory.

The key builds first. A `switchboard` built from a clone knows the main clone of the checkout it was built in, and builds that rather than the checkout, since a worktree is removed once its work lands. It brings the main clone up to date first: when it isn't on its default branch (`origin/HEAD`, else `main`), has uncommitted changes, or has commits origin doesn't, the key says so and builds nothing; otherwise it fetches origin (while Settings' GitHub is on) and fast-forwards to it, and when the fetch fails (offline) or GitHub is off builds the clone as it is, checked against what it last fetched, and says it didn't fetch after the restart's message. Then it runs `cargo build --release --locked` there (cargo rebuilds only what changed, so with nothing new it takes a second) and, when the server runs a copy installed elsewhere (`~/.local/bin/switchboard`), puts the new build in its place. Cargo runs the checkout's own code, so, as with `switchboard try`, it builds only a checkout of yours or one listed in `~/.config/switchboard/trusted-dirs`, never a PR `wtpr --untrusted` set up. A release build has nothing to build, so the key takes the binary on disk as it is; `switchboard update` is how a new release gets there. In a `switchboard try` the key builds the worktree under test, as it is. A binary already put in place of the server's since it started (a build of another worktree installed by hand) is restarted on without building only when it is the latest: built from the commit the main clone is at once brought up to date, or from one ahead of it, by the commit its `--version` says. One that lacks main's commits (a tab installed its landing's build, and more landed after it), or whose commit can't be read, is built over in the same press. The restart's message ends with which it was: "It was built for this restart." or "It was already built, so nothing was built." Otherwise it restarts only when the binary at the server's path is no longer the one it started from, and otherwise says "Already on the latest build". A server running from a cargo target directory (`target/debug`, or one `CARGO_TARGET_DIR` shares between worktrees, whose builds and tests write over it) never restarts on that path: the key puts the build in `~/.local/bin/switchboard` when there is one, else in a copy at `~/.cache/switchboard/build/switchboard`, and restarts on that, or, when a build was installed in `~/.local/bin` since the server started and is the latest in the same sense, restarts on it as it is; a build that fails says why, and the server goes on as it was. Settings' "Build before restarting" (`build_before_restart`, on) off, the key builds nothing. `switchboard upgrade` never builds and always restarts.

Before it stops anything it asks the new binary whether it can take over (`switchboard server --handover-version`), and only one that can is started: a build from before this was possible, or one that hands over differently, is refused, saying why, and the server goes on as it was, as it does when anything else stops the restart. Notes, `M-d`'s diff and the views are programs in their panes, so they keep the build they started with until next opened; and a tab's shell keeps the environment it started with.

### Trying a change

`switchboard try` tries a clone or worktree of Switchboard's source without touching the workspace you use:

```text
switchboard try              the worktree you are in, attached in this terminal
switchboard try <dir>        another worktree
switchboard try --print      set it up and print the environment for another
                             terminal: eval "$(switchboard try --print)", then
                             switchboard
switchboard try --detached   start its server with no client and print the
                             command that reaches it
switchboard try --clean      end its server and remove its home (name a
                             removed worktree by the path it had)
switchboard try --clean-all  the same for every try of yours
switchboard try --sealed [<dir>]
                             someone else's code, in a sandbox that holds
                             nothing of yours (macOS only)
switchboard try --sealed <dir> --run cargo test
                             a command in it, the PR's tests here
```

It builds the worktree with cargo (nothing to do when the build is up to date), then runs that build under a home of its own, `/tmp/sb-<hash>`, made only you can read and refused when something else is there (a link, or a folder that isn't yours): its own settings, state, server and socket, and its own Switchboard plugin, with the statusline it draws, which the build's `switchboard setup plugin claude claude-md skills` lays out there, so setup is tried too (with "Load Switchboard as a plugin" off in the settings it copies, its own `~/.claude` hooks, statusline, permissions, CLAUDE.md and skills instead). Its `~/.local/bin/switchboard` is the build, first on its PATH, so Claude Code's hooks and statusline in its panes reach the try's server. Running it again sets the home up again and keeps what the try saved. Attaching from one of Switchboard's own panes is refused, since the workspace around it would take every key: use a terminal of its own, `--print`, or `--detached`, which is for a Claude working in the worktree: it prints the prefix to put before `send-keys`, `capture-pane` and `list` to press keys in the try and read its screen.

What isn't Switchboard's comes from your real home. Linked: `~/Library/Keychains` on a Mac (without it Claude Code isn't signed in and the Keychain's secrets aren't found), your dotfiles (`.ssh`, `.gitconfig`, shell history), your `~/.config` folders but Switchboard's (gh's sign-in among them), `~/.local/share` but Switchboard's, the programs in `~/.local/bin` that aren't Switchboard's, Claude's plugins and its sign-in on Linux, and your own Claude skills and hooks. Copied: your shell's startup files (each ending by moving any XDG, notes or Switchboard path it set in your home to the same place in the try's), `~/.claude.json`, `~/.claude/settings.json` and `settings.local.json`, `~/.config/switchboard` and the clone your CLAUDE.md's rules come from, so nothing the try does writes to yours. Fresh: `~/.local/state`, `~/.cache`, Claude's sessions and transcripts, and `~/Notes`. Its settings turn off what would duplicate your workspace or spend on its own: the Slack look and view, the standup note, banners, the update check, restoring saved tabs and removing landed worktrees (the try's server knows none of your real tabs), with the todo note and meeting notes starter routines made paused; turn any back on in its Settings. Linked things are yours, so an edit made through them (a git config, gh signing in again) is made to the real one, and keys sent in a try act with your real credentials.

Since its build and setup run with those credentials, it tries only code of yours: a worktree whose `.git` leads back to it, whose commits beyond its origin's branches are on no other remote and were written and committed under your git email, and which isn't partway through a merge, rebase, cherry-pick or revert. Anything else, such as a fork's pull request checked out with `wtpr --trusted`, is refused, saying why, until you have read it and listed its path in `~/.config/switchboard/trusted-dirs`. A PR checked out untrusted (`wtpr`'s default for a fork's) is tried only sealed, whatever `trusted-dirs` says.

#### Sealed

`--sealed` tries someone else's code, a PR `wtpr --untrusted` checked out, without any of it running outside a sandbox: macOS's own (`sandbox-exec`), which every process of the try runs in, the build, the build's own setup, its server and everything the server starts, and every client of it, since a client is the PR's binary too. Linux has no sandbox without installing another program, so there `--sealed` refuses, saying so; read the PR with `switchboard pr-risk` and review it with `switchboard codex-review -C`, and try it on a Mac. Inside the sandbox nothing can read your home but the worktree, its git directory and the toolchain (nor see `~/.cargo`, whose settings cargo would otherwise fail to read), or your per-user temporary and cache folders; write anywhere but the try's home and `/tmp` (not the worktree, its git directory, Claude's or tmux's folders of yours there, nor another try's home, nor a `.git`, `.cargo`, `CLAUDE.md`, `package.json` or the like in `/tmp` itself, which a program of yours run anywhere under `/tmp` would find and take up); reach the network or listen on it; connect to any socket but its own (your Switchboard's, ssh-agent's, the Notes app's and every Neovim's, which take commands from whoever connects); signal any process of yours outside it; send an Apple Event; or ask the Keychain, the clipboard, Launch Services (so no `open`) or the window server. Its rules are written by `switchboard try` into `~/.cache/switchboard/sealed/`, where nothing in the try can write, and given to `sandbox-exec` on each run.

Nothing of yours is in its home: a fresh `~/.config/switchboard` whose settings turn off every feature that reaches a service or Claude, an empty `~/.claude` (Claude isn't signed in there), `~/Notes` with a few made-up notes, and `~/code` with two made-up repositories, one with a branch to see in `M-d`. Its environment is made from nothing, so no token or `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` comes along. Since the try's code can change anything in its home, nothing outside the sandbox writes there once any of it has run: a try still running is only attached to, and any other is stopped, removed and made again from nothing, laid out beside where it goes and moved into place whole.

Building runs the PR's code (its `build.rs` and proc macros), so cargo builds inside the sandbox, offline and locked, into the try's home, with your main clone's toolchain, run directly so the PR's `rust-toolchain.toml` picks nothing. The crates are fetched first, outside the sandbox (`cargo fetch` runs none of their code), into `~/.cache/switchboard/sealed/cargo`, from a folder outside your home and with no rustc wrapper, so none of your cargo config applies (a `rustc-wrapper` such as sccache in `~/.cargo/config.toml` included, which the build inside leaves out too), only from crates.io and each with its checksum in `Cargo.lock`: a git, path or other registry's dependency, a `[patch]` or `[replace]`, or a `Cargo.lock` not as cargo writes it is refused, since cargo would fetch from wherever the PR says. When its `Cargo.lock` differs from your main clone's, only `--fetch` fetches it, once you have read the change. The build's output is shown with its control characters written out, as `pr-risk` shows the PR's lines; what the try draws once you attach, and what `--run`'s command prints, reach your terminal as any program's do. A try is built again each time it is made again, so a sealed try's first start takes a full build.

`--print` prints the command that attaches to it, sealed, for another terminal, and `--detached` starts its server with no client (a session made in it by Switchboard's own binary in a sandbox that reaches that socket alone) and prints the prefix that drives it, the try's binary in the sandbox. `--run <command>...` runs a command there from the worktree and exits with its status, the PR's tests for one (`--run cargo test`), with localhost (any service listening there, yours included) and the sockets in a folder under `/tmp` named as the tests name theirs allowed too. A `--run` again on the same worktree takes up the home the last one made, its crates and build kept, while its `Cargo.lock` is the same; otherwise the home is made again from nothing. `--clean` stops its server from the same small sandbox, removes its home (a link the try put in its place removed as a link) and its rules. It runs a worktree `wtpr --untrusted` made, one of your own, or one listed in `trusted-dirs`.

## The demo

`switchboard demo` runs the workspace on made-up data, to see what it does before setting anything up, to show it to someone, or to test a view without anyone's screen:

```text
switchboard demo               lay it out (the first time) and attach in this terminal
switchboard demo --detached    start its server with no client and print the prefix
                               that reaches it, for list, send-keys and headless
switchboard demo --print       print the environment for another terminal:
                               eval "$(switchboard demo --print)", then switchboard
switchboard demo --reset       lay its data out again from nothing
switchboard demo --clean       end its server and services and remove its home
switchboard demo --tour <dir> [--size <cols>x<rows>]
                               walk its boards with a headless client and save a
                               picture of each in <dir>, at 200x56 by default:
                               the workspace, a tab's history, PR and ticket,
                               M-a, the palette, M-j, the sidebar filtered to a
                               repository, Notes' finder, every mission control
                               view, and
                               the menus and boxes its views open
```

Its home is `/tmp/sb-demo-<uid>`, made only you can read, and refused when something else is there (a link, or a folder that isn't yours). It links nothing of yours: no Keychain, gh sign-in, `~/.config`, Claude sign-in, dotfiles or `~/.local/share`, and its server runs with an environment made from nothing but your terminal's own variables, so no token or agent socket of yours comes along. It runs the binary you ran, building nothing: a demo server another build started (one left running while you rebuilt) is ended and started again on this one, its data kept, so a tour or a script always reaches the build it was run from.

What it shows is made up: three repositories under `acme/`, each in `~/code` with a short history and an origin of its own in the home, and a worktree in `~/code/worktrees` for each PR and for a branch landing straight on main; eight agent tabs on those worktrees, a shell (with a reminder) and Notes on a notes folder with a checklist, its plan and twenty notes opened lately. Paths show from the home, as `~/code/…`, and nothing on its screens names a particular agent but Switchboard's own labels. GitHub, Linear and Slack are stand-ins on this computer (`switchboard demo-services`, beside the server), answering with the demo's PRs (in each of the PR board's columns, with checks passing, failing and running), tickets (`ABC-…`), Linear inbox and Slack's sign-in, as strict as the real ones about how they are asked, so a lookup the demo answers wrong is kept in its `~/.local/state/demo/refused.log`; a view whose lookup the demo has no answer for shows nothing. The calendar and Slack views read what `/today` and `/slack-updates` would have saved: today's meetings, one about to start, each with who is invited and an agenda and one naming a demo tab's ticket, and a mention, a direct message and a thread. The Usage view has a made-up week: each Claude tab's spend through today and the six days before (and a tab closed since), the 5-hour and weekly limits, and the request audit to GitHub, Linear, Slack and Anthropic by hour, all timed from when the demo was laid out. So that every board of the design has something to show, it also has two routines, paused so a demo left running doesn't run them, the ticket triage's last run failed for a tool it wasn't allowed; two tabs closed earlier and a past session in the worktree no tab has, beside a merged worktree, for Reopen; autopilot on the demo note, paused so it opens no tab, with a tab of its own working and a landing of today's; a pinned tab; and reminders on two tabs.

The tour's pictures are drawn as Ghostty draws the workspace: its headless client attaches as Ghostty, so the server draws for it in truecolor with its images (the charts, meters, switches and the boxes' shadows) and the half circles, and each picture is the screen in a monospace font sized to the cell, at twice the pixels, with those images and half circles in their cells. It starts once the demo's agents have asked their questions, about twenty seconds after a demo starts, and waits longer for each board the busier the computer is. A board that never shows stops it: the screen it gave up on is saved as that board's `.txt`, and its last lines are printed with the text it waited for.

The programs the workspace runs by name are stand-ins on its PATH (`switchboard demo-stand-in`): a link or file opened goes to the demo's `~/.local/state/demo/desktop-log`, never your desktop, and what the workspace copies through the clipboard tools to its `clipboard` file (a copy you make yourself in the terminal attached to it, with `y` in copy mode, reaches that terminal's clipboard as it would in any program); `gh` answers `auth token` alone, `security` reads the demo's own `secrets.json` rather than your Keychain; and `claude` is a stand-in that plays a script in each tab, sending Switchboard's hooks as Claude Code does, so the sidebar has a tab in each of its groups: in Needs you, one that finishes turns, one waiting on your yes to a push and one asking a question, which Enter answers; in Working, one that goes on working; in Review, one in `/review-loop`; in CI, one in `/ci-loop` waiting on its PR's checks; in Landing, one in `/queue-and-clean` holding its repository's landing lock (`land-lock take`, run for real); and in Merged, one resumed on a merged PR's branch, idle. It draws itself as a generic coding agent, not as Claude Code: a welcome box, `●` before each tool call and reply with the result under `└`, a spinner while a turn runs, and a rounded input box pinned to the pane's bottom rows with `? for shortcuts` and the statusline (`switchboard statusline`, run as Claude Code runs it) under it, the permission and question dialogs in its place. It reaches no model: `claude -p` is refused, and past its script it answers that it is a stand-in. The PR and ticket image views find no Chrome in the demo, since their page loads its scripts from the web; a page you open yourself in the link view is still fetched from where its link points.
